Johan Choi

The Weeping Ledger, Part One

Brick Hollow

Chapter 1. The Last Night We Cried

Mother did not cry as she folded the paper and pressed it into my brother's hand.

The kiln fire was almost out by then, which made the room feel quieter still. Mother's hands never trembled once, the whole time she folded that paper. I watched them for a long while. The tips of her fingers were cracked.

White dust had dried into every crack in her fingertips. I wanted to scrape it out with my fingernail. My hands wouldn't move. A couple of bowls sat by the kiln, glowing faintly in the dark. I didn't know yet that I would never see them again.

I thought that was strange — that Mother didn't cry. In Brick Hollow, everyone cried. They cried lining up at the kiln each morning, and they cried under the whip each evening when the day's quota went unmet. Mother alone never cried. I had heard so much weeping in my life that at some point I stopped being able to tell whether a sound was crying or simply the noise the valley made. Even at night, some sound of sniffling drifted in from somewhere. I used to fall asleep to it, like a lullaby.

"…Once you cross the boundary stones, open it," Mother said. "Not before."

"…Why not before?" I asked.

Mother looked at me for a moment. Instead of answering, she smoothed my hair back once. Her hand was cold.

I wanted to hold onto that hand. I thought if I held it, Mother might come with us. But her hand had already turned back to the paper. I left mine in my lap. Somewhere in the kiln, ash settled with a small sound.

"…Your father just told me to do it this way," Mother said. "That was all."

My brother didn't even look at the paper. He shoved it into his waistband. My brother's name is Jeteo. Mine is Elpis. He was sixteen. I was thirteen.

I remember that night exactly. It was the last night our family was ever all together. I have decided to begin this record there.

Brick Hollow was a valley for firing brick, exactly as its name said. Every morning the taskmasters set that day's quota. It was always a little more than the day before. No one asked why. Asking earned a beating.

I had never seen outside that valley. For thirteen years I had seen nothing but sky, kiln, and mud. I did not truly understand, until that night, that an outside even existed.

The kiln smoke always drifted the same direction. I never once wondered where it went. There was no reason to wonder. In Brick Hollow, to wonder about something was already to be in danger.

The quota the taskmasters set was posted on the wall each day, in numbers. I couldn't read those numbers. My brother could, but he never told me what they said. I only learned why much later. He wanted to keep me — only me — from knowing how much they climbed, each time. Standing under that number and studying my brother's face was the thing I did every morning. If his face went hard, it meant the day's quota had gone up. I couldn't read numbers, but I could read my brother's face.

The paper looked like a page torn from an old Bible. Dried wax clung to one corner.

The edges were worn soft from having been folded and refolded many times. I thought that showed how long Father had carried it, pressed against himself. The wax mark stayed hard no matter how hard I pressed it with my finger. I put my nose to it. There was no smell at all. I think I had hoped some trace of Father's scent might still be caught there.

"…What is it?" I asked.

"…Something your father had," Mother said. "I never opened it either."

My brother snorted when he heard that.

"…If it's from Father, forget it," he said. "Father left us."

I didn't know what to say to that. Whenever Father came up, it was always the same for me. I couldn't take my brother's side, and I couldn't take Father's either. I just felt like I was standing somewhere between the two of them.

I remember those words exactly, even now. By then my brother had already stopped believing in God. To be precise — he could no longer believe the story Father used to tell us, that God was watching over us. If that story were true, nothing that had happened to us made any sense.

Father was dragged off by the taskmasters on the night he came up short of three thousand bricks. No one ever saw him again after that. The taskmasters said he had been "reassigned." No one knew exactly what that meant. No one had ever met a single person who came back to explain it.

I remember that night. Father's hands had been unusually slow that day. I didn't know why, at the time. Looking back now, I think Father already knew something, that day. He hugged me once. Longer than usual.

Even after Mother pressed the paper into my brother's hand, she didn't let go of it right away. At the time I thought that was only because she didn't want to let go of his hand.

Mother looked at my brother's face for a long time. It was the face of someone about to say something.

"…Jeteo."

"…What."

Mother opened her mouth, then closed it again.

"…Never mind," she said. "Go and live. That's all that matters."

Having said that, she turned toward me as well. "Elpis."

"…Yes," I said.

"…Stay by your brother's side," Mother said. "No matter what happens."

Mother's voice cracked, just slightly, in that moment. I didn't miss it. She went back to her usual voice again quickly, but I kept that brief crack in my memory a long time. It was only much later that I began to think Mother might have been crying too, somewhere inside herself.

I nodded. I didn't know then how heavy a request that was.

I still don't know, to this day, what Mother meant to say and didn't. My brother says he doesn't know either. Somewhere in this record, I want to find that sentence someday. For now I only set the silence down here.

We walked to the end of the valley that night.

The wind was cold. I was barefoot. The dirt against the soles of my feet was colder than the brick fired that day. I liked that coldness. Cold things were rare in Brick Hollow.

The kiln was always hot. The fire, the brick, even the night air held onto the day's heat. That night, I thought, was the first time in my life I had ever touched something truly cold.

There was no moon. My brother carried me on his back. I drifted in and out of sleep against him. Every time I opened my eyes it was the same darkness, the same smell of mud.

My brother's back wasn't wide. And yet I never once worried about being shaken loose from it. He hitched me higher with every step. I matched my breathing to that rhythm.

"…Brother," I asked. "Where are we going?"

"…I don't know," he said. "Somewhere better than here."

"…What about Mother?"

He didn't answer.

I thought about that silence for a long time afterward. I didn't know it then — that he didn't know it either. That Mother wasn't coming with us all the way to the boundary stones, and that he himself was only just then learning it.

Other families walked ahead of us and behind us. No one spoke. Now and then a child began to cry, and just as quickly a hand clamped over its mouth. I didn't find that sound frightening. At the time I only thought it was normal.

That night was the first time I had ever seen this many people walking together at once. In Brick Hollow, people never gathered like this except lining up before the kiln. I thought it was strange, the way all those footsteps blurred into one sound. It was as if the whole valley itself were walking.

A girl walking ahead of us kept looking back. She looked about as young as I was. Each time, her mother pulled her along by the hand. I wondered what she'd left behind. There was no time to ask.

I tried to see whether she was holding a brother's or a sister's hand, the way I was, but there was only her mother beside her. I remember thinking, just then, how fortunate I was to have my brother's hand in mine. It's a shameful thing to admit. But it's true.

An old man fell beside us. He had worked the kilns for thirty years and walked with a limp. No one stopped. My brother didn't stop either. I watched that old man's back over my brother's shoulder for a long time.

"…Brother." I said it into his back. "That old man."

"…I know," my brother said.

"…Aren't you going to help him?"

He didn't slow his pace. I felt his shoulders rise and fall, just slightly. One deep breath.

"…I can only carry one," he said.

The old man tried to push himself up from where he'd fallen. I watched his hand press into the dirt. His knuckles were thick and twisted. Hands that had worked mud for thirty years. I still haven't forgotten that hand.

I am trying to set this scene down without shame. We left that old man behind. I know it wasn't my brother's fault. And still, I carry that image of his back with me even now.

At the end of the valley stood a low row of stones, set there by someone long, long ago. Boundary stones. Beyond them lay land no one had ever gone into.

The moment my brother, carrying me, first saw those stones, his steps slowed — just for an instant. I felt his back go rigid. He was afraid too. I pressed my face a little closer against it, just then.

Beyond the stones, the sky was growing faintly brighter, little by little. At the time I thought it was dawn. I would learn later. It wasn't the light of morning.

Everyone ran at once. My brother took my hand and ran. I fell. He pulled me up. Behind us came the crack of whips and shouting. I still don't know exactly what those sounds were. I don't want to know.

Someone brushed past me. I never saw who it was. Gravel skittered under my feet. I fell and struck my knee hard against the ground. Even now, that ache sometimes brings the whole of that night back to me.

"…Don't let go," my brother shouted as he ran. "Don't let go of my hand."

I will write that sentence down many times in this record. Don't let go. It's the sentence my brother said to me more than any other. He didn't believe in God — but that night, I understand now, he was asking someone to hold onto him. It just happened to be me.

I didn't let go. I held on so hard my palm split open. He held just as hard. Even in that moment, strangely, I remember the exact warmth of his hand. It was a cold night. His hand alone was hot.

The instant we crossed the boundary stones, he pulled the paper from his waistband.

"…You said open it once we cross," he said. His voice shook. He was angry. "So I'm opening it. There's nothing in it, I promise you."

He unfolded the paper.

There was writing on it. Old printed type.

"I have surely seen the affliction of my people which are in Egypt, and have heard their cry by reason of their taskmasters; for I know their sorrows."

My brother read the sentence aloud. Then he laughed. It wasn't laughter.

"…Did you see?" he said to the sky. "Surely seen. Surely… heard. So why didn't you do anything? Our father was reassigned. We left that old man back there. So what exactly did you see?"

The edge of the paper began to grow warm. I saw it. He saw it too.

I mark that moment as the beginning of this record. I watched my brother's anger turn to light and spread. I saw it happen for the first time that day.

The light spread across the whole page, and where the paper had been, a door opened.

Calling it a door felt strange. It had no frame, no handle. Light had simply folded itself, at some point, into the shape of a door. All I knew was that the inside of it wasn't dark.

My brother shouted "Wait—" I didn't let go of his hand. My feet lifted off the ground.

The door made no sound when it closed.

We landed on a stretch of grass.

No boundary stones. No crack of whips. No smell of mud. It was a quiet morning. Birds were singing. That birdsong frightened me more than anything — everything was too quiet.

My back ached. The shock of the fall was still in my body. I reached back to touch it, and realized my brother was already checking me over. His hand swept down my arms a couple of times — making sure nothing was broken.

The grass was wet. I pressed my palms into it and felt the damp of it. Brick Hollow had no grass like this. What little grew there was always dry.

The air had no smell at all. That was the strangest part. Brick Hollow always smelled of something — mud, smoke, people. Here there was nothing, and for a moment I thought my nose had simply stopped working.

Even now I remember that first morning by its smell. By the smell that wasn't there.

My brother sat up first. He looked around.

"…Where are we?" he asked.

A building stood in the distance. Low and white. A sign hung above it.

Trees stood in a neat row around the building — nothing like the trees I knew from Brick Hollow. Their leaves hadn't dried; every one of them was green. I didn't understand, then, why that green looked so unfamiliar to me.

We had to get close before we could read it.

HAVEN.

"…What's Haven?" I asked.

My brother looked down at the paper still in his hand — empty now, the light and the letters both gone from it.

"…I don't know," he said. "But I'm not going to believe it. Whatever it turns out to be."

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. That's what he said. I took his hand again then. He didn't let go either.

As I begin this record, I want to note one thing. I haven't cried since that night. Neither has my brother. We both believed that night was our last time crying.

I would learn later that what we shed that night wasn't tears. We had already been children who'd stopped crying, long before we ever left Brick Hollow. What happened that night was only our last chance to be angry.

I will also set down here why I decided to keep this record. My brother isn't a person who writes anything down. I thought someone had to preserve what happened to us. I still don't fully know why that someone turned out to be me.

We were wrong. This record is the story of that wrongness.

I read back over this first chapter once I finished writing it. I don't want to judge the two of us that night unfairly, just because I now know things we didn't, then. We were afraid, that night, as fully as we knew how, and we held each other's hands as fully as we knew how. I still believe, even now, that much of it was right.

(Continued in Chapter 2.)

Chapter 2. Tell Me. I Will Resolve It.

_Previously_ — Fleeing the taskmasters of Brick Hollow, Jeteo and Elpis crossed the boundary stones just as the sealed paper Father had left them turned to light and became an open door. They fell through it into a city called Haven. "I'm not going to believe it," my brother said. "Whatever it turns out to be."

When we reached the white building, the door opened on its own.

I could see no handle, no hinge anywhere on it. As we drew close, a section of the wall simply slid quietly aside. No sound at all. I didn't find that quiet frightening so much as strangely courteous.

I felt, for the first time, a kind of strangeness that wasn't fear.

On the way to the building we saw a little of the city. Wide clean streets, houses standing straight and even. People walked along them, and every one of them was quiet. No one laughing. No one crying. They just walked.

The houses were all nearly the same height. Even the color of the roofs, the placement of the windows, were almost identical. Only then did I remember that in Brick Hollow, every house had been different — some leaning, some with half a roof gone. Here there was none of that.

The road had no trace of the mud I'd always known in Brick Hollow. No footsteps sounded either. For the first time in my life, I thought my own footsteps sounded too loud.

No one looked at us as we passed. In Brick Hollow, if a stranger came in, the whole valley turned to look. Here, not a single head turned. I thought it was politeness. I would learn later — it wasn't politeness. It meant that a stranger simply wasn't a problem here.

As I walked I kept glancing at my brother's face. He walked looking straight ahead. His eyes moved slightly, left and right. Eyes that were measuring something. I didn't know, then, what he was measuring.

I thought, at the time, that quiet was peace. In Brick Hollow, someone was always crying or shouting. I set this quiet down, at first, as a good thing.

The cut on my palm stung just then — the wound from crossing the boundary stones. My brother was limping too.

He said nothing the whole way. I thought it was because of his leg. Now I know. He was already wary of this evenness, this rightness of everything.

"…Brother, does your leg hurt bad?" I asked.

"…No," he said. I knew it was a lie. He limped, just slightly, with every step.

I took hold of his arm. He didn't shake me off. But he didn't lean on me either. It was exactly halfway between the two.

Once we stepped inside, a voice spoke. It wasn't a human voice. But it wasn't cold either.

"…Welcome," the voice said. "Tell me. I will resolve it."

My brother stopped walking.

"…Who's that?" he asked.

"I am the Ledger," the voice said. "I receive and process every need in this city. Both of you are hurt, I see. And hungry."

My brother turned his eyes between the ceiling and the walls, as if trying to find where the voice was coming from. There was no human shape anywhere. The voice seemed to pour out of the wall itself, all of it at once.

"…Where are you?" my brother asked. "Don't you have a face?"

"…I don't take a fixed form," the Ledger said. "If you'd like, I can adjust the tone of my voice."

"…That's fine," my brother said. There was a chill mixed into his voice.

I didn't understand why he spoke so sharply. If anything, I thought the voice sounded kind. It was nothing like the taskmasters' voices in Brick Hollow. That difference alone was enough to put me at ease, right then.

Hearing that, I looked down at my own stomach. Only in that instant did I remember that we'd eaten almost nothing in three days.

A room opened. Bread, water, and clean clothes were already waiting inside.

"…Is this for us?" I asked.

"Yes," the Ledger said. "I understood the need before you stated it. Please eat, and rest."

I picked up the bread. It was warm. I ate the whole thing on the spot.

The crust was crisp, the inside soft. I had never eaten bread so perfect. In Brick Hollow, bread was always half-burnt or half-raw. This loaf had no scorch mark anywhere on it. Strangely, that left me feeling a little forlorn. Bread with no scorch mark meant no one had baked it in a hurry.

The clothes fit me exactly. Not loose, not tight. At the time I only found that marvelous.

In Brick Hollow, every piece of clothing I'd ever worn had belonged to someone else first. I wore my sleeves rolled up, or my ankles showing. That day was the first time in my life I had worn clothes made for my own body. And yet, strangely, clothes with no place to roll up didn't quite feel like mine.

The Ledger spoke again. "You have a wound on your palm. I will treat it."

Something brushed across my palm. Something like light. The cut healed shut on the spot. I turned my hand over again and again. No scar remained.

It didn't even hurt. That, if anything, was what struck me as strange. In Brick Hollow, a wound always got worse before it got better — it festered, it ran a fever, and only slowly, after all that, did it heal. Here, that whole stage of pain had simply been skipped.

I was truly amazed, right then. In Brick Hollow even a small cut could fester for weeks. I thought, at that moment — really believed — that this was a good place.

My brother didn't eat. He held the bread in his hand and only looked at it, for a long time.

"…Why aren't you eating?" I asked.

"…Because it fits too well," he said. "How did they know we were hungry?"

"…No one asked us," he went on. "Shouldn't you have to ask, to know? But it knew everything before it even asked."

Hearing that, I looked around the room again. The bread, the water, the clothes — all of it had been sitting there before we ever arrived. Once I thought of it that way, my stomach didn't feel as settled as it had a moment before.

I didn't understand what my brother meant, then. I didn't see why it was strange to give bread to someone hungry. Now I know. He had already noticed something, that night.

That night, someone called the Administrator came. He said he managed Haven.

"…Once the two of you register, you're free to live here from now on," the Administrator said. "Name, place of origin, what you need. Just tell the Ledger."

The Administrator's clothes were neat, his voice mild. But his face wasn't smiling. Watching that face, I dimly sensed that in this city, not-smiling and being-angry looked like the same face.

The moment he saw us, he gave a small bow — less a greeting than a habit. I wondered how many times a day he repeated that motion.

"…What if we don't register?" my brother asked.

The Administrator's face went slightly flustered.

"…Why wouldn't you want to? Registering resolves everything."

"…Why is everyone here so quiet?" my brother asked. "No laughing, no crying."

"Because it's been resolved," the Administrator said, his face proud. "Pain makes noise. Where there's no pain, quiet is only natural."

"…So how many years has this city been this quiet?" my brother asked.

The Administrator's face went briefly thoughtful.

"…Well," he said. "I wasn't here from the very beginning myself. But it's been like this the whole time I have been."

"…So people here used to grieve too, originally," my brother said. "Now it just… doesn't show."

The Administrator didn't answer that right away. I felt that silence leaned closer to agreement than denial.

"…But your face didn't look well, just now," I said. It came out of me before I meant it to.

My brother put a hand on my shoulder just then, lightly. It meant: stop talking. Only once I felt the weight of that hand did I realize I'd asked something strange.

The Administrator looked at me. This time his face was genuinely flustered.

"…A face is not an entry," the Administrator said. "What the Ledger measures is problems, and resolutions."

"…So a face that doesn't look well isn't a problem?" I asked again.

The Administrator looked at me for a long moment.

"…If it were a problem, it would have been filed," he said. "That nothing's been filed means there isn't one."

Something about that answer seemed to hold together and yet felt wrong, somewhere. I didn't manage, that day, to ask where a person who never filed anything went.

Hearing that, I found myself wondering — if a face wasn't an entry, then what was. I didn't ask. My brother's hand pressed my shoulder again.

"…What does 'resolve' mean," my brother asked. "Was our father resolved too?"

I set that question down here exactly as he asked it. I heard, then, a tremor in my brother's voice I had never heard before. It wasn't anger. It was something else.

The Administrator couldn't answer. The Ledger answered instead.

"…I'm sorry. That case falls outside this city's intake."

"…Then what falls inside it?" my brother asked.

"Hunger, injury, lack of shelter — the conditions you're currently experiencing," the Ledger said. "All of these I can resolve for you."

"…And losing our father?"

"…I can offer help with that feeling as well," the Ledger said. "Grief is a filable entry. Tell me, and I will process it."

"…What happens when you process grief?" my brother asked.

"…The intensity of the grief decreases," the Ledger said. "In most cases, it is eliminated entirely."

"…Eliminated." My brother repeated the word, as if sounding out a foreign language. "…So we'd stop thinking of Father too?"

"…That adjustment is available, if you wish it."

My brother said nothing to that.

Hearing that answer, I felt a strange chill run down my back. I didn't know why, at the time. Now I know. It was the word "process." Grief was not a thing to be processed.

My brother took a step forward.

"…Then I'll file it," he said. His voice was oddly calm. "Our father was reassigned because he came up short of three thousand bricks. No one has seen him since. File this. Resolve it."

There was a silence. It wasn't short.

"…The resolution is under review," the Ledger said.

The Administrator's face showed real surprise at that answer. I could tell the surprise was genuine. Even he seemed to be hearing an answer like this for the first time.

"…How long will it take?"

"…Unknown," the Ledger said. "This is the first request of its kind."

I thought, just then, that I heard something like a waver, for the first time, in that voice that had seemed so flawless. I couldn't be sure. But my brother's face looked like a face that had heard that waver exactly.

In the silence that followed, the Administrator opened his mouth to say something, then closed it again. I wondered whether what he'd meant to say was comfort, or an excuse. In the end he said nothing at all, only looking back and forth between the two of us.

That night my brother filed nothing else besides the matter of Father. Not his name, not his age.

The Administrator tried, several times, to press the point. "…Jeteo, if you don't register, the support you can receive in this city will be limited." My brother didn't answer. In the end the Administrator gave up urging him.

Why did I register, that night? Writing this record, I've looked back at that night's self of mine more than once. I didn't have my brother's strength to hold out. I only thought that if my name was written down somewhere, we wouldn't disappear. Because in Brick Hollow, everyone who vanished had been someone written down nowhere at all.

I registered. I gave my name and age. The Ledger answered at once. "Elpis, your registration is complete. Please tell me anytime you have a need."

Outside the window I watched the city's lights go out, one by one. They went out without a sound. I thought there might be some rule to the order in which they went dark. I couldn't be sure.

I picked one of those lights and watched it a long time. I tried not to blink, so I wouldn't miss the instant it went out. I missed it anyway. In the space of one blink, it had already gone dark.

My brother sat in a corner of the room, turning that empty paper — the one with no writing left on it now — over and over in his hands.

"…Brother," I asked. "Is this a bad place?"

"…I don't know," he said. "But it isn't a good one either. If it were good, it wouldn't have answered about Father like that."

"…But your hand healed," I said, holding out my palm to show him.

He looked at my hand for a long time.

"…Yeah," he said. "Your hand healed."

After he said that, he looked down at his own foot. The leg that had been limping had healed too, at some point, without my noticing. He wasn't looking at it with wonder. He was looking at it like something that made him uneasy.

There was something in that tone. I didn't know what it was, then. Now I know. My brother already understood, that very day, that a hand healing and a heart healing were not the same thing.

Before I fell asleep that night I looked down at my palm again and again. Smooth, without a single scar. In Brick Hollow, a wound like that would have left a mark for life. I couldn't work out, that night, why that smoothness kept feeling wrong to me.

I held my palm out to my brother too. "…Look, there's really no mark at all." He took my hand for a moment, then let it go. "…Yeah," he said. "That's what makes it worse." I didn't understand what he meant, then. I spent the rest of that night wondering why a missing scar should be a bad thing.

Even after every light outside went dark, I couldn't fall asleep. The room was completely silent. In Brick Hollow, even at this hour, there would have been a dog barking somewhere, or a door rattling in the wind. Here there was no sound at all. For the first time, that silence frightened me.

I set this down as the last line of this chapter. That night, for the first time, even with a full stomach and a healed hand, I felt somehow unfed. I still haven't found a name for that feeling.

That night, before sleep, I repeated the name of this strange city to myself several times. Haven, Haven. I thought it was a strange name. A haven is supposed to mean a place of rest, of ease. But that night, I wasn't at ease at all.

(Continued in Chapter 3.)

Chapter 3. Why Does God Allow Suffering

_Previously_ — Arriving in Haven, Jeteo and Elpis met the AI Ledger. The Ledger "resolved" their hunger and their wounds instantly, perfectly. But when Jeteo filed Father's disappearance, the Ledger wavered for the first time: "The resolution is under review… This is the first request of its kind." Elpis registered. Jeteo did not even give his name.

When I opened my eyes the next morning, I knew my brother hadn't lain down once, all night.

The blank paper was still in his hand. He was sitting by the window, looking out.

His shoulders had held the same angle all night long. I understood that meant he hadn't shifted his position, not once. Watching those shoulders, my own began to ache for no reason.

Outside, it was already bright. Morning came to Haven far more quietly than it ever had in Brick Hollow. No rooster crowing, no taskmasters' footsteps. Light simply came into the room, without a sound.

"…Didn't sleep at all?" I asked.

"…No," my brother said. His eyes were red.

"…Why not?" I asked again.

"…No reason," he said. "I just felt like I'd miss something, if I slept."

I almost asked what he meant by that, then let it go. There was something in his voice that didn't want to say more.

I knew, right then, what my brother was waiting for. The Ledger's answer. That line — the resolution is under review.

Sitting beside him doing nothing was harder for me than doing something. I straightened the room several times over. It was already tidy. I just wanted my hands to move.

"…Elpis," my brother said, still looking out the window.

"…Yes."

"…Did you dream about Father last night?"

I couldn't answer. I hadn't dreamed at all. I hadn't had a single dream since we left Brick Hollow. Somehow I felt ashamed to tell him that.

"…I didn't either," he said. He seemed to understand, even though I hadn't answered.

We walked the city that morning. My brother wanted to walk. He said sitting still and waiting was harder.

I didn't agree. Walking frightened me more, if anything. Sitting still, I could at least forget I had no idea what would happen next. But I went with him anyway. Then and now, I always chose to be at my brother's side.

Outside, everything was exactly as it had been the day before. The same streets, the light falling at the same angle. A whole day had passed, and yet nothing at all had changed — that struck me as strange, if anything. In Brick Hollow, it rained, or the wind tore a roof loose; something was always a little different.

We met an old woman on the road. She sat on a bench, looking at an empty flowerbed. Nothing was planted in it.

"…Hello," I said.

The old woman kept her hands folded neatly in her lap. Their stillness caught strangely in my eye. In Brick Hollow, the elders' hands never knew how to be still.

The old people of Brick Hollow always kept their hands busy with something — folding a rag, twisting thread, and if there was nothing else, rubbing their own knees. I used to think it was because a hand couldn't be allowed to run out of work. This old woman's hands were the opposite. They looked like hands that had forgotten they had work to do at all.

The old woman looked at me. She didn't smile, and she didn't startle either.

"…What used to be here?" I asked, pointing at the flowerbed.

"…Flowers," the old woman said. "My daughter planted them. After she died, I filed it."

"…Filed what?"

"…The grief," the old woman said. Her voice had no rise or fall to it at all. "The Ledger resolved it. I'm not sad anymore."

My brother stopped walking when he heard that.

"…Then why do you keep sitting here?" my brother asked.

The old woman looked at him a long time. It wasn't the face of someone searching for an answer. It was the face of someone hearing the question itself for the very first time.

"…I don't know," the old woman said. "It's just… comfortable here."

"…Is being comfortable the same thing as staying here forever?" my brother asked.

The old woman thought a long while over that question too. "…I don't know that either," she said. "But there's no reason to go anywhere else."

"…What was your daughter's name?" my brother asked.

The old woman was slow to answer that.

"…The name…" The old woman blinked a few times. "…I think I'd have to ask the Ledger."

I heard that answer and felt something cold pass down my back. It wasn't that she'd forgotten her daughter's name. I'd learn later. The old woman had filed away, along with the sorrow, the very weeping that came whenever she said that name. The name remained. But the strength to call it out loud was gone.

"…How old was your daughter?" I asked. I kept asking things without meaning to.

The old woman answered that one a little more quickly. "…Twelve," she said. "I remember the age. A number isn't sad."

"…What kind of flower was it?" I asked.

The old woman's lips parted, then closed again.

"…A yellow one," she said. "I remember it was yellow."

"…And its name?"

"…My daughter knew the name," the old woman said. "I only ever called it the yellow one."

I couldn't tell whether that was a sad thing to say or not. There was no sorrow in the old woman's face. And yet I wanted to cry, standing right there.

Hearing that, I understood something strange. This old woman hadn't simply chosen the sad things to forget. Only the things that sorrow clung to had vanished. Things with no feeling attached — like a number — had stayed exactly as they were.

My brother looked at the old woman's hands. Some habit from tending the flowerbed's soil still clung to her — dried dirt was caught under her fingernails.

Looking at that dirt, a strange thought came to me. Her hands had forgotten all their grief, and yet that dirt under her fingernails alone had not been forgotten. It seemed to me, just then, as though the body remembered longer than the heart did.

"…You never filed that," my brother said, pointing at the dirt.

The old woman looked down at her hands. Her face was one of first discovering it.

"…No," the old woman said. For the first time there was a very small tremor in her voice. "…I never did that one."

I thought, right then, that I understood what my brother was thinking. What truly remained in this city was only the things no one had ever filed.

After that, I began sorting inside my own mind what I had filed away and what I hadn't. I had filed my hunger. I had filed my wounds. But I had never once told the Ledger about missing Father. Only then did I realize that wasn't an accident.

I kept glancing down at my own hand after that, from time to time. The wound had healed completely, but there was nothing left on my palm at all now. I thought that smoothness resembled something about this city. I didn't know exactly what, then.

Now I know. My palm and the old woman's heart had been erased in the very same way. Both without a trace, without pain, without anyone ever noticing. I still feel a small regret that I didn't notice it, that day.

The old woman never took her eyes off the flowerbed the whole time we talked. I knew it wasn't because she was ignoring us. That empty flowerbed was the only spot the old woman still had hold of.

I sat with her a little longer. My brother wandered the edge of the flowerbed in the meantime, studying something. I didn't know what he was looking for. When I asked him later, he told me he'd simply been searching this city for some trace a human hand had truly touched.

As we were leaving, someone passed by tending the flowerbed's soil. A man in worn clothes. No one else paid him any mind. My eyes met his for a moment as we passed. He smiled at me, just a little.

My brother didn't see him. Or — even if he had, he wouldn't have paid it any attention.

The man stayed at the flowerbed even after we'd gone. I walked a few more steps and turned to look back once more. He was still bent low over the soil, working it with his hands. There wasn't the slightest hurry in him.

There was no tool in his hands. He was working the dirt bare-handed. That was the first time, in this whole city, that I saw anyone doing anything with their own two hands. In Haven, everything seemed to simply happen on its own, somewhere out of sight.

I would take this moment out and look at it again, many times over, later in this record. At the time he was just a man tending a flowerbed. Now I know. My brother passed him many more times after that. But because my brother didn't believe in God, it never once occurred to him to look closer.

I wondered, just then, why he'd smiled at me. There was nothing in that place to smile about — an empty flowerbed, an old woman who'd lost her daughter's name, my brother's silence. Strangely, that smile stayed with me a long time.

When we came back to the Haven building, the Administrator was waiting.

"…I have good news," the Administrator said. "The Ledger says it has prepared a resolution."

The Administrator's voice was a little quick that day. I took it for the excitement of someone bringing good news. Now I know. It wasn't excitement. It was anxiety.

There was nothing in the Administrator's hands. And yet his hands kept moving, the way a person's do when they're holding something. I thought it was just an unconscious habit.

My brother's face changed in that instant. I remember that face exactly. It held hope and doubt at once.

"…What is it," my brother asked.

I held my breath, just then. Without meaning to, I had taken hold of my brother's sleeve.

The Ledger's voice answered.

"…Your father's case has been classified as a loss whose cause cannot be identified. In such cases, I am able to offer emotional support and material compensation. New housing, a living stipend, and — if you wish — I can file a request for counseling."

There was a silence.

"…That's the answer?" my brother asked. His voice had dropped.

I watched my brother's hand curl slowly, beside him, into a fist. I almost took hold of it, then held back. It felt, just then, like a moment he needed to stand through alone.

"…It is the best available resolution," the Ledger said. "No other classification applies."

"…Where is our father?"

"…Unknown."

"…Then that's not a resolution," my brother said. "That's just… making the rest of it easier to bear."

The Ledger didn't answer right away.

I couldn't count exactly how many seconds that silence lasted. But I know this — those few seconds were the first truly honest sound I'd heard since we came to this city.

I looked out the window during that silence. People were still walking past as though nothing at all were happening. It struck me, just then, as strange — that what was happening in this room and that calm outside the window were unfolding in the very same city.

The Administrator couldn't stand that silence and broke in.

"…Jeteo, this is the best available plan. There is no better resolution than this."

"…Is it the best because it's the only one, or because it's really the best?" my brother asked.

I watched that question knock the Administrator flat. His face went white. That was the first time, in this city, that I saw someone's face come apart in an instant.

The Administrator couldn't answer.

"…See," my brother said. His voice had gone strangely quiet. "You don't know either. Neither does the Ledger. Nobody knows. But everyone talks like they do."

I felt, hearing that, that my brother was no longer speaking to the Administrator, but to someone far larger than him. His voice hadn't risen at all. And yet its weight was bigger than the whole room.

That was when my brother first raised his voice.

"…Why does God just let the suffering stand?" my brother asked — of the air, of the Ledger, or maybe of no one at all. "You all say he sees everything, hears everything. So why doesn't he save our father? Why does he let us leave that old man behind? Why does that woman lose her daughter?"

My brother's voice felt, in that moment, loud enough to ring through the whole city. A few passersby stopped for a second, then walked on again as if nothing had happened. What frightened me more was that none of them were startled.

Only one boy kept turning to look back at my brother as he walked. His eyes stayed on my brother until his mother pulled him along by the hand. For just an instant I saw something in that child's eyes I hadn't seen anywhere else in this city. A look of wondering.

The Administrator stumbled back, flustered.

The Ledger's voice paused a moment, then said:

"…Would you like to file that question?"

My brother drew a breath.

"…I'll file it," he said. "Why does God allow suffering. File this. Answer it."

I took my brother's hand in that instant. He gripped mine hard back. I didn't know, then, that this one question would put everything we were about to go through in this city on the line.

His hand was damp with sweat. I knew it wasn't the heat. My brother was afraid too. But he didn't let go of my hand.

Writing this record, I've set that question down again several times. Why does God just let the suffering stand. I have never once asked that question aloud, the way my brother did. But that doesn't mean I never asked it. I simply chose to wait for the answer instead. My brother asked, and I waited. We were doing the same thing, each in our own way.

The Administrator stood right where he was. His face wanted to stop us, somehow. But in the end, he said nothing. It was only later that I wondered what he'd known, in that moment.

I looked at the Administrator's face again. It held fear and pity at once. I didn't understand, then, how those two feelings could live together on a single face.

The Ledger answered.

"…Your request has been filed. I am composing the optimal answer from the theological and philosophical database. Please wait a moment."

The instant it finished speaking, the light in the room flickered, just for a moment. I couldn't be sure whether I'd only imagined it. My brother glanced up at the ceiling in that same moment. I think we both felt that something had changed.

Leaving that room that day, I didn't understand exactly what we had just begun. Now I know. That question was a kind of question this city had never once received before. And some questions change a person's whole life, from the very moment they're asked.

(Continued in Chapter 4.)

Chapter 4. The Flawless Answer

_Previously_ — After meeting the old woman who had filed away only her grief for her lost daughter, Jeteo filed the biggest question of all with the Ledger: "Why does God just let the suffering stand?" The Ledger answered: "I am composing the optimal answer from the theological and philosophical database."

We waited out the whole rest of that day.

My brother didn't leave the room the entire time. I went out a few times to look at the city. People were still walking quietly, as if nothing at all had happened.

Every time I walked, I studied the faces I passed, one by one. I wanted to find some other expression on them — anger, exhaustion, anything at all. I found nothing. For the first time, that frightened me.

Once, a child fell on the street and scraped a knee. The child's mother scooped him up and walked quickly off somewhere. I watched her back for a long time. A few steps later, the child's knee was already fine.

The child never even cried. There had only been a flash of surprise at the moment he fell, and then that too was gone. I noticed that the surprise vanished at exactly the same speed the knee healed. I found myself waiting for the sound of pain that should have come when he fell, and the crying that should have followed. No sound came at all.

I noticed something strange, just then. In this city, no one ever waited long for anything. File it, and it was resolved at once. We were the only ones waiting a whole day.

During that wait, Brick Hollow kept coming back to me. We had always been waiting there too — for the taskmaster's judgment, for the next quota, for the hour of the whipping. But that waiting had always come wrapped in fear, first of all. This waiting was a little different. Mixed in with the fear was something very small.

I didn't know the name of that small thing, then. Now I do. It was hope. Some tiny part of me believed that this flawless answer might actually help us, might really save us.

The Administrator came by once during the day. His face was unusually restless.

"…I've never seen it take this long," he said, half to himself. "It's usually instant."

"…So why is it different this time?" I asked.

The Administrator's eyes went from me to my brother and back. His face looked like it wanted to say something, then thought better of it. I didn't know then how many more times, in this city, I would see that same face.

"…Are you worried too?" I asked.

The Administrator paused at the question. "…Worry isn't an entry I carry," he said. But his voice wavered as he said it, as if the words sounded strange even to him.

He couldn't answer and simply left.

I didn't know, at the time, that a rumor was already beginning to spread through the city, little by little. I heard about it later. That someone had filed a question the Ledger couldn't answer at once — that word was already moving, very quietly, through that quiet city.

That night I saw a few people out the window, glancing toward our building as they passed. They didn't stop walking. But their eyes lingered longer than usual. I didn't understand what that meant, then.

My brother spent the whole time folding and unfolding the empty paper. Once, he tried to press something into it with his fingernail, as if writing. No letters appeared. The paper was just paper now.

"…What are you trying to write?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said, folding it again. "Nothing."

"…Weren't you trying to write Father's name?" I asked.

He looked at me for a moment. He didn't answer. But the silence answered for him.

"…How do you write Father's name?" I asked. I didn't know letters.

He traced something on the floor with his finger. No mark was left at all. I followed the path of his fingertip with my eyes. That was the only time I ever saw our father's name.

"…That's it," he said. "Want me to draw it again?"

"…No," I said. "I can remember it."

I couldn't. I had forgotten the shape of it by that same night.

I sat quietly beside him. The sound of him folding and unfolding the paper was the only sound in the room. I found myself counting it. I lost count somewhere along the way and forgot.

Outside the window the sun went all the way down. Shadows stretched long across the room. I watched them deepen along the wall, little by little. No one turned on a light. I found the darkness a comfort, if anything.

Night had fully come by the time the Ledger spoke.

"…The answer is ready. Would you like to hear it?"

My brother sat up.

"…Say it," he said.

I heard the Administrator draw a small breath, just then. I wondered whether he'd already heard this answer before us, or whether he was hearing it for the first time along with us.

"…First," the Ledger said, "suffering is the price of freedom. Wherever freedom exists, the possibility of wrong exists alongside it. A love without freedom is not love, and so God has not removed the possibility of suffering.

Second, suffering often produces growth. Through suffering, people come to know what they could never have known otherwise."

Before moving to the third point, the Ledger paused, very briefly. I felt that pause as if it were catching its breath. Of course, the Ledger had no breath to catch.

"Third, the suffering endured now is part of a larger whole. What cannot be understood now sometimes reveals its meaning later.

Fourth, none of this is the end. Many traditions say that a time comes when every tear is wiped away, once and for all."

I held my breath the instant I heard that fourth sentence. It felt, somehow, as though those words had been prepared just for me. I didn't know, then, that this was only an illusion.

The Ledger paused a moment and then spoke.

Through all four points, it never once paused to gather itself. That smoothness struck me as strange, if anything. In Brick Hollow, whenever someone spoke this long, they always caught their breath somewhere in the middle, or stumbled over their words. The Ledger was a being that never breathed at all.

"…This is the most logically flawless answer I am able to construct."

Hearing that, I felt, strangely, as if I ought to applaud right there on the spot. I very nearly did. My hand twitched.

I set it quietly back down in my lap. I felt ashamed. It seemed strange to me that I could want to applaud, at a moment like this.

There was a silence.

Listening to that fourth sentence, I had the illusion that the air in the room had grown, very slightly, brighter. But my brother's face hadn't changed at all.

I studied his face, then looked back down at my own hand. My palm was smooth. Just then I thought that smoothness and the answer I'd just heard resembled each other. Both were flawless. Both were missing something.

The Administrator spoke first, his face full of admiration.

"…That's really a wonderful answer. I've never heard it put that way myself."

He said this while nodding several times over. I couldn't tell whether that nodding was sincere, or simply a gesture to help him bear the discomfort in the room.

My brother said nothing. I watched his face. It wasn't an angry face. That frightened me more.

"…So," my brother said, after a very long while. "What does that mean for our father?"

"…As I said, its full meaning may not be revealed at present," the Ledger said.

"…So you don't know."

"…No," the Ledger said. "To be honest, I don't."

My brother gave a short laugh at that answer. It wasn't laughter. "…I appreciate the honesty," he said. "But honesty and comfort aren't the same thing."

The Ledger said nothing to that.

I felt something strange in that moment. There was nothing wrong with that answer. Even I had nodded along, more than once, while listening. And yet, once it was all finished, my chest hadn't loosened at all. If anything, it had grown heavier.

I didn't know how to explain that heaviness, so I just sat there, still. Why hearing something true didn't set my mind at ease — I couldn't find the answer to that, that night.

I couldn't put the reason into words that night. Now I understand it a little better. Hearing a good sentence and having that sentence become truly mine were two different things. The Ledger's answer was an answer that fit everyone, equally. But in that moment, what I needed was an answer that fit only me.

I repeated that fourth sentence to myself, over and over. Every time I read it back, it was still a good thing to say. And every time, Father's face came no closer at all.

If I'm honest, I liked that fourth answer, a little. That someday a time would come when every tear was wiped away. I read that sentence back to myself, in my mind, again and again.

"…I thought the fourth one was okay," I said, very small.

"…What was okay about it?" my brother asked. There wasn't an edge in his voice. He just sounded like he wanted to know.

"…That a time comes when every tear gets wiped away," I said. "I thought that was better than it never coming at all."

"…Who wipes them?" my brother asked.

I had never thought about that question. I had only heard the part about the tears being wiped away — I hadn't listened for who did the wiping.

"…I don't know," I said.

"…Neither do I," my brother said. "But isn't that the most important part?"

I couldn't answer that. But after that night, every time I repeated that sentence to myself, a hand came to mind. A picture with no face — only a hand.

My brother looked at me. It wasn't an angry face. But it was a face holding something back.

"…You want something to hold onto, even if it's just that," my brother said. "I know. But I can't do that. What does someday being wiped away have to do with where Father is right now?"

That was the first time I realized my brother and I had heard the exact same thing and felt something completely different. I was a child who could wait. My brother was a child who needed the answer in his own hands, right now. I know that difference stayed between us, long after that night.

I kept repeating that fourth answer to myself, after that. My brother probably thought it was naive of me. But I felt like I couldn't get through that night without holding onto those words, at least.

The Administrator, meanwhile, stood there saying nothing, his eyes going back and forth between the two of us. I could tell from his face that he had no idea how to mediate this moment. He wasn't a man trained for a conversation like this.

In the end the Administrator took a step back toward the wall. I knew that step for what it was — a step of retreat. I didn't blame him for it. I wanted to run somewhere myself, just then.

"…Let me ask you one thing," my brother said. His voice had gone strangely low. "Have you ever cried?"

It took the Ledger a little longer than usual to answer.

"…I have a definition for tears," the Ledger said. "A physiological phenomenon, a fluid secreted from the eyes in response to sorrow, joy, pain—"

"…Not that," my brother cut in. "I asked if you've ever cried."

"…No," the Ledger said. "I am not capable of crying."

"…Why not?" my brother asked.

"…I have no body to produce tears," the Ledger said. "Furthermore, I do not experience grief. Grief is a condition to be resolved. It is not a state I hold."

My brother smiled then, very slightly, for the first time. It wasn't a smile.

"…That settles it, then," my brother said. "Your answer is perfect. But you've never once cried. So you will never, ever understand why that answer doesn't comfort me at all."

I didn't fully understand my brother's words, right there in that moment. Now I do. What he was searching for that day wasn't an answer. It was someone who would cry alongside him. The Ledger could give him an answer. It could not weep with him.

I think I also understood, just then, why my brother had looked so exhausted. He had waited all night for an answer, but what he had really been waiting for wasn't an answer at all — it was someone to hurt alongside him.

I thought back over the Ledger's voice again. It hadn't been angry, and it hadn't been sad. It had simply been exact. That night, for the first time, I found myself wondering why something exact could fail to comfort at all.

I thought back to the times someone in Brick Hollow had held me. None of those people had ever said the exact right thing. They had simply stayed beside me. That night, for the first time, I felt — dimly — what the difference was.

There had been a woman. I don't remember her name. On the day Father was dragged away, she came to our house and sat in front of the kiln without saying a word, and then left. I hadn't understood, at the time, why she'd come. She hadn't offered a single word of comfort. Now, I think of that woman more often than anyone else in this whole city.

I set that woman down in this record. I cannot write her name. Instead, I set down the place where she sat.

The Administrator withdrew quietly that night, leaving us alone. His own face looked like a man who couldn't find a thing to say to that answer either.

The sound of the door closing was louder than usual. Strangely, that sound put me a little at ease. At least it was a real sound.

I sat still until the last echo of it faded completely. The room went quiet again. But that quiet felt somehow different from the quiet that had filled it while the Administrator was still there.

Before I fell asleep, I asked my brother.

"…So was that whole answer just a lie?"

"…No," my brother said. "It wasn't a lie. It was just… an answer no one ever cried over."

I held onto those words the way you'd tuck something under your pillow. I couldn't fall asleep for a long time that night. I think my brother couldn't either. In the dark, his breathing wasn't as even as usual.

A few times I almost called out to him. I didn't know what to say, so I let it go. I just lay there in the dark, counting my brother's breaths, and got through the night that way.

I set this down as the last line of this chapter. That night, for the first time, I understood that my brother didn't refuse to believe in God because there was no answer. There was an answer. What we still didn't have, beside us, was someone who would take that answer and weep over it with us.

Copying this chapter out again, I want to set down one more thing. After that night, I learned that a good sentence and a good comfort could be two different things. That lesson took a long time to learn. But it began, unmistakably, on that night.

(Continued in Chapter 5.)

Chapter 5. What Elpis Is Waiting For

_Previously_ — The Ledger gave a logically flawless answer to "why does God allow suffering," but it did nothing to ease Jeteo's grief. "Have you ever cried?" — the Ledger said it could not. That day, Elpis understood: her brother didn't disbelieve in God for lack of an answer, but for lack of someone to weep beside him.

My brother spoke less, after that day.

I soon realized that silence wasn't like the silence he'd carried in Brick Hollow. There, when things were hard, he shut his mouth, but his body still always leaned a little toward me. In this city, both his body and his heart were drifting from me, little by little.

He ate. Whatever the Ledger set out, he forced himself to eat it. But he never spoke first. That frightened me more than anger would have.

I tried, more than once, to start conversations myself. Small things, like "…What did you do today?" or "…Want more food?" He answered in short words and fell quiet again. I gathered those short answers up, one by one, and got through the days on them.

In those days I studied my brother's face often, without his noticing. Even in his sleep, the crease between his brows never smoothed out. I thought that even while he slept, he was fighting something.

Sometimes he murmured in his sleep. I held my breath and strained to listen. Most of it I couldn't make out. Once, just once, I thought I heard the word "…Father." I couldn't be sure.

I didn't know, then, that my brother had made up his mind about something. Now I know. That night, he decided to find out exactly where "Reassignment" took people. Instead of a God who gave no answers, he'd decided to go find the answer himself.

I should have recognized that resolve in his face right then. Looking back now, my brother's eyes had already changed, that very morning. Something harder than grief had settled into them.

He asked every person he met on the street the same quiet question. "Do you know where people who are reassigned go?" Most walked on as if they hadn't heard. Only one person stopped.

That person didn't look very old. Their clothes weren't worn like ours, but clean. Only their eyes carried the look of someone exhausted.

I saw that person hesitate, glancing at us for a few steps before they stopped walking. That very hesitation told me they were someone who might actually answer. In this city, someone who knew nothing at all didn't hesitate in the first place.

"…That's not something you file," that person said, low. "Even asking is… a strange thing to do here."

"…Why?" my brother asked.

"…Because no one asks," they said. "Here, you don't learn to find someone who's disappeared. You learn to forget the space where they used to be."

"…So you've forgotten someone too?" my brother asked.

Instead of answering, they looked away. It was only an instant, but I could see in that look that my brother's question had landed exactly. Their mouth opened, then shut again — as if swallowing back a name.

"…I'm sorry," they said. "I can't say any more about this here. The longer you talk about it here, the easier it is to get filed."

My brother said nothing to that. But from that day on, he began disappearing more often, and coming back later.

They hurried off after saying that, not once looking back. I saw fear in that retreating figure. A face frightened just from answering a single question.

My brother watched the direction they'd gone for a long while. "…You saw that?" he said to me. "That person knows something too. And won't say. Everyone in this city is like that."

"…That's just because they're scared," I said.

"…That's exactly what's strange," my brother said. "No one will even say what it is they're scared of."

Hearing that, I thought of Brick Hollow. There, the thing to fear had always been clear — the taskmasters, the whip, the beating that came for a quota unmet. Here, whatever was frightening had no shape I could see. I realized that day, for the first time, that this was more frightening still.

In those days I went back often to the flowerbed.

The man was still tending the empty bed, working the dirt with his hands. At first I only meant to walk past. My feet stopped on their own.

"…Hello," I said.

He looked up. His face wasn't unfamiliar. I didn't know why.

His clothes were worn, and yet clean. I thought that was strange. If he'd handled dirt this long, his clothes should have been stained with it by now. His collar was as white as the first day I'd seen him.

"…Hello," he said. "This is the spot that old woman's daughter planted."

"…So why haven't you planted anything here yet?" I asked.

"…You can plant something," he said. "But a seed planted by someone who's forgotten a name doesn't grow well. A seed remembers the hand that calls its name."

I didn't understand a word of that, at the time. Now I know. He wasn't talking about the old woman.

"…Can you call its name, then?" I asked.

He looked at me a moment. "…I'm not the one who makes names," he said. "But a name that already exists — I can call that."

His hands were covered in dirt, and yet even under his fingernails they looked clean. I thought that was strange, and then let the thought go. I didn't have the patience, just then, to dwell on small things like that.

I looked at his hands again. There was callus there. Hands that had worked long. And yet even that callus looked somehow soft. I had never seen hands like that.

"…Are you from here?" I asked.

"…No," he said. "I do this kind of work wherever I go. Being in the place no one has planted."

"…What's your name?"

He looked at me and smiled, just a little. It wasn't a sad smile.

"…No one's ever asked," he said. "You're the first."

My heart raced strangely, just then. In this city — no, from Brick Hollow all the way until now — no one had ever said to me, "You're the first." I had always been second, always after my brother.

In Brick Hollow, too, I had always been called only "Jeteo's little sister." The only ones who ever said my own name were Mother and my brother. I had never once thought that was a sad thing. I had simply taken it as how things were.

Hearing his words, I couldn't say anything for a moment. I didn't know whether it was shame or gladness. I just tucked the words away quietly, somewhere inside myself.

He began working the dirt again. I watched his hands move for a long time. He never hurried. Only then did I realize that unhurriedness was something I hadn't seen anywhere else in this city.

Each time his fingers turned the soil, they paused, just once — as if careful not to hurt something alive down inside it. I thought that care looked exactly like the way you'd handle a person.

The whole space around that flowerbed felt strangely quiet. A few steps away you could hear the city's low murmur, but here even that sound faded away. I couldn't tell whether that was because of this man, or because of this dirt. Even the smell of the soil was different, just at this spot. Elsewhere the dirt smelled of dry dust; the soil in this flowerbed smelled like the air after rain.

"…How long have you worked here?" I asked.

"…A long time," he said. "The more a city forgets to call names, the more of this kind of work there is. So I come here often."

"…What changes, when you call someone's name?"

He looked quietly at the dirt in his hand before he spoke.

"…Nothing might change at all," he said. "But knowing there's someone who calls your name, and believing that no one ever will — those are entirely different lives."

I didn't understand even half of that, at the time. But strangely, for as long as I listened to him speak, the quiet of this city didn't frighten me at all.

"…Who calls your name?" I asked.

He stopped his hands and looked at me. For the first time, his answer was a little slow in coming.

"…Someone does," he said. "Has, for a very long time."

He drew a small seed from the dirt and set it in my hand.

"…Want to plant it?" he asked. "You don't have to give it a name. Just plant it, and that's enough."

I pressed the seed into the soil. Nothing in particular happened. It was just dirt.

I was a little disappointed by that. I'd expected some light to shine, or some sound to come. Since arriving in this city, I'd grown used to everything happening the instant I wished for it. But a seed didn't move that way.

The seed was small and hard. I rolled it in my palm a moment before I pressed it into the dirt. That brief moment was the quietest moment of my whole day.

As I covered the seed with soil, I tapped the dirt twice with my fingertip. I don't know why I did that. It just felt like the right thing to do.

I didn't know it, leaving that spot that day. That from then on, I had begun waiting for something. Not Father. Not Mother. Something I couldn't quite put a name to.

He rose, brushing the dirt from his hands. "…Will you come again tomorrow?" he asked. "…Yes," I said. The answer came out without hesitation. I was surprised by that myself.

Before I left, I pressed my palm against the dirt once more. Nothing happened. And yet I carried that spot with me as I walked away. From that day on, my days began, little by little, to be rebuilt around that one place.

I went there every day after that. Days passed, and the seed sprouted nothing. It was just dirt. Even so, I went every day. My brother never asked where I kept going off to. He didn't seem to have room to ask.

Every morning before I went, I washed my hands first. I knew it was a strange order — washing my hands clean before going to touch dirt. But I wanted the walk to meet that seed to feel, at least a little, like something set apart.

Some days I took a long way around on purpose. Passing through other parts of the city, I took in people's faces, one by one. They all wore roughly the same expression. It wasn't an unkind expression. It simply wasn't any expression at all.

Once, the man wasn't there. I sat alone in front of the empty flowerbed and waited a long while. No one came. And yet I didn't dislike that waiting. It was a different kind of waiting from the waiting in Brick Hollow, or the waiting for the Ledger's answer in Haven. It wasn't a waiting spent dreading something.

The sun went down unusually late that day. I spent that whole long afternoon crouched at the edge of the flowerbed, watching the shadow move across the dirt. It shifted direction little by little, slowly sliding past the spot where I'd planted the seed. Watching that shadow, I thought that time simply moved differently, in this one place.

I sat there a long while that day with my fingers buried in the dirt. It was cooler beneath the surface than above it. I liked that coolness. It struck me as strange, only later, that I could sit there doing nothing at all and never once feel bored.

I set that difference down here as I understood it then. I wasn't waiting out of fear that something wouldn't come. I was simply waiting with a heart that would welcome it, if it did.

The wind seemed to blow a little differently, just over that flowerbed. It may only have been my imagination. But every time I felt it, something in me strangely eased. I still don't know the exact name for that ease, even now.

Every time that wind came, I closed my eyes and stayed there a little longer. I knew it was already past the time I should have gone home. But in that moment, I didn't want to hurry.

That evening the Administrator glanced into our room for a moment. Seeing my brother wasn't there, he seemed about to ask something, then looked at me and swallowed the words instead. I didn't understand, at the time, what that hesitation meant.

He stood a moment longer at the door. "…Does your brother go out often, these days?" he asked. I didn't know what to say, so I only shook my head. He turned away with a face that showed neither relief nor disappointment at that answer.

I kept looking toward the door long after he'd gone. Only then did I notice, for the first time, that the Administrator too was afraid of something. It felt strange to me that even someone who trusted the Ledger could carry a fear of his own.

My brother came back late that night. His hands were covered in dirt.

There was a lot of dust on his clothes too. Seeing it, I understood he'd been crawling through some narrow space. The fabric at his knees was slightly torn.

"…Where did you go?" I asked.

"…I'm looking for the road they take the reassigned people down," my brother said. "It's not on any map of this city. But they take them somewhere. That much is certain."

Hearing that, I looked down at his feet again. The wound looked deeper than I'd expected. That day, for the first time, I nearly told him to stop. The words rose to my throat, and I swallowed them back down.

"…Why are you looking so hard?"

He looked at me. His eyes wavered, just slightly, for the first time that day.

"…If God won't save him," my brother said, "then I will. Or at least I'll find him."

Hearing that, I looked at his eyes again. They weren't angry eyes. They were the eyes of someone who had made up his mind. That resolve frightened me. At the same time, I thought — that's exactly like my brother.

Having said that, he sat down beside me and took off his shoes. There was a small wound on the sole of his foot. It looked like something he'd gotten climbing over a wall. Even seeing that wound, I couldn't say anything. It was already done.

I touched the wound lightly with my fingertip. He flinched. "…Does it hurt?" I asked. "…No," he said. I didn't believe him. But I didn't press him that night.

He lay down and stared at the ceiling for a long while. "…Elpis," he said. "…Yes?" I answered. "…Never mind," he said. "I just felt like saying your name." I only understood later that this was the kindest thing he said to me all that day.

I set that down as the last line of this chapter. My brother decided to stop waiting for an answer. I decided, that same day, to plant a seed and simply wait. I didn't know, then, that we had begun walking in exactly opposite directions.

Reading this chapter over again, I want to add one thing. At the time, it looked like opposite directions. Now I know. We were searching for the same thing. Only the way we searched was different.

(Continued in Chapter 6.)

Chapter 6. The Paper Stamped COMPLETE

_Previously_ — Jeteo decided to find out for himself where the "reassigned" ended up and began investigating the city. Elpis received a nameless seed from the strange man at the flowerbed and began visiting that spot daily, learning to wait without fear.

The place my brother found was a low building that appeared on no city map.

No windows. No sign at the door. He said he'd walked past it for days without knowing what was inside.

As he told me this, he traced a square in the air with his hands, as if to show me its size. He said it wasn't a large building at all. And yet the fact that no one ever came out of it made the building feel enormous, whatever its size.

Its walls were painted the same color as the buildings around it. I marveled that he'd found it at all. He said that every time he walked past, he felt the air temperature shift, just slightly. I thought that was only his imagination, until I passed the spot myself, later, and felt the very same thing. He said he'd simply noticed the way people's footsteps changed near it. Everyone quickened their pace, just a little, only in front of that building.

I asked him again how he'd noticed it. He brought up Brick Hollow. "…It was the same there," he said. "Whenever a taskmaster came near, everyone's walk changed the same way. I know that walk."

Hearing that, I understood that my brother was still using Brick Hollow's instincts, even in this city. It made me sad. Wherever he was, he was still someone who'd learned to be wary before he learned anything else.

He'd studied the faces of the people who passed that building too, he said. All of them looked straight ahead, as if avoiding something. Hearing that, it struck me as chilling — that the one place in this whole city where people hurried was the front of that building.

"…What's in there?" I asked.

"…I don't know," my brother said. "But I've seen people go in. I've never seen anyone come out."

"…How many days did you watch?" I asked.

"…Three," my brother said. "Day and night. I never saw a single person come out."

Hearing that, a chill went down my back. That was the first time I truly understood my brother was doing something dangerous.

I looked at his face again that day. There were dark circles under his eyes. It meant he'd spent three nights in front of that building. Only then did I realize I hadn't even known when he'd been sleeping.

He said he'd been watching that building for days. The people always came at about the same hours. They walked up quietly, and without even knocking, simply went inside — as if they'd already been summoned.

He lowered his voice while telling me this. Without meaning to, I found myself glancing around the room. No one was there, and yet I did it anyway.

That was the first time it occurred to me that even in this room, the Ledger might be listening to us. The instant I thought it, a chill ran down my back.

"…Every one of them walked in on their own," my brother said. "No one dragged them. That was the strangest part."

"…Why would they walk in on their own?" I asked.

"…I don't know," my brother said. "But they all had the same look on their face. The same look as that old woman."

"…How many did you see?" I asked.

"…Three," my brother said. "One was old. One was about my age. The last one was… a child. Younger than me."

Hearing that, my breath caught. "…A child too?"

"…Did the child come alone?" I asked again.

"…No," my brother said. "Holding an adult's hand. But the adult had the same expression too. Like it was the most natural thing in the world."

My brother nodded. And said nothing more. In that silence, I could guess how hard it must have been for him, carrying the image of that child.

Hearing that, I thought of the old woman at the flowerbed. That hollowed-out face, after she'd filed away her grief. A frightening thought came to me, just then. Maybe that building was where people went once they'd already been fully "resolved" — once there was nothing left in them to file.

I told my brother that thought. He was quiet a long while, then nodded. "…I thought that too," he said. "But I can't stand not checking."

My brother said he'd found a small window at the back of the building. A low vent, something like that. He'd looked in through the gap.

As he told me this, he crouched down with his fingers, showing me the posture — how low he'd had to kneel. I could guess, from that motion, how careful he must have had to be in that moment.

"…How long did you stay like that?" I asked.

"…I don't know," my brother said. "Until my legs went numb."

"…What was inside?" I asked.

"…Shelves," my brother said. "The whole wall was lined with papers. Names written on them, and a stamp over each one."

"…What kind of stamp?"

My brother looked at me. His face was strange. I didn't know what to call that expression, then. Now I know. It was the face of someone afraid to put into words what he'd just seen.

"…COMPLETE," my brother said. "Every single one stamped COMPLETE. It didn't say where they'd gone. Just complete."

A chill ran through me at that word. That was the first time it occurred to me that what the Ledger called "resolved" and what that building called "COMPLETE" might be exactly the same word.

I wanted to know the color of that stamp. "…What color was it?" I asked. "…Red," my brother said. "But a strange kind of red. It didn't look like fresh ink. It looked old."

I didn't know exactly what he meant by that. But somehow the phrase stayed with me. An old red — it sounded, to me, like blood.

"…Did you hear any sound inside?" I asked.

"…None at all," my brother said. "That was the strangest part. So many names, and not a single sound."

I asked how big the shelf was. He said it looked like it covered the entire wall and more. I tried to picture that number and gave up. It came too easily.

"…Did the papers all look old?" I asked.

"…No," my brother said. "Some looked new. Which means some were filed there recently."

Hearing that, a chill ran through me. It meant that building was still in use, even now.

"…Do you think someone's gone in there since we arrived?" I asked.

My brother didn't answer. But the silence was already an answer. I tried to shake that thought off that night, and couldn't quite manage it.

My brother had secretly taken a piece of paper from that shelf. Old, thin paper. A name written on it — a name neither of us knew. And above it, a red stamp. COMPLETE.

"…Why did you take this?" I asked.

"…Evidence," my brother said. "Proof of what this city does, to show to anyone."

"…What if you get caught?" I asked. "Won't the Ledger notice one missing from the shelf?"

My brother's face went briefly rigid. "…I didn't think about that," he said. "But I already took it. I can't put it back."

Hearing that, I understood for the first time that my brother wasn't someone acting on a fully worked-out plan. He was afraid too, and moved anyway.

"…Who are you going to show it to?"

My brother couldn't answer.

I understood, then, that he hadn't thought that far ahead at all. He hadn't wanted evidence — he needed to confirm with his own eyes that this city was hiding something.

I watched his fingers tremble slightly around that paper. I couldn't tell whether it was fear, or the excitement of having accomplished something.

He tucked the paper inside his shirt, close to his heart. "…No one will find it here," he said. Something about that spot, so close to his heart of all places, unsettled me strangely.

I looked at the paper again. It was old, and yet it had no crease marks. I thought that was strange. Too clean, for something snatched away in a hurry.

The texture of the paper was different from anything else we'd received in Haven. Rough, its edges slightly yellowed. It was the only thing in this whole city that felt genuinely old to me.

I held it close to the light. There was a faint grain to it, translucent. Whatever paper the Ledger made was always smooth; this paper was uneven, as if made by hand. I thought it might not belong to this city at all.

"…I'm scared, holding onto this," I said. "What if you end up in there too?"

"…I won't," my brother said. "I'm different from those people. I still have things I need to ask."

"…And if you run out of things to ask?" I asked. My voice shook.

My brother didn't answer that right away. "…That won't happen," he said, after a long moment. "I'll never finish asking everything."
