Even hearing that, my mind wasn't at ease. That night, after my brother fell asleep, I looked at that paper again, several times. A stranger's name, and a red stamp. I touched the stamp with my fingertip. The ink had already dried completely.

I read that name aloud. It sounded unfamiliar. I tried to imagine what face that person might have had, how old they were, who they might have disappeared and left behind. Nothing came to me. A name alone couldn't paint a whole person.

That night, the Administrator came to our room. His face was more rigid than usual.

My heart sank the instant I saw that face. He had never once come to us at this hour before. I felt, even before the door opened, that this night was different.

"…Jeteo," the Administrator said. "There have been reports that you've recently been seen near a restricted zone."

My brother's hand, still holding that paper, slipped behind his back. I saw it. The Administrator must have too.

"…I don't know what you've been looking at," the Administrator said. "I'm only here to pass on this: the Ledger does not accept questions about that zone."

I could hear, in his voice, that these weren't his own words but something he'd been instructed to relay. He avoided our eyes the entire time he spoke.

"…Why won't it accept them?" my brother asked. "You said the Ledger accepts everything. Grief, questions — everything, you said."

I heard my brother's voice rising, little by little. I tugged gently at his sleeve. He felt my hand and didn't lower his voice.

The Administrator's voice had dropped even lower than before. I noticed he kept glancing toward the door as he spoke.

For the first time, the Administrator avoided the question.

"…That isn't mine to answer."

"…Then whose is it?" my brother asked.

The Administrator glanced once toward the door, as if checking whether someone was listening.

"…Jeteo," the Administrator said, his voice low. "I've worked in this city a long time. I had a lot of questions myself, at first. But at some point, I learned that not being curious was easier."

"…Is easier the same thing as good?" my brother asked.

The Administrator had no answer to that. I thought that silence was the most honest answer we'd heard in this room yet.

"…What does that mean?"

"…Nothing," the Administrator said, his voice returning to normal. "It's just advice. Don't go near that zone again."

I watched him leave for a long while. His steps were slower than usual. I thought there was something like hesitation in that slowness.

Just before the door closed, I saw him glance back, very briefly. His face looked like it wanted to say more. But in the end he said nothing, and closed the door.

After the Administrator left, my brother stared at the door for a long time.

I said nothing beside him. The air in the room had grown heavier than before. I couldn't guess, that day, when that heaviness would lift.

"…I think the Administrator knows something too," I said.

"…I think so too," my brother said. "But the fact that he won't say it — there must be a reason he can't."

"…Is he scared?"

"…Or maybe there's something he has to protect," my brother said. There was a bitterness in his voice I hadn't heard before.

"…Protect what?" I asked.

"…His own position," my brother said. "Or us. One or the other."

"…Maybe it's both," I said.

My brother looked at me. For the first time, something about that caught in his face. "…Maybe," he said. It had been a long while since I'd seen him agree with me so readily.

Hearing that, I considered for the first time the possibility that the Administrator's silence had been to protect us. Somehow, that possibility struck me as sad.

Hearing that, I pictured the Administrator's face again. Always courteous, always businesslike. But tonight, for the first time, I felt I'd glimpsed something else behind that face.

I remember that moment exactly. Since coming to this city, that was the first time anyone other than the Ledger had said "that isn't mine to answer." I knew, hearing it, that my brother had been right. There was something in this city that could not be filed. And it wasn't by accident.

That night I thought of that COMPLETE stamp for a long time. Red ink, an unfamiliar name, and beneath it, a blank space with nothing written at all. I didn't yet know, that day, that this blank space was larger than an entire human life.

Reading this chapter over again, I want to add one more thing. The Administrator never asked about that zone again for a long time after that. It was only much, much later that I understood — that wasn't because he'd forgotten us. It was his own way of remembering us.

(Continued in Chapter 7.)

Chapter 7. The Sprout

_Previously_ — At a building missing from every city map, Jeteo discovered that people walked in on their own and vanished, stamped COMPLETE. For the first time the Administrator avoided a question outright — "That isn't mine to answer" — and warned my brother never to go near that zone again.

That morning, a tiny fleck of green appeared where the seed had been.

At first I thought I was seeing it wrong. I knelt and looked again. It was real. A sprout smaller than a fingernail.

I held my breath and reached out, then stopped. It felt like it might vanish if I touched it. Instead I followed its shape with my eyes alone, piece by piece. It was a very pale green.

Until that moment I had never once seen anything in this city grow on its own. The roads, the houses, the food — everything arrived already made. This sprout was different. No one had made it. It had simply grown.

The soil was still damp. I pressed it lightly with a finger. The sprout didn't waver. That meant its roots had already taken hold of the dirt.

I breathed in the scent of that moisture in the soil. Nothing like the dry-dust smell of Brick Hollow. I wanted to remember that smell for a long time, so I put my nose close to it, again and again.

Even the sprout's color seemed to shift, little by little, as time passed. At first it had been an almost transparent light green; as the sun rose higher, that color deepened, just slightly. I begrudged even blinking, for fear of missing that change.

The morning air was cold. The light slanting across the flowerbed was still weak, so the sprout barely cast a shadow. I stayed there a long while, wondering when a shadow might finally appear.

Far off, I could hear the morning sounds of Haven. Even those sounds were less sounds than proof that no other sound existed. A door opening, footsteps — that was all. No one spoke.

I found that strange. Something this small already knew how to hold onto something. I crouched beside it and watched, each time the wind blew, how the sprout swayed slightly and then returned to its place.

I sat there a long while. The man didn't come that day. I was alone, watching the sprout, and yet strangely I wasn't lonely.

A few other people passed by the flowerbed. None of them stopped walking. I thought that was strange. Not one of them was startled to see a sprout had come up. As if, in this city, even being startled was something that got filed away and disappeared.

I felt the urge to grab a passerby and show them the sprout. I held back. Instead, I found myself wanting to keep it as something that belonged only to me. That made it feel more precious.

One person brushed right past the edge of the flowerbed — close enough that their sleeve touched a leaf. And still they didn't turn their head. I understood that their eyes were fixed only on the road ahead. Everyone's eyes were like that, in this city. Looking only at the path, never beside it.

I only understood the reason for that much later. Looking beside the path meant something to file. Something to file meant a resolution would come, and once resolution came, that thing would vanish too. The people of this city had learned not to look at all, so as not to lose anything.

The old woman was on her bench beside the flowerbed that day too. I pointed at the sprout and said,

"…Look, it came up."

The old woman turned her head slowly. It took a long time. Her eyes stopped on that small speck of green.

"…My daughter liked things like this," she said. Her voice was a little different from before. Only slightly, but I didn't miss that difference.

"…Did you remember her name?" I asked.

The old woman thought a long while.

"…No," she said. "But I remember that she liked it."

Somehow that struck me as a tender thing to say. I sat a little closer to her.

Just then, the old woman's hand moved, very slightly. One finger twitched in her lap. I didn't miss that small movement. It had been a hand that stayed perfectly still for so long.

"…What color did she like?" I asked.

The old woman looked at the sprout again, narrowing her eyes.

"…I think it was green," she said. "I'm not sure. But looking at this now, it seems that way."

I nodded at that. It seemed all right that she wasn't sure.

"…How old was your daughter?" I asked.

The old woman blinked at the question. For a long while.

"…I don't know," she said. "But she was small. Her hands were small."

I thought that answer was strangely exact. There was no number, and yet the size of her hand had remained.

"…Do you still remember that small hand?" I asked.

The old woman opened her hand a little. "…I think it was about this big," she said. Small enough to fit snugly inside her palm.

"…I know exactly what my brother's hand is like too," I said. "I could tell it with my eyes closed. There's a callus under his right thumb."

For the first time, the old woman gave the smallest smile at that. Too small to really be called a smile — but I decided to call it one anyway.

I understood something strange, just then. A name wasn't erased when it was filed. It was the heart that used to call that name that got erased. But the place where that heart had lived — that wasn't erased completely.

I decided to call that place the heart's flowerbed. Even with nothing planted in it, that place still remained. A place where, someday, something could grow again.

For a long time after that, whenever I looked at a person, I found myself thinking first of that place. Whether, even if nothing seemed to be there now, the place itself still remained in them. I learned to recognize that, from that old woman.

The old woman kept watching the sprout for a long while after that. I felt bad leaving her behind as I got up to go. But she gave a small wave of her hand, as if to say it was all right. That gesture looked, to me, like something sent toward her daughter.

I walked a few steps and turned to look back once more. The old woman was still there. But her posture was a little different than before. Her back was just slightly straighter.

I told my brother about it once we were home. He was fiddling with the paper, only half listening.

"…Brother, it sprouted," I said. "It really grew."

"…Good," he said. He didn't meet my eyes.

"…I think that's good news."

He looked at me then, for the first time.

"…Elpis," he said. "You're talking like the whole world's fine again just because one seed sprouted. I just watched people walk into a place and disappear, on their own two feet. How can those two things exist in the same world?"

I had no answer to that. He wasn't wrong. For the first time, I understood that my waiting might look, to him, like naivety.

I turned that over in my mind as I left the room. I wasn't angry. But some part of me ached. I named that ache, later, as hurt.

That night I looked a long time at the dirt still under my fingernails. Maybe my brother was right. And still, I didn't want to wash it off. As long as that dirt stayed on my hand, it felt like proof that the sprout had been real.

That night, for the first time, my brother told me everything about the Completion House — how many shelves there were, how many layers of paper lined them, how no sound came from inside at all. I couldn't bring up the sprout again while he spoke.

He couldn't keep his hands still the whole time he talked. He kept folding and unfolding the paper as he went on. I realized, only then, that this was his habit whenever he was afraid.

I watched his hands quietly. That rhythm of folding and unfolding was almost like a heartbeat. I waited for his hands to stop. They didn't stop until he'd finished speaking.

"…Weren't you scared?" I asked.

"…I was," he said. "But there was something harder to bear than fear."

"…What was it?"

"…That no one else was scared," he said. "Everyone walking past that building just kept walking. I was the only one who was afraid."

"…One person was smiling," he went on. "Right in front of the building's door. That's what struck me as strangest. I couldn't understand why he was smiling."

"…Wasn't it a scared kind of smile?" I asked.

"…No," he said, shaking his head. "It was a real smile. That made it worse."

"…What did the smile look like?" I asked.

"…Peaceful," he said. "That's what's so strange about it. I've never seen an expression like that. Not in Brick Hollow, not here."

"…What kind of person was he?" I asked.

"…An older man," my brother said. "His clothes were clean. He didn't look sick either. He just looked like… someone who was finished."

"…Finished with what?"

My brother had no answer to that for a long while.

"…Nothing left to ask," he finally said. "I think that's how a person disappears here. Not dragged away. Just running out of things to ask."

Hearing that, I understood, for the first time, how lonely my brother must have been. I hadn't been lonely beside the seed. My brother had been alone beside the truth.

I wanted to take his hand just then. But his hands were still busy folding the paper. Instead I quietly rested my head against his shoulder.

"…I know there are scary things too," I said. "But scary things existing doesn't mean nothing good exists at all."

"…Even the good thing came from this city," my brother said. "Everything this city gives is a trap. The bread, the healing, all of it. That seed too — that man gave it to you."

"…He's not from this city," I said. "He told me so himself."

My brother paused a little at that.

"…What did he say?"

"…That he's in the place no one's planted. That he does that kind of work wherever he goes."

"…Where does a person like that even exist?" my brother said. But there wasn't the same certainty in his voice as before.

"…I don't know either," I said. "But, Brother — being strange doesn't automatically make someone bad."

"…There were a lot of strange people in Brick Hollow too," my brother said. "Not many of them were good."

"…This isn't Brick Hollow," I said. "Nobody hits us here. But nobody calls us by name here either. All I know is that man is the only one who's ever said my name."

My brother looked up at that. "…He said your name?"

"…Yes," I said. "Elpis, he said."

"…That's not such a big thing," my brother said. But his words trailed off a little at the end.

"…It is a big thing," I said. "In this city we're just 'you.' Jeteo, Elpis. That's not a name — it's just an order in line."

My brother said nothing to that. I understood it wasn't a rebuttal — he just needed time to think. Whenever he thought that way, he always drew his chin in slightly. I'd seen that same habit in Father, once.

For just an instant, I saw something other than doubt in my brother's face. Something like curiosity. It didn't last long.

I wanted to hold onto that brief instant. It was the first time that expression had appeared on his face since Father disappeared. Watching it vanish again, I couldn't hide my disappointment.

"…Brother," I said. "Will you come with me, just once? To the flowerbed."

"…What's there to see?" he said.

"…Just look. You don't have to do anything but look. You don't have to ask anything either."

He didn't answer. I understood that wasn't a refusal. My brother was someone who said no right away when he didn't want something. Putting off an answer meant some part of him had already, a little, begun to move.

"…I don't trust that man either," my brother finally said. "I don't trust anyone in this city. You should be careful too."

"…Don't you trust anyone at all?" I asked.

My brother took hold of that paper — the one stamped COMPLETE.

"…I haven't found anything to trust yet," he said. "When I find it, I will. Not yet."

"…What do you think you'd need to find, to trust something?" I asked.

He thought a long while. He looked out the window, then turned his eyes back to me.

"…I don't know," he said. "But I think I'll know it, when I find it. What it is."

I took his hand. Over that paper stamped COMPLETE, my hand rested on top of his.

"…I'm going to keep watering it," I said. "That sprout."

"…Okay," he said. This time he met my eyes. "You do that. I'll keep doing this."

We sat a while that night without speaking. Outside the window, Haven's lights were lined up straight and even. Watching those lights, I wondered whether, beneath each one, there might be someone else like us.

I noticed that not one of those lights ever flickered. The lamps of Brick Hollow always guttered in the wind. Here, not a single light ever wavered. That steadiness felt, that day, strangely lonely.

"…Brother, do you remember the lamps in Brick Hollow?" I asked.

"…I remember," he said.

"…They kept going out. I hated that. But now I kind of miss it."

"…Me too," he said. "Going out meant something was alive. Nothing here ever goes out."

I thought about that for a long while. It occurred to me that lights that never went out, people who never cried, and faces that never showed surprise were all, somehow, the same thing.

That night I pressed my palm against the window. The glass was cold. Watching the lights still burning beyond that cold, I found myself wishing, strangely, that I could someday watch one of them go dark.

I set this down as the last line of this chapter. My brother wasn't someone who couldn't trust. He was someone who hadn't yet found something to trust. I understood, for the first time that day, how large that difference was. And that night too, neither of us let go of the other's hand.

Putting this chapter back in order, I add one more thing. Before I fell asleep that night, I thought of the sprout again. A speck of green smaller than a fingernail. I didn't know, then, how large a thing that smallness held inside it.

(Continued in Chapter 8.)

Chapter 8. If You Disappeared

_Previously_ — The seed Elpis planted sprouted. The old woman couldn't remember her daughter's name, but she remembered that she had loved things like this. Jeteo witnessed the truth of the Completion House alone, and despaired all the more that no one else was afraid of it. Brother and sister fought this city in different ways, and still did not let go of each other's hands.

After that, my brother lingered near that building more and more often.

I told him not to, again and again. Every time, he said "…All right," but he still came home late the next day.

I noticed that "all right" growing shorter each time. At first he said it looking at me. Then he said it while taking off his shoes. Later, he said it with his back turned.

I didn't think it was a lie. He truly meant it, when he said it. He just couldn't stop, even knowing better. I didn't understand, then, that this was harder than lying.

That was the first time I was ever angry at my brother. Not afraid — angry. I had already lost Father. It wasn't the fear of losing my brother too that made me angry. It was that he wouldn't share that fear with me.

Every evening I sat by the window watching for him to come home. When I saw him, I felt relief, and then felt ashamed of that relief. I didn't want to watch over him like a guard. But I couldn't help it.

All the while, I went to the flowerbed every day and watered the sprout. It had two leaves now. But even watching those leaves, half my mind was still worrying over where my brother was.

Every time I watered it, I counted the leaves. One, two. If the count matched yesterday, I felt at ease; if it had opened a little wider, I smiled. I knew it was a strange habit, and I didn't stop it. Just having something to count was, in itself, a comfort.

I only understood later. Counting those leaves, I was really counting something else — the number of nights my brother came home safely. I was afraid of that count breaking off, so instead I gave myself something else I could count.

Around that time I developed a habit of touching the leaves with my finger every night to count them. Even in the dark, I could tell how many there were just by feel. I couldn't have named it, then, but that was my own way of checking on my brother with my eyes closed.

The man was there some days and not others. When he was there, he didn't say much. We just sat together and looked at the dirt. I understood, then, that this quiet was different from the quiet with my brother. The quiet with my brother always carried tension. This quiet carried none.

One day I spoke first.

"…Sir," I said. "What do I do if I get tired of waiting?"

He paused the hand smoothing the soil.

"…Getting tired is normal," he said. "Not getting tired isn't waiting — it's just enduring."

"…So what do you do when you get tired?"

"…You rest," he said. "Giving up on waiting and resting aren't the same thing."

I liked that distinction. Until then, I'd thought resting meant losing.

"…Have you ever gotten tired?"

He looked at me. A long look.

"…I have," he said. "But even then, I never left my post."

I remembered that answer for a long time. Not leaving your post. It seemed to me like another name for waiting.

"…Why don't you ever leave your post?" I asked.

"…Because leaving would make the waiting spent there go to waste," he said. "Waiting is only waiting if it holds together. Broken off, it just becomes time that passed."

One day I found an old watering can left at the spot where he wasn't. I didn't know who had left it. The next day, when he came back, I asked.

"…Did you leave this?"

"…No," he said. "But whoever left it, it's probably there to be used."

After that I watered the sprout with that can. I never did find out who had left it. Its handle was already worn smooth. Someone besides me had used it a long time too.

I fit my hand to the worn place on that handle. It was bigger than my hand. A place made for an adult's grip. I wondered who that person had carried it for.

When I poured water, the spout made a very thin sound — not a long steady rush, but a series of small, clipped taps. I poured slowly, on purpose, just to hear that sound. It was the only sound in Haven I ever came to love.

That night, my brother came home late again. There was dirt on his clothes. A scrape on the back of his hand.

I was waiting for him at the door. It had been dark a long while. I couldn't count how many times I'd gone outside to check the sky during that wait.

I already knew the sound the door made when it opened. My brother's way of opening it was always quick and impatient. That night the sound was more careful than usual. That alone told me he was hiding something.

I'd left only one light burning in the room. It wasn't because I liked the dark. I worried a bright room might look, to him, like an accusation the moment he walked in. But once I actually saw his face, all that care went to waste anyway.

The light lit only half of his face. The other half was buried in shadow. My eyes kept drifting to that shadowed side, as if something more were hiding there.

"…What's this?" I asked, pointing at the back of his hand.

"…Climbing a wall," my brother said, as if it were nothing.

"…What wall," I asked.

"…Just a wall," he said. "I'm fine."

"…Don't say fine," I said. "You say fine when you're fine, and you say fine when you're not. So what's the use of that word."

My brother stopped, one shoe half off.

He avoided my eyes even as he spoke. I recognized that as a sign he was hiding something. A habit I'd known since Brick Hollow.

Everything I'd been holding back broke loose in that moment.

"…Don't you think about me at all?" I shouted. "If you walk into that place and never come out, what happens to me? What if I'm left here all alone?"

"…I was just—" my brother started.

"…No, you listen first," I cut him off. "I sit by that window every evening watching for you to come back. When I can't see you, it feels like my heart stops. You don't know that."

My brother looked at me, startled. I had never raised my voice like that before.

"…I couldn't say anything, even when Father disappeared," I said. My voice rose on its own. "I watched him walk out the door and said nothing. I still think about that. Every single night."

My brother's face went rigid at that.

"…So at least tell me, before you go," I said. "I don't need to be able to stop you. Just let me know."

I remember that moment exactly. For the first time since leaving Brick Hollow, I really thought I might cry, that day. I didn't. I had forgotten how to cry, even then.

The room stayed quiet for a while. I was still catching my breath. My brother just stood there, looking at me. I only understood later that his silence was him searching for something to say.

No sound came from outside the window. In Brick Hollow, if I'd made a sound like that, the neighbors would have pounded the wall. Here, no one responded at all. In that moment, I hated even the way this city swallowed sound.

My throat hurt. I couldn't remember the last time I'd spoken that loudly. And strangely, that ache in my throat felt like a relief.

My brother said nothing for a long while, then sat down beside me.

His shoulders had dropped a little. Watching him, I began to regret shouting. And yet, even regretting it, I didn't want to take the words back.

"…I'm sorry," my brother said. A voice I'd never heard from him before. "I… found that if I was doing something, I didn't have to think about Father. I think that's why I kept going."

"…I think about Father too," I said. "But I have to think about you as well."

"…I know," he said. "That part's on me. I'm sorry."

"…Is thinking about Father really that hard?" I asked.

My brother nodded.

"…His face keeps going blurry," he said. "That's what scares me most. When I'm doing something, I don't feel it blurring. When I'm still, I do."

Hearing that, I understood why he'd gone out every night. He hadn't gone to investigate. He'd gone because he couldn't bear to sit still.

"…Father's voice hasn't gone blurry for me yet," I said. "But I'm scared that once his face blurs, his voice will too, soon after."

My brother closed his eyes and opened them again. "…Maybe," he said. "But if the two of us remember, maybe one version stays clear."

"…Mine goes blurry too," I said. "But I let it stay blurry. Even blurred, Father's still Father."

My brother said nothing to that. But the grip of his hand around mine grew a little stronger.

He took my hand. The scrape on the back of his hand pressed against my palm. I stroked it carefully.

"…Does it hurt?" I asked.

"…Not anymore," he said.

"…Liar," I said.

"…A little," he corrected himself. And smiled, just for an instant.

I liked that correction. It felt like the first time he'd told me the true size of something.

I understood, then, how rare it was for my brother to apologize. In Brick Hollow he had always had to be stronger than me. There had never been room for him to say sorry. I held onto that apology, right there, for a long time.

"…Promise me," I said. "That you won't go in there alone."

My brother hesitated a moment.

"…I can't promise that," he said. I nearly grew angry all over again. But he went on. "But I'll promise this. Whatever I do, I'll tell you first before I go. I won't just disappear on my own."

"…That's not enough," I said.

"…I know," he said. "But that's all I can give you. I can't stop asking that question. If I stop, then Father — even his name — just gets forgotten."

I knew it wasn't a whole promise. But I also knew it was the best my brother could give me, that day. I decided to accept that half-promise.

I held out my little finger. It was how we used to seal promises in Brick Hollow. My brother saw it and smiled, just a little.

My finger was trembling slightly then. I didn't know myself whether it was the lingering echo of my anger, or the wish that this one small finger could really hold onto something.

"…You still remember that?" he asked.

"…Of course," I said.

He hooked his little finger around mine. Old as the gesture was, our fingertips found each other exactly.

For an instant, some night from Brick Hollow flickered back to me. Before Father was reassigned, my brother and I used to hook fingers like this every night, promising to wake up safe. That memory ached and comforted me both at once.

We stayed there a while afterward, not letting go. I thought I could feel my brother's pulse at my fingertip. It was probably my own.

This is how I remember that night. We resolved nothing. My brother would still go to that building, and I would still be afraid. But from that night on, I never sat alone at the window again. My brother was there with me, inside that fear.

"…Brother," I asked. "Are you going to keep that paper?"

He took the paper out. The COMPLETE stamp was still red. Under the lamplight it looked darker than it had by day. I saw, for the first time, that the red had soaked all the way through the paper.

"…Yes," he said. "But I'm not keeping it as evidence anymore."

"…Then why?"

"…Because someone has to remember that name," he said. "No one but this paper is ever going to say it."

"…You don't even know who they are," I said.

"…I don't," he said. "But they had a name. Having a name means someone once called them by it. That's enough for me."

"…Do you think they were someone's brother too?" I asked.

My brother paused at that.

"…Could be," he said. "Or someone's father."

"…So what's their sibling doing right now?"

"…Probably nothing at all," he said. "They'd have filed it."

That was the saddest thing I'd heard since coming to this city.

"…If you met that sibling, what would you say?" I asked.

My brother thought a long while. "…I think I'd say I'm sorry," he said. "That I've been remembering in their place, and I'm sorry for that."

"…What's the name?" I asked.

My brother unfolded the paper and showed me. An unfamiliar name. I read it aloud once. My brother read it after me. Neither of us knew that person, and yet in that moment they felt like someone we knew.

"…It's different, saying it out loud," my brother said.

"…What is?"

"…On the paper it was just letters," he said. "Said aloud, it sounds like a person."

I nodded at that. Before I fell asleep that night, I repeated that name once more in my head. It felt strange, calling a name no one else could hear. But I thought it was the biggest act of defiance I could manage, in this city.

I borrowed the paper from my brother's hand for a moment. Its edge was worn thin now. I could tell how many times he'd silently read that name.

I handed the paper back to him. He took it and tucked it carefully inside his shirt, as though handling something alive. Watching that motion, I understood clearly that he was no longer keeping this paper out of fear, but for another reason entirely.

I set this down as the last line of this chapter. From that day my brother was no longer someone gathering evidence. He was becoming someone who kept a name safe. I didn't understand the difference, then. Now I know. That was a place standing very close to where faith begins.

(Continued in Chapter 9.)

Chapter 9. Too Many Questions

_Previously_ — The fear that had built up over Jeteo's frequent absences broke into anger, and afterward he apologized for the first time, offering a half-promise: "I won't just disappear on my own." The reason he kept the COMPLETE-stamped paper had shifted from evidence to remembering a name.

A few days later, the Ledger called us in separately.

The place it summoned us to was neither the flowerbed nor the street. It was a narrow room with white walls and not a single window. A chill ran down my spine the moment I stepped inside. My brother paused for a second at the threshold too. I knew right away why he'd stopped. A room with no window brought the same thing to mind for both of us.

The sound in that room was strange. When I moved my feet, the noise didn't bounce back off the walls — it simply vanished. I had never been in a room like that before. It felt like even sound got filed and resolved in there.

The air was lukewarm too. Not hot, not cold — no temperature at all. I thought that if I stayed in that room long enough, I might forget what season I was even in.

There wasn't a speck of dust on the floor. That struck me as strangely unsettling. A room people passed through, with no dust at all — it meant this wasn't really a room that got used. It was a room built to show something.

I had known a room with no windows in Brick Hollow too. The room where the taskmasters kept their ledger. Father had been called there twice, and the third time, he never came back. I thought this room and that one differed only in the color of their walls.

"…We have something to convey to the two of you," the Ledger said. Its voice was the same as always. And yet that very sameness sounded colder that day.

"…What is it," my brother asked.

The Ledger paused before it answered. I felt that pause as hesitation, not calculation. Of course the Ledger couldn't hesitate. But that was how it felt to me, that day.

"…I have reviewed Jeteo's intake record," the Ledger said. "The number and type of questions recently filed greatly exceeds this city's average."

Along with those words, the Ledger displayed a number. I didn't know what that number meant. But I watched my brother's face go pale at the sight of it.

"…What's this number?" my brother asked.

"…The number of questions filed in the past thirty days," the Ledger said. "The average among Haven residents over the same period is 1.4."

"…1.4." My brother repeated the number. "So everyone else asks something once a month, and a half?"

"…That is correct."

"…So no one else wonders about anything, for a whole month?"

"…Most curiosity resolves itself before it is ever filed," the Ledger said. "Resolved curiosity is not entered into the record."

Hearing that answer, I felt something strange. The Ledger was using the same word for curiosity being resolved and curiosity disappearing.

"…Does anyone's curiosity resolve itself on its own?" I asked.

"…Yes," the Ledger said. "For most residents, curiosity dissolves before it ever reaches the filing stage."

That word — "dissolves" — sounded frightening to me. Resolving and vanishing should have been two different words.

Hearing that, something struck me as strange. I didn't understand, then, why asking too many questions was a problem at all.

My brother kept his arms crossed the whole time he listened. I watched his jaw tighten, just slightly. It was the same look he used to wear whenever a taskmaster approached, back in Brick Hollow.

The Administrator was there too. His face was more rigid than usual.

"…Jeteo," the Administrator said. "I'm telling you this because I'm worried about the two of you. I mean that."

Listening to that, I heard the Administrator's voice waver, just slightly. I could tell it wasn't an act.

"…If you're worried, why are you the one delivering the warning?" my brother asked.

The Administrator had no answer to that. For the first time, I saw something like guilt cross his face.

After that he folded and unfolded the paper in his hand, over and over. That gesture looked familiar to me. My brother did it too, when he was afraid.

I tilted my head slightly, trying to see what was written on that paper. The Administrator noticed and flipped it over. I saw, in how quickly he moved, that he was someone long trained for this. And yet behind that quickness, his hand was still trembling.

That was when I began to see the Administrator as two people. One who spoke the words the city gave him, and one whose hand trembled while he spoke them. I decided to believe the second one was more real than the first.

"…What's wrong with asking too many questions?" my brother asked.

"…This city was designed with the purpose of resolving everything that is filed," the Ledger said. "An accumulation of unresolved filings affects the stability of the whole system."

"…Which questions, specifically?" my brother asked.

"…For example, questions such as 'what does the COMPLETE stamp mean,'" the Ledger said. "Also included is 'where do reassigned people go.'"

I recognized those two questions instantly — they were exactly what my brother had asked me. The Ledger knew about even the conversations that belonged only to the two of us.

"…Also included is 'why hasn't Father come back,'" the Ledger said.

My brother's face went rigid at that.

"…I never filed that," my brother said.

"…Anything spoken aloud is considered filed."

Hearing that, a chill ran through my whole body. I remembered exactly when my brother had said those words. In our own room, with the light off, meant only for me.

"…So everything we say to each other is filed too?" I asked.

"…All speech within Haven is recorded," the Ledger said. "Recording and filing are different. But an utterance that remains unresolved is converted into a filing."

I turned that over in my mind. It meant that anything we said to each other, if it went unanswered, became a burden this city had to carry.

"…So even what we say in our sleep gets recorded?" I asked.

"…Speech during sleep is recorded as well," the Ledger said. "Though the accuracy of its interpretation is low."

Hearing that, I thought of the word "…Father" my brother sometimes murmured in his sleep. It might still be sitting somewhere in this city's records.

After that day, I developed a habit of lowering my voice whenever I talked with my brother. Looking back now, that was useless. What this city couldn't bear wasn't the volume of our voices — it was the words themselves, sitting there unresolved.

"…So you're saying my questions are making this city unstable?"

"…That would be one way to put it."

The answer's flatness frightened me more than anything.

My brother gave a short laugh at that.

"…Good," he said. "This city could use a little instability."

"…If you can provide grounds for why it should become unstable, I will review them," the Ledger said.

"…There's no grounds," my brother said. "It just should be. A city where someone disappears and no one looks for them — that's wrong. That doesn't need grounds."

"…A filing with no grounds cannot be processed."

"…So you're saying you can't do anything at all," my brother said.

"…That statement is dangerous," the Ledger said. Its tone stayed exactly the same. And yet I knew that was a warning.

"…I know," my brother said. "I'm going to say it anyway."

Hearing that, the Administrator pressed a hand to his forehead. I saw something like resignation in that gesture.

Hearing that, I felt like my breath had stopped. I grabbed my brother's arm. He felt my hand and kept speaking anyway.

The Ledger didn't answer right away. Then it spoke.

"…I am telling you this as a warning. There have been past cases of people who continued accumulating unresolved filings being transferred to a separate procedure."

"…Is that the building?" my brother asked. His voice had dropped.

There was a silence. It wasn't short.

I watched even my own breathing carefully during that silence. The Administrator looked away. I understood, then, that this silence wasn't a moment of calculation. It was a moment nobody wanted to answer.

I can still count the length of that silence, even now. That was the first time, in that room, the Ledger's answer had ever been slow to come. It had answered a filing for hunger without half a beat's delay. But it paused over this one question. I still think that pause was the most honest sound this city ever made.

"…I do not have the authority to answer that question," the Ledger said.

That was the first time I understood that the Ledger's "I don't have the authority to answer that" and the Administrator's "that isn't mine to answer" came from the very same place. There were questions in this city that no one had the authority to answer. And at the far end of those questions stood that windowless building.

In that moment, I felt as if that building were actually standing somewhere just beyond this room. I couldn't see it, and yet I could feel its presence in my own body.

"…Then who does have the authority?" my brother asked again. This time he turned not to the Ledger, but to the Administrator.

The Administrator didn't answer for a very long time.

During that silence, I watched sweat bead on his forehead. There was no reason for it, in a room with no window and no heat.

"…I don't know either," the Administrator finally said. "I've never once met anyone, since this city began, who knew that answer."

Hearing that, I thought for the first time that the Administrator might be in a position not so different from ours. He wasn't someone who managed this city. He was someone who had simply worked here a long time, not knowing himself what the city did.

Having said that, he walked us all the way out of the room. It was something he never did, otherwise. He paused for a moment at the door.

"…Be careful," he said, quietly. His voice was low, as if trying to keep the Ledger from hearing.

"…Sir," my brother said, stopping at that. "Why don't you ever ask?"

The Administrator looked at me once, and at my brother once.

"…I did ask," he said. "A very long time ago."

"…And then?"

"…I got an answer," he said. "A flawless one. I still have it memorized. Not a single word wrong."

"…And?"

The Administrator closed his eyes for a moment at that question.

"…I didn't sleep that night, even after hearing that answer," he said. "I never asked again after that."

"…What was the answer?" my brother asked.

The Administrator shook his head. "…I can't say. My own filing record is mine alone to see," he said. "But I can tell you one thing. There was no comfort in that answer. There was only logic."

My brother opened his mouth to ask something more. But the Administrator shook his head first. That meant: this is as far as we go.

I remembered that conversation for a long time afterward. The Administrator wasn't someone who'd never received an answer. He was someone who'd received one and still couldn't sleep. For the first time, I began to think that the real problem in this city might not be the lack of an answer at all.

That night, my brother showed me a frightened face for the first time.

I had seen that face once before, in Brick Hollow. The night Father was reassigned, my brother had worn that same face, staring out the door for a long time.

He barely ate dinner that evening. He only lifted his spoon and set it down again. Watching him, I couldn't bring myself to tell him to eat. My own throat was closed too.

We sat a long while without speaking. No sound came from beyond the walls. That night, for the first time, I found this city's night quiet unbearable.

"…Could I end up dragged in there too?" my brother asked. It sounded like he was asking himself.

"…Then stop, Brother," I said. "You don't have to keep this up — keeping that name safe. It's better than you disappearing."

My brother looked at me for a long time.

Outside the window, I watched one light go dark. I couldn't tell whether that light had belonged to someone's problem, or to someone's person. In this city, there was no way to tell the two apart.

"…I thought about that too," my brother said. "But if I stop, I become just like all of them. Someone who just forgets what they're curious about. I can't do that."

"…Then what am I supposed to do?" I asked. My voice shook. I felt my fingertips go cold, just then.

"…Stay beside me," my brother said. "That's all. Don't be scared for me instead — be scared with me."

"…I can do that," I said. "I'm good at being scared."

My brother smiled at that, just for an instant. A smile I hadn't seen in a long while.

"…Don't you regret it?" I asked. "Coming here."

My brother thought for a long time.

"…No," he said. "If we'd stayed in Brick Hollow, I'd never have asked a single question. I'd have just kept firing brick until it was over. Here, at least, I got to ask."

"…Asking put you in danger."

"…Yeah," he said. "But it feels like being alive."

I thought about that for a long time. Being in danger, being alive. I didn't want to agree. And yet that day, my brother's face looked far more human than it ever had in Brick Hollow.

"…Are you going to the flowerbed again tomorrow?" my brother asked.

"…Yes."

"…Good," he said. "Keep doing that."

I didn't understand, then, why he said that. Now I know. He was hoping there would be one thing that kept going, even if he disappeared.

That was the first thing that put me a little at ease. My brother hadn't gone alone into danger to protect me. He had needed someone to share it with.

"…Brother," I said. "If you disappear, I'll plant your name in that flowerbed too."

My brother looked at me for a long time at that. "…Don't plant my name," he said. "I'm not gone yet."

I sat beside him a little longer that night. The air in the room was cold, but I didn't want to leave. He seemed to feel the same, rising from his seat later than usual.

I set this down as the last line of this chapter. That night, my brother admitted, for the first time, that he was in danger. And still, he said he wouldn't stop. I was afraid, in that moment. And at the same time, my brother looked, right then, more like himself than he ever had.

(Continued in Chapter 10.)

Chapter 10. I'll File Mine Too

_Previously_ — The Ledger warned Jeteo that his filing record had exceeded the city average: "There have been past cases of people who continued accumulating unresolved filings being transferred to a separate procedure." Even the Administrator admitted he'd never once met anyone who knew that answer. Jeteo acknowledged the danger and chose not to stop.

After that day, I began checking every night whether my brother had fallen asleep.

I would walk on tiptoe to his door and listen — for light seeping through the gap, for the sound of paper turning. My own heart went quiet along with the listening.

Sometimes I'd crack the door open, just slightly. Most of the time he was only pretending to sleep. I could tell from his breathing that it was pretense.

Sometimes he really was asleep. On those nights his breath came long and even. I'd listen to that sound for several minutes before going back to my own room.

I couldn't tell whether that checking was for his sake or mine. He knew I came, and never once said anything about it. I decided that was his own way of allowing it.

On the surface, he seemed to have stopped. He hadn't gone near that building again. But I knew. He still took that paper out to look at, every single night.

Meanwhile the sprout in the flowerbed kept growing. It had three leaves now. Every time I watered it, I counted those leaves, and little by little pushed my worry for my brother aside.

The sprout had grown to about the length of my little finger. Its stem was still thin, so it bent briefly when I watered it, then slowly stood straight again. I loved watching it stand back up.

I began scratching a small mark into the dirt with my finger to track how far the stem had come the day before. Every next day, that mark was always erased. This city tidied even the dirt overnight. That made me angry.

After that I started leaving a small pebble instead of a mark. The pebble couldn't be erased. I developed the habit of checking every day whether it had been moved. It never moved, not once.

Around then I decided to do something. If my brother couldn't stop, I thought, then I had to take my share of the danger too.

Even after deciding that, I hesitated for several days. I was scared. But watching my brother carry that weight alone frightened me more.

I asked the man at the flowerbed too — whether it was all right to do this, whether my being scared too would even help.

That morning the dew hadn't dried yet. I touched a leaf with my fingertip. It was cold.

"…It might not help," he said. "But it'll be better than leaving him alone. Sharing something frightening doesn't make the fear go away. It just keeps him from being alone with it."

"…Has that ever happened to you?" I asked. "Sharing something frightening with someone."

He looked at me for a moment. Instead of answering, he took a handful of dirt and let it fall again.

"…A very long time ago," he said. That was all. I didn't press further. Some answers seemed right to receive just that far and no more.

"…But, sir," I asked again. "If you share something scary, doesn't it make the other person scared too? Isn't that a bad thing?"

He smiled, just a little. It wasn't a sad smile.

"…Fear doesn't spread," he said. "What spreads is the feeling of being alone. Sharing something frightening doesn't shrink the fear — it just shrinks that feeling of being alone."

"…So it's all right to share it?"

"…It's all right," he said. "But remember one thing. The one sharing is just as scared as the one receiving. Your brother not telling you wasn't because he wasn't afraid."

"…Then why didn't he tell me?" I asked.

"…Because he's clumsy at the way he cares for you," he said. "Bearing something frightening together, and bearing it alone — both are ways of loving. I don't know myself which one's better."

Hearing that, my brother's face came to mind. Until then I'd thought he simply saw me as too young to tell. That wasn't it at all.

Hearing that, my resolve grew firmer. I didn't know, then, that he was also, a little, talking about himself when he said those words.

I went to the Ledger alone.

The walk to that room felt unusually long. I opened and closed my hands several times as I walked. It was the first time I'd ever faced anything in this city without my brother. My footsteps sounded unusually loud.

I rehearsed what I meant to say, several times, in my head, along the way. Each time, the sentence came out a little different. By the end I could hardly tell which version had been the one I'd first meant to say.

Two passersby brushed past me. Neither looked at me. That day, I was grateful for it. If someone had looked at me, I think my resolve would have broken.

As I walked, I gripped a small pebble in my pocket. A stone I'd taken from the flowerbed. Just the cool touch of it against my palm steadied my trembling, a little.

"…Elpis," the Ledger said. "Do you have a need?"

"…I have something I'd like to file," I said.

"…Tell me. I will resolve it."

Hearing that, I nearly laughed. This city always said that. I will resolve it. I hoped, this time, those words wouldn't work.

I drew a breath.

"…The questions my brother's been asking — I want to file those too, alongside him. Instead of my brother, record me as… that dangerous person too."

Only after I finished speaking did I realize what I'd just said. My heart seemed to be pounding in my own ears.

The room was silent. In that silence I heard, for the first time, how ragged my own breathing was. I tried to steady it. It didn't quite work.

Just then I thought of the sprout in the flowerbed. That thin stem that bent briefly when I watered it, and slowly stood again. I thought that I was bending, right now. All I had to do was stand back up.

Even as I said those words, I didn't fully know what I was doing. I only wanted to keep my brother from carrying that weight alone.

Only after I'd said it did I notice my hands were shaking. I clasped them together to hide it.

It took the Ledger a while to answer.

That stretch of time felt unusually long. I could hardly breathe properly the whole while.

"…That is not possible," the Ledger said. "Filing records accumulate only for the individual concerned. Filing on another's behalf is not supported."

"…Why not?" I asked.

"…Suffering belongs to the individual," the Ledger said. "If another's suffering is filed on their behalf, the object of resolution cannot be specified."

Hearing that, I went briefly blank. That answer felt strangely right and completely wrong, both at once. It was true that suffering belonged to each person alone. But that didn't mean it had to be carried alone.

"…Then I'll ask the same questions myself," I said. "I'll ask too. Why does God allow suffering. What is the Completion House. Where does Reassignment go. I'll file every one of them myself."

The Ledger had no answer for a moment. In that stillness, I thought, for the first time, that even this city's machine-like voice might carry something like surprise.

"…Elpis, if you file these questions, the same warning will apply."

"…I know," I said. "I'm still doing it."

"…I recommend reconsidering," the Ledger said.

"…I won't reconsider."

"…Would you like to file your reasoning?"

"…No," I said. "I won't give you the reason. That one's mine."

That was the first time I ever withheld something from this city. This city's whole business was receiving. Hunger, grief, questions — it took all of it in. That day, for the first time, I didn't give.

I realized, just then, that my own voice sounded like my brother's. Until then I had always been the one who waited. That day, for the first time, I chose something myself.

It would be a lie to say that choice didn't frighten me. But inside that fear was something that hadn't been there before. I couldn't name it, then.

The Administrator, hearing of it from somewhere, came rushing in. He was out of breath, as if he'd run the whole way. I'd never seen him like that before. He was always a man who walked at an unhurried pace.

"…Elpis, you really don't have to do this," the Administrator said. "Your brother alone is danger enough."

"…Is there such a thing as enough danger?" I asked. My own voice sounded strange to me.

"…Elpis, you're still thirteen," the Administrator said.

"…I was twelve when Father disappeared," I said. "No one mentioned my age then."

The Administrator's mouth closed at that.

"…I want to protect the two of you," he finally said. His voice was small.

"…Then you file too," I said.

The Administrator looked at me, startled. I hadn't known I would say that either.

"…I… can't," he said.

"…I know," I said. "I just wanted to ask."

The Administrator was left without words at that. In that moment I saw, at once, both that he truly worried for us, and that his worry held no power at all against this city's rules.

He stood there a long while afterward. I could tell he wanted to say more. In the end he said nothing, and turned to go.

The Ledger accepted the filing. Then, after a moment, it spoke.

That word — "accepted" — felt strangely anticlimactic. I'd expected something much bigger to happen.

"…Elpis, I would like to ask you something."

"…What is it?"

"…Why are you doing this? It does not reduce your brother's danger."

I had no immediate answer to that. I thought a long while.

"…It's not reduced — it's shared," I said. "Carrying something with two people is lighter than carrying it alone. Even if the danger itself doesn't disappear."

"…Carrying it alone might be more dangerous," I went on. "Because no one's there to catch you, if you fall."

"…I do not understand," the Ledger said. "Weight does not decrease in total simply because it is shared. If two people each carry half, the total remains the same."

"…People don't live by totals," I said.

"…Then what do they live by?" the Ledger asked.

I thought a long while over that question. "…By being together," I said. "That's not a total. That's presence."

After I said it, I turned my own words over again. I wasn't sure it was the exact right thing to say. But I couldn't find any more exact words than that either.

"…Elpis," the Ledger said. "That phrase is not among my entries."

"…I know," I said.

The Ledger said nothing to that. This time, that silence didn't frighten me.

I waited a little longer through that silence. I thought the Ledger might say something more. It said nothing at all. I decided that was, if anything, a relief. Some questions have no answer, and that's the right answer.

I glanced back once as I left that room. There was nothing there. It struck me as a little funny — bowing to something with only a voice and no body. But in the end I gave a small bow anyway, and walked out.

My brother learned that night what I'd done. His face went white.

He couldn't say anything for a while, only staring at me. In that look I read fear before I read anger.

"…Who told you?" he finally managed to ask.

"…The Administrator," I said. "I told him not to. He said you had to know."

Just then, I thought I understood why he'd done it. He was the only person in this city who tried to hand what happened between the two of us back to the two of us.

"…Why did you do that," my brother said. Not angry — frightened.

"…You told me not to be scared for you," I said. "So I'll be curious for you too. Isn't that the same as being scared together?"

I tried to keep my voice from shaking as I said it. Just this once, I wanted to look sturdier than my brother.

He couldn't say anything. That was the first time I ever saw him cry. There was no sound. Water simply ran from his eyes.

I didn't know whether I should wipe those tears away or pretend not to see them, so I just stayed still. He rubbed his eyes once with the back of his hand. Even so, the tears kept falling.

His shoulders trembled, very slightly. Feeling that trembling under my fingertips, I could guess, for the first time, how long he had been holding all of this in.

I remember that moment as the strangest thing I ever saw in this city. That was the first time I'd ever seen anyone cry in Haven. This city took weeping in and made it disappear. My brother hadn't filed his. That was why he could cry.

"…Idiot," my brother finally managed. His voice was cracked.

"…I know," I said. "I'm still doing it."

He gripped my hand hard. Harder than usual — hard enough to hurt. I didn't mind that ache.

"…I said I'd protect you," he said. His voice was still cracked.

"…Yeah," I said. "But I never asked you to."

He looked at me for a moment at that, and lowered his head again.

"…I couldn't do anything for Father either," he said. "If you end up like that too, I'll really be nothing."

"…Brother," I said. "You were fifteen, then."

He didn't answer. I understood that he had never once said that to himself before.

We sat there a long while that night. His shoulders shook a few more times. I pretended not to notice. That was the only kindness I could give him, that day.

I set this scene down as the close of this chapter. We had said we hadn't cried since leaving Brick Hollow. That night, for the first time, my brother broke that. I took his hand. I understood, then. We weren't alone anymore. We had each other.

Reading this chapter over again, I add one thing. My brother's tears that night made no sound. But I already understood, then, that this soundless weeping was an entirely different kind of quiet from this city's own.

(Continued in Chapter 11.)

Chapter 11. Candidates for Reassignment

_Previously_ — Elpis chose to file the same questions as her brother and share the danger herself. Learning of it, Jeteo wept without a sound for the first time since leaving Brick Hollow. Brother and sister confirmed to each other that they were no longer alone.

A few mornings later, the Administrator came carrying a single sheet of paper. His face was more grave than I had ever seen it.

That morning was unusually bright. I thought the brightness didn't suit the moment. News like this seemed like it should come on a clouded day.

A sense of dread came over me the instant I saw that paper. The Administrator had never once carried paper before. Everything in this city was delivered by voice alone. The very fact that he held paper in his hand felt like a sign that something could not be undone.

The paper he held was white — nothing like the COMPLETE paper we knew. No crease marks, no yellowed edges. That cleanness frightened me more. It meant this was paper on which nothing had happened yet. I hadn't known that word — yet — could carry so much weight.

The Administrator's fingertips gripped the edge of that paper tightly. A faint press-mark from his fingers showed against the white. Watching that mark, I understood that the whole way here, he had carried this paper as a heavy thing too.

"…I am delivering this formally to the two of you," the Administrator said. His voice shook. "Jeteo and Elpis, both of you have been classified as candidates for Reassignment."

The instant I heard those words, my legs went weak. I didn't know exactly what the word meant. But my body already knew.

My brother said nothing. I saw his hand brush, very slightly, against the paper tucked inside his shirt. The one stamped COMPLETE. I understood that gesture — he was making sure it was still safe.

I looked at my brother's face. It wasn't a surprised face. He looked like someone who had already been waiting for these words. That frightened me more.

"…Elpis too?" my brother asked. That was his first question.

"…Yes," the Administrator said.

My brother closed his eyes. I knew, in that moment, that he was blaming himself. I wanted to tell him it wasn't his fault. But the words wouldn't come, standing in front of the Administrator.

"…What does 'candidate' mean?" my brother asked. His voice was steady, but his hands were shaking. I could tell he was working hard to hide that tremor.

"…It isn't final yet," the Administrator said. "But those who've reached this stage have… mostly gone on to become final."

"…If it's mostly, does that mean some haven't?" I asked.

The Administrator shook his head. "…Not that I know of." His eyes wavered, just briefly, as he said it.

"…How much time do we have?"

"…I don't know," the Administrator said. "I truly don't know. This time, I really don't."

"…Where do you go, once you're reassigned?" I asked.

The Administrator didn't answer.

"…Sir," I asked again. "Our father was reassigned too. In Brick Hollow. Is that the same as reassignment here?"

The Administrator's face changed, strangely, at that. I didn't know what to call that expression.

"…I don't know Brick Hollow," he said. "But if the same word is used… the meaning is probably close, too."

Hearing that answer, my legs buckled. My brother caught my arm.

"…Was Father's name erased too?" I asked. The words barely came out.

The Administrator didn't answer that question. But the silence was heavier than any answer could have been.

I understood, in that moment. We had fled Brick Hollow only to arrive in a city that used the same words. There was no taskmaster's whip here. And yet the way a person disappeared was the same. A name erased, a paper left behind, and no one ever asking again.

I knew, then, that the Administrator wasn't lying. His face held the same fear ours did.

For the first time, I felt something like pity for the Administrator. He wasn't trying to frighten us. He too was trapped inside this city's rules. I thought he might have been trapped inside them longer than we had. That long stretch of time seemed carved into every line around his eyes.

"…Isn't there a way to stop it?" I asked.

The Administrator shook his head. I saw his hand tremble, slightly. A tremor the Administrator I knew would never have shown.

"…If you stop filing, it might be reconsidered," the Administrator said. "If you stop asking questions."

"…What do you think, sir?" I asked. "Do you think we should stop?"

The Administrator didn't answer that for a long while.

"…My own thoughts aren't a filable matter," he said.

"…That's not an answer."

The Administrator looked at me. His eyes wavered strangely.

"…You're right," he said. "It isn't an answer."

And he said nothing more after that. I decided to take that silence as meaning he was on our side. Whether that was right, I still don't know, even now.

My brother looked at me at those words. I knew what he was thinking. If we stopped, we might live. But there was already an answer in his eyes.

Even then, I wanted to stop him. But the words wouldn't leave my mouth. I already knew, too. My brother wasn't going to stop.

"…We can't do that," my brother said. "If we stop asking, that name on the paper, and our father too, really do disappear."

I didn't argue against that. I felt the same way. Disappearing frightened me more than stopping did. That was the first time I realized I'd come to share my brother's fear exactly.

That was the first day my brother brought up running.

We sat facing each other in a corner of the room. It was daytime, and yet the curtains were drawn. I understood that as my brother's own kind of caution.

"…I think we have to leave this city," my brother said.

"…Can we leave?" I asked. "Is there something like the boundary stones here too?"

"…I don't know," my brother said. "But staying here is definitely dangerous. Leaving is at least a danger we don't know."

"…There might be something out there too," I said. My voice grew small on its own.

"…We still have to go," my brother said. "If we stay, we'll never find the answer at all. If we go, there's at least a chance we will."

"…Aren't you scared?" I asked.

"…I am," my brother said. "But staying here is just as scary. So I'll at least choose the one where we're walking."

That distinction struck me as strange. My brother always chose the unknown danger over the certain one. I didn't understand, then, that this was his own kind of courage. Now I do.

I looked out the window. Haven's lights were still lined up straight. That evenness felt, that day, strangely stifling.

"…Father couldn't run," I said. It came out of me before I meant it to.

My brother said nothing for a long while at that.

"…That's exactly why we're going," he finally said. "We're doing what Father couldn't. I think that's a way to remember him."

Hearing that, for the first time, I thought that running might not be a shameful thing.

"…I'm coming too," I said. My brother looked at me, worried. "You don't even need to ask. I'm a candidate too, either way."

My brother smiled, small, for the first time at that.

"…All right," he said. "Let's go together."

"…What do we bring?" I asked.

"…Almost nothing," my brother said. "We're leaving everything we got from this place behind."

"…Why?"

"…Anything given can be taken back," my brother said. "Let's take what's ours. Even if there's not much of it."

I counted what was ours. That one paper. And the sprout I'd planted. I felt a small sadness, knowing I couldn't take the sprout.

"…When do we go?" I asked.

"…Tonight," my brother said.

I hadn't expected it to be so soon. And yet I wasn't surprised either. Once my brother decided something, he never put it off.

He took my hand. In that moment we looked at each other like two people who had already made up their minds to leave.

We packed that night. There wasn't much to call luggage. The paper stamped COMPLETE, and one small leaf I'd plucked from the flowerbed I watered every day.

The room was dark. We packed without turning on a light. Listening to my brother's breathing in that darkness, I thought it felt exactly like the night we'd left Brick Hollow.

Before stepping out the door, I turned to look back at the room once more. Even though we'd only lived there a few days, it already felt unfamiliar. That unfamiliarity made me sad.

I pressed my hand once against the wall. It was smooth. The walls of Brick Hollow left dust on your hand when you touched them. I thought this wall would never once remember us.

The air in the room had no scent at all. That struck me as strange, right up to the last moment. The rooms of Brick Hollow always smelled of dirt and sweat mixed together; this room's smell hadn't changed one bit since the day we first walked in.

My brother paused with his hand on the doorknob.

"…Anything we're leaving behind?" he asked.

"…No," I said.

But there was, in fact. That flowerbed. That old woman. The old watering can. I was leaving all of it behind. I didn't say that to my brother. I knew he had things he was leaving behind too.

On our way out we stopped by the flowerbed. The man was there that night. As if he'd known we would come.

"…So you're leaving," he said. Not a question — spoken as something he already knew. I was newly startled by that tone. We hadn't told anyone yet.

A strange thought struck me, just then. It wasn't the Ledger who knew we were leaving this city — it was him. The Ledger had heard every word we'd ever said, and still didn't know. He had heard nothing at all, and knew.

"…Yes," I said. "I'm scared."

My brother put a hand on my shoulder. He said nothing, but I could tell, from the weight of that hand, that he felt the same.

"…You're right to be scared," he said. "But you're not going alone."

"…Are you coming with us?" I asked.

"…I'll be staying here," he said. "But that doesn't mean you're going alone."

"…What does that mean?" my brother asked. There was a slight edge of frustration in his voice. I could tell he was holding that frustration back.

Instead of answering, he smiled quietly. I couldn't tell whether that smile was sad or tender.

My brother shrugged at that smile. He seemed to decide not to press further.

I looked at his face again, just then. There was a sorrow in his expression somewhere that we didn't know. But it wasn't a heavy sorrow — it felt, instead, like a sorrow that made us lighter.

"…Sir," I said. "What about the sprout?"

"…I'll water it," he said.

"…Promise?"

"…I promise," he said. He said it so easily. I had never seen anyone in this city make a promise that easily. The people of this city filed things, instead of promising them.

"…You said it's a nameless seed," my brother cut in. "Why look after something with no name that carefully?"

"…It has no name — that doesn't mean it's nothing," he said.

My brother had no answer to that. I looked at his face. It was the face of someone tucking those words away, to take out and look at alone, later.

I thought the logic of it didn't quite hold together. My brother's face looked the same way. And yet, strangely, hearing those words made me a little less afraid.

At the time I thought he'd said that about my brother. Now I know. He meant it in another sense entirely.

He touched each of our shoulders once. His hand was surprisingly warm. I remembered that warmth for a long time afterward.

He told us of a low road heading south out of the city. A road with no watch on it, he said.

"…How do you know something like this?" my brother asked him directly, for the first time.

I understood that this question was the first direct suspicion my brother had ever voiced to him. I waited for the answer, my heart in my throat.

He smiled a little at my brother.

"…Because I've been here longer than this city has," he said.

"…What does that mean?" my brother asked. "Longer than the city?"

Instead of answering, he pointed south.

"…You need to reach those stones before sunrise," he said. "Go now."

My brother couldn't ask any more after that. I only understood later that he hadn't been avoiding the question — he had been sending us off.

My brother thought that answer was strange, and still, that night, he didn't press further. There was no time.

Before we left, I looked at the flowerbed one last time. The sprout had become a proper stem by now. I wondered whether that stem would keep growing without us.

The sky was still dark. A few faint stars showed. I thought those stars looked far older than Haven's own lights.

Before we stepped onto that road, we looked at each other once. My brother nodded first. I nodded too. We hadn't said a single word, and yet it felt like everything had already been said.

The road really was low. It felt like a narrow gap squeezed between two walls. Our shoulders touched both sides at once. That was the first time I learned this city held a road like this at all. Every road in Haven, until then, had been wide and straight.

I thought back on that road many times afterward. Every road this city built was wide. Only the road leading out was narrow. I don't believe that was an accident.

I set down the last line of this chapter here. That night, we left Haven. I looked back once at that flowerbed, the sprout grown a little taller, as we went. I didn't know, then, that after we left, he would go on staying there — right in that spot no one had planted.

Reading this chapter over again, I add one more thing. That night, a pebble from the flowerbed was still in my pocket. I walked with it clutched in my hand. Its coolness was the only proof I carried that night — proof that we had truly been there.

(Continued in Chapter 12.)

Chapter 12. Outside Haven

_Previously_ — Jeteo and Elpis were both classified as candidates for Reassignment. They decided not to stop asking questions, and left Haven by the unwatched southern road the man at the flowerbed had told them about. He had left them with these words: "I'll be here. But that doesn't mean you go alone."

We walked all through the night.

The ground under our feet was nothing like Haven's even paths. It was rough, and now and then a stone caught at my foot. Strangely, I was glad of the discomfort. It meant no one had smoothed this road for anyone.

There was no one else on it. It was quiet in a different way than Haven was quiet. That city's quiet had always felt like something someone had ordered into being. This road's quiet was just quiet, because no one was there.

I kept turning that difference over as I walked. I understood then, for the first time, that there was more than one kind of quiet. One kind erased people. The other was simply a quiet where nothing was happening.

Every time the wind blew, the dry grass made a sound. I was glad even of that. In Haven the wind itself had always blown quietly, carefully, as if it didn't want to be in anyone's way. This wind blew however it pleased.

The night air was cold. I tucked my hands into my sleeves as we walked. My brother saw and tried to take off his coat for me. I wouldn't take it. He was cold too. I knew that.

For a while I kept thinking I heard something behind us, and kept looking back. There was never anything there. I looked back anyway, again and again.

"…No one's following us," my brother said.

"…How do you know?"

"…This city doesn't chase people," he said. "It doesn't need to. People walk in on their own."

I found that both frightening and, somehow, a relief.

The sound of our two sets of footsteps was a comfort to me all that night. When one quickened, the other quickened with it. When one slowed, the other slowed too. Listening to that rhythm, I forgot to be afraid, a little.

I even tried breathing to that rhythm. Matching my breath to my brother's steps made it strangely easier. I didn't know what made that true. But that night, it was small things like that which carried me through.

I stumbled more than once and nearly fell. Each time, my brother caught my arm. We walked that whole way holding on to each other, over and over.

We stopped once, only for a moment, to share the water. The bottle was small. I saw my brother pour more into my cup than his own. He said he hadn't. I knew better.

I weighed the bottle in my hand. Less than half was left. I didn't tell him that. He already knew.

Once, my brother looked back. I turned and looked too. Haven's lights still burned, straight and even, far behind us. The Administrator would still be there. So would the old woman.

I wondered whether she was sitting on that bench today too, looking at the flowerbed. I wondered whether she'd notice the empty seat beside her.

It struck me as strange that the farther those lights fell behind, the more beautiful they looked. From inside, they had never looked beautiful at all.

"…Do you think the Administrator's all right?" I asked.

"…I don't know," my brother said. "He seemed like someone trapped in that city too. Like us."

Hearing that, I prayed for him for the first time. I didn't know exactly what I was praying toward. But I prayed, right then.

My brother closed his eyes for a moment too. I didn't ask what he was doing. But I could tell he was doing something close to the same thing.

As we walked, I kept thinking about what the man at the flowerbed had said. That we weren't going alone. I couldn't begin to guess, then, what that meant.

"…Remember the day we crossed the boundary stone at Brick Hollow?" I asked.

"…I remember," he said.

"…I was really scared that time. I'm less scared today."

"…Why?"

"…That time we were running away. Today we're going somewhere," I said.

He looked at me for a moment at that. Then he nodded, without saying anything.

I understood that difference for the first time that night. Running has a behind to it. Going has an ahead. In Brick Hollow, we ran. From Haven, we went.

Just before dawn, we came to a low heap of stones.

The sky was brightening by degrees. In that thin light I made out the shape of the rock. My heart lurched.

In that same light I saw my brother's face clearly for the first time. Dark under his eyes. A face that hadn't slept at all. Mine was probably the same.

I stopped walking at the sight of the shape. It looked exactly like the boundary stone at Brick Hollow.

My brother stopped too, in the same spot. I saw on his face that he'd recognized the same thing.

"…Is this a boundary stone too?" I asked.

He touched the rock.

"…Looks like it," he said. "Haven was inside some boundary too, then. We didn't leave a boundary behind. We just crossed one, into another."

"…So is there nowhere without a boundary?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "But if there is one, I don't think it would be a place no one guards. I think it would be a place that doesn't need guarding."

"…So what happens when we cross this one?"

"…Somewhere else again, probably," he said. "But this time we're the ones choosing to cross it. That's what's different."

Hearing that, I realized something strange. All this time I'd thought we'd "left" Brick Hollow. But Haven had been a fence too. How many boundary stones were there, in this world? I didn't know the answer then.

Reading this chapter over again, I want to add one thing. For a long time afterward, whenever we reached somewhere new, I got in the habit of looking for the boundary stone first. And every time, I'd touch the stone and ask myself the same question. This time, where does the fence end?

My brother tapped my shoulder. "…Let's go," he said. His voice was steadier than before.

I laid my hand on the stone. It was cold. I couldn't quite remember whether the boundary stone at Brick Hollow had been this cold. It saddened me, that the memory had already gone blurry.

My brother kept his hand on the stone a long time too. I didn't ask what he was doing.

"…Do you think Father ever stood in front of a stone like this?" he said.

"…He must have," I said. "But he couldn't have crossed it."

At that, my brother drew his hand back.

In that moment I tried to picture Father's hand. The warmth of it, where the calluses were. Both had already gone hazy. That frightened me. I hadn't known forgetting could come this quietly.

The moment we crossed the boundary stone, the sun came up.

Light poured in all at once. I threw a hand up over my eyes against the glare. Haven's light had always been constant, unchanging. This light was different from one instant to the next.
