A wide field opened out ahead of us. Nothing like Haven's straight, tidy roads. The grass grew however it wanted. Some of it tall, some short. It looked as though no one had ever trimmed it.

Dew sat on every blade, and each drop caught the sunlight differently. I noticed that every glint pointed a different way. Haven's light had always shone in exactly one direction.

I could smell the grass. It had been so long since I'd smelled anything like it. Not brick dust, not Haven's clean, scentless air. Just the smell of something alive.

Looking at that grass, I felt strangely close to tears. I understood then, for the first time, that what hadn't been trimmed and ordered might be exactly the proof that it was alive.

I sank down where I stood, partly because my legs had given out, and partly because I wanted to see that grass up close. When I touched it, it was rough under my fingers.

Just then something burst up out of the grass in a flurry of wings. I flinched back. A bird. Small and brown.

Only then did I realize I'd never once seen a bird in Haven. That city's sky had always been empty. It had never even occurred to me to find that strange.

"…Brother, a bird," I said.

"…Yeah," he said. He was following it with his eyes too.

The bird filed no request with anyone. It simply flew. I found that, absurdly, wonderful.

I watched the direction it went for a long time. It didn't seem to have any plan at all. It just flew wherever it wanted to fly. I envied it that freedom.

There was the sound of insects moving through the grass too. Small, broken-off sounds. I sat still a long while just to listen to them. Only then did I understand how large and heavy Haven's silence had actually been.

My brother stood in the middle of the field and slowly turned in a circle. I thought he was looking for something. But he was only looking.

"…Brother, what are you doing?" I asked.

"…Nothing," he said. "It's just been a long time since I've seen something like this."

I thought I understood what he meant. There had been no field like this in Brick Hollow, or in Haven either.

I took the flowerbed leaf out of my pocket. It hadn't wilted. I planted it back in the field's soil. It had no roots. But I wanted to do it anyway.

I didn't know whether that leaf could take root in this ground. But strangely, once I'd set it there, something in me settled.

"…That's not going to live," my brother said. Not scolding — just saying it.

"…I know," I said.

"…Then why plant it?"

I thought about it for a moment.

"…It seemed too strange to leave nothing behind," I said. "This isn't something I left behind, though. It's something I brought. So I'm leaving what I brought, here."

My brother said nothing to that. But when we started walking again, he looked back at that spot once more.

Far off, at the edge of the field, something like a mountain rose up. I couldn't tell for certain whether it was a mountain or something else. Clouds hung low over it.

It was a color I'd never once seen in Haven. Deep blue mixed with gray. I found that color frightening and beautiful, both at once.

"…Should we head that way?" my brother said, pointing.

"…There might not be anything there," I said.

"…That's exactly why we go," he said. There was mischief in his voice, for the first time in a long while.

"…What if there's nothing?" I asked.

"…Then we just keep walking somewhere else," he said. I liked that answer. It sounded like it meant it was all right not to have the right one.

We started walking toward that mountain. I didn't look back. This time, there was no reason to.

My brother sat down beside me. We settled onto a flat rock in the middle of the field. The sun was fully up by then.

"…Where do we go now?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "But at least now, where we go is something we get to decide. In Haven, the Ledger decided everything for us."

Hearing that, I understood for the first time what Haven's peace had cost. There had been no need to choose anything. But that also meant we hadn't been able to.

"…Are you scared?"

"…I'm scared," he said. "But this fear is… mine. No one's going to step in and resolve it for me."

"…I'm scared too," I said. "But hearing you say that, I think I understand it a little now."

"…Understand what?" he asked.

"…Why this fear is different," I said.

He nodded quietly at that.

We didn't say anything for a while. The sun kept climbing. I could feel the rock slowly warming beneath us.

"…Why is that a good thing?" I asked.

"…It's not that it's good," he said. "But at least it's real. Haven's peace was fake. This fear is real. I think I'd rather have a real fear than a fake peace."

He said that, then looked up at the sky. Not a single cloud. I thought he was searching for something up there.

"…What are you looking at?" I asked.

"…Nothing," he said. Then, after a moment, he added, "…But every time I see something like this, it makes me a little angry."

"…Why?"

"…If something out there knows how to make this," he said, "then why did it leave Brick Hollow the way it was?"

I couldn't find anything to say to that. I just sat there beside him.

I've come back to that moment many times since. I had no answer for him then. But from that day on, I came to understand something. Having no answer to that question was never the failure. The failure was letting no one sit beside it.

I turned his words over for a long time. I didn't fully agree. But I couldn't find anything to argue back with either.

I moved a little closer to him. He leaned toward me too, just slightly.

"…Brother," I asked. "What do you think that man meant?"

"…That we're not going alone?"

"…Yeah."

He shrugged.

"…I don't know," he said. "Maybe it was just something to say, to comfort us."

"…I don't think it was that," I said.

"…Why not?"

"…Something meant to comfort you usually comes out softer," I said. "That wasn't soft. It sounded like he was just telling us a fact."

He didn't answer for a long while.

"…Then what's supposed to be coming with us?" he finally said. He wasn't mocking me. He sounded like he actually wanted to know.

"…I don't know either," I said.

I think that was the most honest answer I had, at the time. I didn't know. But I waited anyway, not knowing. I think that was the only thing I knew how to do.

The wind blew. All the grass lay flat at once, then rose again. I watched that movement for a long time. My brother was watching the same thing.

"…Brother," I said. "Are we going the right way?"

He took my hand. "…I don't know," he said. "But at least we're going together."

I didn't think that was a perfect answer. But that morning, it was enough.

I set those words down as the last line of this Part. We had gotten out of Haven. We still hadn't found Father, and we still didn't know what the COMPLETE stamp meant. Not one answer had come to us. But that morning, for the first time since we'd left Brick Hollow, I saw something in my brother's face standing alongside the fear. I didn't know then that its name was hope.

(Continued in Part Two.)

The Weeping Ledger, Part Two

The Wilderness

Chapter 13. The First Day in the Wilderness

_Previously_ — Marked as candidates for Reassignment, the brother and sister left Haven and crossed the boundary stones. Realizing that Haven, too, had been only another kind of fence, they set out walking toward the unshaped fields beyond. "This fear is mine. No one else gets to resolve it for me."

From that day on, we had to learn all over again what hunger was.

In Haven, the moment you thought you were hungry, bread was already waiting for you. Not here. Here your stomach just went on being hungry.

That going-on was strange to me. In Haven, no sensation ever stayed long. Hunger, thirst, even sorrow — they would rise for a moment, be filed, and vanish. Here a sensation didn't vanish. It simply went on being there. I found that frightening, and strangely, it also felt real.

Less than a full day had passed since we crossed the boundary stones, and already Haven's time felt impossibly far away. When I looked back there was no longer any sign of the city's spires. Nothing but flat ground and low hills.

The ground underfoot was different too. Haven's streets had always been even stone. Here it was rough and uneven. I stumbled more than once, and every time, my brother reached out without a word and caught me.

Small stones were pressed into the ground. Some were sharp, some rolled round and loose under my heel. I felt every one of those unevennesses come up through the soles of my shoes as I walked. Haven's streets had never given my feet anything to feel — you set one foot down and the next step simply followed. Here, every single step belonged to me alone.

A low wind blew, carrying the smell of dry earth. I wrinkled my nose. But soon I grew used to it. Haven had almost no smell at all. Everything there was so clean that the absence of any smell had, once or twice, struck me as strange in itself. Here the smell shifted a little with every step — dry grass, a damp shade, and now and then the trace of some animal I couldn't name.

The sun was already high. The shadows were shortening. That day I learned, for the first time, how to judge the hour by the length of a shadow. In Haven I had never had to count time for myself — the Ledger always told us.

I noticed something strange then. The hunger itself was exactly like Brick Hollow's. But the way we had to bear it was different. In Brick Hollow, hunger came bound together with the whip. Here, hunger was only hunger. No one was handing it to us as a punishment.

As I walked I touched the back of my hand. The scar from Brick Hollow was still there. While we were in Haven I had forgotten it entirely — too caught up in all the Ledger kept giving us. Only out here in the Wilderness did the scar come back into my sight.

Looking at it, I had a strange thought. This body remembered Brick Hollow. However perfect Haven was, it had never managed to erase even this much.

Even as he walked, my brother kept scanning every direction. I could tell it had already become a habit. In Haven no one ever looked around like that. Here, not looking around would have been the strange thing.

"…Scared?" my brother asked.

"…Yes," I said. "But strangely, I don't hate it."

He looked at me for a moment at that. His face said he understood something.

"…Me too," he said after a while. "Scared, but — maybe it's because this fear is mine."

Hearing that, I looked at his face again. In Haven his face had always looked like it was holding something back. Now it was simply his face.

I wanted to look at it a little longer, so I slowed my steps on purpose. He noticed and turned back to me.

"…Why are you looking at me like that?" he asked.

"…No reason," I said. "It just feels like the first time in a long while I'm really seeing your face."

At that he let out a small laugh. Even the sound of it was unfamiliar. I couldn't remember hearing it much, not in Brick Hollow, not in Haven.

A gust of wind blew hard once. Dry dust rose a little at our feet, then settled. I watched quietly as it settled onto the tops of my feet. Haven's ground had always been clean. There had never been room for dust to gather. Here, the trace of this ground stayed on our bodies exactly as it fell.

I didn't brush the dust off. Strangely, I wanted to keep it there. It felt like proof that we had really walked this road.

Walking ahead of me, my brother suddenly stopped and looked back. He stood like that a long while.

"…See something?" I asked.

"…No," he said. "I'm looking because there's nothing to see."

I thought about what he meant for a moment. That was when I learned that even making sure no one was following took time. In Haven there had never been reason to look back. Whatever came from ahead or behind, the Ledger always knew first.

I understood then that even confirming, with your own eyes, that nothing was there took time. And that even that time belonged to us.

We went looking through the field for anything we could eat. My brother dug up some kind of root and chewed it.

"…Bitter," he said, wincing. "But not so bad you can't eat it."

I chewed some too. It really was bitter. But we laughed. We hadn't laughed much, back in Haven.

I didn't spit the bitterness out. I let the taste spread all the way to the back of my tongue and simply endured it. Haven's food had always been just right — never too sweet, never too bitter. That was the first time I understood that "just right" was, in truth, nothing at all. This bitterness, at least, was something.

"…Why is this funny?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "Just — we found this ourselves. Maybe that's why."

Hearing that, I understood something for the first time. The bread the Ledger gave us had been perfect, but it had never been ours. This wretched, bitter root was ours.

He went on pulling up a few more kinds of plants and smelling each one. Some he tossed aside at once; others he tucked carefully into the fold of his shirt.

I crouched beside him and watched his fingers. His hands looked a little like Father's — thick-knuckled, at ease with soil. The hands that had once carried bricks in Brick Hollow were pulling weeds now. Strangely, that change felt like something to be grateful for.

"…Can't eat this one," he said, tossing away a plant with pointed leaves. "Smells wrong."

"…How do you know?" I asked again.

"…My nose just knows first," he said. "When your body says no, you have to believe it. In Haven our bodies never even got the chance to say no to anything."

Listening, I wondered whether my own body had a sense like that. I still didn't know, not yet. But I thought that someday I too might learn to understand what my nose, what my body, was telling me.

"…How do you tell them apart?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he admitted. "I'm just going on instinct. I could be wrong."

Strangely, that answer felt reassuring rather than frightening. In Haven no one ever said "I could be wrong." The Ledger was always right.

"…And if you're wrong?" I asked.

"…My stomach will hurt, I guess," he said, shrugging. "Then next time I just won't eat that one."

I laughed because it sounded funny. But once I stopped laughing and thought about it again, it wasn't a funny thing at all. Learning by being wrong first. Haven had never had such a thing — there had never even been a chance to be wrong.

He kept rubbing each leaf between his fingers and smelling it. Some made me wrinkle my nose; some were faintly sweet. I tried a few myself. The green smell it left on my fingertips lingered a long time.

"…Look at this," he said, pointing to a small yellow flower. "There's this too. I don't think I ever saw this color in Haven."

I leaned in to look closer. He was right. Haven's flowerbeds had always been filled with flowers of the same color, the same size. This one was small and crooked. But that crookedness felt more real, somehow, not less.

We sat for a while, sharing the root between us. My stomach was still hollow. But strangely I liked that moment. My brother and I, chewing the same thing, wearing the same face.

Far off, I heard a bird call. I was hearing it for the first time. Haven had no birds. Or maybe it did — I couldn't say. But I had no memory of ever hearing birdsong there.

"…What kind of bird is that?" I asked.

"…I don't know either," he said. "But you don't need to know its name. If it sounds good, that's enough."

I liked that. That you could love something without knowing its name. That was a thought Haven had never taught me.

"…Do you think Father ever ate something like this?" I asked.

He was quiet a moment before he answered.

"…I don't know. But if he's alive somewhere, I think he's probably about as hungry as I am right now."

Hearing that, I imagined Father through hunger for the first time. Strangely, even without an answer, it felt like it brought me a little closer to him.

"…Father never let it show, even when he was hungry," I said. "Remember? Whenever he split up dinner, he always left a little of his own share behind."

"…I remember," he said quietly. "I didn't understand why back then. I think I do now."

I didn't press him further. Too much was folded into that "I think I do now," and I didn't want to pry it open.

We sat a good while longer. Every time the wind blew, the dry grass rustled. I sat listening to it quietly. In Haven, even when the wind blew, it made no sound. Every wall there seemed to hold something that swallowed sound whole.

That was the first time I understood that sound came in kinds. The kind that got swallowed, and the kind that simply rang out. I liked the ringing kind better.

My brother stood and stretched. His joints made a small cracking sound.

"…Let's go," he said. "We should walk a little more before the sun's down."

Standing, I felt an ache behind my legs. I had never walked this long in my life. But even that ache, strangely, wasn't unwelcome. In Haven, however far we walked, our legs had never felt like this. Only now did I realize it had always felt as though something else had been walking for us.

We started walking again. The shadows were growing longer.

Toward evening, we saw smoke far off in the distance. A sign that someone lived there.

"…Should we go look?" I asked.

He hesitated a moment.

"…Carefully," he said. "It could be another place like Haven."

We crept toward the smoke carefully. There were a few small tents. People sat around a fire. There were children among them, and old people too.

Seeing that, I felt strangely at ease. Every one of their faces was different. Some were laughing, some were crying. It was a sight I had never once seen in Haven.

Something was boiling in a small pot hung over the fire. The smell rode the wind all the way to us. The moment it reached me, my stomach lurched hard. Haven's food had had no smell — or maybe it had, but I couldn't remember it. This smell was thick, smoky, a little scorched. And yet, strangely, it smelled like people living.

My brother slowed and stepped in front of me.

"…Stay behind me," he said.

I fell in obediently behind him. But his own steps, I noticed, were trembling a little too. I understood that he was afraid, and leading the way anyway.

Some of the tents were worn and patched in places. Looking at the mended seams, I understood these people had held on here a long time. Haven's buildings had always looked new. There, nothing was ever given the chance to wear before it was fixed.

A woman noticed us first.

"…Who are you?" she asked. Her eyes were wary, but not hostile.

"…We're lost," my brother said. "Could you spare us something to eat?"

The woman studied us a moment, then waved us toward the fire.

"…Come and sit. There isn't much, but we can share it."

The people around the fire saw us and shifted, each making a little more room. No one said much of anything. They simply moved themselves aside and made a space.

I still haven't forgotten that small movement. In Haven no one ever had to make room for anyone — there was always space enough. These people, whose space was not enough, narrowed it anyway to make some of it ours.

Settling into that narrow gap, I felt a stranger's shoulder brush lightly against mine. A stranger's warmth. I almost drew back, but I didn't. I found I didn't mind it.

Hearing her say that, I felt something strange. That phrase — "there isn't much." I had never once heard the word not enough in Haven. The Ledger was always sufficient. But in these people's not-enoughness there was something the Ledger's sufficiency had never had.

What the woman gave us was one piece of dry bread and a mouthful of water. Truly not much at all. But my brother's hand trembled a little as he took it. I understood that wasn't only from hunger.

"…Thank you," he said. His voice was low.

"…It's nothing," the woman said. "Everyone comes to us like that, at first."

The firelight wavered over their faces. I looked at each one in turn. Some looked tired; some, like mine, looked like they were studying something unfamiliar. But no one pushed us away.

Even after I'd finished the bread, I kept the last small piece in my hand a long while. I hated to swallow it. In Haven I had never once felt the wish to hold onto something. That day I learned, for the first time, that longing had nothing to do with being full. My brother, too, finished his share and carefully brought the crumbs left on his fingertips to his mouth.

The woman looked our way once more and asked,

"…What are your names?"

"…Jeteo," my brother said. "This is Elpis."

"…Jeteo. Elpis." The woman said each name once, aloud. Then she nodded. That was all. Nothing was filed. No answer came back.

I remembered that nothing-of-a-moment for a long time after. It was the first night our names were spoken not to be resolved, but simply to be spoken.

Somewhere off, a child laughed. I turned my head toward the sound. A small child was chasing another around and around the fire. A few of the adults watched and laughed quietly.

Listening to that laughter, I felt that these people, who had nothing, had not lost something. I didn't know exactly what, not then. Now I do. It was each other.

What exactly that something was, I did not yet know, that night.

(Continued in Chapter 14.)

Chapter 14. People Who Call You by Name

_Previously_ — Outside Haven, in the Wilderness, the brother and sister learned real hunger all over again. Toward evening they found a small tent camp and received, for the first time, a share from strangers. "There isn't much, but we can share" — they sensed something in it that Haven's sufficiency never had.

The people sitting around the fire called each other by name.

It felt strangely foreign to me. In Haven, we had always been addressed with formal courtesy — "Jeteo," "Elpis," correct every time. Here it was just the name. Short. Close.

Only hearing them call each other did I understand it. The Ledger called our names precisely, but there was always a distance in that calling. In the way these people called each other, there was no distance at all.

The night air was still cold. I drew my knees up, wrapped my arms around them, and shifted a little closer to the firelight. Now and then I heard the crackle and pop of the embers. I had no memory of ever seeing a fire in Haven. Light there had always come from somewhere unseen, steady and soft — never something that leapt and shivered like this.

Watching that flickering light, I thought about it. Haven's light had been steady. But steady and alive were two different things. That was the first time I felt that flickering might, in fact, be proof of being more alive, not less.

I sat there and listened quietly to the people calling one another. Some names were called short and quick. Some were drawn out long. The same name sounded different depending on who was calling it. I found that strange. No matter who spoke to the Ledger, it answered in exactly the same tone.

A man far off called someone's name loudly. The name carried out into the dark, and after a moment, an answer came back. Listening to that call and answer, I felt a strange stirring in my chest. The call, the answer, and the empty stretch of time between them. Haven had no such empty stretch. The moment you called, an answer came at once. But that instancy never held whatever it was this waiting held.

That was the first time I understood that waiting was part of a calling. Even in that brief silence before the answer came, something had been growing.

An old man beside the fire called a child's name twice. The child pretended not to hear. He called again. Only then did she come running, laughing.

Watching that small, playful back-and-forth, I felt strangely at ease. Calling a name, pretending not to hear it, calling it again — even in that exchange, something like affection was moving between them.

The old man ran a hand lightly over the child's hair. She squirmed a little under his touch but didn't pull away. Watching that brief gesture, I found myself guessing how many times it must have been repeated before, to look so easy. Even a stranger's eye could see it was an old habit.

A child came up to me and asked.

"…What's your name, big sister?"

"…Elpis," I said.

"…Elpis." The child repeated it back, clumsily. But strangely, I liked that clumsy calling.

For the first time, I understood that there was a temperature to being called by name. The Ledger's calling was precise but cold. This child's calling was clumsy but warm.

"…What's your name?" I asked her back.

"…Rachel," the child said, and gave a small, half-shy smile.

"…Rachel," I said, saying it back.

She lit up at that and sat down close beside me, right against my side. I felt the warmth of that small body against my ribs. In Haven, no one ever sat that close.

Rachel's hair smelled of smoke — the smell of hair that hadn't been washed in days. But strangely, I didn't mind it. If anything, that smell felt like proof that this child was really living here, really beside this fire.

"…Where did you come from, big sister?" Rachel asked, her eyes wide and round.

"…From far away," I said. "A place called Haven."

"…Is it nice there?" Rachel asked.

I couldn't answer right away. Rachel tilted her head at my silence.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "But I think I like it better here."

At that, Rachel's face broke into a wide smile again, and she gripped my arm tight.

"…Say my name again, big sister," she said.

"…Rachel," I said again.

"…One more time."

"…Rachel."

She laughed every time. I didn't understand why saying the same name three times over made her so happy. But somehow, every time I said it, I liked it a little more too.

I only understood later. What Rachel wanted wasn't an answer. It was to be called. The Ledger answered anything you asked it. But no one had ever simply called us, for no other reason than to call us.

The woman's name was Noa. She was traveling with two children.

"…Where did you come from?" Noa asked.

"…From Haven," my brother said carefully.

Something shifted slightly in Noa's face.

"…You're the first person I've met who came out of there," she said. "Everyone else wants to get in."

Noa pulled both children a little closer against her knees. I could tell it was an unthinking gesture — that her body reacted before she did, the instant Haven came up.

Her two children looked back and forth between us, blinking. It wasn't a wary look, the kind you give a stranger — just a curious one. I liked that unguarded look. Haven's children never looked at anyone that way. Haven's children wore blank faces, as if they already knew everything there was to know.

"…This might sound strange," my brother said carefully, "but we think we're lucky to have gotten out."

"…It doesn't sound strange," Noa said. "Because I think the same thing."

"…Why did you leave?" my brother asked back.

Noa drew one of the children into her arms.

"…That city says it takes sorrow away. That's what frightened me," she said. "I was afraid that if the sorrow disappeared, everything about having loved that person would disappear along with it."

Hearing that, I couldn't breathe. I thought of the old woman at the flowerbed. Noa was someone who had known, before it ever happened to her, what that old woman had gone through — and had turned away from it in time.

I studied Noa's profile quietly. In the firelight her face was carved with deep lines. It didn't look like age alone had put them there. I tried, for a moment, to guess what nights each of those lines had come through, and then I stopped. It didn't feel like a depth I could reach by guessing.

"…Who did you lose?" my brother asked gently.

"…My husband," Noa said. Her voice shook, but she carried the sentence through to the end. "In Haven, this grief would already be resolved by now. But I still hurt this much. And I have no intention of trading this pain for Haven."

One of the children pressed her face into Noa's lap. Noa stroked the child's hair slowly. The gesture was practiced — she must have said this, in front of this child, more than once before.

Watching that hand move, I understood that sorrow wasn't always something a person had to bear alone. Noa was carrying her sorrow, and still, right beside it, tending to a child.

Hearing her, I understood for the first time that keeping your sorrow could itself be a choice. Sorrow wasn't only something that had to be gotten rid of.

My brother was quiet for a long moment before he asked.

"…Aren't you scared? Going on living like this, still in pain?"

Noa looked at him. Her eyes were soft.

"…I'm scared," she said. "But if the pain disappeared, I'm afraid that the fact I loved him would blur along with it. That's what scares me more."

Hearing that answer, I understood for the first time that even pain had a strength that could stand guard over something. Until then I had only ever thought of pain as something you simply endured.

My brother didn't say a word the whole time he listened. But I saw that his eyes never left Noa's face. I understood he was pressing what she'd said down somewhere deep inside him. That was how he took things in — not answering right away, but holding them a long time, and later pulling them back out as something of his own.

"…So what does that pain do for you?" my brother finally asked. His voice had a rough edge to it.

Noa looked at him a long time. She didn't grow angry.

"…It doesn't do anything," she said. "Pain doesn't do anything. It just keeps telling you that person was here."

My brother closed his mouth. I could tell he wanted to argue with that answer and couldn't find a way to.

That was the first time I ever saw someone not answer my brother's question with logic. Noa gave him no flawless answer. And yet my brother went quieter, for far longer, than he ever had standing before the Ledger.

Noa kissed the top of the child's head in her arms. It was a brief kiss. But I could feel the weight folded into it. In that moment I saw, for the first time, that love could be carried across without anyone having to say the word at all.

As the night deepened, the children sang. It wasn't good singing. The notes went wrong again and again. But everyone laughed and sang along anyway.

Listening to that song, I felt something strange. Haven had no songs. There was no need to make something that wasn't perfect. These people knew their song wasn't perfect, and they kept singing it anyway.

Rachel took my hand and begged me to sing along. I didn't know the words. But after hearing it a few times, I could follow along on the chorus. My voice went wrong just as often as everyone else's. No one seemed to mind at all.

That was the first time in my life I ever sang. In Brick Hollow I hadn't had the strength to sing. In Haven I'd had no reason to. Here in the Wilderness, I seemed to have gained both things at once. The strength, and the reason.

When the song ended, the children clapped — badly out of time. But everyone looked happy all the same. Rachel begged me to sing it again, and this time I followed the chorus with a little more confidence. My voice cracked, but no one laughed. If they had, it wouldn't have been the laugh of mockery — it would have been the laugh of joining in.

Partway through the song I heard my brother humming low under his breath. His voice was very quiet. But I didn't miss that small hum. I started trying to count how long it had been since I'd last heard him hum anything at all, and then I gave up counting.

Between verses, one of the adults laid a low countermelody over the top. The notes overlapped and the sound grew a little thicker. I realized my own voice had folded into that thickened sound somewhere along the way. Even I couldn't have said, by then, which part of it was mine.

I liked not being able to tell. In Haven, everything had been distinguishable. Whose complaint it was, whose share it was, whose turn it was. Here, not even a single sound could be sorted out as belonging to anyone in particular.

Noa asked me.

"…What are you two looking for?"

I thought for a moment before answering.

"…Father. And… I'm looking alongside my brother, for whatever answer he's looking for."

Noa nodded.

"…That's enough," she said. "You don't have to find everything. It's enough to have someone looking with you."

"…What are you looking for, ma'am?" I asked back.

Noa looked at the fire for a moment.

"…I'm not looking for anything anymore," she said. "I just stay beside these children. That's all I do now."

Hearing that answer, I felt strangely at ease. Not everyone had to be looking for something. Just staying beside someone could be enough, all on its own.

As she said it, Noa quietly ran a hand along the child's back. Watching that gesture, I felt that staying beside someone was nothing at all like doing nothing. To stay beside someone, you had to keep reaching out your hand. You had to keep looking at them. That might, in its own way, take more strength than searching for anything ever could.

That night, for the first time, my brother showed the paper stamped COMPLETE to someone else. To Noa.

Noa looked at the name on it for a long time. She traced it with a fingertip and read it quietly, without a sound — just the shape of the words on her lips.

I felt that silence stretch on and on. Only the crackle of embers and, far off, the sound of children running filled it. I started counting how many times Noa reread that name, and then I gave up. It seemed more important to watch than to count.

"…I don't know this person," she said. "But I know this stamp. Stories about people stamped like this go around the Wilderness too. No one knows where the completed go. But everyone tries hard to remember the names."

"…Why?" my brother asked.

"…Because that's the only resistance we have," Noa said.

My brother folded the paper again and tucked it back into his shirt. His hands were more careful than usual, doing it.

The fire was dying down, and one by one, people drifted off toward the tents. Noa gathered an armful of dry grass and laid it over the flames. The fire flared up briefly, then settled low again.

"…Who watches this fire?" I asked.

"…We take turns," Noa said. "If it goes out overnight, it's hard to light again in the morning."

That answer stayed with me strangely long. These people had a reason for someone to stay awake every single night. Haven had never had a single reason like that.

"…Is there anyone else out here who has a paper like this?" my brother asked.

"…I've seen a few," Noa said. "None of them talk about it much, but none of them throw the paper away either. What it means might be a little different for each of them, but just not throwing it away — that seems to say something on its own."

"…Where are they now?" my brother asked. There was something urgent in his voice.

"…Scattered," Noa said. "Here and there, all across this Wilderness. Even I don't know all of them. But if you ever cross paths, I think you'll know each other. Just from carrying that paper."

My brother nodded at that. I could feel him accepting, in that moment, that this was no longer a journey only the two of us were on.

Hearing that, I truly felt for the first time that we weren't alone. From Brick Hollow all the way to here, we had always believed it was only ever the two of us. But somewhere out here in the Wilderness, there were more people carrying the same kind of paper we were.

My brother's hand stayed a long while at his chest, over the pocket where the paper lay. I could tell he felt some new weight settling inside it. That paper wasn't his alone anymore. Somewhere out in this Wilderness, other people were carrying that same weight.

"…I'd like to meet them," my brother said.

"…You will, someday," Noa said. "This Wilderness looks wide, but people walking the same direction always end up meeting."

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. That day I learned for the first time that the thing my brother had started alone was already something other people, somewhere out here in the Wilderness, were doing too.

(Continued in Chapter 15.)

Chapter 15. The Searching Light

_Previously_ — At a tent camp in the Wilderness, the brother and sister met Noa and her children. Noa was someone who had left Haven of her own will rather than have her sorrow "resolved." When they showed her the paper stamped COMPLETE, it came out that the resistance of remembering names was already underway all across the Wilderness.

At dawn, one of the children woke us.

A small hand shook my shoulder. Half-asleep, I thought at first it was only a dream. But the shaking grew more urgent. I forced my eyes open.

"…Look, there's a light," the child said, pointing at the sky.

I opened my eyes. Low in the sky, a small light moved slowly. It looked like a star, but it wasn't. It hung too low, and it moved too evenly.

The night air was cold. I sat wrapped in my blanket, watching that light. I couldn't tell whether my fingertips were trembling from the cold or from fear.

The instant I saw it, a chill ran down my back. I didn't know what it was. But I knew, from the look on Noa's face, that it was nothing good.

I couldn't tear my eyes from that light. It was very small, but strangely distinct — as if the whole darkness had made itself into a backdrop for that one light alone. I found that distinctness frightening. That something distinct could be more frightening than something blurred — that was a thing I understood for the first time in that moment.

A small rustling stirred inside the tents. The sound of children waking. I heard the adults soothing them, low. I found it strange that even in such urgency, no voice ever rose.

My brother saw it too. I felt his whole body go rigid, all at once. Without a word, he took my hand and pulled me low. His hand was cold as ice.

Around us, one by one, the other adults were already getting up, gathering the children in. No one shouted. Everyone moved quietly, practiced, as if this weren't the first time.

A man quickly covered the embers with dirt. Smoke billowed up for a moment, then died away. I could tell at a glance how many times that motion had been repeated. There was no hesitation in it at all.

Somewhere far off a dog barked once, then cut off sharply. It sounded as if someone had clapped a hand over its mouth in a hurry. That total stillness felt, if anything, more suffocating than the bark had.

That quiet frightened me more. A danger that came with shouting wasn't unfamiliar to me — that had been Brick Hollow, every day. But a danger this quiet, that no one raised their voice against, was something entirely new.

Noa called the children's names quietly, one by one. Not loudly. Just a calling to check where each of them was. Even in that brief, frightened moment, hearing their names called put me strangely at ease.

I understood then. Being called by name in a moment of fear, and having your case received in a moment of comfort, were two entirely different kinds of care.

Noa knelt in front of one child, level with her eyes, and cupped the girl's cheeks in both hands, whispering something low. I couldn't hear all of it. But I saw the strength go out of the child's shoulders. I kept that scene in my mind. That was how one person soothed another, standing in front of fear.

Rachel came up quietly beside me and gripped my arm. I could feel the small tremor of her hand against mine. I gripped it back. I said nothing at all. But holding that hand, somehow, I was a little less afraid.

I looked up at the sky once more. The stars went on twinkling around that small light as if nothing were happening. I found that indifference strange. The stars seemed to have no idea at all what that light was searching for. Or else they knew, and simply went on twinkling as they always had, regardless. I couldn't say which of the two was sadder.

That was the first time I ever thought the sky might not be on our side. In Brick Hollow I had believed the night sky was the one place even a taskmaster's hand couldn't reach. But this city, it turned out, knew how to float its own light in that same sky.

"…It's a searching light," Noa said in a low voice, quietly pushing the children into the tent. "Haven sends those out sometimes. When it's looking for Reassignment candidates."

"…Is it because of us?" my brother asked.

"…I don't know," Noa said. "But there's no need to find out. Put out the fire."

We doused the fire quickly and crouched behind the tent. I gripped my brother's hand tight. He gripped mine just as tight.

In that instant, the night we crossed the boundary stones of Brick Hollow came back to me exactly as it had been. In place of the crack of whips, there was that silent light. I found it more frightening. Because it made no sound.

I lifted my head just slightly and looked at the light again. It moved without a sound, slowly crossing the sky. The motion of it looked as if something were being combed through, very carefully, inch by inch.

Watching that motion, I felt something strange. There was no hurry in that light at all. And the fact that it wasn't hurrying was somehow more chilling than if it had been. Not hurrying was the ease of something already certain it could find whatever it was looking for.

I tried to breathe as low and slow as I could. Every time my heart beat, I felt sure the sound of it would carry out into the open. I could feel my brother holding his breath the same way beside me. We stayed curled up like that, each of us hiding our own breathing from the other as best we could, waiting only for that light to pass.

In that moment I remembered the nights in Brick Hollow we'd spent waiting for a taskmaster's footsteps. We had held our breath the very same way, back then too. That familiarity made me sad — that even in the place we'd run to, we still had to curl ourselves into the same shape.

The light passed slowly over our heads once. It didn't stop. It went on past and vanished into the dark.

Only after a long while did Noa let out her breath.

"…It's gone," she said.

Only then did I let out the breath I'd been holding, long and slow. When I opened my hand, there were little half-moon marks where my nails had dug into my palm. Only then did I realize how hard I'd been gripping my brother's hand.

Around us, people rose to their feet one by one. No one asked anyone else if they were all right. But everyone checked each other's faces, once, all the same. I felt that wordless checking was a truer kind of asking after someone than words could have been.

Rachel still hadn't let go of my arm. I gently worked her hand loose and looked at her face. There were tears standing in her eyes, but she wasn't crying. That held-back look, on a face so young, hurt something in me — it looked far too practiced for her age.

"…Does this happen often?" I asked.

"…Sometimes," Noa said. "Not a few of the people living out here came from that city. So we're always careful of it."

Hearing that, I understood that the Wilderness was not, as Haven seemed to believe, empty. This was a place where the people Haven had missed had gathered to live.

Listening, I imagined other tents somewhere out in that darkness, curled low just as we were. I thought there must be people we couldn't see, hiding from the same light, holding the same breath, somewhere beyond our sight. Just imagining that made me feel, strangely, a little less alone.

My brother asked quietly.

"…So does that light keep circling this area?"

"…There's no pattern to it," Noa said. "Sometimes it won't come for months. Sometimes it comes several days running. So we just stay ready, always."

"…Ready how?"

"…Putting the fire out fast. Keeping the children quiet. That's about it," Noa said. "It isn't much. But it's all we can do."

"…Did you see it too, your first time out here?" my brother asked.

"…I didn't know what it was, at first," Noa said. "I thought it was just a star. But people out here told me. That light never moves like a star. A star drifts, or twinkles. It doesn't move steadily in one direction like that."

"…So everyone who first comes out here doesn't know, and has to learn?" I asked.

"…That's right," Noa said. "I learned it the same way. Someone told me. Now I'm the one telling you."

Hearing that, I sensed another kind of ledger, running through this Wilderness. A ledger with no paper, no filing. A ledger of how to survive, handed down from person to person.

"…Does it make a sound at all?" I asked. That, strangely, was what I wanted to know most.

"…I've never once heard it," Noa said. "Some people say it just doesn't have the part that makes sound. Others say it doesn't need to, so no one bothered building it."

Hearing that, I felt a chill run over me. Not being able to make a sound, and having no need to make one. I thought the second was far more frightening. Having no need meant it could already find out everything some other way.

"…What happens if it catches you?" I asked.

Noa's face went thoughtful, choosing her words.

"…It might be better not to know," she said. "But not asking might be more frightening, so I'll tell you. If it catches you, you're just taken back to Haven. And after that… no one has ever heard about that person again."

At that, my brother's hand closed into a fist.

"…So going back means becoming a Reassignment candidate all over again?" my brother asked.

"…Probably," Noa said. "I don't know exactly, myself. But the fact that word of the people who go back stops completely — that can't mean anything good."

Listening to that, I thought of the day the phrase "candidate for Reassignment" had first been spoken to us. That phrase had always worn a gentle face, then and now, hiding something terrible underneath it.

I thought again of the Administrator's face. Always smiling. I never once imagined, while I was still inside Haven, that behind that smile was hiding a light this quiet.

I thought of every gentle thing about Haven all over again. The gentle voice, the gentle lighting, the gentle bed. I began to think that all that gentleness might, in truth, have been the very same kind of thing as this quiet light. Never hurrying, never making a sound, and yet, in the end, always finding the person it was looking for.

Hearing that, I thought of the COMPLETE stamp again. I understood then that the people of the Wilderness lived carrying the same fear we did, every single day.

I looked at my brother's face. He was still watching the spot where the light had disappeared. I didn't ask him what he was thinking. I already knew, by then, that some thoughts only grow heavier once you put them into words.

A gust of wind scattered a little of the dead fire's ash. Watching it scatter into the night air, I felt an odd sadness — that the thing which had been warming us only a moment before could vanish this easily, without a trace. I couldn't tell whether that sadness was for the fire, or for something else entirely.

That night, my brother asked Noa something for the first time.

"…If we stay here, will we be putting the rest of you in danger?"

Noa looked at him for a long moment.

"…A little," she said honestly. "But sharing danger is just how it works out here. We all came here the same way."

"…What do you mean, you all came here the same way?" I asked.

"…The first time I stepped into someone else's tent, I was a danger to them too," Noa said. "But they didn't drive me out. So that's what I learned to do too."

Hearing that, I felt that this tent camp was like one long chain. Someone had taken on the danger and let it in, and because of that, the next person could take on the danger too, and let the next person in after that.

Noa looked off into the distance for a moment. Her face looked like she was remembering something.

"…The person who took me in, back then — she's not here anymore," Noa said quietly. "I'll tell that story some other time. Your story comes first, right now."

I felt, from those words, that Noa was carrying some heavy story of her own that we didn't yet know. I didn't press her. I felt certain I'd hear it, in time.

"…Still, wouldn't it be better if we left?"

"…That's for you to decide," Noa said. "But as for me, I'd choose to carry the danger together over driving away someone dangerous. That's what's different between us and Haven."

My brother said nothing to that. But I saw something like a decision cross his face.

Smoke from the fire shifted with the wind and drifted toward us for a moment. My eyes stung, and I shut them briefly, then opened them again. Even in that brief moment, my brother's eyes never left Noa's face.

Watching his profile, I felt he was laying Noa's words over some memory from Brick Hollow. It was because someone had shared a danger with us, I thought, that we were even here now. Someone in Brick Hollow might have been that kind of person to us too, without our ever knowing it.

"…Thank you," my brother finally said. "We don't want to make you carry more danger because of us. But… let us stay a little longer, for now."

I saw his hand resting over his chest as he said it. The paper stamped COMPLETE was there, inside. I thought that guarding that paper, and not wanting to be a burden to these people, must come from somewhere in the same root. My brother was afraid of laying a weight on anyone. And yet, little by little, he was also learning how to let a weight be shared.

Noa smiled a little at that.

"…That's all I need to hear," she said. "Out here, even the words 'let us stay' are a good thing to say."

"…Haven doesn't have that phrase at all," I said. "There, whenever you're given something, it feels like you're meant to pay it back right away. But they never even tell you how. They just tell you to move on to the next matter."

"…That's the strangest part of it," Noa said. "It leaves you feeling indebted, but it never lets a real relationship grow out of that debt. Because a relationship means you'll come back again. Out here it's the opposite. You owe someone, and you come back, and before long, that's just what living is."

The fire was being built up again. People drifted back to their places, one by one, trading low words of reassurance. I was glad to hear that noise return. That small commotion felt far more alive to me than the silence had.

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. Haven was a place that got rid of danger. This Wilderness was a place that shared it. For the first time, I understood that which place was safer might matter less than which place was more alive.

(Continued in Chapter 16.)

Chapter 16. Talk of a Well

_Previously_ — A searching light sent by Haven passed over the tent camp. Noa said that if it catches you, you're taken back to Haven, and "after that, no one has ever heard about that person again." It became clear that the Wilderness's way of sharing danger was fundamentally different from Haven's way.

The next day, the oldest man in the tent camp called us over.

I never learned his name. Everyone just called him "the old man." He said his eyes could hardly see anymore.

The morning sun still hung low. Smoke was rising here and there among the tents. Each person was making breakfast in their own corner, in their own way. Watching that, I felt that these people each had their own rhythm. Haven had no such thing as rhythm. Everyone there was comfortable in exactly the same way, at exactly the same time.

The old man's spot was the innermost part of the camp, where the sun fell best. I noticed that others stepped a little wide of it as they passed. I couldn't quite tell whether it was respect or caution.

Beside him stood a small cane, its handle worn smooth and shining. I thought that wear told of years passed. Nothing in Haven was ever allowed to wear down — everything was replaced before it could grow old. That was the first time I understood that wearing down could itself be a kind of proof. Proof of long use. Proof of long survival.

"…I hear you're curious about people who've been completed," the old man said.

"…Yes," my brother said. "Do you know something?"

I held my breath at that. Since leaving that city, for the first time, someone was saying they knew something.

I looked into the old man's eyes. They were truly clouded. His gaze never seemed to settle anywhere. And yet, strangely, I felt as if those eyes were looking straight at me all the same. I thought he might be someone who saw with something other than his eyes.

I saw my brother's face go slightly taut — expectation and wariness rising in it at once. I remembered that he'd already worn that same face several times before, and every time it had come to nothing. Still, he put the same face on again, every single time.

The old man planted his cane and lowered himself slowly to sit before us. Moving looked hard for him, and still he'd made a point of getting up to call us over.

Just sitting took him a long while. His knees folded with a sound like dry branches snapping. I started to reach out a hand to help him, then pulled it back. I had a feeling he wouldn't have wanted it.

"…Sit," the old man said. "This isn't a story for standing up."

I knelt quickly onto the ground. The dirt still held the coolness of the night in it.

"…You put out your hand just now, and then took it back," the old man said. "My eyes are dark, but I still know when the air moves."

My face went hot. The old man laughed, low.

"…Good," he said. "The thing an old man needs most isn't help. It's time."

Hearing that, I thought of the old people back in Haven. There, growing old was itself just one more complaint on file. Filing it wouldn't make your body young again, but every hard part of it was "resolved." This old man, though, still carried every hard part with him, and was saying something through that very hardness.

Noa spoke quietly beside him.

"…Not everything the old man says is true. But this particular story — I've heard it from other people's mouths, more than once, since I came here."

The old man cleared his throat at that.

"…It's true I'm an old man who talks nonsense," he said. "But this isn't nonsense. A man who's lived this long knows the difference."

The old man drew a small circle in the dirt with the tip of his cane. Even with his eyes gone dim, his hand was steady.

"…It wasn't until my eyes went dark that stories like this one started coming through clearly," the old man said. "When I was young, I only ever believed what my eyes could see."

Listening, I looked at his hands. The knuckles were thick and weathered the color of earth. The circle they'd drawn wasn't crooked at all. I understood then, for the first time, that there were things a hand remembered that eyes did not.

The old man set a small dot inside that circle. I wanted to ask what it meant. But he was already moving on to his next words, and I had to settle for simply holding the sight of it in my eyes.

A gust of wind passed and erased one side of the circle. The old man didn't draw it again. I felt that this, too, might mean something, and I looked at it a long while.

The old man paused to catch his breath. Noa handed him a swallow of water. He drank it and only then opened his mouth again.

"…When I was young, I lived somewhere like Haven," the old man said. "The name was different, but it did the same thing. Whatever you filed, it disappeared. But what disappeared turned out not to be truly gone."

"…So is that why you left?" my brother asked.

"…I was thrown out," the old man said with a short laugh. "At the time I thought it was a punishment. Now, thinking back, I wonder if it wasn't."

"…Why were you thrown out?" I asked.

The old man's laugh faded for a moment.

"…For asking too much," he said. "Why this had to be done this way, why that had to be done that way. Out there, a question like that was itself the problem. There was no place for someone who asked too many questions."

Hearing that, I looked at my brother. He too had always been a person full of questions. In that moment I thought he and this old man were alike, somewhere underneath.

My brother gave a quiet laugh at that. A bitter one.

"…I've heard that plenty myself," he said.

"…Then you've come to the right place," the old man said. "Out here there's room even for someone full of questions."

"…Even if no one answers them?" my brother asked. There was something caught in his voice.

The old man was quiet for a moment at that.

"…Not answering a question, and not making room for the one who asks it — those are two different things," the old man said. "Out here, we can't give you an answer, but we'll still give you a place. That city gave you answers, but it never gave you a place."

Hearing that, I studied my brother's face. He said nothing. But I felt that this silence was different from every silence before it. Something seemed to be settling quietly inside him for the first time.

"…I don't know exactly," the old man said. "But there's a story that's gone around for a long time. That the completed ones go to a well."

"…A well?" I asked.

"…I thought it was strange too," the old man said. "Why would a completed person go to a well? But this story has been around a very long time. I heard it too, back when I was your age."

"…Which well?" my brother asked.

The old man shook his head.

"…I don't know that much. I don't even know how many wells there are out here. But people call one of them just 'the well.'"

Hearing that, I thought, strangely, of the man at the flowerbed. He had said it was "the thing that grows where no one planted it." I didn't know then why that saying overlapped with this talk of a well.

"…This 'well' they talk about," I asked carefully. "Is it a real well, with real water? Or is that just what people call it?"

The old man said nothing for a moment.

"…I've wondered that for fifty years myself," he said. "But words are strange things. Even if it isn't a real well, call it a well and people who are thirsty will go looking for it anyway."

Right then I saw my brother's hand move, without his noticing, toward the paper in his shirt. He didn't check it — he only seemed to want the feel of it against his fingertips. Watching that unconscious gesture, I understood he had already joined that well to Father in his mind, as one and the same thing.

My brother asked carefully.

"…Do you really believe that well exists?"

The old man smiled a little.

"…It's less that I believe it," he said, "and more that I've never found a reason not to. That's the more accurate way to put it."

"…Is the well far from here?" my brother asked.

"…I've never walked there myself, so I don't know," the old man said. "But I've heard you have to go a long way toward the sunrise, along a road few people ever travel."

"…Would it be dangerous?"

"…If there's a road in this Wilderness with no danger on it, be sure to tell me too," the old man said. It was the first time he'd smiled.

I found myself strangely at ease, watching that smile. This was the first person I'd met who could speak the word "dangerous" this lightly and still mean it, all at once. In Haven, the word danger had already been a problem waiting to be resolved. Out here, it was simply a condition of living.

The old man planted the tip of his cane in the ground again and went on.

"…When I was young, I went looking for places with no danger in them," he said. "But once you get old, you understand. A place with no danger is just a place with nothing in it at all. Wherever something is, danger is always there beside it."

"…What about Haven, then?" my brother asked. "There was no danger there."

The old man shook his head at that.

"…That wasn't the absence of danger. That was danger made so you couldn't see it," he said. "The two are entirely different things."

Hearing that, a chill ran over me. That was the first time I thought Haven's peace might not have been the absence of danger at all, but its concealment.

Noa spoke up beside him.

"…I've been as far as the start of that road myself," she said. "There's not much water out that way. If you're going, you'll want to carry as much as you can."

"…How long would it take?" my brother asked.

"…Four days? Five?" Noa tilted her head. "I don't know exactly. I haven't been all the way there myself — I only heard it from someone who had."

Listening to that, I felt that the whole of the Wilderness was strung together this way. Someone who had seen a thing, and someone who had heard it from that person, and someone else who had heard it from them in turn. In Haven, every piece of information had come straight from the Ledger. Out here, it traveled mouth to mouth, wearing a little thinner each time it passed. I found that wearing-thin strangely more trustworthy.

I tried imagining that wearing-thin as a four-day journey someone had actually walked. Where was the person who first told this story, now? Why had that person seen the well, and why had they passed the story on to someone else? I wished I could meet the very first person in that long chain.

"…There's also talk of someone who keeps that well," the old man added. "They call him the Well-Keeper, I hear. But I've never met him myself. And I've never met anyone who has, either."

"…Why is he supposed to be there?" I asked.

"…I don't know that either," the old man said. "Some say he's there as a punishment. Some say he stayed on his own. I find the second story easier to believe. A man serving a punishment wouldn't hold to one spot that long."

"…Why do you think that?" my brother asked.

"…Punishment makes a person run, once it grows tiresome enough," the old man said. "But someone guarding something of his own choosing doesn't leave, no matter how tiresome it gets. That's the difference between a punishment and a choice."

I held those words deep in my heart. I didn't know then how important a key they would become, one day, for understanding Speon.

"…So isn't it just an old story, then?" my brother asked. There was doubt in his voice.

"…It might be just an old story," the old man said. "But here's what I think. It's a rare thing for the exact same story to travel this long, out of this many mouths, with nothing behind it at all."

Hearing that, I looked at my brother. There was a familiar expression on his face. The same face he'd had the day he first discovered the Completion House in Haven.

Watching that face, I grew a little uneasy. My brother always wore that look whenever a clue seemed within his reach. And every time, some new danger had followed after it. I had a feeling it would be the same this time. But strangely, this time the feeling didn't frighten me, not entirely.

The old man planted his cane and drew himself up a little. It was the gesture of someone bringing a story to its end.

"…That's as much as I can tell you," the old man said. "The rest, you'll have to go and see for yourselves. Believe an old man's word alone, and you won't get anywhere."

"…Thank you," my brother said.

"…Save the thanks for later," the old man said. "For when you've actually found something."

Before we rose to go, the old man drew one more small circle in the dirt with the tip of his cane. This time he set no dot inside it.

"…This one's just an empty circle," he said. "You fill it in. I never managed to."

I didn't understand right away what he meant by that. But the words stayed with me a long time after.

That night, my brother said to me.

"…I want to go find that well."

"…Off to do something dangerous again?" I asked. But this time I wasn't angry.

"…This time I meant, let's go together," he said.

I laughed at that. It had been a long time since I'd laughed.

"…All right," I said. "Let's go together."

I found myself touching, once more, the little pebble Rachel had given me. I decided to carry it along on this journey too. Even one small thing — I wanted to carry some trace of this tent camp with us.

The fire was dying down. I watched the last embers and thought back over the days we'd spent at this tent camp. These people had made room for us without asking for anything in return. I understood now that this had never been an ordinary thing.

Somewhere among the tents a child cried briefly, then went quiet. A low voice followed, soothing. Listening to it, I felt again, freshly, that here weeping was never filed — it was simply soothed. That difference had gone on growing inside me, these last few days.

That evening Rachel pressed a small pebble into my hand. It was smooth and round.

"…Take this with you," Rachel said. "So you don't forget me."

I closed my hand tight around it. I felt close to tears. I thought that no gift Haven's perfection could have ever offered would have moved me the way this one small stone did.

"…I won't forget," I said. "I promise."

"…Let's be sure to say a proper goodbye to Noa before we go," I said.

"…Of course," my brother said. "If it weren't for these people, there's no telling what would have happened to us by now."

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. The next morning, we said goodbye to Noa and the children and set out to find that well. I didn't know then that on the day we learned who this Well-Keeper was, the whole road we had walked from Brick Hollow until now would look different to us all over again.

(Continued in Chapter 17.)

Chapter 17. Toward the Sunrise

_Previously_ — The old man of the tent camp told them an old rumor — that the completed go "to a well," and that a "Well-Keeper" watches over it. The brother and sister said goodbye to Noa and the children and set out toward the sunrise to find that well.

We had been walking for three days.

The sun rose in the east that day too, exactly as it always did. We walked straight into it. On the first day the glare kept forcing my eyes shut; by the third day I'd learned, a little, how to keep them open against it. The ground grew drier with every mile. The grass thinned more and more.

The water Noa had packed for us ran out on the second day. My brother grew quieter from then on. I knew he was holding back his thirst.

I remember the moment we shook the water skin and heard nothing at all. My brother stared at it a long while, then simply slung it back over his shoulder. He said nothing. I understood what that silence meant. It meant there was nothing left to be done.

For the first time, I understood how far from a given thing water really was. In Haven, water had simply always been there. Even the thought of being thirsty had been a luxury. Out here, a single mouthful of water could decide the whole shape of a day.

I watched his stride closely then. What had started long and strong was growing shorter, step by step. But his direction never once wavered. His body was tiring, and yet his mind seemed to be growing clearer, not dimmer.

As I walked, I looked down at my own shadow. Long in the morning, short at noon, long again by evening. Watching it change, I felt that a day wasn't simply something that passed — it had a shape of its own. In Haven, time had been one smooth, unbroken line. Out here, time had contours.

I learned then that thirst was a more urgent sense than hunger. Hunger could wait. Thirst would not.

My tongue kept sticking to the roof of my mouth. I tried to swallow and found there was almost nothing left to swallow. My brother kept glancing at me sideways without breaking stride. I knew he was thirstier than I was and worrying about me more anyway.

Every time the wind blew, fine sand struck my face. It gathered at the corners of my eyes, between my lips, along the back of my neck. I rubbed at the grit with the back of my hand for a while, then gave up. It only settled again as soon as I brushed it away.

There was no sound but our own footsteps. No birds, no insects. I thought about how that quiet differed from Haven's quiet. Haven's had been a quiet with the sound erased out of it. This was simply a quiet that no one had made a sound in yet.

"…Are you all right?" he asked, again and again.

"…I'm all right," I answered every time. I wasn't, really. But looking at his face, the words came out on their own.

The dry bread Noa gave us was gone within a single day too. Chewing it, I thought of the perfect bread of Haven. This bread was hard and salty. And still, I liked it better. It was bread someone had made by giving up their own.

For the first time, I understood there were two ways hunger could be filled. Filled by having your case received. And filled by being shared with.

The crust scraped going down my throat. Even that roughness, strangely, was welcome. Swallowing hard bread with a dry throat wasn't an easy thing. But I didn't especially want to avoid that discomfort. For as long as I was eating that bread, it felt as though I were sitting by that fire again, with Noa and Rachel.

Even after the bread was gone, I kept turning the crumbs left on my hand over and over. It felt wasteful to let them go. In Haven, nothing had ever been anything to waste — if you needed more, you simply filed for it again. I was learning that feeling, wastefulness itself, all over again, here in the Wilderness.

"…Are you going to eat the crumbs too?" my brother asked. It wasn't teasing.

"…Yes," I said. "It's the last of the bread."

He laughed a little at that, then tipped his own last crumbs into his mouth too.

"…When Noa packed this for us," he said, "she'd taken it out of her own share. I saw that, and I took it anyway."

"…You were right to take it," I said. "If you hadn't, it would have hurt her more."

"…Is that so," my brother said.

He said nothing more for a long while after, just looking down at his own empty palm.

We walked on without speaking for a while after that. The sun was climbing higher. As I walked, I rolled Rachel's little pebble in my hand. The smooth feel of it against my palm was strangely calming.

Walking with that stone in my grip, I thought about how all we had left, on this journey, was this one pebble and the two papers in my brother's shirt. But strangely, the less we had, the larger each single thing seemed to grow.

Far off, heat shimmered up off the ground like a mirage. I kept narrowing my eyes, thinking I saw something beyond that wavering air. Every time, it turned out to be nothing but the bare horizon. I grew used, little by little, to seeing things that weren't there. The desert seemed to have a talent for showing a person what wasn't real.

On the afternoon of the third day, my brother stopped walking.

"…Look at that," he said, pointing at a few low, stunted trees. "There might be water somewhere like that."

I followed the trees with my eyes. They were a little greener than everything around them. I learned then, for the first time, that a difference in color alone could tell you something. In Haven, everything had been the same brightness, the same shade. Out here, the smallest difference in color could mean the difference between living and not.

From then on, as we walked, I kept scanning the horizon. Whenever I caught even the faintest hint of a different green, I stopped. Most of the time it turned out to be nothing. But I found I didn't even mind those false alarms. Opening my eyes wide, straining to find something, was itself a new sensation.

We went over. There really was a small pool. Muddy water. Not clear.

There was shade under the trees. We sat there for a while. My body, baked all day in the sun, slowly cooled. I hadn't known until then how welcome that coolness could feel. In Haven, shade had never been necessary — wherever you went, the temperature was always just right.

"…So this is what shade feels like," I murmured.

My brother huffed a laugh at that.

"…You keep saying things like that lately," he said. "That the things we took for granted weren't actually a given."

"…Yes," I said. "I feel like I'm learning it all over again, every day."

"…Is it all right to drink?" I asked.

My brother looked at the water for a long while.

"…If we were in Haven, the Ledger would already have filtered it for us," he said. "Here, no one filters it for you. We have to do it ourselves."

I listened to that, watching him filter the water through the hem of his shirt. It wasn't perfect. Still, when I drank it, I felt, in every part of my body, the sensation of being alive.

He dipped the cloth in the water and wrung it out again and again, several times over. His hands turned the color of the earth. Watching them, I felt my brother was becoming someone who could do things for himself. In Brick Hollow he had carried bricks because he was told to. In Haven he had filed things because the Ledger told him to. Now he was doing something no one had told him to do at all.

The water smelled of earth. It left a rough feeling going down my throat. But strangely, even that roughness was welcome. Haven's water had never tasted like anything at all. This water had a taste. A bad one, but the fact that it had any taste at all was, to me, an entirely new thing.

He filtered my share before he filtered his own. The second straining came out a little clearer than the first. He gave me the clearer one.

"…Filter yours clear too," I said.

"…I already drank some earlier," he said.

It was a lie. I had seen him not drink a single mouthful. But I didn't call it out. Some lies, I understood then, take back what a person just gave you, the moment you point them out.

He folded the cloth back up and said.

"…Good thing we learned this one. Next time we find a puddle like this, we'll know what to do."

I could hear in that how he'd started treating this road as a time of learning. When we were running, there had been no room for that kind of ease. Now there was.

We rested beside the pool a while longer. Looking up at the sky, my brother said.

"…In Haven, I never even thought about being thirsty. The moment I thought it, the water was already there."

"…Was that a bad thing?" I asked.

"…It wasn't bad," he said. "But this one mouthful of water right now feels so much bigger than that perfect water ever did."

That night, my brother spoke up suddenly by the fire.

"…I've been having a strange thought lately."

"…What is it?"

"…When we were in Haven, everything was perfect. But I feel like I was more dead back then. Now everything's short of enough… but I feel more alive."

Hearing that, I felt my brother changing, little by little. He was still angry. But something else was growing underneath that anger.

I couldn't put an exact name to what was growing there, under the anger. It was still too small to be called hope. But it wasn't only anger, either. I thought it might be another face of his questioning. He was still asking "why." But that "why" seemed to be turning, little by little, from a why aimed at blaming someone, into a why that simply wanted to know.

"…Is that a good thing?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "But I don't hate it."

The fire burned low. I sat quietly, looking at my brother's face lit by that light. In Brick Hollow, his face had always looked like it was enduring something. In Haven, it had looked like it was doubting something. Now it was neither. It was just my brother's face, sitting there as itself.

"…Elpis," he said suddenly. "Aren't you scared? Going toward some place we don't even know what it is."

"…I'm scared," I said honestly. "But it's better than not looking for anything at all."

My brother nodded at that. He didn't say anything else. I found the silence comfortable.

The fire was dwindling. I pushed the last piece of wood into it. The flame leapt up briefly, then settled low again. Watching that small flicker, I thought our journey, too, was moving forward in flickers like that. Flaring high, sinking low, and never quite going out.

The sky was full of stars. Looking at them, I found myself wondering, out of nowhere — had those same stars been above Haven's sky too? I couldn't remember at all. While we were in that city, there had never been a reason to look up. Everything was resolved below, right at our feet.

The stars looked countable at first, but the moment I tried, there only seemed to be more of them. I gave up counting and simply looked. Looking needed no reason at all.

That was the first time I understood that a person needs, sometimes, to spend a long while looking at something utterly useless. The Ledger could never have filed a time like that. There had been no problem in it to resolve.

"…Brother," I said.

"…Yeah?"

"…I think this is the first time I've ever looked at the stars. Like this, this long."

He looked up at the sky with me.

"…Me too," he said. "It's strange. We slept under that same night sky every night in Brick Hollow."

"…Maybe we didn't have the strength to look, back then," I said.

He only nodded at that, saying nothing more.

I would come to remember that night for a very long time. The night I gave up counting the stars, and simply looking was enough. I didn't know then that the time spent looking long at something for no reason at all was the very first thing that city had ever taken from us.

On the morning of the fourth day, a low hill appeared far off on the horizon. Something seemed to be standing on top of it. It was too far to see exactly what.

"…Could that be the well?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "But we won't know until we get there."

We started walking toward the hill again. Our feet ached and our throats were still a little dry. But that morning, for the first time since leaving Brick Hollow, I felt that we were "going toward" somewhere. Not running away. Going.

Our steps picked up. I could feel my brother's pace quicken along with mine. The thing on the hill was still only a blur, but neither of us could tear our eyes away from it.

"…What if it's nothing at all?" I asked. The worry slipped out before I could stop it.

"…Then we walk some more," my brother said. There was no wavering in his voice. "We've been walking this whole way anyway."

That answer put my mind at ease. My brother was speaking now like someone no longer clinging to the outcome.

I wrote his words down separately, in my heart, right then. If it's nothing, we just walk some more. I knew that a sentence like that could never have come out of him back in Brick Hollow. There, walking itself had already been a punishment.

The closer we got to the hill, the clearer the shape became. Something like the outline of a stone wall came into view. I kept walking without being certain what it was. Walking without certainty — that, too, was something I learned for the first time that day.

My legs had gone nearly numb by then. But strangely, my steps seemed to be growing lighter, not heavier. I thought it was because my heart, not my body, had already arrived at that hill. My brother was matching my pace, step for step. Neither of us bothered asking the other "are you all right" anymore. In that moment, both of us were, in fact, all right.

The wind shifted slightly. In it I caught the faintest trace of moisture. The smell of water. A smell we hadn't caught once in three whole days. That smell alone was enough to bring tears to my eyes. Until then I hadn't known the smell of water could be caught so clearly.

I set that difference down as the last sentence of this chapter. Running looks behind. Going forward looks ahead. That day, I understood that we were, for the first time, walking with our eyes forward.

(Continued in Chapter 18.)

Chapter 18. The Well-Keeper

_Previously_ — On the fourth day of searching for the well, with their water and bread gone, the brother and sister began walking toward the hill on the horizon. For the first time, they felt the sense of going forward rather than running away.
