What stood on the hill really was a well.

A small well ringed by a low stone wall. Someone sat beside it. A young man. He looked a little older than us.

The last few steps up the hill were the hardest. There was no strength left at all in my legs, and still, from the moment the well came into view, I couldn't stop walking. My brother was the same. We climbed the last stretch of that hill almost at a run.

Low grass grew here and there around it. A clearly greener shade than anywhere else. Just that color of grass alone told me this place was different from everywhere around it.

A small insect lifted off from among the grass and settled again. Even that small movement caught my eye. Along the whole four days of dry road behind us, I'd barely seen so much as a single bug. This one hill alone seemed strangely full of life.

I felt something strange then. The well stood alone in the middle of the Wilderness, in a place with nothing around it. And yet the stone wall alone was clean, as if someone tended it every single day.

A few small wildflowers bloomed in cracks between the stones of the wall. I wondered whether someone had planted them, or whether they'd grown up on their own. Either way, the fact that something alive had taken root on this bare hilltop was, strangely, a comfort.

On legs that had walked four days, we arrived, more collapsing than sitting down before it. My brother looked like he had no strength left even to speak. I could feel the man's eyes reading our condition in a single glance.

He rose to his feet at once and came toward us. His steps were quick. But even in that quickness I could feel something careful in him — like someone worried that if he came too fast, we might vanish like a mirage.

"…Sit," he said first. "Drink water before you talk."

His voice was low and calm. There was no hurry in it at all. I found that calm strangely foreign. Everyone we'd met since coming out into the Wilderness had carried some measure of urgency. This man alone did not.

Beside where he'd been sitting, a small heap of stones was stacked with care. I couldn't guess what it was for. But I could tell those stones hadn't been set down carelessly. They'd been stacked in order of size, very deliberately.

Beside the stone heap, a few worn wooden bowls sat overturned to dry. Not one bowl — several. I understood they'd been set out for more than one person. Bowls turned over and set to dry, every day, for guests who never came.

One bowl was noticeably smaller than the rest. I knew at a glance it was made for a child. I didn't know who it was for. But that one small bowl alone seemed to say more than the whole careful order of the hilltop around it.

The man looked us over first. He wasn't surprised.

"…It's been a while since anyone came by," he said. "Here for water?"

"…Yes," my brother said. His throat was parched.

The man stood and looked us over once more. Worn clothes, cracked lips, feet caked in dust. It wasn't a judging look. Just a careful one.

"…You've come a long way," he said. "Which way did you come from?"

"…From a tent camp," my brother said. "The one where a woman named Noa lives."

Something flickered faintly in the man's eyes at that name.

"…You know Noa?" my brother asked.

"…I've heard word of her," the man said. "Never met her myself. But I've heard she's a good person."

"…We walked four days," I said.

"…Four days." The man repeated it back, something turning over behind his eyes. "Walking this direction four days means you had a reason for it."

The man lowered a bucket and drew up water for us. It was clear. Not muddy.

"…Why is this one so clear?" I asked.

"…This well is deep," the man said. "Water that comes from deep down is always clear."

Drinking that water, I thought his words weren't only about the water. I didn't know exactly why I felt that.

The bucket knocked against the wall of the well as it rose. A hollow, resonant sound, twice over. I felt that sound had been keeping this place company for a very long time. The way he wound the rope back up looked practiced — the motion of something repeated more times than could be counted, plain in the shape of his fingers.

The water was cool, and just faintly sweet. I had never drunk water like it. Not the muddy water, and not Haven's flavorless water either. I looked back down into the bucket. It was too deep to see the bottom.

I felt the coolness spread through my whole body as it went down my throat. I closed my eyes and gave myself over entirely to that feeling. Four days of gathered thirst seemed to wash away all at once. I could feel warm blood returning even to my fingertips.

"…Drink slowly," the man said. "Drink too fast and you'll make yourself sick."

Only hearing that did I realize how frantically I'd been gulping it down. I forced myself to slow. My brother slowed to match me.

"…I've seen a lot of children drink like that," he said. "Everyone's the same, the moment they get here. They're afraid the water's going to run out."

"…Does it?" I asked.

"…It doesn't," he said. "This well has never once gone dry. But believing that takes a little time."

I remembered those words a long while after. That believing something won't run dry takes time. I understood then that everything we'd learned in Haven had, in the end, been the opposite of that. In that city, everything was filled the instant you wanted it — but nothing could ever be believed in.

"…How deep is it?" I asked.

"…I've never measured it all the way down myself," the man said. "But I know it's very deep."

I watched his hand lower the bucket back down into the well. The sound of the rope unspooling went on and on. Just from the length of that sound alone, I could guess how deep this well must be. By the time I heard the bucket touch water, I had almost started counting my own breaths.

My brother drank too, and seemed for the first time to breathe properly.

"…Thank you," he said. There was real feeling in his voice. It was the first time, outside that city, I'd heard him say that to anyone.

Speon looked briefly startled at that, as if he'd been handed something enormous.

"…It's just a bowl of water," he said. But his voice sounded genuinely glad.

"…It doesn't look like many people come here," I said.

"…Almost none," Speon said. "Once every few months, or rarer than that. So when someone comes, I'm glad. And a little afraid, too."

"…Why afraid?" I asked.

"…In case it's nothing again," Speon said quietly. In that, I could feel that he greeted every visitor already hoping for something.

"…What kind of news?" I asked.

Speon chose his words a moment, then shook his head.

"…I'll tell you that later," he said. "Right now I'm more curious how you two ended up here."

I didn't press him further. But in that brief hesitation, I could feel that some very old story was waiting inside him.

When my brother finished drinking, he asked carefully.

"…Is this… the well people say the completed go to?"

The man's expression changed at that. He looked startled.

"…Where did you hear that?" he asked.

"…From people in the Wilderness," my brother said. "And about the ones stamped COMPLETE, too."

"…Who told you this?" the man asked again. There was urgency in his voice now.

"…An old man at a tent camp," my brother said. "And someone named Noa, too. We don't know the details, but they said the story's been around a long time."

The man looked at the worn paper in my brother's hand. He looked at it for a long time.

"…Would you show me that paper?"

My brother handed it over. The man read the name, and ran his fingertip over the stamp.

In that moment, I saw something in the man's face come apart. It was too complicated an expression to call sorrow alone.

I haven't forgotten that expression. Relief and dread crossed it at the very same instant. It was the face of someone who had waited a very long time for something to finally arrive, and at the same moment had hoped it never would.

His fingertip paused for a moment over the stamp. I saw it tremble slightly. It was the hand of someone touching, again, a very old wound.

"…This name — I don't know this person," he said quietly. "But this stamp… I know this stamp. I know it very well."

"…How do you know it?" my brother asked.

Instead of answering, he looked off into the distance. He said nothing for a long time.

I could see, in that silence, the faintest tremor in his eyes. The eyes of someone holding words back. I understood then that this man, too, was carrying a great deal that he had not yet found a way to say.

While the silence held, I looked around the well. There were small marks scored into one side of the stone wall — nicks someone had cut in, over and over, for a long time. Numbers, or a tally of some kind. I couldn't tell which.

"…What's that?" I asked, pointing at the wall.

The man seemed to come back to himself, and looked over.

"…Days," he said. "I was counting how many I'd spent here. I started counting, and then I stopped. There got to be too many."

I tried, for a moment, to count those marks with my fingertip, and gave up. There truly were too many to count. That number frightened me. Those marks were telling me that the length of time this man had spent here was an entirely different kind of time than the four days we had walked to reach him.

In that moment I thought of Haven's COMPLETE stamp. Time that ended with a single stamp, and time that could only pass by being scored, one mark at a time, into stone. I think I already knew, even then, which of the two was the truer kind of time.

Some of the marks were cut deep; some were barely there at all. I thought that difference in depth must have left behind, exactly, whatever mood each of those days had carried. What had happened on the days cut deep? I couldn't bring myself to ask.

"…My name is Speon," the man finally said. "People say I'm the one who keeps this well. Though to be exact… it isn't keeping. It's waiting."

"…Speon," I said, saying the name back. An unfamiliar name. But it settled onto my tongue strangely easily.

"…That's right," he said, smiling a little. "Just call me that. It's been a while since anyone's said my name here."

"…No one says it?" I asked.

"…There has to be someone to say it," Speon said. A trace of loneliness crept into his voice. "Even the ones who come by, once every few months, often don't ask my name at all. Everyone just drinks the water and hurries off."

Hearing that hurt something in me. I tried to imagine how long Speon must have gone here, beside this well, with no one to call him by name at all.

As he said it, he glanced back toward the well. It looked like an old habit. I noticed that even in the middle of talking, he kept checking on it out of the corner of his eye, as if something might rise up out of it at any moment.

"…Waiting for what?" I asked.

The wind swept across the well's edge. His clothes, caught on the rim of the stone, stirred faintly. In that brief silence, I felt that it had been a very long time since anyone had asked Speon that question.

Speon looked at us for a long time. His face was that of someone making up his mind about something.

"…To answer that question, it'll be a long story," Speon said. "Rest here tonight, and tomorrow I'll tell you everything I know. It's been… a very long time since anyone came here carrying that question."

Speon stood and walked a slow circle around the well's edge. It was the walk of someone confirming something. I guessed it was a round he made every night, something like a watch. No one had ordered it of him — it looked more like a rule he'd set for himself.

"…Do you go around like this every night?" my brother asked.

"…Yes," Speon said. "It's a habit. I can't sleep until I've made sure nothing's wrong."

"…And tonight?" I asked.

Speon stopped at that question and looked at us for a moment.

"…Tonight, something happened," he said. "You two came."

Speon came back and sat down beside us again. Then, after a moment, he got up once more and brought back two blankets. Worn, but clean.

"…Bear with it if it's uncomfortable," Speon said. "There's no guest room out here."

"…This is more than enough," my brother said. "Better than Haven's beds."

He set a small stone down beside our spot. I almost asked what it was for, then decided not to. Probably something to keep insects away overnight. That small, ordinary care lodged itself in me strangely.

Speon smiled a little at that. It was the first smile I'd seen from him.

"…So you're children who know Haven," he said. "That'll make this easier to explain."

The blankets smelled of sunlight — the smell of something dried a long time in the open air. Underneath that smell was a faint trace of earth. Breathing it in, I thought about how many times Speon must have hung these blankets out and taken them back in, for guests who never came.

A night wind blew across the well and lifted the edge of the blanket faintly. I didn't try to escape the chill of it. That coolness, arriving after the day's heat, felt like the last word of goodnight to the day.

Lying under that blanket, I still couldn't fall asleep for a long while. From the well's direction I thought I heard the faint sound of water moving, low. Maybe it was only my imagination. But listening to it, I thought this well seemed almost as if it were alive, breathing on its own.

Beside me, my brother's breathing settled into an even rhythm. I could tell he was sleeping properly for the first time in days. A little way off, Speon was still sitting up. Not lying down — facing the well. I watched his back for a while before I finally fell asleep.

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. That night, for the first time, I felt that we were drawing close to a real answer. But strangely, that feeling was closer to fear than to joy.

(Continued in Chapter 19.)

Chapter 19. Those Who Lost Their Names

_Previously_ — After four days of travel, the brother and sister found the well. A man named Speon, who kept it, was deeply shaken by the paper stamped COMPLETE. "I know this stamp," he said. He promised to tell them everything he knew the following night.

The next night, Speon began to speak by the fire.

The fire burned low. Speon studied it for a long while before he opened his mouth. I could tell he was choosing his words — the face of someone who had spent the whole day thinking about where to begin.

I remember my brother standing there that night, unable even to sit down. I thought then that he was ready to hear it. Looking back now, I don't think anyone can ever really be ready for a story like that one.

The night air was growing cold. I sat with a blanket around my shoulders, watching only Speon's mouth. His lips opened and closed several times before any words came. Watching that hesitation, I braced myself in advance for how heavy this story would be.

From the well came the faintest sound of water moving. I hadn't known, until I heard it, that the well made any sound at all.

Speon held a hand out near the fire, then drew it back. He did this several times. I understood it wasn't the cold. It was the gesture of someone who didn't know where else to put his hands.

"…This well is connected to the Completion Houses in the cities," Speon said. "I don't know exactly how it's connected. But some of the people who are completed — they come up out of the bottom of this well."

My breath stopped the moment I heard that. I understood at once — if that were true, it meant Father might not be dead at all.

My heart was beating so fast in that moment that everything else seemed to go silent. My brother's voice, Speon's voice — they both sounded as if they were coming from somewhere far away. I only barely held on to my own attention and forced myself back to the words.

My brother shot to his feet right there.

"…Are you sure?" he asked. "They really come out alive?"

"…Every one I've seen with my own eyes, yes," Speon said. "Not many. But every one of them was alive."

My brother's hands were shaking. It was the first time I'd seen them shake.

I wanted to take that hand in mine. But I couldn't bring myself to reach for it. In that moment my brother looked like someone who needed no comfort from anyone — only Speon's next words.

Instead, I gripped my own knees tight enough that my fingertips went numb. I had to bear the moment somehow, even like that. I hadn't known that the words "Father might be alive" could make my whole body shake this much.

An ember popped somewhere in the fire. I flinched even at that small sound. The night was that quiet, and all of us were strung that taut.

Speon watched him a moment, then motioned for him to sit back down.

"…Sit and listen," Speon said. "If your legs give out, you won't hear the rest of this either way."

Only then did my brother sit. I saw that his knees were still trembling.

"…They come out? Alive?" my brother asked. His voice trembled.

"…Yes, alive," Speon said. "But not whole."

"…What do you mean?"

My brother asked it again. I had a feeling he would ask it a second time, and a third. It wasn't an answer a person could take in all at once.

Speon closed his eyes for a moment.

"…They come out having lost their name," Speon said. "And along with it, they lose who they loved. The most painful memory is erased, and along with it, it seems, the names and faces bound up in that memory disappear too."

Hearing that, I thought of the old woman at the flowerbed. That blurred place where she had forgotten her daughter's name but "having loved her" alone remained. For the first time, I understood that the two things worked by the same principle. Haven's "resolving" and the Completion House's "completing" only differed in degree — they were looking in the same direction.

I looked at my brother's face in that moment. He seemed to have already understood it before I had. He was biting down hard on his lip. I didn't need to ask what he was thinking — I already knew. That even if Father was alive, he might not know us at all.

"…So nothing at all is left?" I asked. My voice had gone small.

"…I said something is left," Speon said. "It isn't erased completely. But what that something is differs from person to person. For some it's a habit of the hands. For some it's a line of a song they liked."

"…How do you know what's left?" my brother asked.

"…You only find out by watching them, after they come out," Speon said. "That's why I'm here. To watch."

"…So where do they go?" I asked. "After they come out here."

"…Not right away," Speon said. "They sit here a few days first. Then one day they just stand up and walk off. Even they don't know where they're going."

"…Don't you try to stop them?" I asked.

"…I can't," Speon said. "I have no reason I can give them to stay. I don't know their names, and they never ask mine."

I thought that was the saddest thing said at this well. Speon was a man who waited for people. And even the people he waited for, and finally met, only ever passed him by.

"…How long does it take? For them to come out. After they're completed."

"…That differs too," Speon said. "Some come out within days. Some take months. I don't know the exact rule myself."

"…How long has it been, since Father was completed?" I asked.

"…I'd think you two would know that better than me," Speon said carefully.

My brother thought for a moment before he answered.

"…Almost two months," my brother said. His voice cracked.

That was when I understood that my brother had been counting the days. I had never once counted. I was afraid that if I counted, the number would only keep growing. My brother had done the opposite. He had been holding on to that growing number, every single day.

Speon heard the number and closed his eyes for a moment.

"…Then he might already be out," he said. "Or he might not be, yet. I'm sorry I can't tell you anything certain."

"…What are people like, the first time they come out?" I asked. "Aren't they afraid?"

"…They're afraid," Speon said. "They don't know who they are, or why they came up out of the water. But strangely, at first, they're all quiet. They don't even cry. They just sit still and look around."

"…Why don't they cry?" I asked.

"…To cry, you have to know you've lost something," Speon said. "But those people don't even know what they've lost. So there's no way for them to cry."

Hearing that, I felt a chill run over my skin. I understood then, for the first time, that forgetting wasn't only a sad thing — it was a thing that made you unable to even grieve.

"…So does a day come when they cry again?" I asked.

"…It does," Speon said. "But not because they remember something again. It's because they come to cherish something new. And once they grow afraid of losing that — that's the first time they cry again."

Hearing that put me strangely at ease. It seemed to mean that a road existed that wasn't about getting back what was lost, but about building something new to cherish.

Listening to him, I felt my fingertips go cold. That was the first time I understood exactly what Haven's "resolving" took from a person. It didn't erase sorrow — it erased the very right to grieve. I thought that was a far more frightening thing to lose.

"…So could our father come out somewhere here too?" I asked.

"…He could," Speon said. "But it might not be this well. Each Completion House seems to connect to a different well. I only know this one."

"…Then how many wells are there?"

"…I don't know," Speon said. "I'm still looking too."

I could feel, in that answer, that Speon had been doing this for a long time. And that even after all that time, he still hadn't found every answer.

In that moment I felt, for real, that this world was far larger than we'd imagined. In Brick Hollow the whole world had been the brick kilns and the taskmasters. In Haven I'd believed the world ended at that city's boundary stones. But now I understood that this world might hold more wells than could ever be counted.

People usually say a wide world sounds like hope. But that night, to me, it sounded like the opposite. A wide world also meant there were that many more places where I might lose Father for good.

"…Does every one of these wells have someone keeping it, the way you do?" I asked.

"…I don't know," Speon said. "I've heard that some wells are just left, with no one at all. Someone who comes out of a well like that… truly meets no one, and just wanders alone."

Hearing that gave me a chill. I felt my heart clench again, thinking that Father might have come out of a well exactly like that.

"…Then we'll have to hurry even more," my brother said. There was urgency in his voice.

"…Hurrying is fine," Speon said carefully. "But take care of yourselves first. Set out exhausted, and you'll only be slower in the end."

"…Then we'll have to find the other wells too," my brother said. His voice already sounded decided.

"…It won't be easy," Speon said. "This world is wide, and the wells are hidden. It took me years just to come to keep this one."

"…We'll do it anyway," my brother said.

There was no wavering in his voice. Beside him I felt that steadiness, and at the same time accepted that this journey might turn out to be far longer than we'd thought. When we left Brick Hollow, I'd believed it would be over in a matter of days. Now, for the first time, it occurred to me that it might take years.

Speon looked at my brother a long time after that answer. I felt something like pity in that look, and something a little larger than pity — a kind of respect.

"…I made a decision like that once too, when I was your age," Speon said quietly. "It didn't turn out well, back then. But that doesn't mean the decision itself was wrong."

"…What happened?" my brother asked.

Speon shook his head.

"…That's for later," he said. "Your story comes first, right now."

"…Why do you do this?" my brother asked. "What does it have to do with you?"

Speon was quiet for a long time.

"…I once made someone else wait, a long time ago," he said finally. "Back then, I couldn't wait, and I handed someone else over instead. Waiting here, at this well, now… it's doing the thing I couldn't do then."

I didn't fully understand what he meant. But seeing my brother offer no argument at all to that, I knew there was something heavy and true in it.

The firelight covered half of Speon's face in shadow. I wondered what might be sitting there, in that shadowed half. Even from the other half alone, I could tell he was someone who had blamed himself for a very long time.

I understood then, all over again, that Speon had reached this spot far, far before us. We thought we'd walked four days. Speon had not moved an inch from this place in years. I could only guess, then, at what kind of walking that was.

I found myself wondering which was harder — walking, or staying. Walking wore out the body. But staying meant bearing, every single day in the same spot, a different weight of heart each time. I couldn't reach an answer, not that night.

The fire had burned down low. Speon fed in another branch. The flame caught again and lit his face. For the first time, I really saw the old exhaustion that had settled into it.

"…What does 'handed someone else over' mean?" I asked carefully.

Speon looked at me. He didn't answer. But the silence said more than an answer would have. I decided not to press further.

I learned later that some stories have to wait until the person telling them is ready. And that waiting, itself, is already a way of giving that person something.

My brother changed the subject, as if to honor that silence.

"…So are you still looking for that person? The one you say you handed over?"

Speon shook his head.

"…No," he said. "That person is somewhere else now. I'm only repaying that debt a different way. I can't repay it to them, so I repay it to the people who come here instead."

Speon went on.

"…I've told you everything I know. But there's one more thing. The people who come out here — even having lost their name, they don't become an entirely new person. Something remains. I help them hold on to what remains."

The fire kept sinking lower. No one spoke again. In that silence I felt my brother settling something inside himself. His eyes weren't on Speon anymore — they were on the distant horizon.

That night, lying down to sleep, I couldn't close my eyes. I could hear my brother tossing beside me too. The sky was full of stars. Looking up at them, I thought of Father — sitting somewhere, perhaps, without a name. Was he looking at these same stars right now? And if he was, did he even know they were stars?

My brother's breathing had already settled into a steady rhythm somewhere along the way. I found it strange that he could fall asleep even now, body worn out and heart so heavy. Watching that, I thought people must be made, somehow, to go on living even inside their sorrow.

I found one star shining unusually bright and looked at it a long while. It didn't move. Only once I'd confirmed it was a real star did I feel a little at ease. I prayed silently that the quiet light wouldn't come, not tonight.

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. That night I learned, all at once, that Father might be alive somewhere, and that he might not remember us at all. I didn't know then which of the two was harder to bear.

(Continued in Chapter 20.)

Chapter 20. The One Who Was Already There

_Previously_ — Speon revealed the truth of the Completion House. A completed person comes out alive through a well, but loses their name and the faces of those they loved along with their most painful memory. Hope that Father might be alive, and terror that he might not remember them, arrived together.

That night I barely slept.

I lay under my blanket, but my eyes kept opening. I could hear the wind striking the stone wall beside the well — a sound I hadn't heard in daylight. Listening to it, I thought that this well wore a different face at night.

I turned onto my side and looked toward the well. In the dark I could just make out the faint outline of the stone wall. My eyes kept drifting to it, at the thought that Father might really be somewhere inside it. It would have been another well, not this one, of course — but even so, looking at this well, I found myself thinking of him.

Neither did my brother. I heard him fold and unfold that paper, over and over, all through the night.

The paper had gone thin along every crease. Just from the sound alone, I felt I could tell exactly which fold he was touching. That short rustle went on, at a steady interval, all through the night. I thought it sounded like my brother's own heartbeat.

I understood then that we were afraid of the same thing, but afraid of it differently. My brother was afraid of not finding Father. I was afraid we would find him and he wouldn't know us.

Somewhere off, a night bird cried once, briefly. When it stopped, the silence closed back in, complete. In that silence I felt as though my brother and I were the only two awake in the whole world — as if, while everyone else slept, only the two of us were holding on to the same fear, each in our own way.

I closed my eyes and tried to picture Father's face. Strangely, it wouldn't come clearly. I remembered the shape of his eyes, but the shape of his nose had gone blurry. That blurring frightened me all at once. Were we already starting to forget him too? I pushed the thought away quickly.

After a long time turning restlessly, I opened my eyes a little and looked over at my brother. He was sitting in the moonlight with the paper spread on his knee. He didn't seem to be reading the letters on it. He was just running his fingertips over it, again and again. I thought that gesture looked like someone touching Father's face.

Toward dawn, my brother spoke quietly.

"…Elpis, if we find him and he doesn't recognize us… will you still stay beside him?"

I couldn't answer right away. Only after thinking for a long time did I speak.

"…Yes," I said. "We can just tell him his name again. Even if it takes time."

At that, my brother let out a breath of relief for the first time.

"…Thank you," he said. That was all. But I could feel that those two words carried far more than themselves.

He reached over and gave my head a brief stroke. It was an awkward gesture. He'd never been someone who was good at that kind of thing. But in that moment, it came naturally. I felt, in that touch, that he was making a promise — to me, and probably to himself as well.

"…Brother," I said, quietly.

"…Yeah."

"…Can I ask you something too?"

"…Go ahead."

"…If we never find him," I said, "will you keep looking anyway?"

He said nothing for a long while. I thought he'd fallen asleep.

"…Yes," he finally said. "There's nothing else I know how to do."

That answer was sad, and yet strangely reassuring. My brother wasn't searching because of hope. He was searching because he knew no other way to go on living. I didn't know, then, that this too was a kind of hope.

After that we lay side by side without speaking anymore. The sky was slowly growing lighter. Watching that dawn light, I felt afraid of what else this night might teach us, once it ended.

In the morning, Speon asked.

He had gotten up before us and was building the fire back up. There was practice in the way he warmed water and set breakfast out for us. I tried to guess how many mornings like this he must have repeated, and gave up. It was a number beyond counting.

The morning air was cold. I felt the stiffness in my body as I threw off the blanket and stood — proof that I'd slept on hard ground. And yet, strangely, I had slept deeply. I thought I might even have slept more deeply than I ever had in Haven's soft beds.

I felt again, in that moment, that comfort of the body and comfort of the heart didn't always travel together. On that hard ground, I had heard news about Father, and had my first real conversation with my brother. Not one of Haven's beds could have ever given me a night like that.

"…What will you do now?"

"…We need to find another well," my brother said. "Do you know of one?"

"…I've heard of a few," Speon said. "But I haven't been able to check them all. I can't leave this place empty for long."

"…Why not?"

"…Because someone might come out while I'm gone," Speon said. "That person comes out remembering nothing at all. If no one's here then… they'll just wander this Wilderness alone."

Hearing that, I understood why Speon had kept this place so long. He wasn't looking for his own father. And still, he waited for someone every single day.

I thought that reason might, in its own way, be harder than searching for our own father. Searching for Father gave us a clear purpose. Speon had no purpose like that at all. He had only this — that someone had to be there.

"…So does that mean you can't leave even for a day?" I asked.

"…I can leave for a few days," Speon said. "But not long. If someone comes out and there's no one here, they don't even know where they are, and they end up wandering alone until they're lost. That's what I'm most afraid of."

"…Have you seen someone like that?" my brother asked.

Speon nodded. He said nothing more. I decided not to ask what memory that silence was holding.

His eyes seemed, for a moment, to be looking at something very far away. I felt, in that instant, that Speon was standing here in front of us and, at the same time, somewhere else entirely — some other time, some other place. Memory seemed able to put a person in two places at once, like that.

As if to change the subject, Speon got up and walked toward the well. He lowered the bucket again as he spoke.

"…So a day became two days, and two days became a month, and that's how I ended up staying here. At first I only meant to stay a little while."

"…How long has it been?" I asked.

Speon started to count on his fingers, then gave it up.

"…If it were something I could count, it wouldn't have felt this long," he said.

The dull thud of the bucket striking water rose up from far below. Speon waited for that sound before he began winding the rope back in. I noticed that even though he could probably have done it without waiting to hear it, he chose to wait anyway.

"…I feel like you'd know without listening for it," my brother said.

"…I do know," Speon said. "But I listen anyway. If I wind it in without listening, it just becomes a chore."

I thought about that a long while. Speon was working, every day, not to let anything he did at this well become merely a chore. The same bucket, the same rope, the same water, every single day. And every single time, he listened for that sound as if it were the first.

"…So does that mean you never find anyone?" I asked. "If you only ever stay here?"

Speon smiled a little. A lonely smile.

"…I already found the person I was looking for," he said. "Now my work is helping other people find what they're looking for."

I didn't know exactly what he meant. But something in the way he said it told me Speon had once been like us.

I looked at Speon's hand. It was the hand holding the bucket's rope. There was an old scar on the back of it. I wondered where it had come from. Like the scar on my own hand from Brick Hollow, I thought it must be carrying some story of its own.

Speon took a sip of water. The morning sun fell across the side of his face. I tried to guess his age from that face and failed. In some moments he looked young. In others, he looked like someone who had lived far longer than that.

"…Did you used to look for someone too?" I asked.

"…No," Speon said. "I didn't go looking. If anything, I ran. Because waiting frightened me. So now I'm learning how to wait."

"…Is waiting something you have to learn?" I asked. It seemed like an odd question, but I was genuinely curious.

"…Yes," Speon said. "I thought waiting just meant staying still. But it isn't that. Waiting is something you have to decide to do again, every single day. Waiting yesterday doesn't mean you'll wait today, on its own."

I didn't understand that exactly, either. But I saw my brother go silent at those words and look at Speon for a long time.

I felt, in that silence, that my brother was asking himself something. He had always been someone who moved forward. Waiting must have been a foreign idea to him. That day, for the first time, I watched him handling that foreign idea carefully, turning it over.

I felt, in that moment, that something was passing between my brother and Speon that I didn't know. Both of them had once run. One had stayed here, learning how to wait. The other was only just beginning to walk.

"…Do you regret running?" my brother asked suddenly.

Speon paused a moment at the question.

"…I don't regret it," he said slowly. "But I do think I should have stopped sooner. Running becomes a habit at some point. You keep running even when there's no reason left to."

My brother turned that answer over for a long time. I could tell he was hearing it as if it were about himself.

"…You ran too, though," I said carefully. "From Brick Hollow."

He looked at me. He looked startled.

"…That's true," he said slowly. "But I always thought that wasn't running away. I thought it was going toward something."

"…Do you still think that?" I asked.

He was quiet a long while before he answered.

"…I don't know," he admitted. "Maybe it was both."

Speon, listening to that exchange, spoke quietly.

"…Running and going forward start out as the exact same step," he said. "What tells them apart comes later — the moment you decide whether to keep taking that step, or to stop."

"…Then I still haven't decided," my brother said.

"…It's all right to take time deciding," Speon said. "It took me far longer than it's taking you."

Listening to that exchange, I felt that Speon wasn't trying to give my brother an answer — he was only standing beside him. I thought it was something like what Noa had done for us. The people of this Wilderness all seemed to treat each other this way. Not with answers, but by giving up their own side to stand beside you.

As he prepared to leave, my brother asked.

"…Have you ever seen a strange man around here? Working at a flowerbed, or… somewhere no one planted anything."

Speon stopped walking at that.

I saw his back go slightly rigid in that instant. The hand packing his bag had stopped too.

"…What does he look like?" Speon asked. His voice had changed.

My brother described him. Worn clothes, soil-stained hands, the habit of never giving his name.

Speon was quiet for a long time.

"…I've seen him too," he said. "Here, and elsewhere. But every time, I thought it was just coincidence."

"…When did you see him?" my brother asked.

"…The first time was a very long time ago," Speon said. "Back then I thought he was just a passing traveler. Then again before I came here, somewhere else. And here, a few times as well."

Listening, I felt a strange chill run down my spine. Until then I'd thought of the man at the flowerbed only as a stranger we'd happened to cross paths with. But looking at Speon's face, I understood it went further than that.

"…Do you know his name?" my brother asked.

"…No," Speon said. "I asked once. But he never answered. He just smiled."

That question felt strangely familiar to me. I felt as though I'd brushed past a smile like that somewhere before — in Brick Hollow, inside Haven, even somewhere along this road here. I couldn't remember exactly where.

"…What kind of smile was it?" I asked.

Speon thought for a moment.

"…Sad, and gentle, all at once," he said. "It's hard to explain. You'd have to see it yourself to understand."

Hearing that, I tried to picture the smiling face of the man at the flowerbed. I couldn't remember it exactly. But the moment Speon said it, something in me stirred with a strange familiarity.

"…What did he do?" I asked.

"…Nothing at all," Speon said. "He just was there. But strangely, wherever he'd been, something always looked different afterward."

"…What kind of different?" I asked.

Speon looked toward the well.

"…Once, I'd already packed everything to leave this place," he said. "That day, he came by and asked for a cup of water and drank it, and left. He said nothing at all. But after he'd gone, I found myself unpacking everything I'd just packed."

"…Why?" my brother asked.

"…I don't know," Speon said. "That's the strangest part of it."

My brother stood there a long while even after his pack was ready. I could see he wanted to ask more and was holding himself back. It was probably about the man at the flowerbed. But looking at Speon's face, he seemed to decide not to press it further.

Before we left that place, I looked once more at Speon's face. He had already returned to that familiar expression — the face of someone waiting for something. I recognized it as the very same face we'd seen the moment we first arrived. In the space of a single day, he had gone back to his place, and his face.

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. For the first time, I understood that the man at the flowerbed had passed through Brick Hollow, and Haven, and this well in the Wilderness, all of them. That night, I began, faintly, to feel that it was no coincidence at all.

(Continued in Chapter 21.)

Chapter 21. In Search of the Recorder

_Previously_ — Speon gave us a brief glimpse into why he keeps this well every day, and into his own past: "I didn't go looking. I ran. Now I'm learning how to wait." When he told us he had seen the man at the flowerbed in more than one place, I began, faintly, to feel it was no coincidence.

Morning fog lay low over the well. Inside it I could see Speon already up, pacing beside the water. It didn't look as though he'd slept at all.

The fog rose only to waist height. Speon's upper body seemed to float above it. I watched him a long while. He looked like someone walking on water, and at the same time like someone half sunk in it.

Every blade of grass held a bead of moisture. I swept my palm across it once. My hand was wet in an instant. It struck me as strange that water which existed nowhere else in the Wilderness gathered on this hill every single morning, on its own.

I think I understood, a little, why this hill was different. Wherever water comes up from somewhere deep, water gathers above it too. I couldn't tell whether that was because of the well, or because of Speon.

"…Go find someone," Speon said the next morning. "Her name is Mena. She's a Recorder."

My brother's hands stopped packing.

"…What does she record?" he asked.

"…The names of people who've been completed," Speon said. "At every well scattered across this world, she gathers up as many names as she can, from whoever's come out of them. I send her word myself, sometimes."

Hearing that, I understood for the first time how many more people like us there must be, scattered across this vast Wilderness. Speon wasn't the only one.

"…Is Mena a Well-Keeper too?" I asked.

"…No," Speon said. "Mena doesn't keep a well. She keeps names instead. Everyone keeps something different."

Listening to him, I thought there must be far more people in this world than we knew — people waiting for someone, keeping something, in ways we hadn't yet imagined.

"…How do you keep a name?" I asked. "A name isn't a thing you can hold."

Speon smiled a little at that.

"…I thought the same, at first," he said. "But a name really does disappear, if no one calls it anymore. A thing stays where it is whether anyone's looking at it or not. A name doesn't. So someone has to go on writing it down, and go on calling it."

"…If you write it down, does it stop disappearing?" I asked.

"…It slows down how fast it disappears," Speon said. "That's the whole of what any of us can do."

I remembered those words for a long time afterward. Not stopping it. Only slowing it. I understood then that everything these people did was of that kind. Not a work of winning, but of holding the line without giving ground.

"…Who is that record for?" my brother asked.

"…I didn't know myself, at first," Speon said. "But I know now. It's for the day someone who lost their name wants to hear it again. Someone has to be holding that name, when that day comes."

Hearing that, I thought of the paper with the COMPLETE stamp that my brother carried. He had already been doing that same work, alone. Mena was someone doing that same work on a far larger scale, for far longer.

At those words my brother reached up and pressed his hand briefly against the paper inside his coat, checking it was there. I couldn't count how many times I'd seen him do that by now. It had become as much a part of him as any of his own movements.

"…I guess I'm supposed to be doing something like this too," my brother murmured. It sounded less like something said to Speon than something said to himself.

"…You already are," Speon said. "That one piece of paper is where it starts."

Listening to that exchange, I felt my brother becoming, little by little, a different person. When we'd left Brick Hollow, he had been someone trying to leave behind evidence. Now he was becoming someone trying to keep something safe. I understood that the difference looked small, but was actually enormous.

Evidence was something meant to be held up in front of someone. Keeping was something you went on doing whether anyone saw it or not. My brother didn't seem to have noticed that about himself yet. I didn't bother telling him. Some changes, I think, are better noticed by the person going through them, in their own time.

"…Why does Mena do this?" I asked.

"…By her own account, she used to be someone who kept records, once before," Speon said. "I've heard she recorded one great story somewhere. I don't know exactly what it was myself."

Something in that pulled at me strangely. I was recording this story too. I didn't know it then — that someday I'd sit and talk with Mena about exactly that.

Up until then I hadn't even known what I myself was doing. Every night I only went back over, in my head, what I'd seen that day. Noa's face. The pebble Rachel had given me. The soil on the flowerbed man's hands. I did it because it felt like something I couldn't let myself forget. I didn't know that was a record.

From that moment I found myself curious about this person, Mena. I tried to imagine her face, her voice. In my imagination she always sat surrounded by stacks of paper.

"…A great story — what kind?" I asked.

"…I don't know exactly, either," Speon said. "But every time Mena talks about it, something changes in her eyes. Like something she's kept a very long time, and very carefully."

I held on to that. Somehow it felt like I would hear that story myself, someday.

"…Where are the people from that story now?" I asked.

Speon didn't answer right away. The fog had thinned a little by then.

"…Scattered," he said. "Each to wherever they needed to be."

"…Do they meet again?" I asked.

"…Sometimes," Speon said. "Very rarely."

His voice dropped just slightly as he said it. I could hear a longing inside that lowered voice. But it wasn't a sad longing. It sounded more like the longing of someone who knew something for certain.

"…Has Mena been here a long time too?" I asked.

"…Longer than me, I'd guess," Speon said. "She was already doing this work when I first came here. She's never once stopped, from then until now."

"…That's amazing," my brother said.

"…It's not amazing. It's just going on," Speon said. "The two look alike, but they're different."

"…What's the difference?" my brother asked.

"…Amazing is only amazing if people are watching," Speon said. "Going on is going on whether anyone's watching or not. Mena has been writing down names for more than twenty years, where no one was watching at all."

Before we left, Speon packed us dried bread and a water skin.

"…Where Mena lives is two days from here," Speon said. "There are marks along the way. Small notches cut into the stones. I don't know who cut them. But follow those notches and you won't lose the road."

He pressed the water skin into my hand and tied the strap tight himself. That touch felt familiar to me. It was close to the way Father used to pack our things.

The bread was more than we could eat. I understood it was the amount Speon had decided to give us by choosing to go a few days hungry himself.

"…This is too much," I said.

"…I'm here," Speon said. "There's water here. With water, a few days is nothing."

I knew that wasn't quite true, and I took it anyway. I'd already learned from Noa that refusing something can be the ruder choice.

Speon looked last at our shoes. The soles had worn thin.

"…Don't walk at night," he said. "You won't see the marks, and you'll lose the road. And if a quiet light passes overhead, that day, just lie down and don't move."

My brother's face went tight at that.

"…Does it come even this far?" he asked.

"…Not here," Speon said. "But it passes over the road you're taking."

"…Aren't you coming?" I asked.

"…I have to stay here," Speon said. He looked back at the well. "I never know who might come out."

Hearing that answer, I understood that Speon might never be able to leave this spot for the rest of his life. I couldn't decide, then, whether that was a sad thing or a beautiful one.

I looked at the well one last time. The water was still clear. The bottom still couldn't be seen. I found it hard to leave that spot, thinking that someday Father might rise up out of that water.

"…Elpis," my brother called. "We have to go."

I ran my hand once more along the stone wall before I finally started walking.

My brother turned back for one more look at the well.

"…If our father ever comes out here," he said, "could you tell him we came looking for him?"

Speon gripped his shoulder briefly at that.

"…I will," he said. "I promise."

As we left, Speon said one last thing.

"…Tell Mena Speon's still waiting. She'll understand what that means."

"…What does it mean?" my brother asked.

Speon smiled a little.

"…That's something only Mena and I know," Speon said. "You'll have words like that too, someday. Words you never have to explain to anyone."

We walked a few steps and looked back. Speon was still standing there, watching us. I held that image in my eyes. A single figure standing on a hill, beside a well. I already knew, in that moment, that the shape of him wouldn't leave me for a long time.

When I looked back again, much later, he was still there. We must have looked much smaller by then, but he didn't wave. He just stood. I understood that was a farewell that lasted longer than any wave could.

I learned, later, that Speon always stood like that until whoever was leaving had disappeared completely down the far side of the hill. He didn't only wait for the ones who came. He saw off, to the very end, the ones who went.

The first notch was on a rock not far from the well. My brother found it first and traced it with his fingertip.

"…Here," he said. "Look."

It might have been a small arrow, or it might have been nothing but a scratch. I stared at the shape a long while and still wasn't sure. But my brother had already turned his body toward it.

"…How do you know?" I asked.

"…I don't," he said. "But this direction feels right."

We walked the whole day, finding those notches one by one. On rocks, sometimes at the base of a dead tree, the marks appeared at steady intervals. Proof that someone, a very long time ago, had traveled this road again and again, faithfully.

Once, we went almost two hours without finding a single mark. My brother stopped and started retracing our steps.

"…I think we missed one," he said.

"…Should we turn back?" I asked.

"…No," he said. "Let's wait here. Once the sun sinks a little, the shadows will make it easier to see."

We sat with our backs against a rock and waited for the sun to sink. Strangely, that waiting felt peaceful to me. In Haven, we would have filed a request the moment we lost the road. And a road would have appeared right away. But it wouldn't have been the road we'd found for ourselves.

Sure enough, once the sun sank a little, a thin shadow fell across the side of the rock, and the notch showed itself. We both saw it at the same instant.

"…There," I said.

My brother laughed. It was the first time I'd seen him laugh in days.

Looking at those notches, I felt a strange comfort. Someone, before us, had found their way through this same hopeless Wilderness. We weren't entirely alone.

That night we slept huddled together beneath a large rock. We didn't light a fire. Speon's words wouldn't leave my mind.

Once it went dark, nothing was visible at all. That was the first time I'd ever known a night so black I couldn't tell whether my eyes were open or shut.

"…Brother, are you awake?" I asked.

"…Yeah."

"…I'm scared."

"…Me too," he said.

I liked that answer. Back in Brick Hollow, my brother had never once said he was scared. Not because he wasn't, I think, but because he thought he wasn't allowed to say it to me.

"…It helps, a little, saying it out loud," I said.

"…Yeah," he said. "Strange, isn't it."

That night I learned, for the first time, that fear can shrink without ever being solved. Just from someone else knowing it's there with you.

The sky was full of stars again that night. No quiet light passed over.

As we walked, my brother kept scanning for those notches, as if he couldn't afford to miss one. That carefulness in him felt unfamiliar to me. Back in Brick Hollow he had always been someone who walked looking only straight ahead. Now he walked with his eyes going back and forth between the ground, the rocks, the roots of trees. I thought that was a new way of walking he'd learned in this world.

By the afternoon of the second day, the notches had begun coming closer together. The shorter the gaps grew, the more I understood we were nearing something.

"…Someone must have come this way often, from here on," my brother said.

"…Or maybe," I said, "from here on, they thought it couldn't be missed."

My brother nodded at that.

As the sun went down, I traced one of the notches with my fingertip. It was cut deep. It didn't look like something carved in a hurry — it looked worked over, again and again, over time. I wondered whose hand had made it.

It hadn't been cut all at once. It had been added to, a little at a time, on every pass. I couldn't count how many times that person had traveled this road. But I could tell one thing for certain — that every single time, they'd been thinking of whoever would come after them.

I thought then, for the first time, about how lonely a thing it is to leave a mark for someone else. The one who leaves the mark almost never meets the one who follows it. And still, they leave it.

I set those words down as the last line of this chapter. That morning we left the well behind and started following those notches cut into stone after stone. I didn't know yet that they were the footprints of someone very old, marked all across this world, long before us.

(Continued in Chapter 22.)

Chapter 22. The House of Records

_Previously_ — Speon introduced the brother and sister to Mena, the Recorder. The work of gathering the names of completed people turned out to run in the very same direction as what my brother had been doing alone all along. We set out walking a two-day road, following the notches cut into every stone.

On the evening of the second day, we came to a low earthen house.

Two days of keeping my eyes fixed to the ground so I wouldn't lose the notches had left my neck stiff. I only lifted my head the moment that earthen house came into view. The sky had already gone red.

It looked less like a house than a storeroom. The walls were earth, and the roof was dried grass pressed flat and weighted down. There was only one window, and from it came a very faint light. It was the first light of another person's making that I had seen in two days.

There was a small sign by the door. The House of Records.

The letters had been carved into the wooden board with the point of a knife. The strokes ran deep. I touched the letters with my fingertips. The way they'd been cut looked like the notches on the stones.

Seeing that sign, I felt oddly at ease. This world had places that erased names, and it had places that wrote them down and kept them. Just knowing that both kinds of place existed gave me, in that moment, a small measure of comfort.

Beside the door stood a row of small flowerpots. It struck me as strange that anything was being grown at all, in this parched Wilderness. I guessed that someone gave those pots water, every day, without fail.

One of the pots had already dried up and died. I wondered why it hadn't been cleared away. Even that dead pot felt like some kind of record kept by this house. Here, it seemed, not even the dead were simply thrown out.

I could feel the heat the dried grass roof had held all day slowly leaking back out of it. Feeling that warmth cool against the back of my hand, I thought that this house, too, seemed to breathe out its day like something alive.

The whole way here, my brother had barely spoken. I knew what he was afraid of. No answer at all, or an answer that turned out worse — he kept weighing those two, back and forth, without settling on either.

I could read that weighing plainly on his face. I said nothing, the whole way. I knew that no words of mine could stand in for that scale he was carrying.

I walked a little ahead of him and looked around, so that at least I wouldn't disturb his thinking. Dust drifted a little on the wind. Breathing it in, I realized that I, too, had begun to be afraid of what might be waiting on the other side of that door.

My brother lifted his hand at the door, then lowered it. Then lifted it again.

"…Knock," I said.

"…I know," my brother said. And still he didn't knock.

That was the first time I truly understood him. He had come all this way to find an answer, and now that he was standing in front of the door, the answer itself had begun to frighten him. Every step from Brick Hollow to Haven, from Haven to the well, from the well to here — all of it had come to a stop in front of this one door.

"…Let's knock together," I said.

I laid my hand over the back of his. Together, we knocked.

A woman opened the door. She looked much older than us. There was ink on her fingers, as if she'd spent her life with something in her hand, writing.

Her eyes were sharp. But sharp didn't mean cold. I felt, instead, that those were eyes trained a very long time to look at something exactly as it was.

She didn't open the door wide. She opened it just enough to show her face, and looked past us first, into the dark behind our backs. She hadn't looked at us — she'd looked behind us. From that order alone, I understood that this woman, too, was used to being followed.

"…Who's there?" the woman asked.

"…Speon sent us," my brother said. "Are you Mena?"

Something shifted, faintly, in the woman's face at that name.

"…That's me," she said. "What did Speon say?"

"…That he's still waiting," I said.

Mena heard that and closed her eyes for a moment. She said nothing. But I felt something very old, in that silence.

"…Is he all right?" Mena asked. Her voice trembled slightly.

"…Yes," my brother said. "He watches over that well every single day."

At that, Mena gave a small laugh. Something like tears was mixed into it.

"…That sounds like him," Mena said.

She opened the door a little wider as she said it. I felt, in that motion, that she had already decided to let us in. A single name — Speon's — had been enough to open that door.

"…Come in," Mena said. "This isn't a conversation for standing outside."

Mena led the way, lighting a lamp as she went. The light caught the ink stains on her fingers. I could tell that stain hadn't come from a day or two of work.

As she closed the door, Mena dropped the latch into place. Then she reached over and pulled the cloth across the window a little tighter.

"…Is it all right if the light shows?" I asked.

"…It's all right if it shows a little," Mena said. "But only a little is good. Too dark, and no one can find their way here at all. Too bright, and it draws the ones you'd rather it didn't."

I remembered that for a long time afterward. Every person in this world who waited for someone seemed to live like that — never able to hide completely, and never able to be seen completely, either.

Mena brought us inside. The inside was nothing but paper. Every wall had shelves, and every shelf was crowded with paper bearing names.

The room had a smell all its own. Old paper and ink, and beneath it a faint smell of melted candle wax. Breathing it in, I tried to guess how long it must have taken to fill this room like this. I couldn't begin to guess.

Some of the shelves reached all the way to the ceiling. I wondered how old the papers up there must be. I doubted Mena's hand alone could reach that high — until I saw the old ladder standing in the corner, and had my answer.

A small piece of wood hung from every shelf. Each one had letters carved into it too. I was about to ask what they marked when Mena spoke first, as if she'd already noticed the question in my face.

"…Those are well names," Mena said. "One shelf, one well."

I tried counting the shelves. There were more than twenty. I gave up partway through.

"…This many?" my brother asked.

"…This is only the wells I've had word from," Mena said. "I don't even know how many I haven't."

Hearing that answer, my legs went weak. More than twenty shelves. And that wasn't even all of them. That was the first day I felt, in my body and not just my mind, how enormous this search really was.

I walked between the shelves, running my eyes over the names carved into the little wooden markers. Most of them were names I didn't know. But strangely, that unfamiliarity felt heavy. I thought that behind every single name, there must have been some Brick Hollow of its own.

One shelf, alone, stood nearly empty. Only a few papers on it.

"…Why is that one empty?" I asked.

Mena looked over at it.

"…That's a well with no one keeping it," Mena said. "No word ever comes. Because no one's watching."

I looked at that empty shelf for a long time. Paper missing didn't mean no one had come out of that well. It meant no one had been there to see them.

"…Are all of these the names of completed people?" I asked.

"…Only the ones I've been able to gather," Mena said. "I haven't gotten them all. This world is too wide."

I ran my eyes down the shelves, one by one. The handwriting differed slightly from paper to paper. I understood that these weren't Mena's own hand copying names — they were the traces of word carried to her by many different people, and written down exactly as it came. Some papers were old and faded. Others looked as if the ink hadn't even dried yet.

"…Who brings you these names?" I asked.

"…Anyone passing through," Mena said. "Well-keepers, traders, people on the run. Whoever brings me a name, I write it down."

"…What if it's a lie?" my brother asked.

Mena glanced at him for a moment at that question. It wasn't an unkind look.

"…Then I write it down as a lie," Mena said. "The moment I start deciding what's true, people stop bringing me the names they're unsure of. And then the real ones disappear right along with them."

I thought that one line explained this whole room. This room didn't hold only what was certain. It held the uncertain too, all of it, right alongside the rest. Haven's ledger kept only what was certain. That's why nothing at all was left, in that city.

My brother looked over the papers and asked, carefully.

"…Could we look for our father's name too?"

Mena looked at him for a long moment.

"…Tell me the name," Mena said. "I'll look."

My heart pounded so hard in that instant that it hurt. Listening to my brother say Father's name aloud, I felt that even after all this time, that name still sat perfectly formed in his voice.

I said the name over too, silently, inside myself. I didn't say it aloud. But even that alone was enough to make my eyes burn. I realized, right then, just how many times I must have repeated that name to myself, just so I wouldn't forget it.

I understood later that repeating a name like that isn't only a way of remembering the person. It's also a way of telling yourself, over and over, that they still exist.

Mena wrote the name down on the paper. I saw that her fingertips were trembling, just slightly.

"…Is it a common name?" Mena asked.

"…No," my brother said. "In Brick Hollow, there was only one Father."

"…Then it might be a little easier to find," Mena said.

Mena checked the paper once more and asked.

"…Anything else — a mark on him somewhere, a way he walked?"

My brother thought for a moment before answering.

"…One finger on his left hand is shorter than the rest," he said. "He got hurt carrying bricks."

Mena wrote that down small, beside the name.

"…This kind of thing helps," Mena said. "People forget names before they forget bodies."

As she said it, Mena underlined that detail on the page once more, to mark it. Her fingers moved with familiar ease. I could tell, from that gesture alone, that she had taken down details like this more times than could be counted.

"…Have you heard, all this time, of anyone with a short finger like that?" my brother asked, carefully.

Mena shook her head.

"…Not yet," she said. "But I'll start looking, from now on."

"…Why do you do this?" my brother asked, suddenly.

Mena set down her pen.

"…A long time ago, I wrote down something very important," Mena said. "Back then I thought — what's the use of writing it down? What does writing change? But I learned, later. Only what gets written down stays."

"…What did you write?" I asked.

Mena smiled. She didn't answer.

"…That's not a story for today," Mena said. "Right now, your father's name comes first."

She said that and rose from her seat. I saw her back hesitate for just a moment. Setting her own story aside didn't seem to come easily to her, either. But she turned at once and walked toward the shelves.

Mena went back and forth among the shelves for a long time. Only the sound of turning paper filled the room.

My brother stood in one place the whole time, without moving. I sat beside him and took his hand. His hand was cold. I hoped mine might warm it, even a little.

Every time the candle flame flickered, the shadows of the papers on the shelves flickered with it against the wall. Watching those shadows, I thought that every single paper there must have once been someone's entire world.

Each time Mena turned a page, she touched the tip of her finger lightly to her tongue first. Then she ran her eyes down it, top to bottom, one sheet at a time. She skipped nothing. I found that thoroughness almost frightening. This woman was rereading hundreds of names, right now, for the sake of one person — our father.

Sometimes her hand paused. Every time it did, my own heart stopped along with it. But then Mena would turn the page again. Through however many times that pause and turn repeated, I hoped and then collapsed, over and over, that same number of times.

I don't know how much time passed. I couldn't breathe properly, the whole time it did.

That night, I understood for the first time that waiting could hurt the body itself. My shoulders stiffened, my jaw ached, my fingertips went cold. I thought it was almost fortunate that there was nowhere to file a complaint about that pain. That pain was the only road there was to Father.

At last Mena stopped in front of one shelf. She turned back to us. Her face was complicated.

"…It's here," Mena said. "But it isn't the answer you're hoping for."

Mena carried the paper back to us the way you'd hold something against your chest. Her steps were slower than before. I felt, in that slowness, that she was still choosing, even now, how to give us these words.

She didn't set the paper down on the table. She kept holding it. It seemed she thought that the moment she set it down, we would read it ourselves. I could only see the back of the paper. Something was written on that side too, but the ink showed through too faintly to read.

The lamp wick sputtered faintly as it burned. Other than that sound, there was nothing. In that stillness, I understood that Mena was trying, right now, to be gentle with us. She was someone who knew how to deliver bad news carefully.

"…Sleep here tonight," Mena said. "I'll tell you in the morning, once your minds are clearer."

"…We want to hear it now," my brother said. His voice had cracked.

Mena looked at him for a long time.

She turned the lamp wick up a little higher. The room brightened, just slightly. I hoped that one small bit of extra light might help us bear whatever we were about to hear.

"…All right," Mena said. "Then sit down."

I set that line down as the last sentence of this chapter. In that moment, I learned that Father really was alive, somewhere in this world. And yet, until I learned what "not the answer you're hoping for" meant, I had to endure that entire night not knowing.

(Continued in Chapter 23.)

Chapter 23. An Answer We Didn't Expect

_Previously_ — At the end of a two-day road, the brother and sister reached Mena's House of Records. Mena found Father's name, but closed the chapter with the words, "It isn't the answer you're hoping for."

The lamp burned low. Mena settled back into her seat and unfolded the paper once more. I could feel that her hands were moving more slowly now than before.

"…About a month ago," Mena said, unfolding the paper, "I got word that someone who might be your father came out of a well to the north."

"…He's alive?" my brother asked, urgent.

"…He was. At least then," Mena said.

I remember feeling, in that moment, that all three of us had stopped breathing at once. The lamp's shadow trembled faintly against the wall. The room was so quiet that even that small tremor felt loud.

"…North?" I asked.

"…North of here," Mena said. "Several days on foot. I haven't been there myself, so I can't say exactly."

At that, my brother had already leaned forward. I gripped his arm lightly, afraid he might stand and walk out right then.

"…I haven't finished," I said.

My brother looked at me. His eyes already held half a night's walking in them.

I already felt the unease in that word "then." I knew it meant this story wasn't finished.

My brother seemed unable to breathe properly where he sat. I saw his hands clenched tight on his knees. His knuckles had gone white.

Mena studied our faces and went on, carefully.

"…Can I keep going?" Mena asked. "Once you hear it, there's no taking it back."

My brother looked at me for a moment. I nodded. He nodded too.

"…Please, keep going," he said.

I felt, in his voice just then, both fear and longing at once. I felt the same. I wanted to know. And at the same time, I felt that knowing might break me.

Mena closed her eyes for a moment, then opened them again. I felt her steadying herself for this. Delivering news like this didn't seem to come easily to her, either.

I learned later how rare a kindness it was, that Mena warned us first, that night, that there was no taking it back. Haven had never once asked us that. That city always resolved things first, and told us afterward that this was what we'd wanted.

The lamp wick crackled faintly and flickered. I flinched even at that small sound. Everything inside me had drawn so taut that it answered to the smallest change in that room.

"…This is what the well-keeper there told me," Mena said. "The moment he came out, he kept counting something. On his fingers, without a sound. He didn't know himself why he was counting."

"…At first he didn't say anything at all," Mena added. "Not his name, not where he'd come from — no answer to anything. He just kept folding and unfolding his fingers."

I tried to picture that, then made myself stop. Even the thought of it hurt.

But the picture had already started forming. A man sitting at a strange well, folding and unfolding his fingers. No one knowing who he was, and he himself not knowing either. I closed my eyes and opened them again, several times, trying to erase that image. It wouldn't go.

"…What was he counting?" I asked. I already thought I knew the answer.

My voice came out shaking. Tears had already started rising before I even heard it.

"…I don't know," Mena said. "But at some point, in the middle of counting, he stood up all at once and walked off somewhere. No one could stop him."

Hearing that, I felt tears coming. I knew it was the brick count. Father had forgotten his name, forgotten our faces, and still that number went on counting itself, as if it had been carved into his body.

I remembered the way Father used to count that number every evening in Brick Hollow. Even when his fingertips split and bled, he never let that count slip. I hadn't understood, back then, why it mattered so much to him. I understand now. That number was proof that he'd survived one more day.

If he lost count, he started over from the beginning. As a child, that used to frustrate me enough that I asked him once — couldn't he just count roughly? Father had laughed and told me: count roughly, and the whole day turns rough with it.

I had gone a long time without remembering those words. And yet here, in Mena's room, under the lamplight, they came back to me whole, word for word.

I found it strangely steadying, that I hadn't forgotten Father's voice. His face kept blurring on me, again and again — but some turns of phrase stayed perfectly clear. I thought that might be what real memory actually is. Not everything, but a handful of clear pieces.

I understood, in that moment, that the Completion House had taken Father's name and his face. But it hadn't been able to take that one habit — his refusal to live a day roughly. I think that was because it lived in the deepest place in him.

"…Three thousand," my brother said, low. "He must have counted all the way to three thousand. And then started over from the beginning again."

Mena didn't ask him to repeat it. I understood that she'd already guessed what it meant.

Mena looked back down at the paper and touched a line near the bottom with her fingertip.

"…It says here," Mena said. "'Stops at three thousand each day.' The well-keeper watched for three days and sent this back."

"…Three days?" I asked.

"…Three days," Mena said. "He didn't even eat. He just kept counting. And strangely, he always stopped at exactly three thousand."

My brother let out a short, broken laugh. It wasn't really a laugh. It was another sound that only came out sounding like one.

"…That was a day's quota," my brother said. "Three thousand bricks was a day's quota. If you didn't reach it, there was no food that night."

Mena set down her pen. She said nothing.

The room went very quiet. Quiet enough that I thought I could hear even the trembling of the lamp's flame. I sat close beside my brother and held onto his arm. He didn't move at all.

After a long while, my brother spoke in a very low voice.

"…He wasn't counting us."

Mena looked up at that.

"…What do you mean?" Mena asked.

"…He counts bricks," my brother said. "He doesn't count us. Because we're not a number."

Hearing that, the breath caught in my throat. My brother was saying that truth right now in the way that hurt him most.

"…That isn't because he loved you less," Mena said quietly. "The body remembers numbers. The heart remembers love. What those people took from him was the heart's side."

My brother said nothing back.

"…And after that?" my brother asked. His voice had cracked.

"…There's no word after that," Mena said. "The well-keeper hasn't seen him since. He must still be somewhere. But exactly where — I don't know that either."

"…No one went after him?" I asked.

"…Someone did," Mena said. "But they lost him. It was night, and he walked faster than they expected."

Even in that brief moment, my brother's voice was already trying to fix a direction. I thought that was exactly like him.

"…Which way did he go?" my brother asked.

"…That's not exact either," Mena said. "But the well-keeper wrote this much: 'Not toward the sunset.'"

"…That's all?" my brother asked.

"…That's all," Mena said. "I'm sorry. I can only write down what's given to me. The moment I start filling in what isn't there, this whole room stops being a room anyone can trust."

Hearing that, I found strange comfort in the fact that Father had been hurrying. Even with his name forgotten, his steps had still been urgent. I wanted to believe that meant he was still moving toward something.

My brother sank down where he stood. I sat beside him and took hold of his shoulder.

He made no sound at all. But I could feel his shoulder trembling, very faintly. I thought, in that moment, that my brother looked like someone who had forgotten how to cry out loud. From Brick Hollow until now, he had always wept only inward, like this.

I thought, just then, of Haven's ledger. The ledger took in your weeping and resolved it. But my brother had never once filed his. He had never handed his weeping over to anyone. And so all of it was still inside him, every bit of it, still there.

"…Was this the answer you didn't expect?" I asked Mena.

Mena nodded.

"…Coming with no idea whether someone's alive or dead can hurt less than learning they're alive, and losing them again," Mena said. "But I don't tell lies."

"…Why not?" my brother asked. It wasn't resentment. He seemed to genuinely want to know.

"…Because Haven already does false comfort perfectly well," Mena said. "I started this work because I wanted to do something different from that."

"…Doesn't it wear on you?" I asked. "Having to deliver news like this every time?"

Mena looked at me for a long moment.

"…It does," Mena admitted. "But I think it's better than no one delivering it at all. That's what I believe, and it's why I keep doing this."

Mena looked down at her hands for a moment. They were covered in ink stains.

"…I don't even know how many times these hands have written down news like this," Mena said. "But it's just as hard every time. I never get used to it."

I found that answer strangely comforting. I thought it was a good thing that Mena hadn't gone numb to this work.

I thought, just then, of Haven's Administrator. That man was always smiling. He had never once said this was hard. I used to think that was strength. Now I know better. That wasn't strength. It was only that he, too, had already gone numb to it.

"…Wouldn't it be easier to get used to it?" I asked. "You'd hurt less."

"…If I got used to it, I'd have to stop doing this work," Mena said. "News delivered by someone who's gone numb ends up sounding exactly like the ledger's."

I thought about that all through that night. The same words, spoken by someone who hurts along with you as they say them, become different words entirely. That was exactly the thing Haven could never do. That city's answers were all correct. But not a single person there had ever hurt, speaking one of them.

Saying that, Mena carefully folded the paper and held it out toward my brother.

"…Take this," Mena said. "You might need it again someday."

My brother took the paper with trembling hands. He folded it away beside the COMPLETE-stamped paper he already carried.

"…This is the original, though," my brother said.

"…I can copy it out again," Mena said. "Copying is the thing I do best."
