I understood, from that answer, that this was someone who could hand over the thing she treasured most without a second thought. It was only that day that I understood why giving away a single sheet of paper, in a room filled with nothing but paper, could mean so much.

Mena stepped quietly out of the room, as if trying not to disturb us. She left only one lamp burning and put out the rest. In that dim light, I sat beside my brother.

Outside, I could hear the wind brushing against the earthen walls of the house. Listening to it, I thought that somewhere out there, beyond this house, Father might be standing in that same wind right now — with no name, not knowing us at all.

Thinking that, my mind wandered somewhere strange. Was he cold, right now? Had he eaten? Did he have somewhere to sleep? I found it strangely fortunate that I could still worry about things like that. You can't worry about things like that for someone who's dead.

That was the first time I truly felt, all over again, how cruel Haven's "resolving" had been. There, a night like this could never have happened at all. The pain disappeared before it even had the chance to hurt. I thought that this pain, right now, felt more human, somehow, than that smooth, seamless resolution ever had.

I sat beside my brother a long while that night. He didn't cry. But I could see that his face had come apart more thoroughly than crying ever could.

"…Brother," I said. "He's alive, though."

"…Yeah," my brother said, after a long pause. "He's alive."

"…Say that again," my brother said.

"…He's alive," I said.

My brother closed his eyes. I understood that he was trying to take those three words and press them down inside himself. It was something we hadn't once been able to say with certainty, since we left Brick Hollow.

"…And he's still counting bricks," I said. "That's proof he's still Father, isn't it. Even with his name gone, Father is still inside him."

My brother couldn't answer that. I understood it wasn't because he had nothing to say back — it was because his throat had closed.

At those words, my brother lifted his head and looked at me for the first time. I saw his eyes brimming with tears. He tried to hold them back. But this time, it seemed, he couldn't.

My brother covered his face with both hands. At first there was no sound. Then his shoulders heaved once, hard, and after that, the sound came. It was the first time in my life I had ever heard my brother cry.

I cried too. We held onto each other and cried for a long time. I don't even know when the lamp went out.

Mena didn't come in. I knew she was there, just beyond the door. But she didn't come in to comfort us, and she didn't tell us to stop. She simply stayed there.

A long while later, two bowls of water were quietly pushed under the door. I thought that was the smallest and, at once, the largest thing Mena knew how to say. Asking nothing, demanding nothing — just water, set there in case we grew thirsty.

I still think, even now, that it was one of the greatest gifts I received in this world. No one filed our weeping. No one resolved it. Someone simply stayed on the other side of a door, until we had finished crying.

I set that down as the last sentence of this chapter. That day, I cried for the first time. It was the first time since we'd left Brick Hollow. My brother cried along with me, following me into it. That night, we wept together for the father who had forgotten his own name.

(Continued in Chapter 24.)

Chapter 24. Northward

_Previously_ — Mena brought news of Father. A month ago he had come out alive from a well in the north, but with his name and face forgotten, and had vanished somewhere in the middle of counting three thousand bricks. For the first time since leaving Brick Hollow, the brother and sister wept together.

The next morning, my eyes were swollen. So were my brother's. And yet, strangely, my body felt lighter.

Morning sunlight was coming in through the window. Watching it, I learned for the first time how strange a morning can feel after a whole night of crying. Nothing in the world had changed. And yet I felt like an entirely different person.

I understood then, for the first time, that tears didn't erase anything. They only lifted away, by their own weight, what had been piling up inside.

Mena's house had one small window in its wall. I watched dust drift slowly through the shaft of light coming in through it. It was a peaceful morning, as if nothing at all had happened. That peace felt strange to me, so different from last night's tears. But that the two of them could exist inside the same day — that, too, I was learning for the first time.

The moment my brother opened his eyes, he checked the paper stamped COMPLETE again. It had become a habit by then. But that day was a little different. He looked at the paper for a long time, then folded it carefully and tucked it into his innermost pocket.

Something had gone out of my brother's face overnight. It took me a long while of thinking before I understood what it was. It was the tension that had clung to his face ever since Brick Hollow. His anger hadn't disappeared. What had ended was not knowing where to put it.

"…It's not evidence anymore. Not a way of holding onto his name, either," my brother said, seeing my expression. "Now this is… proof that we made it this far."

Hearing that, I felt all over again how completely my brother had changed in these last few days. When we left Brick Hollow, he had been a person moved only by anger. The paper in his hand now was no longer evidence of that anger.

"…Brother," I asked. "Did you sleep last night?"

"…A little," he said. "Crying made me sleepy."

He said it as if it were nothing. But to me it wasn't nothing at all. It was the first time my brother had ever spoken of his own crying so plainly.

I realized something strange then. In Haven, we had never once cried, and never once truly slept. I hadn't known that those two things were bound together.

I reached out and touched the dust settled on the windowsill. I watched it scatter in the shaft of light, then slowly find its place again. Watching that small movement, I thought that something inside us, too, must have scattered like that last night, and slowly settled back into place.

Mena asked while setting out breakfast for us.

Bread and dried fruit sat in a small bowl. Looking at that plain breakfast, I found it strange that even in this house, someone made a meal like this every morning. In a room crowded with paper, the smell of cooking felt like an odd kind of comfort.

"…What will you do now?"

"…We'll go north," my brother said. "To that well, and then follow whatever path Father walked from there."

His voice was different from last night. Sorrow still clung to it, but something unshaken had settled inside it now. Hearing it, I knew my brother had worked something all the way through, sometime in the night.

"…And if you can't find him?" Mena asked. It wasn't a test. She seemed to genuinely want to know.

My brother thought for a moment.

"…Then while we're looking, we'll tell everyone we meet about him," my brother said. "That way, little by little, more people will know Father's name."

Mena looked at him a very long time at that answer.

"…Do you know something?" Mena said. "That's exactly what I do. You just gave me the same answer I would have given."

My brother's face didn't know how to answer that.

Mena nodded. She didn't try to stop us.

"…I thought as much," Mena said.

"…Won't it be dangerous?" I asked.

"…It's dangerous," Mena said honestly. "But you're people who already knew that much when you left."

At that, my brother smiled for the first time — the first smile I'd seen from him in days.

"…Take whatever you need," Mena said. "I've heard it's colder to the north than here. Take another blanket, too."

"…Thank you," I said.

Mena folded the blanket and laid it on top of my pack as she spoke.

"…This is nothing," Mena said. "Not compared to the road you're about to carry."

My brother asked while repacking his bag.

"…Do you know if there's another well anywhere along our way?"

Mena thought a moment, then nodded.

"…I've heard of one," Mena said. "I don't know exactly where. But if you keep heading north, people will tell you. This world is more connected than you'd think."

"…How?" I asked.

"…By rumor. By footprints. Sometimes just by chance," Mena said. "You two got here that way yourselves."

"…Doesn't it seem like there's too much chance in it, though?" my brother asked.

Mena's hands stopped for a moment at that.

"…I used to think that too," Mena said. "But when there's too much chance, at some point it starts to feel strange, calling it chance at all."

"…Then what do you call it?" my brother asked.

"…I still haven't found a name for it," Mena said. "You two might find one before I do."

I let that conversation pass by, at the time. Now I think it was the most important part of this chapter.

After that, Mena helped us pack without another word. I felt that silence carrying forward the full weight of what we'd just said. Sometimes not answering is the more honest answer.

Before we left, Mena called me aside, alone.

She led me to one side of the room, to the shelves standing thickest with papers. Standing among all those names, I felt, strangely, at ease. I knew that every paper here had once been someone's everything — and it comforted me that all those everythings hadn't been thrown away, but gathered here like this instead.

"…I heard you've been keeping a record," Mena said. "Speon told me."

"…I just write down what happens," I said. "It's nothing special."

"…That's exactly what makes it special," Mena said. "That's how I started too. Just writing down what happened."

Mena handed me an old pen.

Its handle had gone smooth from long use. I took it in my hand and wondered how many years it must have carried stories out of someone's grip.

The tip had worn down and shortened. Looking at that wear, I thought of the old man's cane handle at the tent settlement. Everything old in this world wore down like that. In Haven, nothing ever wore down at all.

"…Was this his, originally?" I asked.

"…Yes," Mena said. "I think your hand needs it more than mine does now."

"…Keep writing," Mena said. "Someday, your father might need this record too."

Taking that in, I felt tears rising again, strangely. I understood then, for the first time, that what I'd been doing wasn't so different from what my brother had been doing. He had tried to keep hold of a name. I had tried to keep hold of a story. We had only been holding the same thing with different hands.

"…Doesn't it frighten you?" Mena asked. "Keeping a record isn't just writing down the good things. You have to write down days like today too."

"…It frightens me," I said. "But if I don't write it down, today just disappears. That frightens me more."

Mena looked at me a long time at that. Then, for the first time, she gave a real smile.

"…That's it," Mena said. "That's exactly why I can't let this work go. You're so much younger than me, and you already know that."

I didn't know how to answer that, so I only held the pen tight against my chest.

"…I'll ask you one thing," Mena said. "When you write, don't write down only what you've understood. Leave what you don't understand written as not-understood. More often than not, that's what turns out to matter most, later."

"…Why?" I asked.

"…What you've understood has already become yours," Mena said. "What you haven't understood still belongs to someone else. You have to leave it as it is, or it can't become real later."

I've kept that from that day on. That's why so much in this ledger remains what I never understood.

We left Mena's house that afternoon.

At the door, Mena hugged each of us once. It was a short, awkward embrace. I didn't get the sense she was someone who did this kind of goodbye often. And yet the hug was sincere.

"…Tell Speon," Mena said, last of all. "That I'm still waiting too."

My brother said he'd remember it exactly.

Hearing it, I felt again that something very old stood between those two. I found myself wondering, then, why Speon and Mena didn't wait together, in the same place. I thought I'd learn that answer too, someday.

"…Can I ask you one thing?" I said.

"…Go on."

"…Why do you two live apart?"

Mena rested a hand on the doorframe at the question.

"…If everyone guarding the same thing gathers in one place," Mena said, "then no one standing between them ever gets found."

I turned that answer over for a long time. The two of them weren't apart. They were standing in different places.

I wondered, then, if the whole world might be built that way. People waiting, each in a different place, each in a different way. Maybe it was precisely because they never all gathered in one place that more people, somewhere, could find their names again.

We left the earthen house behind and turned north. The sun already hung close to the middle of the sky. I looked back once. Mena stood at her door, watching us go. It looked exactly like the way she must have watched Speon leave.

The road climbed almost at once. The ground grew rougher and rougher, and far off on the horizon I could just make out the blurred ridge of low mountains. Looking at that ridgeline, I felt that our journey had entered an entirely new stage.

I thought of the first day we left Brick Hollow. That day, we had only been running. Now, our direction had a reason behind it. I understood that the difference made it a completely different kind of walking, even if, from the outside, it looked like the exact same steps.

As he walked, my brother checked once more the two papers in his hand — the one stamped COMPLETE, and the one Mena had given him. He laid them together, neat and even, and tucked them back inside his coat.

"…This is where it really begins," my brother said. It sounded half like something said to himself, half like something said to me.

"…Yes," I said. "I think so too."

We walked without speaking for a while after that. I noticed the earth underfoot slowly changing color — from red to gray. I thought that change was proof we were leaving the Wilderness behind.

The wind changed too. The Wilderness's wind had been dry and stinging. What blew down from the north carried something damp in it. That dampness felt strange against my skin, and I rubbed my arms more than once. Breathing that scent in, I thought that Father must have felt this same wind. A month ago. Before us.

That thought comforted me, strangely. We were following Father. Even if we never found him, one thing was certain — we were walking through the same wind.

As I walked, I touched the pen tucked in my coat once. Rachel's pebble was still there too. I counted, one by one, everything this journey had given us. One pebble, one pen, two papers. And a handful of people's names.

Father had counted bricks. From that day, I decided I would count people.

As the sun began to sink, my brother stopped and looked back. I turned and looked back too. The road we'd traveled stretched away into the distance, hazy and far. Haven. The tent settlement. That well. The House of Records. It felt strange to think all those places now lay behind us.

I counted how, at every one of those places, someone had given us something. Noa had given us a place. Rachel had given us a stone. The old man had given us a rumor. Speon had given us water. Mena had given us a name. We had walked this whole way receiving, again and again, without ever repaying a thing.

"…Will we ever be able to pay it all back?" I asked.

My brother thought a moment.

"…Probably not," he said. "But maybe we can just give it to someone else instead. That seems to be how everyone here does it."

That my brother was the one who said that was, to me, the most surprising thing that whole day. In Haven, he had always been someone who calculated — what you filed, and what you got back for it. But that day, for the first time, he accepted a debt he couldn't repay, without doing the math on it at all.

"…We've come so far," I said.

"…Yeah," he said. "But there's still more road ahead."

"…Aren't you scared?" I asked.

He looked at me and smiled. A very small smile, but a clear one.

"…I'm scared," he said. "But it's all right now, because I'm not scared alone."

At that, I took my brother's hand. He took mine back. We started walking again, just like that, hand in hand.

Before the sun went fully down, its last light stretched our shadows long across the ground. I looked down at the two of them, lying side by side, reaching out ahead of us. When we'd been running, our shadows had always seemed to chase us from behind. Now they pointed the way before we did.

I set down here the last sentence of this Part. We began walking north. Whether we could find Father, and whether he would know us even if we did — none of it was certain. But that day, for the first time, I saw something close to certainty on my brother's face. It wasn't the certainty of someone who had every answer. It was only the certainty of someone who had a reason to keep walking.

(Continued in Part Three.)

The Weeping Ledger, Part Three

The Northward Road

Chapter 25. Following the Trail

_Previously_ — After Mena brought word of Father, the brother and sister wept together for the first time since leaving Brick Hollow. Elpis learned that tears erase nothing — they only lift the weight, by their own weight — and the two of them set out north, following Father's trail.

The road north was rougher than the Wilderness had ever been.

The land grew stranger with every mile. Brick Hollow's soil had been red and heavy. Here the dirt ran pale and light, so light that a single gust erased our footprints within minutes. That frightened me in a way I couldn't quite explain. If our own tracks disappeared that easily, then any tracks someone might follow toward us would disappear just as easily. But it also meant the opposite — the tracks we were following could vanish before we ever found them.

The grass thinned out, then stopped altogether. The ground hardened underfoot. My brother kept walking anyway, every day. I walked behind him, and every night I wrote down what had happened that day.

That was the first time I really used the writing tool Mena had given me. My hand didn't know it yet — the letters kept slanting sideways. I kept writing anyway.

There was no shade on that road. By day the sun sat straight over our heads and beat down without mercy; by night the wind cut clear to the bone in the opposite direction. I was startled every time by how far apart those two temperatures stood. It was a cold Brick Hollow had never taught us.

Within days, the sole of my brother's shoe began to split. He tried to hide that from me too. I only found out because I noticed him limping.

"…What's wrong with your shoe?" I asked.

"…Nothing," my brother said. His usual answer. I didn't push. Pushing would only have gotten me the same nothing, over and over, until I gave up.

That evening, while he wasn't looking, I wrapped a strip of cloth around the broken sole. The next morning he saw it and said nothing. He just put the shoe on and walked. I understood that silence as his way of saying thank you.

The cloth wore through again within three days. Each time, I wrapped it again in secret. Each time, he wore it in secret. We were passing something back and forth between us, wordlessly, over one shoe. I came to think it was the only language the road had left us.

Once, the cloth had frozen stiff overnight, soaked through with dew. My brother rubbed it between his palms for a long time before he could get it on. I lay there and listened to that sound — a dry, papery rubbing that went on and on. I felt both sorry and grateful, hearing it.

Water ran short again. We filtered muddy water the way Noa had taught us. Even that, my brother made me drink first, and drank his own share after.

"…You go first," I told him, more than once. He always pretended not to hear.

Once I wrestled the water bowl out of his hands and pressed it to his mouth before he could stop me. He laughed, just for a second. Then he took exactly one swallow and handed the bowl straight back. I understood that single swallow was the only concession he would ever allow himself.

At night we slept back to back, trying to block a little of the wind. I could feel my brother's back growing thinner by the week. He never seemed to close his eyes until he'd made sure I was already asleep. More than once I pretended to sleep just to watch him finally let go.

There were an unusual number of stars at night. I looked at them and tried to remember the constellation names Father used to point out to us in Brick Hollow. Not one came back to me. That grieved me. On the road to find Father, the first things I was losing were the things he had taught me.

That night I understood for the first time that keeping a record wasn't only for my own sake. I wasn't writing to keep from forgetting. I was writing to catch hold, again, of things I had already forgotten.

That night, instead of the constellations' names, I counted how many stars I could see. I got to thirty and stopped. There was no counting the whole sky anyway. I thought that was a little like this journey — instead of trying to count what couldn't be counted, you counted what was in front of you.

My brother had unusual trouble falling asleep that night. I lay still, pretending to sleep, and listened to his breathing. He turned over several times before finally settling with his back to the sky. That back looked, to me, like a back trying to avoid something.

Food ran low too. We dug up dry fruit and roots when we found them. Even after Noa's lessons in what was safe to eat and what wasn't, we were never quite sure, so my brother always tasted things first, in small bites.

"…Spit it out if it's bitter," he'd say. Every time he said it, I understood that he was swallowing the risk before it ever reached me.

Once he chewed a root and winced and spat it out fast. I asked, frightened, if he was all right. He said, like it was nothing, "…just a little bitter." That night I saw him quietly rubbing his stomach. I pretended not to see. He probably knew I was pretending.

On the fifth day, we found a stranger's footprints.

My brother knelt and studied them closely.

"…One person," he said. "But it's strange. They circled the same spot, over and over."

"…Maybe someone who got lost," I said.

"…Or," my brother went on, "someone who kept searching for something and never found it."

His finger traced print after print. The depths were all different. Some were shallow. Some were pressed unusually deep.

I crouched beside him and studied the prints too. Grains of dirt were still crumbling loose at the edges, not fully settled. They weren't old. That fact frightened me and gladdened me at once.

"…Look at this," my brother said, pointing to one print. "This one's much deeper than the others. Like someone stood here a long time."

I laid my palm on the spot. The soil was still cool. I thought that coolness held, pressed into it, the shape of however long someone had stood there.

"…Brother." I asked. "If a person stands in one place long enough, does the ground remember?"

He looked at me for a moment.

"…That's not remembering," he said. "It's just pressed down."

"…But it's still there," I said.

He didn't answer. Instead he laid his own hand over the mark once more. I noticed, then, that his hand was bigger than the print.

He looked up at me. We had the same thought at the same moment.

My heart started pounding again right then. I couldn't be certain whose print it was. But I could see in my brother's eyes that he already was.

We stayed there a long while. My brother circled the prints again and again, studying them. Watching him, it looked as though he were retracing Father's steps with his own body.

"…What was this footprint doing here?" I asked.

He touched the ground again. The soil there was packed unusually hard.

"…Going in circles," he said. "Like… like someone searching for something they couldn't find."

That answer ached in my chest. Father had forgotten his own name, and still he'd kept searching for something. I felt like I both understood what it was and didn't.

"…Do you think," I asked carefully, "he might have been looking for us?"

My brother didn't answer. Instead he stared for a long time in the direction the tracks pointed. I understood that silence as his own kind of answer.

He rose and brushed the dirt from his knees. His hands were shaking. I pretended not to see. He probably knew I was pretending.

We followed the tracks a little further. After a few steps they suddenly changed direction. Then changed again. That zigzag looked, to me, like the walk of someone who couldn't decide where to go.

"…Whoever this was, they wandered a lot here," I said.

My brother nodded and said nothing. I felt that he was understanding that wandering in his own way. He, too, had walked lost for years now.

Following those tracks, we met a man. Not the one who'd left them — a different traveler, wandering the same stretch of ground.

He carried a heavy pack on his back. His face was sun-darkened, his gait slow. He wasn't startled to see us. Two children on this road, it seemed, wasn't so rare a sight.

A smell of dried herbs rose from his pack. Catching it, I guessed he'd traveled this road a long time. It struck me as strange that I let my guard down so quickly around a stranger — and yet that single smell alone put me somewhat at ease.

"…Have you seen anyone strange near here?" my brother asked. "Someone who counts numbers to himself?"

The man nodded.

"…I saw someone," he said. "A while back. Walking along, counting the whole time. Didn't answer when I spoke to him."

"…How long ago?" my brother asked, impatience bleeding into his voice.

"…Let's see. A day? Two?" The man tilted his head. "I don't know exactly. I only saw him passing by."

"…Which way did he go?" I asked urgently.

"…Don't know," the man said. "But there's someone who's good at finding people like that. I heard his name's Temel."

"…Where would we find him?" my brother asked.

"…Keep heading north and you'll run into him," the man said. "He's always on the move. Finding people's what he does."

"…Does he take payment?" I asked. We had nothing to give.

The man shook his head.

"…Never heard of it," he said. "I hear he just does it."

"…That's a strange person," I said, without thinking.

The man laughed a little at that.

"…Strange," he agreed. "But you get a few strange ones like that on this road. People who lend a hand for nothing in return. I doubted it too, at first. But after you run into a few, you just start figuring that's how it is."

"…Why do they do it?" I asked.

The man shrugged.

"…I asked one of them that myself," he said. "He told me someone had done the same for him first. So now he does it too."

I wanted to write that answer down on paper. But my hands were too cold to write just then. So instead I recited it to myself, several times over.

Hearing that name, I felt a strange familiarity, though I didn't know why at the time. I know now. That name, too, was one that had been quietly finding people all across this world.

With that, the man went on his way. I watched his back for a long time. A name a stranger had thrown out in passing had, by that day's end, become the most important piece of information in the world to us.

My brother stood watching the direction the man had disappeared into, then turned back to me.

"…Temel," he said aloud, testing the name. He seemed to be pressing it into his mouth so he wouldn't lose it.

"…I'm memorizing it too," I said. "Temel, Temel."

That made him smile — the first smile I'd seen from him in days. I was so glad to see it that I looked at his face longer than I needed to.

That night we started walking again in the direction the man had pointed.

It was an unusually bright moon. I picked my way by its light, watching the ground. Our shadows stretched long ahead of us, shifting shape with every step. I tried to gauge, from those shadows, how far we'd come. I couldn't.

"…This Temel person — do you think he's worth meeting?" I asked.

"…If these footprints belong to Father," my brother said, "I'll meet anyone. I'm not letting anything slip past me now."

"…What if they're not Father's?" I asked again. Asking that question frightened me, but that night, for some reason, it came out on its own.

My brother stopped walking and looked at me. He was quiet for a moment.

"…I'll still meet him," he said finally. "Even if it's not Father — if whoever left those tracks is someone else lost and wandering without a name, like us. Then it's still good for someone to go find them."

That answer surprised me a little. My brother had always been someone with a clear purpose. But that day, there was something in his answer that went beyond purpose. I didn't know exactly what it was, not then.

That night we walked under the stars again for the first time in a while. There were no clouds, so the road showed clearly. I wanted to believe that was a good sign.

Walking, I watched my brother's back. His shoe was still coming apart. But his stride had picked up. I couldn't tell whether that quickness came from anxiety or from hope. Probably both.

He kept lifting his eyes to the stars as he walked. I couldn't tell if it was to find the way, or just because he wanted something to lean on. He had never been a person who prayed openly to the sky. But that night, I watched his eyes rest there, longer than usual.

I wrote down what had happened that day as we walked — the footprints, the man, the name Temel. My handwriting still slanted sideways. Still, I tried not to leave out a single letter. I believed a day would come when I'd read this whole road to Father, start to finish.

As night deepened, the wind turned cold again. I folded the paper and tucked it inside my clothes, and took my brother's arm instead. He lowered his arm without a word so I could hold on. I think that small kindness was the warmest moment of that whole day.

Somewhere far off, a wild animal cried once. I froze. My brother gripped my hand and said, "…It's all right, that's far away," and I believed him. Most things my brother said, I could believe.

The sound didn't come again. Instead, the wind kept combing through the dry grass. That sound felt louder to me than the animal's cry had. Even on a night when nothing happened, the world kept making some sound or other.

We walked quite late that night. Our legs ached, but neither of us suggested stopping. I don't think it was only impatience. The moment we stopped walking, it felt like the hope we'd been building would stop too, if only for a moment.

Finally my brother slowed and spoke first.

"…Let's stop here for today. I know your legs hurt."

"…Yours hurt too," I said.

He laughed instead of answering. We set down our packs and settled in. Before lying down, I looked up at the stars one more time. Still not one constellation name came back to me. But that night, strangely, it didn't feel sad.

I set that thought down as this chapter's last line. I understood, that day, that my brother was no longer someone gathering evidence, or someone raging alone. My brother had become someone searching. And I had become the one who kept the record of his search.

(Continued in Chapter 26.)

Chapter 26. The Man Who Finds People

_Previously_ — The brother and sister found a stranger's footprints. A traveler confirmed that the man had been walking along counting numbers, and told them the name of someone who finds people: "Temel."

Two days later, we really did meet Temel.

He wasn't a big man. He carried his pack light. His eyes never stopped moving over his surroundings.

His pack was far more modest than ours. That struck me as odd. I'd expected someone who traveled the country finding people to carry more, not less — but he looked instead like a man who carried only what he strictly needed. I didn't know the reason then.

His walk was unusual. Not hurried, not slow. It was the walk of someone who already knew exactly where he was going. Watching it put me strangely at ease. I felt certain this man wouldn't get lost.

"…Are you the one who finds people?" my brother asked.

Temel stopped and looked at us. For just a moment, something flickered in his eyes.

"…Something like that," Temel said. "Who are you looking for?"

In that moment I felt something strange in Temel's eyes. The look of someone who had repeated the same work for a very long time. Not tired — worn familiar.

His hands drew my attention too. Dirt was packed into his fingernails in layer upon layer — not dirt that would wash off in one washing. They were the hands of someone who had walked a long way.

There were small scars scattered across the backs of his hands as well, like marks from pushing through thornbrush. Looking at them, I understood that for this man, finding people wasn't simply walking a road — it was a thing his body took blows for.

"…Don't your hands hurt?" I asked without thinking.

Temel looked down at his own hands.

"…They hurt when they hurt," he said. "But this isn't pain. This is just old."

That distinction stayed with me. I'd never before met someone who separated old from painful like that.

"…Our father," my brother said, his voice trembling slightly. "He walks counting numbers. He's forgotten his name too."

Temel closed his eyes briefly at that, as if remembering something.

"…Finding someone like that can take a long time," Temel said. "Are you all right with that?"

"…Yes," my brother said immediately. No hesitation at all.

"…We met Speon and Mena," my brother added. "They didn't mention you, but we found our way to you on our own."

Something shifted, faintly, in Temel's expression at those two names.

"…Are they well?" Temel asked. His voice held both gladness and caution at once.

"…Yes," I said. "Each of them is doing well, where they are."

Temel nodded and didn't ask further. In that silence, I felt there was an old story between the three of them that we knew nothing about.

A faint shadow crossed Temel's eyes for a moment. I couldn't tell if it was grief or longing. Maybe both. What it might feel like to hear news of people you hadn't seen in a long time, delivered by two strange children's mouths — I couldn't have imagined it, then.

"…Does Speon still keep the well?" Temel asked suddenly.

"…Yes," I said. "People keep coming to it, I hear."

Temel smiled faintly at that. It looked like a smile he hadn't worn in a long time.

"…That's him," Temel said. And didn't go on.

He gazed off into the distance for a moment. I felt that his eyes weren't turned toward Speon's well, but toward somewhere much further away. Probably toward some era we didn't yet know.

"…And Mena?" I asked carefully. "I heard she became a Recorder."

Temel's eyes softened at that name too.

"…She's probably doing it well, being Mena," Temel said. "She was always careful about everything. The one of the three of us who forgot the least."

Listening to that, I thought of the writing tool Mena had given me. It was still in my hand even then. That was the first time I truly felt how far the things these three people had left with each other still reached.

My brother told Temel about Father. The brick-counting habit, the footprints, the numbers.

Temel listened and nodded.

"…I've seen people like that a few times," Temel said. "People who've lost their names usually repeat something with their hands or their mouths. Something they used to do stays in the body."

"…Why is that?" I asked.

"…I don't know exactly either," Temel said. "But the body seems to remember longer than the mind. The mind loses hold of a name. The hands keep counting bricks."

"…Then can you tell what someone used to do, just by looking at their body?" my brother asked, leaning forward slightly.

"…To some degree," Temel said. "If there's a callus on the hand, it's someone who held something for a long time. If the walk leans to one side, it's someone who carried something for a long time. The body doesn't lie much."

My brother glanced down at his own palm. There was still a callus there from Brick Hollow. I saw him slide that hand quickly into his pocket.

"…Can you tell about us too?" my brother asked.

"…I can. A lot," Temel said. "Both of you have hands that carried something for a long time."

Hearing that, I thought of Father's hands. I tried to imagine what they might be doing right now. Just imagining it hurt.

"…If he finds his name," I asked, "does that mean he'll go back to how he was?"

Temel shook his head.

"…Not always," Temel said. "Even finding a name doesn't mean going back to exactly who you were before. But it's better than not having one."

That answer didn't comfort me much. But strangely, I was grateful for the honesty. Temel didn't give us false hope.

"…Then why do you keep going out to find people?" I asked. "You just said they don't come back exactly the same."

Temel stopped walking for a moment and looked at me.

"…Even if they don't come back exactly the same, it's still better than not looking," Temel said. "Finding someone's name doesn't bring everything back about them. But at least, you don't leave them wandering alone."

Hearing that, something in me ached. Temel wasn't promising a perfect outcome, and yet he had a reason not to give up. I didn't understand, then, how that was possible.

"…So can you find him?" my brother asked urgently.

"…I'm someone who finds people," Temel said. "But I'm not someone who carries answers around. I'm someone who goes looking."

I didn't really understand the difference in those words at the time. I do now. Temel wasn't someone who gave you certainty. He was someone who walked alongside you.

"…What if we find him and he's not Father?" I asked. It frightened me to ask, but it had to be asked.

My brother turned to look at me. His face said it was unexpected, that I'd been the one to raise it. I didn't look away from him. I thought someone had to name that possibility out loud.

"…Then that person still deserves to be found, on their own terms," Temel said. "There's no wasted step in the search. Whoever you're looking for, the walking still means something."

"…Has that happened to you? Finding someone and it wasn't the person you wanted?" my brother asked.

Temel nodded.

"…It has," Temel said shortly. He said nothing further. I felt a long story hiding inside that shortness.

That answer comforted me in a strange way. For the first time, I understood that this journey might not be a complete failure even if we never found Father.

"…What happened to that person, then?" I asked again. "The one you found who wasn't who you wanted."

Temel stopped and looked up at the sky for a moment.

"…That person was also looking for their own family," Temel said. "So I helped find them too. In the end, we found them. I stood beside them and watched that reunion. It wasn't the person I'd been looking for. But I cried, that day."

Hearing that story, my chest filled strangely full. For the first time, I sensed, dimly, that searching might be something larger than simply getting what you'd hoped for.

My brother didn't answer that. But I saw his expression loosen a little. He had always measured a journey only by its outcome. That day, I felt something like a different standard beginning to grow in him.

"…Why do you do this work?" I asked.

Temel looked off into the distance for a moment.

"…A long time ago, I ran from a place where I was supposed to be guiding someone," Temel said. "I was afraid, back then. Now I'm still afraid sometimes. But I don't run anymore. That's the only way I know how to pay it back."

Hearing that, I thought it sounded like something Speon had once said. I didn't know it then — that these two had truly come out of the same story.

"…Where did you run from?" my brother asked, his voice careful.

Temel hesitated before answering.

"…I had to guide some people across a certain place," Temel said. "But I got scared and stopped partway. Some of them made it across without me. Some didn't."

Temel's voice cracked slightly there. I didn't press further. Some stories, I learned in that silence, it's a courtesy not to hear to the end.

"…Weren't you scared? The moment you wanted to run again?" I asked.

"…Every time," Temel admitted honestly. "Even now, sometimes. But if you run because you're afraid, you find yourself running from then on. So I just walk, still afraid."

I held onto that sentence for a long time afterward. I didn't fully understand its weight then. I do now. It was a sentence we would need to learn again and again for the rest of this journey.

"…Can't you wait until you're not afraid anymore?" I asked.

Temel laughed shortly at the question.

"…I tried waiting like that too," Temel said. "But the day of not-being-afraid never came. So I decided to just start while still afraid. It was the only way there was."

My brother listened to this quietly. I could feel his eyes turning something over. He, too, had always been someone who waited for certainty before he acted.

A gust of wind blew hard, once. Temel shouldered his pack and started walking again. I noticed that he didn't stop walking even right after saying he was afraid. I'd never before met someone whose words and steps stayed that closely joined.

"…Do you still see that person now?" I asked.

Temel laughed briefly. There was a trace of loneliness in the laugh.

"…Sometimes," Temel said. "Not as often as you'd think. But every time I do, it feels exactly the same as before."

Temel led us north for several more days, watching the ground the whole way — the direction dry grass had been trampled, scratches on stones, even the depth of footprints.

Temel's own steps looked far more at ease than ours. But watch closely, and his eyes never rested for a moment. Following his pace, I felt for the first time how exhausting searching really was. It wore out the eyes before it wore out the body.

On the second day, Temel suddenly stopped and crouched to the ground. We stopped too. He studied one very small mark in the dirt for a long time, then finally shook his head and stood back up.

"…Not it," Temel said briefly.

I understood, only then, that this small disappointment was something Temel lived through several times a day. A searcher's day was mostly filled with confirming not this one.

At night, Temel always kept the fire small. "Too big and you can't find anyone," he said. "Someone searching shouldn't stand too bright. The one you're looking for won't be able to see you." I understood that and didn't understand it, both at once.

I sat by that small fire that night and tried to measure with my eyes how narrow its reach was. Only as far as our three sets of knees. Beyond that, all darkness.

I understood, then. A person who searches doesn't light up the whole world. They light only the ground at their own feet, and keep walking by that light.

Through those four days, my brother slept fitfully every night. I heard him turning and turned restlessly too, alongside him. Temel never tried to stop us. He simply, wordlessly, packed up each morning and walked on.

On the second night, half-asleep, I heard Temel humming low to himself. It wasn't really a song, more like breath given shape. I listened without opening my eyes. There was, strangely, an old grief in that sound, and beneath it, some rhythm that refused to stop.

On the third morning I saw Temel's shoes. As worn as ours. Layers of patched sole overlapped several times. I thought those shoes showed, plainly, all the time this man had walked.

On the third day, the path narrowed and trees began to appear here and there. Temel's steps slowed bit by bit — steps that were watching something more carefully.

"…We're getting close," Temel said. Not a voice full of certainty. But my heart began pounding at those words anyway.

"…Found it," Temel said quietly on the fourth night. Far off, a small light was visible.

That light was very small. So small that I checked, several times, whether it was really fire or only something my eyes had made up. A single small light had never felt so enormous.

Temel pointed at the light and lowered his voice.

"…Don't rush," Temel said. "Confirm first, then approach. If gladness gets ahead of you, you could end up hurt."

At that, I let some of the strength go out of my legs. I'd wanted nothing but to run. My brother looked the same. His hand was gripping mine hard enough to hurt.

"…This is the closest we've ever gotten," my brother said quietly. His voice shook. "I… I don't know what to do."

"…Me neither," I said. We stood there and listened to each other's breathing for a moment.

The moment I saw that light, I set down this chapter's last line here. I gripped my brother's hand tight again. I couldn't know whether that light was Father or not. But in that moment, for the first time, I felt with my whole body that you could walk forward without knowing the answer.

(Continued in Chapter 27.)

Chapter 27. Before the Firelight

_Previously_ — The brother and sister met Temel. Along with his words — "There's no wasted step in the search" — they spotted, on the fourth night, a small light far off in the distance.

We crept toward that light with careful steps.

The grass brushed quietly underfoot. I set each step down heel to toe, trying to kill even that sound.

A faint scent of smoke was mixed into the night air. The instant I caught it, my heart reacted before my mind did. There are things the body knows faster than thought.

A small campfire. Someone sat beside it. His back was to us, so I couldn't see his face. But his hand kept moving — a motion of counting something in the air.

The back was bent. The set of the shoulders was unfamiliar. And yet I couldn't tear my eyes away from that silhouette.

The hand moved with a steady rhythm, like a motion carved into the body long ago.

It never stopped, never hurried. That regularity seemed, somehow, less like a person than a clock.

There was no wind that night. The flame burned straight up, hardly flickering at all. I found that stillness strangely frightening.

A few dry branches lay neatly beside the fire. Not piled carelessly — laid side by side, matched for length.

That neatness troubled me. I couldn't understand why someone alone would keep things arranged so carefully.

I slowed my steps. My brother did too. We both tried not to make a sound. Even the sound of grass underfoot felt like something to be careful of.

We stopped every few steps. The wish to get closer and the wish to draw this moment out a little longer were both in me at once. I felt those two wishes fighting each other as I walked.

Temel walked half a step behind us. He didn't lead. It was the distance of someone who knew this walk belonged to us.

The moment I saw that hand, I stopped walking. I felt my brother stop at the same instant. We gripped each other's hands tight, right there.

The closer we came, the more a small sound reached us. A voice made almost without moving the lips.

"…two thousand nine hundred ninety-seven, two thousand nine hundred ninety-eight…"

Hearing those numbers, the strength went out of my legs. I knew exactly where that number stood. It was nearly at three thousand. I understood, for the first time, that Father had not once, not a single time since that night, made it all the way to three thousand.

I tried to measure the distance between those two numbers — two thousand nine hundred ninety-eight, and three thousand. Barely two. And yet Father had failed to cross those two for years.

I stood there and folded down my fingers. One, two. That was all. That short distance had become, for Father, a wall he could not climb.

A moment later, the count started over from the beginning. One, two, three.

I listened to that return and couldn't say a word.

I understood then. Father hadn't failed to reach three thousand. Every time he came close, he was dragged back to that night, and made to start again from the beginning, every time.

His voice was low and cracked. And yet the accent — the accent alone — was exactly the one I remembered from Brick Hollow.

I felt years of time collapse in an instant, hearing that single accent.

Temel stopped too, and listened to the sound in silence. He said nothing. I understood that silence as the silence of someone who had already lived through moments like this many times before.

Even Temel's breathing went very quiet. I understood that quiet as a kindness meant for us.

Temel's hand rested on my shoulder for just a moment. No words. But the weight of that hand alone told me: nothing should be rushed now.

Temel moved ahead of us, quietly.

"…Excuse me," Temel said, low. "Are you all right?"

The voice was very small. And yet it carried clearly through that stillness.

The man's hand stopped. The counting stopped with it. Even that stillness felt as sharp as a sound.

The man slowly turned his head.

The movement was slow, like a neck creaking.

That short instant felt very long to me. For those few seconds, I forgot how to breathe.

The moment I saw that face, my breath caught. It was a face that hadn't aged, hadn't changed. I saw that face for the first time since the last night in Brick Hollow. It was Father.

I reached for my brother's hand in that instant. He was already reaching for mine. Our hands found each other exactly, in the dark.

The firelight flickered faintly across that face. The eyebrows, the nose, the lips. I ran my eyes over each of them again, checking, as if confirming.

My brother had frozen in place too. He couldn't say a word. I saw years' worth of feeling pass across his face all at once — anger, relief, fear, and fear again.

His two hands opened weakly at his sides, then closed again. Hands that wanted to grab hold of something but couldn't find what.

Temel turned quietly to look at us. He seemed about to say something, then simply stepped back instead. A step back that left this moment to us.

Even so, Temel didn't disappear completely. He stood still a few steps off in the dark. A place from which to watch without stepping in.

I still remember that step back. That there are moments when giving someone space matters as much as staying beside them — I learned that for the first time then.

The campfire crackled once. In that moment, it sounded like the loudest sound in the world.

A single spark leapt up and vanished into the dark.

I followed that spark's vanishing point with my eyes for a long time. Where it rose to, nothing remained.

The night sky hung low. The stars looked unusually close. I felt that night stepping quietly aside for us.

I felt my own heart beating along with that sound.

Father looked at us. But there was nothing in that gaze. It was the gaze one gives a stranger.

Seeing that gaze, my breath caught. We had walked for years to get here, and what we met was strangeness.

Father's clothes were worn. A different cloth from what we knew. His shoes had split soles too, just like ours. That long walk had settled itself into his body, exactly the way it had settled into ours.

A thin layer of dried dirt sat on the backs of his hands. Hands that hadn't been washed in a long time.

"…Who are you?" Father asked. His voice was exactly the same. And yet in that voice there was no weight toward us at all.

I couldn't say a word in that moment. My fingertips were trembling.

Heat rose in my throat. No words came out past it. I just stood and looked at Father's face.

I looked at his face again. The shape of his eyebrows, the small scar on his left cheek — all the same. But the look held in that face belonged entirely to someone else.

My brother stepped forward instead, one step. Dry leaves crunched underfoot.

"…Do you… know us?" he asked. His voice had completely broken down.

Father shook his head. His face was apologetic. But there was strangeness in that apology too.

The shake of his head was slow, careful — the movement of someone unsure even of that much.

My brother stood motionless for several seconds. I was watching only his back. His shoulders sank, just slightly.

There wasn't a breath of wind that night. Only the campfire's smoke rose straight up.

I followed that smoke with my eyes. Somewhere at a certain height, it scattered without a sound.

I thought, that night, for the first time, that some things don't disappear — they simply travel to where they can no longer be seen.

I wanted to reach out and touch that shoulder. But I couldn't move. Neither my brother nor I had the strength, in that moment, even to touch each other.

"…I don't know," Father said. "I'm sorry. I… don't really know who I am, either."

Father's voice trembled slightly at the end of that sentence. Something other than apology was mixed into that tremor. I didn't know what it was, then.

I hesitated, again and again, over whether to reach out my hand. It felt like taking a stranger's hand. It also felt like taking the most familiar hand in the world.

My fingertips paused in midair for a moment. Father waited quietly for that hand. He didn't hurry it.

Only then did I reach out and take Father's hand. It was trembling. It carried the exact same warmth as the hand that had held mine the night we crossed the boundary stones, long ago.

I crossed years of time on that single warmth.

Father's hand was cold at first. It warmed, little by little, while I held it.

I couldn't tell whether that warmth was mine or Father's. I didn't want to tell the difference either.

"…I'm Elpis," I said. My voice cracked. "I'm Elpis. I'm your daughter."

Only after I'd said it did the tears finally come.

"…I'm Jeteo," my brother managed to say. His voice shook. "I'm your son."

My brother said it with his fists clenched tight. I knew that was the hand that was holding back tears.

Father quietly tried the name out too. "…Jeteo." Slowly, like someone learning an unfamiliar sound.

My brother heard that pronunciation and couldn't say anything. He just watched the shape of Father's mouth.

The firelight lit only half my brother's face. The other half stayed sunk in darkness. I couldn't see what expression he was wearing in that dark half.

Father's hand gave a very faint twitch in that moment. His eyes still saw nothing they recognized. But that hand alone was responding to something.

I watched that tremor exactly. I didn't know, then, whether it was memory or just a habit left in the body. I still don't know exactly, even now. But I decided to hold onto that single tremor.

My brother must have seen it too. He said nothing, but his eyes never left that hand.

I stroked that hand gently. The knuckles were thicker than they used to be. A hand that had held something and walked for a long time.

There were new calluses in the palm. I didn't know what road they'd been made on. But I wanted to feel each of them out with my fingertips all the same.

I thought those calluses were a list of the days Father had passed through. I couldn't read that list. Still, I didn't lift my fingertips away from them.

I held that hand a little longer. Father didn't pull it back.

Somewhere far off, a night bird cried once, briefly, and stopped. Besides that, there was no other sound at all.

My brother came quietly closer too and took Father's other hand. Father didn't shake that one off either. We sat like that for a while, saying nothing, just holding hands in front of the fire.

The firelight lit each of our three faces in turn. No one broke that silence first.

The campfire was slowly dying down. No one added more wood. Even that fading felt like something none of us wanted to disturb.

I set that answer down as this chapter's last line. In that moment I learned, for the first time, that finding and losing could happen at once. Father was there before us. And Father was, at the same time, farther from us than anyone.

(Continued in Chapter 28.)

Chapter 28. How to Sit Beside Someone

_Previously_ — The brother and sister found Father at last. But he recognized neither their names nor their faces. "I don't really know who I am, either." When Elpis took his hand, it trembled faintly — a response that might have been memory, or might have been only habit.

That night, my brother sat alone a little apart from Father.

The embers had nearly burned out already. Only a thin thread of smoke still rose. I watched that smoke disappear for a long time. It felt like I couldn't bear it unless I was watching something.

The night air had turned far colder than before. I felt that chill sinking into me and still didn't want to move. There was a strange fear that if I left this spot, everything that had happened today would stop being real.

I went and sat beside my brother. Neither of us said anything.

The night air was cold. I leaned lightly against my brother's shoulder. He didn't pull away. But he didn't wrap his arm around me the way he usually did, either. That didn't hurt my feelings. He looked, right then, like he didn't even have the strength for it.

His breathing was much shallower than usual. Listening to it, I felt how hard he was working not to cry. He was always like that. When he held back tears, his breath went careful first.

I understood, then, what my brother was holding back. He must have imagined this moment for years. And nowhere in that imagining, I think, had there ever been a scene where Father didn't recognize us.

I tried to picture the reunion my brother must have imagined all this time. Probably Father, seeing us, calling our names and running to us. I tried to measure the distance between that imagined scene and tonight's reality, and gave up quickly. It hurt too much.

"…I feel really wrong," my brother said suddenly. "We found Father, and instead of joy, what I feel most is fear. Is that normal?"

"…I don't know," I said. "But I feel something similar too."

He looked at me, a little surprised. I hoped that knowing he wasn't the only one who felt this strange would bring him at least some comfort.

Insects called low, steadily. I was strangely grateful that the sound never broke off. It felt like proof the world hadn't stopped completely.

Far off, I could see Father still counting something with his fingers. He didn't seem to be sleeping either.

His profile showed in the moonlight — the silhouette of a stranger. And yet the line of that silhouette's shoulders was exactly as I remembered. I couldn't tell whether that fact made me glad or sad.

"…How long does that go on for?" my brother asked quietly. I couldn't tell if he was asking me, or just thinking aloud.

I couldn't answer. I didn't know either.

"…It used to be," my brother said again, his voice thick, "the goal was to make that counting stop. But now I'm scared that if it stops, Father will turn into someone completely different."

"…Same for me," I said. "That motion — I hate it, and somehow I'm still scared it'll disappear."

My brother turned to look at me. It was the first time he'd heard someone say exactly what he was thinking.

"…You thought that too?" he asked.

"…Yes," I said. "I just didn't say it."

Hearing that, I understood how tangled my brother's heart really was. He wanted to have Father back, and at the same time he was afraid of losing even this Father in front of him.

Temel sat down quietly beside us.

"…I've seen this happen a few times," Temel said. "Forcing memory to come back only makes it more afraid. Just staying beside someone comes first."

"…What good does that do?" my brother asked. There was an edge to his voice. "That person doesn't even know who we are."

He rubbed roughly at his eyes as he said it. I understood that gesture was closer to grief than to anger.

"…Whether it does any good, you might learn later — or you might not," Temel said. "But staying beside someone is the one thing you can actually do right now, whether they recognize you or not."

"…But he doesn't know us," my brother said again. His voice shook a little. "Doesn't know we're here right now. Doesn't know what we're holding back."

He glanced toward Father as he said it. Still sitting there, fingers moving. I saw resentment and longing pass through my brother's eyes at the same time.

"…That's true," Temel admitted plainly. "But staying beside someone was never about being recognized for it. It's just being there."

Hearing that, I strangely thought of the Ledger's voice. The Ledger would never take in what it couldn't "resolve." Temel was telling us to stay beside someone even when it couldn't be resolved. I thought, for the first time that day, that difference might be the answer this whole book was reaching for.

I remembered the smooth screen we'd seen back in Haven. Intake, resolution, completion. Nowhere among those three words was there any entry called "staying beside." I felt, for the first time, that I could name exactly what the Ledger couldn't do.

"…Did you have someone who stayed beside you like that?" I asked.

Temel was quiet for a long time, then nodded, small.

"…I did," Temel said. "If it hadn't been for that person, I'd probably still be running."

"…What kind of person were they?" I asked.

Temel searched for words a moment.

"…Hard to explain," Temel said. "But one thing's certain. That person never chased after me when I ran. They just kept being there, in the place I'd come back to."

"…Did that person ever fail to recognize you too?" I asked again.

Temel looked at me for a moment, choosing his answer.

"…No," Temel said. "It was the opposite. I was the one who failed to recognize them. More than once."

Hearing that, something in me settled strangely. It comforted me to know that failing to recognize the person staying beside you didn't only happen to our family.

"…So do you recognize them now?" I asked.

Temel paused before answering.

"…Sometimes I do, sometimes I still get confused," Temel said honestly. "But I recognize them much faster than I used to."

"…When?" my brother asked, unable to hide his curiosity.

Temel smiled slightly.

"…Plenty of times," Temel said. "There were times I was wary of them, thinking they were a stranger. There were times I didn't recognize them even standing right in front of me. I only understood, afterward."

"…And they still stayed with you, even so?" I asked.

"…That person stayed even when I couldn't recognize them," Temel said. "That came first. Not my recognizing."

"…So it didn't depend on whether you recognized them — they were on your side from the start?" my brother asked. There was a caution in his voice I hadn't heard from him before.

Temel looked at my brother for a long moment at that question.

"…Seems that way," Temel said. "But back then, I didn't even think to call it being on my side. I just thought they were a nuisance."

I copied that line down on paper that night. I read that sentence over several times. Being there before being recognized. I only half understood what it meant, back then.

"…When you didn't recognize them, did they get upset?" I asked.

Temel shook his head.

"…Didn't even seem upset," Temel said. "Just stayed, like they were waiting for the day I'd recognize them. I found that stranger than anything. If it were me, I'd have given up long before and left."

That night I took out paper again after a long while and wrote down what had happened. My hands were too cold and the letters kept shaking. Still, I tried not to leave out a single line. Father, the hand-counting, Temel's words. I wrote it imagining the day I'd someday read all of this back to Father.

At the bottom of that page I added one more line. "Being there, before being recognized." I traced my fingertip over that sentence several times. As if doing so might press its meaning into my own body.

My brother glanced over at the paper beside me.

"…Can you write that down for me too?" he asked.

The request surprised me enough that I looked at him for a moment. He'd never shown much interest in my writing before. Without a word, I tore a corner off the page and copied the sentence again, and handed it to him. He folded it carefully and tucked it inside his shirt.

The next morning, the sun still hung low. Dew beaded on every blade of grass. Father was already up, tidying the fire pit. It looked as though his body moved on its own, without anyone telling it to.

There was still a chill of night left in the air. I breathed it in deep. I wanted to confirm that yesterday hadn't been a dream.

My brother had woken before me. His eyes were swollen. I pretended not to notice the swelling. There was no need to say out loud that he'd cried alone all night.

Watching him, I felt a strange feeling. A man who had forgotten his own name, who didn't recognize even us — and yet the touch of his hands, tidying the fire each morning, remained exactly the same. I thought that was another trace still left inside Father.

Father covered the ashes with dirt and stacked the remaining firewood neatly. The motion was so smooth that, for a moment, it overlapped in my mind with Father tending the fire each dawn back in Brick Hollow. I held onto that overlap for a long time.

My brother went over to Father.

"…Will you come with us?" he asked carefully. "In case you don't know where else to go."

Father looked at him for a long moment. The eyes of someone looking at a stranger, still. But in that gaze there was, faintly, something that wasn't wariness.

I tried not to let that shift in his eyes slip past me. A tiny difference between yesterday and today. But that small difference felt, to me, like an enormous signal.

Father was fidgeting with a small twig in his hand. It looked like a habit — hands that couldn't stay still when something felt uncertain. That habit, too, was both unfamiliar and familiar to me.

Father set the twig down and looked back at my brother. Wariness and curiosity were mixed in that gaze together.

"…Why are you helping me?" Father asked.

My brother lost his words for a moment. I knew he wanted to say because you're my father. But he chose different words.

"…No reason," my brother said. "It's fine to help without a reason."

Hearing that answer, I was startled. My brother had always believed there was no such thing as a reasonless act — back in Haven. I felt, right then, that he was slowly becoming a different person.

I saw my brother look surprised at himself, even as he said it — like someone hearing his own words a beat late. He paused, then went on.

"…The old me wouldn't have said this," he told Father. "But lately… I think there are things without reasons after all. No — I think there are reasons even when you can't put them into words."

Father listened to that quietly. His face wasn't one of full understanding. But it was a face that felt something.

Father tilted his head at that answer.

"…That's a strange answer," Father said. But he was smiling. "But it's not a strangeness I dislike."

"…People who help without a reason always seem strange," my brother said. "I didn't used to trust people like that either, until not long ago."

"…And now?" Father asked.

My brother thought for a moment.

"…Now I trust it a little. Just a little."

Temel stood watching this from a short distance off. I saw a very faint smile cross his face. A smile that looked, somehow, quietly pleased.

Temel kept glancing our way several times that morning while packing his things. In that gaze I felt relief, and also a small trace of loneliness. It was the face of someone watching the people he'd found find their own way to each other.

"…Have you seen a lot of us turning out like this?" I asked, going over to Temel.

"…Not often," Temel said. "But every time I see it, I'm glad. It's not something happening to me directly, and still it fills my chest, strangely, like it's my own."

"…Why does someone else's business fill your chest?" I asked.

Temel laughed a little.

"…There's a moment when it stops being someone else's business," Temel said. "Spend a few days delivering someone somewhere, and it starts feeling like your own business whether they turn out all right. Strange, but true."

Hearing that, I looked at Temel again. I thought, then, that finding people might be, in its own way, the same as sharing your own portion of joy with someone else.

In the end, Father nodded.

My brother held out his hand. Father looked at it a moment, then slowly laid his own hand on top of it. Less a handshake than a gesture of confirming something for the first time.

I stood quietly watching those two hands. One was a stranger's hand, and one was a hand long waited for. But in that moment, I couldn't tell which was which.

The morning sun fell slanting across the two hands. I watched that light fall equally on both. Under the light, the stranger's hand and the long-awaited hand weren't different at all.

Far off, a bird passed low, calling once. I thought that sound was carving this morning into some place, somewhere. A sound that, heard again later, would bring this day back.

"…Then… I'll take you up on that," Father said.

I found myself wondering, out of nowhere, what Speon would have said if he were here. Probably, that's the well's way. Giving someone a place beside you without asking why.

Temel heard that and turned back from packing his things.

"…You know," Temel said, "take you up on that — I haven't heard someone say that in a long time. These days everyone tries to handle everything on their own. Nobody says they'll take anyone up on anything anymore."

I listened to that and thought of Haven. No one there had ever taken anyone up on anything. The Ledger resolved everything for them instead. Only now did it seem to me that hadn't been convenience — it had been loneliness.

I set that line down as this chapter's last line. Hearing it, I nearly laughed. Father had told us he'd "take us up on it." I understood, then, for the first time, that becoming someone who walks alongside another can come before becoming someone they recognize again.

(Continued in Chapter 29.)

Chapter 29. While He Had No Name

_Previously_ — The brother and sister began learning not to force Father's memory back, but simply to stay beside him. Father, to these strangers, said, "…I'll take you up on that," and agreed to walk with them.

From that day on, the four of us walked together.

The road was often too narrow for four to walk abreast. Even so, we lined up single file if we had to, keeping close enough that our hands could still reach each other. Strangely, I felt a kind of sturdiness even in that narrow line.

A faint scent of dry grass hung in the morning air. Every time I caught it, I felt, a little more, that we were heading a little further south, a little further toward safety. It wasn't certain. But I wanted to believe it.

Father's steps were a little slower than ours. Legs unused to long walking. But he never once said he was tired. He just stopped, now and then, to look up at the sky.

Every time he did, my brother matched his pace to stand beside him. I'd thought that was only consideration for Father — later I realized my brother was every bit as worn out as Father was. He was simply a boy used to hiding his own exhaustion.

The shadows of the trees along the road always lay long before we reached them. Every time I stepped on those shadows, I found myself gauging what part of the day we were passing through.

I listened to the sound of my brother's footsteps as we walked. A little heavier than before. I didn't think that weight came only from fatigue. Lately he seemed like someone constantly weighing something in his mind.

Every time the wind blew, the dry grass leaned low and rose back up again. Watching it, I thought that the four of us were doing the same — leaning down and rising back up, over and over.

Father still counted on his fingers sometimes. But he no longer counted without stopping, the way he used to. I didn't know if that was a good sign or not.

Every time I saw that gesture, my heart felt tangled. I wanted it to stop. But at the same time, I was afraid to see it stop — afraid it might be the only proof left that Father had ever been Father.

Sometimes I secretly counted along with the movement of Father's fingers. One, two, three. I wanted to see how far he got before he broke off. Most times it scattered somewhere around twenty. I couldn't tell if that meant he was improving, or if his mind had simply wandered somewhere else.

Every time my brother saw that gesture, he would quietly take Father's hand. At first Father startled. After a few days, he stopped pulling the hand away.

"…Strangely, holding this hand makes me feel calmer," Father said. "I don't know why."

Hearing that, I looked at my brother's face. He said nothing. But I saw that his eyes were wet.

He turned away and looked off into the distance for a moment. I knew he was hiding his tears. I pretended not to see.

I too sometimes took Father's other hand. Father always let it go into mine easily. I noticed the temperature of that hand was a little different every day. Some days it was cold; some days, surprisingly warm.

I wondered if that shift in temperature might be tied to something in how Father was feeling. There was no way to check. But every time I held that hand, I felt like I could tell, a little, what kind of day Father had had.

Father found that talk of temperature strange and interesting.

"…I never even paid attention to my own hand's temperature," Father said.

"…I pay attention," I said. "Because it's your hand."

Father smiled a little at that. Half embarrassed, half glad.

"…It's warm today," I said.

"…Is it?" Father said, looking down at his own hand. "I can't really tell. But if my daughter says so, I suppose it is."

That was the first time I heard him use the word "daughter." A word Father had chosen to explain me. He didn't know my name. But he knew my place. That single fact alone let me get through that day.

That evening I told my brother the story. He listened quietly and laughed shortly.

"…Something similar happened to me too," he said. "Yesterday, when we shared the water, Father told me, '…drink first, since you're the older one.' Older one — first time he'd used that word too."

"…Older one?" I asked. "Father called you older brother?"

"…Yeah. I was surprised too," my brother said. "But that word keeps circling back in my mind, for some reason."

Hearing that, I understood. Father had lost his name, but he still distinguished the places we held between us. I thought that wasn't complete memory, but it wasn't complete forgetting either.

"…Why did the word older one stay with you like that?" I asked.

My brother thought for a moment before answering.

"…I don't know, it's just been a long time since anyone saw me as an older brother," he said. "Nobody in Haven ever called me that. They only ever called me by a number, or a status."

I nodded, hearing that. I'd felt something similar in Haven myself.

"…What should we call you?" my brother asked carefully, that first evening.

Father looked flustered at the question.

His eyes wandered the empty air for a moment. His brow drew together faintly, like someone searching for something.

"…I don't really know either," Father said. "It seems like… I don't have a name."

We couldn't say anything for a moment. Then I spoke first.

"…Then would it be all right if we chose one for you? If your real name comes back someday, we can just change it then."

Father looked at me for a moment. In that gaze there was strangeness, and also, faintly, something like trust.

"…Doesn't it feel strange, asking someone else to name you?" Father asked.

"…It is strange," I said. "But right now, strange feels like the ordinary thing."

Father smiled a little at that. It looked like a smile I hadn't seen in a long time.

"…I'd like that," Father said.

"…If it's ever a burden, just say so," my brother added. "We'll just make something up."

"…No," Father shook his head. "It's not that. It's just unfamiliar. But unfamiliar isn't necessarily unwelcome."

My brother said nothing through that whole exchange. I could feel him turning something over deeply.

"…What's wrong?" I asked.

"…Nothing," my brother said. "I was just thinking about what it must be like, living without a name. We always had names. I thought that was just how things were."

Hearing that, I thought for the first time about how much space a name really takes up. Not having a name wasn't only having no word to be called by. It meant having no way to explain yourself to yourself.

"…You know," my brother said quietly, "everyone in Haven had a name. But it seemed like none of them really knew who they were, either. Like they had names and had still lost themselves."

I was surprised to hear that. I hadn't expected my brother would ever look back on Haven that way.

"…Is having a name different from knowing who you are?" I asked.

"…Yeah," my brother said. "I've been thinking that, lately."

That night we tried to think of a name. My brother couldn't settle on one at all. I felt like I could.

By the campfire, my brother opened his mouth several times and closed it again. He only muttered, "…there should be a name that fits him." I found it new, somehow, that my brother could grow this careful over choosing a single name.

"…What about Waiting?" I said. "It's the meaning behind someone's name I know."

Father said the name aloud, several times.

Slowly, rolling it in his mouth, then a bit faster — like someone testing whether the sound could belong to him.

"…Waiting," Father said. "Not bad. It does seem like I'm truly waiting for something right now."

"…What are you waiting for?" my brother asked.

Father thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. But the feeling of waiting for something — that much is certain."

Hearing that answer, I nearly cried. I understood, then. Father had forgotten his name, his face — but he hadn't forgotten the shape of that particular feeling, waiting. I couldn't be sure whether it was us he was waiting for. But I chose to believe it was.

In that moment I remembered how we'd been, back in Brick Hollow. It had always been us waiting for Father. Now Father was the one waiting for something. I found that reversal strange, and yet, somehow, natural too.

My brother said nothing in that moment. But I could tell he was repeating that name over and over in his mind. His lips moved soundlessly along with the two syllables.

From that day on, we called Father "Father" between ourselves, but used the name "Waiting" in front of others. It felt strange at first. Father, too, missed his cue to answer to his own new name more than once.

Every time I said that name, a strange feeling came over me. Calling out a name I myself had given. I thought it felt, somehow, like giving Father life a second time. I knew it was a strange thought, but I couldn't quite shake it.

"…Mr. Waiting." When someone on the road called out to him that way, Father always reacted a beat late. Every time, my brother would quietly tap Father's arm. A signal that meant: that's you being called.

Every time I saw that tap, I felt strangely settled. Beside someone who couldn't answer to their own name, there was someone else, sending the signal in their place. I thought that was needed even before a name was.

Once, in a market, a merchant asked, "…Mr. Waiting, how much would you like to pay for this?" Father let the words pass right by him. My brother quickly tugged his sleeve. "…Father, they're talking to you." Only then did Father startle and answer, "…oh, yes! Me!"
