The merchant laughed and asked, "…why so surprised, when someone calls your name?" Father smiled sheepishly and only said, "…I'm still not used to it."

I watched that exchange and felt something cold settle in my chest. Even Father's own name was still, to him, an unfamiliar coat.

Temel walked with us for a few more days, then stopped at a fork in the road.

"…I have to take a different way from here," Temel said. "There's someone else waiting to be found."

"…Thank you," my brother said. There was real feeling in his voice.

I looked at Temel's worn pack. It looked even lighter than when we'd first met him. I thought it was probably from all the things he'd shared with us while we walked.

"…Where will you go now?" I asked.

"…Wherever someone needs me next," Temel said, smiling. "You never know where that is until you get there."

"…Doesn't living like that wear you out?" my brother asked.

Temel thought a moment before answering.

"…It does," Temel said. "But it beats stopping."

I turned that answer over for a long time. Someone who kept going despite being worn out. I hadn't seen many people like that, in Brick Hollow or in Haven. Most people, once worn out, either stopped, or never set out to begin with, so as never to be worn out at all.

Father had been quietly watching Temel pack his things all this while. He watched, unblinking, like watching a familiar gesture. I couldn't tell what that gaze was directed at. Maybe Father, too, was faintly recalling some parting he himself had forgotten.

"…Have we been a burden to you at all?" I asked. "We kept you from your own work, following you around."

Temel shook his head.

"…Never thought of it as a burden," Temel said. "If anything, thanks to you two, I got to walk a few whole days alongside someone again, for once. Usually I find someone and we part right away."

"…So we were good for you too?" I asked again.

"…Yes," Temel said, smiling. "Much more than I expected."

Far off, I could hear wind combing through the dry grass. That sound seemed to go on, like the background music of this fork in the road. I thought that if partings had a fitting sound, it would be one like that.

Temel adjusted his pack straps and looked at each of us for a long moment. I understood that gaze as one preparing a last goodbye.

"…Will we get to see you again?" I asked. My throat tightened as I asked it.

"…I don't know," Temel said honestly. "But this road isn't so wide. We might cross paths again someday."

"…Please," my brother said, with unusual force.

Temel smiled at that.

Temel looked at us and spoke one last time.

"…Hold his place until your father finds his real name again. Losing a name doesn't have to mean losing the place where you're loved."

Father heard that and held out his hand to Temel. Temel took it.

"…Thank you," Father said. "For treating me this way, when even I don't know who I am."

"…There's always a reason to treat someone well, whether you know who they are or not," Temel said.

Father closed his eyes for a moment at that, then opened them. It wasn't the face of someone who'd fully understood. But it was the face of someone who had received something.

With that, Temel turned and walked off toward the other branch of the road. I watched his back grow smaller and smaller for a long time. At the last, Temel turned once and waved. I waved back.

Father waved toward that retreating back too. It was a strangely earnest gesture, for a farewell to a stranger. I thought that even if Father couldn't fully remember Temel, his body still knew, somehow, that he'd been someone precious.

My brother stayed rooted to the spot until Temel had completely disappeared. I stood quietly beside him, watching that empty road together.

Even after the sun had fully set, we stood there a little longer. No one suggested moving on first. I understood that silence as grief at the parting.

I set that line down as this chapter's last line. We parted with Temel that day and set off again, the three of us. I felt, for the first time, that this journey's purpose was shifting — from finding an answer, to holding a place.

(Continued in Chapter 30.)

Chapter 30. The Man Who Came in the Night

_Previously_ — The brother and sister gave Father a placeholder name, Waiting. Parting from Temel, they heard: "Losing a name doesn't have to mean losing the place where you're loved."

That night, Father woke suddenly, screaming.

We had made camp under a small tree that day. It had been a long walk, so everyone fell asleep early. The last thing I remember is counting stars and drifting off.

That day's walk had been unusually long. For the first time, Father let a little of his tiredness show. "…Should we walk a bit more?" he'd asked, in a voice that watched our faces even as he said it. I thought that carefulness was another habit that had stayed with him.

Moonlight filtered through the leaves and trembled across Father's face. I remember it was the last thing I saw before falling asleep. He'd looked peaceful.

My brother was already sound asleep beside me. Days of built-up tension had made him fall asleep unusually fast. I remember listening to his breathing and feeling my own eyes drift shut, slowly.

Insects called low in the grass. I fell asleep to that sound like a lullaby. I never imagined that peace would be shattered completely before the night was through.

I've gone back to that night many times since. That evening, nothing had been wrong with any of us. Father had smiled twice; my brother had tended the fire; I had counted stars. I learned then that the nights that come apart never announce themselves first.

"…Where am I! Why am I here!" Father shouted. His eyes were wide open. Looking straight at us, and recognizing nothing at all.

My brother tried to go to him and Father recoiled. The movement was so fast that, for an instant, I was terrified he'd turned into an entirely different person.

Father's back struck the tree trunk. He didn't seem to feel it at all. Both hands were thrust forward, pushing us away.

A branch shook and a few dry leaves fell. Even that small sound made Father flinch and curl in on himself. I understood, then. It wasn't us Father was afraid of. It was this whole night.

"…No one's here but us," my brother said. "It's just the three of us."

"…Three?" Father asked back. "Who are the three?"

My brother couldn't answer that. I saw his mouth open, then shut without a word. He must have understood, right then, that saying our names would do no good at all.

I froze with fear in that moment. What frightened me more than the thought of Father attacking us was the sight of that gaze — a gaze that saw us as complete strangers.

"…It's all right, it's all right," my brother said, holding up both hands, keeping his voice as low as he could. "We won't hurt you."

Father didn't calm at that either. He kept gasping and looking around wildly. Watching him, unable to do anything, only made it more terrifying.

His eyes darted here and there. As if trying to recognize something, or as if just searching for a hole to run through. I couldn't find, in that gaze, a single trace of the gentle Father I'd seen by day.

I half hid behind my brother and watched Father. Under the moonlight his shadow looked unusually large and unsteady. I thought that shadow looked like it belonged to someone else entirely.

I understood, for the first time then, that getting Father back wasn't a single event. The Father who had held my hand last night, and the Father who didn't recognize us tonight, being the same person — that was what frightened me more.

My brother stood there, hands still raised, not moving. I saw him frozen in place, unable to move forward or back. The stance of someone who didn't want to fight but couldn't run either.

I hated myself in that moment for being useless. All I could do was stand off at a distance, looking back and forth between my brother and Father.

That night I mimicked something like prayer for the first time. I didn't even know who I was praying to. I just kept repeating, in my mind, "please." I didn't know if that counted as a prayer.

I had my fists clenched so tight my nails dug into my palms. I didn't even feel the pain. I only noticed the marks it had left afterward.

"…It's all right, no one's getting hurt," my brother said again, low. His voice shook, unlike his words.

Father still looked around with eyes that didn't recognize us. I found, in that moment, that I was afraid even to call Father "Father" — those eyes didn't look like his at all.

Then, quietly, someone stepped out of the dark.

"…It's all right," said a familiar voice. "Breathe slowly."

I recognized that voice. The man from the flowerbed. Or — no, just the man, now. The one who'd been standing where no one had planted anything, the one who'd also been seen at the well.

"…What are you doing here…" My brother couldn't finish the sentence, too startled.

I could tell there was relief mixed into my brother's shock. In that moment he said neither scared nor glad. But he must have been both.

He'd made almost no sound as he walked. I had no idea how long he'd been nearby. He appeared as quietly as if he'd always been part of the darkness itself.

I looked again toward the direction he'd come from. No trees, no brush that way. Just darkness. I had no time to think it strange — I was only relieved that he had come.

He said nothing, and quietly sat down in front of Father.

There was no hurry at all in his movement. Strangely, that unhurriedness seemed to be settling Father down even faster. As if the not-rushing itself were a kind of comfort.

He didn't touch Father. Didn't grip him, didn't pat his shoulder. He simply sat at a distance where an arm might just barely reach, and looked in the same direction Father was looking.

I only understood later how exact that distance was. Too far, and it's leaving someone alone. Too close, and it's pushing them. He knew that single hand's-width between the two, exactly.

I stood beside them, holding my brother's hand. His hand was still shaking. I gripped it tighter. He gripped mine back. We endured that moment together, leaning on each other.

Moonlight lit the man's back. A blurred outline showed through his worn clothes. I thought that silhouette wasn't unfamiliar. At the flowerbed, by the well, and now here. I found that repetition strange, and at the same time, strangely comforting.

Leaf-shadows trembled across his back. I noticed that shadow brushed against his body without changing him at all. Wind, darkness — to him, they were only things passing through.

I had a strange thought in that moment. That we had never called him here. It was Father who had shouted; no one had asked anyone for help. And yet he had come.

Looking back, I think I already heard one answer that night. That there are those who come even when unsummoned. I didn't understand what that meant, then.

"…Who am I?" Father asked him. A question he hadn't asked us, he asked him.

A strange hurt passed through me in that moment. A question he hadn't asked us, he was asking a stranger. Looking back now, that wasn't because Father distrusted us. It was, I think, simply that there was nothing he needed to prove to this man.

I looked at my brother in that moment. He was looking at me too. We confirmed the same small hurt in each other's expressions. Neither of us said a word. But something passed between us anyway, in that look.

Father was leaning slightly toward the man. I thought that posture looked far too at ease, for a posture toward a stranger.

My brother was watching quietly too, beside me. I could feel him wanting to step in and holding himself back. His hand twitched, more than once, and returned to stillness.

"…You don't need to know right now," the man said. "You don't have a name, but you're here right now. That's enough."

Father's breathing gradually settled.

I watched that shift happen beside them. Watching the shoulders of someone who'd been screaming minutes ago slowly lower — it was like watching a storm die down.

"…Aren't you afraid of me?" Father asked.

"…I'm not afraid," he said. "I've met many people who lost their names."

There was no exaggeration at all in his voice. He spoke plainly, like stating a fact. I found that plainness, somehow, more trustworthy than anything else.

"…Did they all find their names again?" Father asked. There was longing in his voice.

"…Some of them," he said. "Some are still looking. But either way, they weren't lost while they were looking."

Father sat quietly, turning that answer over. I watched his shoulders lower, bit by bit. He didn't seem to understand the full meaning of it. But something was calming him.

"…Will I find mine too, someday?" Father asked again. His voice was much calmer than before.

"…Yes," he said. "But I don't know when. No one does."

"…And you wait, even so?" Father asked.

"…Yes," he said. "Waiting is a little easier, too, when there's someone staying with you."

"…Is that what you're doing here with me right now?" Father asked.

"…Yes," he said simply. No hesitation at all.

"…Who are you waiting for?" Father asked.

I held my breath, listening to that question. I'd wondered it myself, badly.

He was quiet for a moment.

"…It's less waiting," he said, "than going out to search."

"…For whom?"

"…For those who haven't come back yet," he said.

Father nodded slowly at that. Not the nod of someone who understood completely. But the nod of someone trying to understand.

Hearing that, I looked at my brother. His face was complicated. Suspicion, and something he couldn't quite overcome, both there at once.

He went on speaking to Father, low, for a while. I didn't catch all of it. But that tone was strangely like the one our mother had used to sing us to sleep in Brick Hollow.

I couldn't explain that resemblance then. I still can't, exactly, now. But from that night on, I learned that I could feel familiarity even in an unfamiliar voice.

I edged a little closer, trying to hear that voice more clearly. But strangely, the closer I got, the fainter the words became. As if those words weren't meant for me at all, only for Father. I stepped back quietly at that.

Father's breathing slowed, little by little. I sat beside him and listened to that rhythm. It was hard to believe it was the same person who'd been shouting like the world was ending, only minutes before.

After Father had fallen back asleep, my brother went up to the man.

"…Who exactly are you?" my brother asked. There was an edge in his voice. "Brick Hollow, Haven, and now here too… why do you keep showing up?"

He looked at my brother for a long time.

"…I've always been here," he said. "You just didn't recognize me."

"…What does that mean?"

"…In Brick Hollow, in Haven, and here too, now," he said. "I've never once left you."

My brother let out a hollow laugh at that.

"…Then why can't you help Father?" my brother asked. His voice rose. "Why don't you give his memory back? You just said you were always here."

He didn't answer, and my brother stepped closer.

"…If you were really always here," my brother said, "were you there when we fell carrying bricks? When Mother was sick? Were you there then too?"

He nodded at my brother.

"…I was."

"…Then why didn't you do anything!" my brother shouted. At that shout, Father stirred in his sleep. My brother clamped his mouth shut instantly. Then, in a much lower voice, he said it again. "…Why didn't you do anything."

He smiled quietly. He didn't answer.

I couldn't bear that silence and wanted to say something in his place. But nothing came to mind. All I could do was lightly grip my brother's arm.

He simply rose and looked up at the night sky for a moment. Starlight passed across his face like a brush. In that brief instant, I thought I saw something like grief cross his features. I couldn't be certain.

I still can't describe that expression exactly, even now. It wasn't anger. It wasn't grief either. But it was, unmistakably, the face of someone who had endured something for a long time.

Then he turned once to look at us, and walked back into the darkness he'd come from.

Wind blew from the direction he'd disappeared into. I caught no scent at all in that wind. If a person had passed through, something should have been left behind. There was nothing.

Somewhere in the grass, the night insects began to call again. Only then did I realize the sound had cut off completely, just moments before. It felt like the world had held its breath and now let it go.

I went and stood beside my brother, watching that retreating back. He didn't say anything. He just stood there for a long time.

"…Brother," I said quietly.

"…I'm not angry," my brother said. But his voice shook, unlike his words. "I just… don't know."

I took his arm. He didn't pull away.

"…Brother, I'm cold," I said. It wasn't true. But I thought if I said it, he might sit down.

Only then did he sit. We sat side by side beside the dying fire and listened to Father's breathing for a long time. It was even, and slow.

I set that unanswered silence down as this chapter's last line. That night I saw something in my brother's face deeper than anger. I thought, then, that it was fear. I know now. It was my brother's certainty, beginning to crack — just a little.

(Continued in Chapter 31.)

Chapter 31. Humming

_Previously_ — When Father woke in the depths of a violent confusion, the man from the flowerbed appeared again and calmed him. Jeteo asked him outright who he was, and got only: "I've always been here. You just didn't recognize me."

The man was gone the next morning, without a word.

I don't know when he left. Once, waking in the middle of the night, I'd seen him clearly, sitting across the fire. The next time I opened my eyes, he was gone.

The instant I woke, I looked first at the spot where he'd sat. Only the grass, faintly pressed down, remained. Not even a clear footprint. The trace was so slight it made me doubt whether he'd really been there at all.

I touched that pressed patch of grass. It still felt faintly warm, or maybe I only imagined it. I couldn't be certain.

Morning mist hung low. Dewdrops glinted on every blade of grass. I noticed, belatedly, that there was no dew at all in the exact spot where he'd sat. I thought about mentioning it to my brother and decided against it. It seemed like it would only make him angrier.

My brother stared hard at that spot for a long while, then finally turned away. I felt that he was angry at the lack of any trace, and at the same time, hoping for something. Hoping that next time, a little more would be left behind.

"…Gone again, just like that," my brother said, his voice hollow. "Every time. Shows up, then no explanation at all."

"…Couldn't even say goodbye," my brother went on. "Could've at least said where he was going."

"…There wasn't time to ask," I said.

"…He didn't give us time to ask," my brother said.

I nearly laughed at that. When my brother was angry, his words got precise.

But even after saying that, he stood there a long while. I understood he wasn't standing there out of anger. He was waiting, in case the man came back. Neither of us said that out loud.

My brother repacked his things twice that morning. I understood he wasn't packing so much as looking for something to do with his hands.

I had nothing to argue against what he'd said. But strangely, his vanishing felt different to me from being abandoned. I couldn't explain exactly why, not then.

I thought about that difference all through the morning. Someone who leaves you behind, and someone who leaves once their work is done. The two look the same from the front, but different from behind. I had never once seen him leave with the look of someone in a hurry.

Father didn't even remember that the man had come at all. That grieved me. The voice that had calmed us so completely the night before — Father himself didn't carry so much as a trace of it.

"…Did someone come last night?" Father asked over breakfast, as if it were nothing.

My brother and I exchanged a glance. Neither of us knew how to explain it.

"…Yes, briefly," I managed to say.

"…Someone you know?" Father asked.

"…No, a stranger," my brother answered for me. His voice had a slight edge.

"…A stranger, and he came at night?" Father asked again.

My brother struggled to find words for a moment.

"…That's the part I find strangest too," my brother finally said.

"…I see," Father nodded. He didn't press further. I found that indifference a relief, and grieving, both at once.

Father tore off small pieces of stale bread and ate. It was hard to believe those were the same hands that had pushed us away in fear the night before. Between the Father of daytime and the Father of night, there seemed to be no connection at all.

Crumbs fell onto Father's knee. He picked them up one by one with his fingertips and ate them. That ordinary little gesture was so plain that, strangely, it felt more unfamiliar to me than anything.

I studied Father's face for a long time that morning. There was a slight hollowness under his eyes. His body remembered last night. Only his mind did not.

I thought, then. Perhaps there are things that stay even when memory doesn't. Like that song.

That afternoon, Father hummed something low as he walked.

Sunlight fell in mottled patches through the tree shade. A gentle breeze blew. It was an ordinary afternoon. I think that sound came through so clearly precisely because it was such an ordinary afternoon.

We walked without much talk that day. Everyone was a little worn from the night before. There was nothing but the rustle of dry dirt underfoot and, far off, a bird's call.

In that quiet, Father's voice leaked out very softly. It wasn't words. There were no lyrics. Just a melody.

The moment I heard that sound, I stopped walking. I knew that melody. It was the one Mother used to sing to put us to sleep.

In Brick Hollow, Mother had always paused in the same place in that song. Whether it was because she ran short of breath, or because the song itself was written to pause there, I don't know. Father paused in exactly that same spot too.

I heard that breath and understood. Father wasn't remembering the song. He was remembering the room. The room where Mother sang, where we lay down, where Father stood by the door.

"…Father, what song is that?" I asked urgently.

Father stopped humming and looked at me. His face was startled.

"…I don't know," Father said. "It just… came out on its own."

Father touched his own mouth, smiling in wonder. It seemed strange even to him, that his body could remember something before his mind did.

"…Father, just a little more," I said. I found I'd caught his sleeve without meaning to.

Father looked down at my hand. He didn't look startled.

"…I'm sorry," Father said. "I didn't even know what I was singing."

"…Nothing to be sorry for," I said quickly. "There's nothing at all to be sorry for."

My heart felt like it would burst in that moment. I looked at my brother. His face showed he'd caught the melody too.

Some of the color had drained from my brother's face. I couldn't tell if he was glad or frightened. Probably he didn't know either.

My brother had stopped walking and couldn't say a word. I saw his lips tremble slightly. He looked like someone who wanted to sing along with that melody, and was holding back for fear something might break if he did.

"…Can you keep singing?" my brother asked. His voice shook.

Father tried to hum again. But it broke off after a few bars.

"…I don't remember what comes after," Father said, an apologetic look on his face.

His eyebrows drew together faintly. The expression of someone trying to grasp something that kept slipping through his fingers. I found watching that expression more painful than watching the night he'd screamed.

Father went back to the beginning several times and started over. Each time he stopped at the same place. As if the road itself broke off there.

"…This is as far as it goes," Father said. "There's nothing past this."

I felt that this wasn't only about the song.

My brother quietly came and sat beside Father.

"…It's all right," my brother said. "Maybe it'll come out again later."

Father looked at my brother and gave a small nod. I felt my brother was saying that to himself, as much as to Father.

My brother must already have known then that there was no guarantee it would come out again next time. And still he said it. I only understood later that this wasn't a lie — it was another name for waiting.

A strong gust of wind blew through and passed. The branches knocked together and made a brief clatter. I waited for that sound to settle and said nothing.

I got up quietly and walked off a little way from the two of them. I needed time to steady myself. As I walked, I looked up at the sky. A pale daytime moon hung faint above.

I looked at that daytime moon and, in my mind, sang the song from the beginning. It came out to the end without a single break. I felt sorry for that. That I knew all of it, while Father knew only half — it felt, strangely, like my own fault.

I understood then, for the first time, that memory isn't fair. The same people had been in that same room. And still, what remained to each of them, in what amount, was different.

"…It's all right," I said. Tears came. "That's enough."

I picked up the rest and sang it, exactly as Mother used to. Father closed his eyes, listening to that sound.

My voice shook. My pitch wavered more than once. I sang it through anyway. My brother came quietly beside me and started humming along. Three voices — or more exactly, two voices and Father's silence — blended together, right there.

As I sang, I watched Father's face. His mouth wasn't moving. But his fingers were tapping very lightly against his knee. The beat was exact.

I watched that finger for a long time. It was the same hand that had counted numbers. That day, that same hand was counting a beat.

"…Strangely, it makes me feel at ease," Father said. "Even though I don't know whose song it was."

"…It was Mother's song," my brother said quietly. The first piece of information he'd offered up first, on his own.

Father opened his eyes and looked at my brother.

"…Mother," Father repeated the word slowly. "There was someone like that."

"…What kind of person was she?" Father asked.

My brother couldn't answer for a long moment. I understood he wasn't struggling for words — he was gauging where to start without falling apart.

"…Her voice was low," my brother finally said. "And she didn't laugh often, but when she did, she laughed for a long time."

"…Sounds like she was a good person," Father said.

"…Yes," my brother said. "A good person to you, too."

Father tilted his head slightly at that, but didn't ask further.

Hearing that, my chest ached. To Father now, Mother was only "someone like that." And yet, even at those words, there was some kind of longing in his expression. He might not know it in words, but somewhere, his heart seemed to know.

In that moment, I thought of the room back in Brick Hollow, on the last night, where Mother had sung us that song as we fell asleep. The low lamplight, Mother's voice and Father's breath side by side in that room. I felt that memory completing itself again, right here, right now.

In that moment I understood again why I was still keeping this record. This song, this moment — these were the things I would give back to Father someday, when he came fully home.

That night I tried to write the melody down on paper. But I didn't know how to put a tune into words. In the end I just wrote: "Today, Father hummed Mother's song. He couldn't finish it. But he sang."

Under that sentence I added one more line. "Someday, when Father reads this paper, even if he doesn't remember today, he'll know that today happened." Writing that line, I felt, for the first time, sure that this record wasn't only for me.

I folded the paper and pushed it deep into my pocket. The paper was already worn soft from being folded and unfolded so many times. I decided that the more those creases multiplied, the more days we'd walked together.

Father fell asleep unusually early that night. I couldn't tell if singing had worn him out, or if his heart had simply been at ease for once. I watched his sleeping face for a long time. Asleep, at least, Father looked like someone who hadn't lost anything at all.

I sat beside him and hummed that song once more, very quietly. I don't know if Father heard it. But his eyebrow twitched once in his sleep.

I decided to count that twitch as an answer. That night, there was nothing else left to count.

That night, my brother spoke to me quietly.

"…I still don't know," he said. "Who that man is. Whether I should believe in God."

The firelight's shadows flickered across my brother's face. I could tell he was choosing his words more carefully than usual.

The fire crackled small every now and then as it burned down. Each time, my brother would pause and then go on. I understood he wasn't startled by the sound — he was buying himself time to speak.

"…But?" I asked.

"…But today, listening to that humming, I thought — just for a moment — that maybe, like whatever's still left in Father, there's something left in me too. Something not completely erased."

I said nothing and just listened. I thought if I interrupted, he'd close back up.

"…I still don't know what it is," my brother went on. "But I can feel that it's there. Strange, isn't it."

"…Not strange," I said. "I have something like that too."

I really did feel that. I couldn't say exactly what it was. But that day, the certainty that something uneraseable existed inside me too felt unusually clear.

My brother looked at me. A little surprised.

"…What is it?" he asked.

"…I don't know either," I said, laughing. "But it's there."

My brother laughed out loud at that. It had been a long time since I'd heard him laugh.

The laughter faded and he went quiet again.

"…Elpis." My brother called my name.

"…Yeah."

"…I still hate him," my brother said. "I hated him last night too. Even watching him put Father to sleep, I hated him."

"…I know," I said.

"…But even hating him, I want him to come back," my brother said. "That's what makes me angriest."

"…Can you hate someone and wait for them at the same time?" I asked.

"…I don't know," my brother said. "But that's what I'm doing right now. Both of them are real."

I didn't know what to say to that, so I just leaned my head against his shoulder. He didn't push me away.

Even now, thinking back on that night, that sentence is the first thing that comes to mind. Wanting him to come back, even while hating him. I think that was my brother's first prayer. He would never have called it that himself.

The embers gave one last small pop and died down. I watched that small light go out, still leaning against my brother's shoulder. Neither of us moved until the darkness had covered us completely.

I set that line down as this chapter's last line. I understood how enormous it was that my brother had said those words, out loud, to himself, and to me, for the first time.

(Continued in Chapter 32.)

Chapter 32. The Light Returns

_Previously_ — Father, without meaning to, hummed a fragment of Mother's lullaby as he walked — the first piece of his earliest memory left in his body. Jeteo, for the first time, said aloud to himself: "Maybe there's something in me too that hasn't been erased completely."

Some nights later, that low light appeared again in the sky.

We were crossing a broad open field that day. There was no good place to take cover. The moment I realized it, a chill ran down my spine.

The grass in that field rose to our knees. By day, that openness had felt refreshing. By night, that same openness turned into pure defenselessness. There was only, here and there, far apart, a single tree, a single boulder.

I found myself, out of habit, counting hiding places as we walked. One, two. I couldn't count a third. I realized, that day, that this habit had formed after we'd left Haven.

We had all but forgotten that light in the meantime. Caring for Father, walking, simply surviving each day — we'd been consumed by all of it. I understood, that night, all over again, how dangerous that forgetting had been.

Even now, I find that strange. People forget fear too. There isn't enough strength in the heart to be afraid every single day. I used to think that forgetting was laziness. Now I think it was also a way of staying alive.

My brother spotted the light first and stopped walking. I watched his body go rigid in an instant. The same reaction as weeks before. But this time, the speed of it had grown even faster.

Watching that speed troubled me. That my brother had gotten better at sensing danger was a relief. But at the same time, it meant he was carving that much more fear into his body.

"…Searching light," my brother said, low. A familiar fear had returned to his voice.

The light moved very slowly, over near the horizon. Unhurried. That unhurriedness frightened me more. A hurried thing might miss you. That light looked like it had no intention of missing anything.

The insect sounds cut off abruptly right then. I couldn't tell if that was coincidence. But it felt like the whole field was holding its breath.

The instant I saw that light, I checked on Father first. He had no idea what it was — he was simply gazing up at the sky.

I envied that ignorance. One glimpse of that light and our hearts dropped, while Father just stood there admiring something curious in the night sky. I thought, in that moment, that Father having been "completed" might, in some way, be a kind of shield.

I was ashamed of that thought later. If ignorance was a shield, it was a shield built by taking something from Father first and covering the wound with it. I wanted Father to come back to being someone who could feel afraid. Knowing how to fear is part of being alive, too.

"…It's pretty," Father said, innocent. Something in me ached to hear it. Father had no idea at all what that light meant.

Father even lifted a hand and pointed at the light. A gesture as pure as a child pointing at a star. I found that purity unusually precarious that night.

"…It's not pretty," my brother said. His voice was harder than usual.

"…No?" Father asked. "Then why does it shine like that?"

My brother didn't answer. I think it wasn't that he couldn't answer — it was that he didn't want to. To explain what that light shone for, he'd have had to explain, first, why we were running.

"…Father, quickly," my brother said urgently, tugging Father's arm. "We'll explain later."

Father let himself be pulled along, still with no idea what was happening. I found that compliance both a relief and a grief. The old Father would have looked after us first and led the way himself.

"…We need to hide," my brother said. We ducked quickly behind a low boulder. Father crouched down with us. He probably didn't know exactly why. But he followed without resistance.

The boulder was small for three people. My brother pushed his own body toward the outer edge. I knew that wasn't an accident. He always positioned himself that way.

"…Brother, get in," I whispered.

"…I'm fine," my brother said.

I don't know how many times I've heard those two words over the course of this journey. My brother always said he was fine. I know he was never once fine.

That compliance struck me as strange. Two strange children suddenly pulling at his arm, and Father crouched down without a moment's resistance. It was as if some sense for danger was still alive, somewhere in his body.

As Father lowered himself, he spread both arms toward us. A sheltering posture. He himself didn't seem to notice he was doing it.

I remember those arms even now. Father didn't know who we were. But his body knew how to protect us. That night, I understood for the first time that what is lost and what is erased are not the same thing.

The light drew closer. I held my breath.

The boulder's cold surface pressed against my back. Strangely, that cold helped me focus. Fear, I learned that night, sharpens every sense in the body.

A damp smell rose from the earth. Beside me I heard my brother's breath, very shallow. Father's breathing, by contrast, was even and deep. I found that difference strange enough to glance his way. His eyes were closed.

In that moment I thought of Haven's quiet corridors. We had held our breath there too. But back then, we hadn't even known what we were hiding from. Now I know. I still don't know whether knowing is better.

A strange thought crossed my mind in that moment. Whether that light was searching for us, or for Father. Father was already a "completed" person. Does someone completed still get come looking for?

A single blade of grass tickled my cheek. I couldn't brush it away, couldn't move at all. Enduring that small itch was the only resistance I had left in that moment.

The light stopped, briefly, right over our heads.

The light made no sound while it hovered there. That was the most frightening part. If it had made a sound, I could at least have guessed where it was. That light only shone.

Wherever the light touched, the grass cast long shadows. I turned my head, very slightly, trying to gauge how far our shadows stretched out past the boulder. My brother pressed down on my wrist. A signal not to move.

That moment felt eternal. My brother gripped my hand tight. Father took my other hand too. He didn't know the reason, but he seemed to sense that something was dangerous.

The color of the light shifted, just slightly. The white flickered, for a moment, into a pale yellow. I didn't know what that meant. I only watched that flicker and held my breath.

That yellow tinge lasted only an instant. But in that instant, a strange thought crossed my mind. That maybe this light was wandering too, unable to find what it was looking for.

I told my brother that thought later. He was baffled. "That's a machine," he said. I thought he was right, and still thought that not finding what you're looking for must be just as frustrating for a machine as for a person.

In that moment I prayed, in my mind, though I didn't know to whom. I only asked for one thing — that the light would pass by.

The light moved again. This time, not toward us, but off in a different direction.

I followed that direction with my eyes. The light was heading toward the path we'd come from, toward the spot where we'd rested earlier. I couldn't tell if that was pure chance, or if something had turned it aside.

I've gone back over that night many times since. That light had been directly above us. We were three people crowded onto one small rock, and Father's coat had spilled out past its edge. There was no reason we shouldn't have been seen. I still can't explain, to this day, why we weren't found that night.

I couldn't breathe properly until that light had disappeared completely. What came before relief was a strange guilt. The thought that this light might be searching for someone else right now.

In the stillness of that night, I heard only my own heartbeat, unusually loud. It took a while for that sound to settle.

"…Are you all right?" my brother asked me. Only then did I realize I was trembling.

"…Yes," I said. It was a lie. My brother's face showed he knew I was lying. But he only squeezed my hand once more.

"…That was scary," my brother said. Not a question.

In that moment I found myself thinking, strangely, of the man from the flowerbed. If he'd been here right now, what would he have said? I thought he probably would have just sat beside us in silence.

"…Weren't you scared?" I asked.

My brother was quiet for a moment.

"…I was," he said. "But I'm not someone who's allowed to be scared."

"…There's no such person," I said.

My brother didn't answer that. He just looked up at the sky once more.

Father sat quietly between us. He clearly had no idea what had happened, but he seemed to understand it had been something serious. Father carefully laid a hand on my shoulder. I still remember, even now, how great a comfort the weight of that hand was, in that moment.

We stayed crouched there a good while longer. My legs began to cramp, but no one spoke first about moving. I didn't think that silence was only about fear. All three of us, I think, were being careful, afraid that something would change if this moment stretched even a little longer.

By the time my legs had gone fully numb, Father shifted position first. He asked me, "…are you all right?" I nearly laughed at that question. The person we'd thought we needed to protect, just moments before, was the one looking after us first.

Once the light had disappeared, my brother spoke low.

"…We really do need to get far from Haven now. This place must still be inside that light's reach."

"…Where do we go?" I asked.

"…Anywhere," Father cut in carefully. "As long as I'm not a burden to you."

Father said that while watching our faces closely. I found that carefulness unfamiliar. Father had never been the kind of person to watch others' faces like that — at least, not the Father I remembered.

My brother thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. But we have to keep walking. Father, and both of us — staying here is dangerous."

"…How much further until it's safe?" I asked.

"…I don't know," my brother said. "That's the whole problem. No one tells you how far you have to go."

Father looked up carefully at the sky at that.

"…I don't know the direction," Father said, "but the constellations somehow look familiar to me. Would that help?"

My brother and I looked at each other. It was an unexpected thing to hear from a father who claimed to remember nothing.

I thought about that for a long time. Neither when we left Brick Hollow, nor when we left Haven, did we know how far we had to go. We had always walked without knowing the end. I think that not-knowing is the name of this whole story.

I saw, on my brother's face, the confident look he used to wear before. He'd always led the way with a face like that, leaving Haven, leaving Brick Hollow. That face was welcome to see again, and at the same time it worried me — that he might be trying to shoulder everything alone once more.

"…You don't have to decide alone," I said.

My brother looked at me. A little surprised.

"…I know," he said. "But someone has to say which way to go."

"…Then let's say it together," I said.

My brother smiled faintly at that. There was relief in that smile, and also a strange, unfamiliar lightness.

"…Me too," Father said carefully. "I don't know anything, so I can't decide the direction. But I can help say it, at least."

My brother looked a little startled at that. Then he laughed.

"…Yes," my brother said. "That's enough."

Father heard that and asked quietly.

"…Am I a burden?"

Father's voice was calm. But behind that calm there was something braced, as if for the worst. My heart sank, seeing that expression.

My brother looked at Father, startled.

"…No," my brother said firmly. "You're not a burden. We're going together."

I took Father's hand too.

"…That's right," I said. "Don't ever say burden again."

Father looked back and forth between the two of us, then nodded, small. Not a face that fully believed it. But in that moment, at least, he didn't question it further.

The night wind blew and stirred the hems of our three coats. I felt that even in that wind, the hands the three of us held onto each other never wavered.

Somewhere far off, I heard something like a dog barking. It meant people lived nearby. I started to feel relieved at the sound, then remembered — where there are people, there's a searching light too — and tightened my guard again.

From that night on, I learned to see the world in two layers. Behind every welcome thing, there was always some danger attached. I thought that was what it meant to grow up. It was early to learn it, at thirteen.

Father heard all that and laughed quietly. A laugh I hadn't seen from him before. A laugh with both relief and shyness mixed into it.

"…Thank you," Father said. A short sentence. But I could feel the weight carried inside it.

"…For what?" I asked.

"…I don't know," Father said. "I just felt thankful. That word came first."

My brother heard that answer and laughed quietly too.

"…I have that too," my brother said. "Words that come out without a reason. It keeps happening to me lately."

"…Such as?" Father asked.

My brother hesitated a moment.

"…The word it's all right," my brother said. "Even when it's really not all right, that word comes first."

I liked that answer. It meant that words coming out without a reason were still alive somewhere inside Father.

I set that line down as this chapter's last line. I saw my brother look surprised at himself as he said those words. As he said them to Father, he seemed to be saying them to himself, at the very same time.

(Continued in Chapter 33.)

Chapter 33. A Night on the Run

_Previously_ — The searching light appeared again. It passed, but relief left guilt behind it. When Father asked, "Am I a burden to you?" Jeteo answered, "We're going together."

Two nights later, it wasn't the light that came — it was people.

That day had been unusually peaceful. Father had even picked a small wildflower along the road and handed it to me. Looking back, that peace hadn't given the slightest warning of what the evening held, which only made it more frightening.

The wildflower was yellow and very small. Handing it over, Father said, "…this is for you." The tone of someone for whom giving something to another person felt awkward, even to himself.

I pressed that flower between two sheets of paper. Later it dried flat, leaving only a faded stain on the page. I still have that stain.

My brother had been unusually on edge since evening. He stopped several times and looked back over his shoulder. I'd thought it was just habit. Looking back now, I think he'd already sensed something.

Toward evening the wind changed direction. It carried, not the scent of dry grass, but something like burning. I didn't know what that smell was, then. I know now. It was the smell of a fire people had lit.

Father, too, had been unusually quiet that day. I still wonder whether that was coincidence, or whether the three of us had already been sensing something, without knowing it.

At dinner, Father suddenly asked.

"…Are we being followed?"

My brother and I both looked at Father at once.

"…Why do you think that?" my brother asked.

"…I don't know," Father said. "I just keep wanting to look back. Both of you keep doing it too."

I felt ashamed at that. We hadn't explained anything to Father. And yet he had already known, just from watching our bodies. There are things people know even without being told.

At first I thought it was the wind. But there was a rhythm to it. Wind has no rhythm.

Footsteps. Several of them. We ducked quickly behind a stand of dry brush.

In that moment I gripped Father's hand with everything I had. I wanted him to understand that we needed to stay quiet. But Father, frightened by the unfamiliar sound, began trembling instead.

The brush's dry branches rustled against our clothes. Even that faint sound felt far too loud, so I made myself breathe with the greatest care.

The ground still held a little of the day's heat. I felt that faint warmth against my back and had the strange thought that this earth knew we were here.

Voices. Low, businesslike voices.

No anger, no urgency in that tone. I had heard that tone every day in Haven — behind the intake counters, in the corridors, handing over receipt slips. That night, the most frightening thing was that this same tone had followed us all the way out here to this field.

Their shadows stretched long under the moonlight. I counted them. Three.

"…You said there was a signal near here?"

"…Two Reassignment candidates. And a report that a completion record broke away too."

"…A completion broke away?" another voice asked. "Can that even happen?"

"…Rare," said the first voice. "That's why recovery's a top priority."

I heard the word "recovery" for the first time that night. A word used for objects. From the moment I heard it, I began to think of Haven differently. That city had never once hated us. To hate someone, you have to see them as a person first.

The instant I heard that, my whole body went cold. I understood the phrase "a completion record broke away" meant Father. It was the first time I truly grasped that even Father, to this city, was not a person but an entry.

I glanced sideways at Father's face. He had no idea what that conversation meant, and just gripped our hands tight. I thought his ignorance was, right then, a mercy. If he had known he was being called an "escaped record," I don't think he could have borne it.

Far off, insects called in fits and starts. I thought that irregularity resembled our own heartbeats. Even the world, it seemed, was frightened alongside us in that moment.

"…Shh." My brother pressed his hand gently over Father's mouth, as softly as he could. "We have to be quiet. Please."

Father nodded, eyes wide. He didn't know why, but he seemed to sense the danger.

That moment stayed with me a long time. Father didn't know who my brother was. And yet he trusted the hand covering his mouth. I thought, for the first time then, that trust lives somewhere beneath memory.

Father's hand kept gripping mine tightly. I felt a faint tremor through it. A body that had forgotten both name and face had, all the same, kept perfect hold of fear.

I wanted to calm that trembling. But I was shaking myself, so I couldn't tell who was calming whom.

The footsteps drew closer. Three people in Haven uniforms passed by. Bright tools in their hands.

Their clothes were clean. Their shoes were clean. Not the clothes of someone who'd walked a dirt road for days. I couldn't even imagine how or from where they had come this far.

One of them looked down at his wrist, reading something. Then shook his head. I understood our whole night hung on that single shake of the head.

Their pace didn't hurry at all. That very leisureliness frightened me more. It was the walk of people certain they would find us in the end.

I didn't know what that device was. But I knew it was for finding us.

"…The signal's weak around here," one said. "Why is that."

"…That's the boundary," another said. "The Ledger doesn't reach well out here either."

I held onto that line the longest that night. That there was a place in the world the Ledger couldn't reach. It should have sounded frightening. It sounded, instead, strangely welcome.

My brother pushed me gently behind him. Even in that moment, the gesture of protecting me first. I found that gesture familiar, and yet it hurt more than usual that night.

I gripped Father's hand tighter still, from behind my brother's back. Three of us were joined, that way, in a single line. I understood, in that moment, that there was nothing left we could do but hold onto each other.

I knew my brother was afraid too, in that moment. But he was trying to carry all of that fear alone. As he always had.

I watched my brother's back. His shoulders trembled faintly, holding his breath. I reached out and gently caught the hem of his shirt. I said nothing. I only wanted him to know I was here too.

My brother reached back without turning and took my hand. His eyes stayed forward the whole time. That hand trembled a little less than before, I thought. I learned that night: holding on makes the trembling smaller.

I understood, then, for the first time, how much sturdier a family of three could be. Alone, I'd have collapsed right there. Even the two of us together might have been much the same. But three of us — we became the strength that held each other up.

One of them stopped directly in front of the brush where we were hiding.

I could see the tips of his shoes. Through the leaves, only that much showed. I calculated that his shoe was one hand's-width away from touching Father's knee. I don't know why I calculated something like that.

I remember hearing no sound at all in that moment. But that can't be true. The wind was blowing, insects were calling. I think a person, truly frightened, loses the sounds of the world for a moment.

Time seemed to stop right there. I was terrified my heartbeat might carry outside. I could feel sweat breaking out on my brother's hand too.

I shut my eyes tight in that moment and thought of the last night in Brick Hollow. Holding our breath, hiding from the taskmasters' footsteps that night, felt exactly the same as this moment now. We were still surviving the same way.

I thought I would stop breathing. My brother pulled Father in tighter still.

Then Father tried to draw a deep breath. My heart plunged in that instant. If Father coughed, it was over.

But Father swallowed that breath back down. No one had told him to. I saw his throat move once, hard.

Even now, thinking of that moment, my throat tightens. Father hadn't forgotten how to protect us. He had lost his name, and still he hadn't lost that.

The man swept his light toward the brush. The beam passed right beside us.

I nearly imagined that light brushing my skin. Neither hot nor cold, and still every hair on my body stood on end.

The light swept through the inside of the brush. The dry branches went briefly white under it. I saw, in that instant, the back of my own hand go white too. I quickly pulled it inside my clothes.

I shut my eyes tight in that moment. For the first time in my life, I asked for something — not in words, just in my heart. I don't know if that counted as a prayer. I just asked.

In that brief moment, all the nights we'd run — Brick Hollow's night, Haven's alleys, every one of those fleeing nights — came flooding back at once. We had always been hiding from something. In that moment, I couldn't imagine when it would ever end.

I understood something strange, then. We had always been running from something. And yet inside that running, we had never once stopped holding onto each other. Maybe, I thought for the first time in that moment, that wasn't only running. Maybe it was something else too.

"…Nothing here," the man said, back toward the others.

"…You sure?"

"…Sure. Just brush."

I couldn't relax even hearing that. Because I alone knew the words weren't true.

The man soon turned and walked off in another direction.

I still couldn't open my eyes in that moment. Only once the footsteps had faded completely did I dare open them, carefully. My fingertips tingled and stung. I'd been gripping far too hard, for far too long.

There were my nail-marks left in Father's palm. I let go the instant I saw them. Father never once said it hurt.

"…I'm sorry," I whispered.

"…It's all right," Father said. "I liked being held."

Father said that while looking down for a moment at his own palm. He touched the marks there like something curious. He never once let on that they'd hurt.

Even after the footsteps had disappeared completely, we couldn't move from that spot for a long while.

My legs had gone numb and I couldn't stand. My brother seemed the same — he stayed sitting there, still buried in the brush. Father was the first to gather himself and help us both up.

As he lifted us, Father brushed the dirt from our knees. My brother's knee first, then mine. I thought, somehow, that order felt like a very old one.

Father's hand felt unusually steady in that moment. Memory was gone, but the body still knew how to raise its children back onto their feet.

Feeling that touch, I suddenly thought of some morning back in Brick Hollow. Back then, too, Father had brushed the dust from our clothes to start the day.

My brother finally let out a breath and looked up at the sky.

"…Where are you," my brother said, low, but clearly. I knew who it was addressed to. "You don't show up when it matters, and yet that time — that time you did."

My brother's voice wasn't loud. But the field was wide enough that the words traveled far, and came back bringing nothing at all.

"…You came the night Father was screaming," my brother said. "Nobody died that night. Today all three of us nearly died. And today — where were you."

I set that question down here now, exactly as it was, without softening it. It was the most honest sentence in this whole story. I have no intention of making it gentler.

No answer came at all. The night sky was just a night sky.

There were so many stars. So many. I found myself angry at how many there were, that night. All that many, and not one had answered.

Father watched my brother quietly. He clearly understood nothing. And still Father went and stood beside him, and laid a hand on his shoulder without a word. My brother didn't shake it off.

"…Hard, isn't it," Father said. That was all. Not an explanation, not comfort. Just the words of someone who had recognized that another person was in pain.

My brother bowed his head at that. I saw his shoulders heave once, hard.

I looked at that hand and felt a strange comfort. Someone who had lost both name and memory still knew how to lay a single hand on the shoulder of someone breaking down over an answer he'd never received. I thought that might be the deepest thing still left in Father.

I watched the two of them's silhouette for a long while. A son who'd gotten no answer, and a father who couldn't even understand the question, standing side by side. And yet the picture, strangely, didn't feel only sad.

After a while I went and stood beside them too. The three of us stood in a row and looked up at the same sky. No one said a word.

"…I'm hungry," my brother said, after a long while.

I burst out laughing at that. Just moments earlier he'd been shouting up at the sky, and this was his very next line.

"…Me too," Father said.

That night we split what bread was left, three ways, in tiny pieces. I think that was the only answer we received that night.

Chewing his bread, Father said suddenly.

"…It's good." Such an ordinary thing to say. But somehow, in that moment, it made the three of us smile, just a little.

Even after we'd finished the bread, we stayed sitting there a long while. The night wind was gradually dying down. I felt it fading and held onto one single fact — that this night, too, would eventually pass.

Father pushed the last piece of bread toward my brother. My brother refused it a moment, then finally took it. I think that small back-and-forth was the most ordinary moment of that whole night.

I set that silence down as this chapter's last line. I understood, then, how justified my brother's question was. I couldn't give him an answer. No one could. And yet, in that silence, I held onto one single fact — that we were still alive.

(Continued in Chapter 34.)

Chapter 34. The Wrong Direction

_Previously_ — A Haven search party truly did come after the siblings. Once the danger had passed, Jeteo asked the night sky, "You don't show up when it matters, and yet that time you did?" — and got only silence in return.

The next day, we set out again, as quietly as we could.

None of us had slept properly. Everyone's eyes were hollow. I flinched all day, overreacting to every footstep. Even the crack of a breaking twig made me jump more than once.

My brother changed our course twice that morning. Not the direction we'd meant to go, but a wide, deliberate loop around it. "…We're not going the way those people went yesterday," was all he said.

Because of that we walked far slower than usual. I found that slowness frustrating, and also a relief. Staying alive mattered more than being fast.

The sky was as clear as it had been the day before. I found that clarity strangely alien. The world seemed entirely untroubled by what had happened to us the night before.

I thought, for the first time then, about the world's indifference. The night we held our breath, so close to death, had been, to the sky, just one more night passing by. I found that indifference bitter, and at the same time, strangely comforting. It meant the world wasn't singling us out specifically.

I thought about mentioning that to Father and decided against it. Father had all but forgotten the night before already. Bringing that night back up in front of someone who'd forgotten it seemed cruel.

My brother stayed almost entirely silent all that day. I didn't want to disturb him, so I simply walked beside him.

I understood, then, that my brother's silence was connected to the question from the night before. A question left unanswered, I learned that day, stays on afterward, in silence, in place of an answer.

Now and then I saw my brother glance up at the sky. It looked like searching for something, and also like resentment. I didn't ask.

Once, he stopped walking entirely and looked up. He looked for quite a while. I looked up too. Nothing there but a few clouds.

"…What are you looking at?" I asked.

"…Nothing," my brother said. And started walking again.

I turned that word — "nothing" — over for a long time. No one stares that long at a sky with nothing in it. My brother hadn't been looking at something. He'd been watching for something to appear.

Father, unusually, was in high spirits that day. I didn't know why. He simply said walking felt good. I envied that lightness. Father, having no memory, seemed to have all but forgotten last night's terror by that morning.

Father even crouched down beside the road for a long while, watching a small insect. I found the sight strange, and somewhere in it, endearing too. Father looked like a man fully present in this one moment.

Father mimed counting the centipede's legs with his finger. Three, four, five — and every time the centipede moved, he'd start over from the beginning. I saw, in that gesture, the old habit of Father counting numbers on his fingers. But this time it didn't frighten me.

"…Look at this," Father called us over. "So many legs."

My brother came over reluctantly and looked.

"…It's a centipede," my brother said.

"…It has a name," Father said, marveling. "Even something this small has a name."

My brother went quiet for a moment at that. I understood exactly why he couldn't answer.

My brother watched Father from a little way off, then said quietly to me.

"…Father seems a lot freer than we are," my brother said. "Because he has no memory."

I didn't know how to answer that. It seemed both right and wrong at once.

I thought, for the first time then, that forgetting might not be only a curse. It was a strange thought. I didn't hold onto it long.

Father walked along, kicking small stones now and then with the toe of his shoe. A childlike gesture. Watching it, I laughed, and then, right after, my chest ached, somewhere.

I only understood the shape of that ache late that evening. Father wasn't only turning into more and more of a stranger — he was, at the same time, growing up all over again, alongside us. I found it strange that both things were happening at once.

Even now I don't know how to settle that thought. Father was a person losing something, and at the same time, a person learning something new. I didn't know which side to root for. In the end, I decided to root for both.

That afternoon, we met a merchant coming from the opposite direction. He spoke to us first.

He carried a large pack on his back. He looked like someone who traded goods between towns. I saw the fatigue of a long-traveling man in his clothes.

Pots and bowls hung from his pack, clattering with every step. I'd been hearing that sound from a long way off. But none of the three of us hid. That wasn't the sound of someone who traveled in hiding.

"…How far are you going?" the merchant asked.

"…South," my brother answered shortly.

"…All three of you?" the merchant asked, glancing at Father. "Is that your father?"

My brother paused a moment before answering.

"…Yes," my brother said. "He's our father."

I nearly cried, hearing that answer beside him. It was the first time my brother had introduced Father to a stranger that way.

"…There was quite a stir around here last night," the merchant said. "Heard a search party chased someone clear off in the wrong direction."

"…Wrong direction?" my brother asked, ears pricking up.

His voice cracked slightly. I understood he was reliving the night before, hearing that.

I nearly dropped the water jug I was holding. Only then did I remember — the direction those people had gone past our brush, the night before, was exactly opposite the direction we'd come from.

"…Yeah. Some old man apparently gave them the wrong directions. Then when they went looking for him afterward, he'd vanished too. People are telling all kinds of strange stories about it now."

I looked at my brother in that instant. He looked at me too. Neither of us said a word. But we both knew we were thinking the same thing.

The merchant, not noticing our changed expressions, kept talking.

"…Funny thing is, there wasn't a single trace where the old man disappeared from. Not even a footprint." The merchant shrugged. "People are saying all sorts of things about it."

"…What did the old man look like?" my brother asked, unable to hold back.

The merchant shrugged again.

"…I only heard it secondhand, so I don't know. Just that he was wearing worn-out clothes, that's all I heard."

I found that description so simple that it only made me more certain, not less. Nothing flashy, nothing distinctive. That was exactly the man we knew.

The merchant's pack clattered again. That ordinary sound felt, in that moment, oddly loud. For a second I even had the illusion that the world was listening in on us.

"…Why did the old man do that?" I asked. "Why would he lie to the search party?"

"…Who knows," the merchant said. "Nobody knows the answer, that's why the strange stories keep going around."

"…Is it wrong to tell a lie?" Father suddenly cut in.

The merchant looked at Father, a little startled.

"…Well, I suppose that depends on who it's for," the merchant said.

Father nodded slowly at that. I thought Father seemed satisfied with that answer.

That answer was enough for me too. I could see, in my brother's expression, that he felt the same. For the first time, I thought of the man in the worn clothes who had moved for our sake, and felt something warm settle in my chest. I learned, that day, that fear and gratitude can live in the same place at once.

Before leaving, the merchant added one more thing to us.

"…You be careful too. People around here are on edge these days."

We nodded. That parting word didn't feel like a passing remark to me. It felt like real concern.

The merchant walked a few steps, then turned back to add one more thing.

"…And don't build your fire too big at night. It shows for miles."

My brother bowed his head at that. I remember it as the most formal bow my brother ever gave a stranger.

After the merchant left, my brother was silent a long while before he spoke.

"…Was it him."

Having said it, my brother rubbed his own mouth once with his hand. Like someone not quite trusting the words that had just come out of it.

There was both certainty and resistance in my brother's voice at once. I felt he wanted to accept the fact, and at the same time was afraid that accepting it would change something.

"…I don't know," I said. But in my heart, I was already almost certain.

I didn't tell my brother that certainty out loud. I thought if I showed it too plainly, he'd push back against it even harder.

I learned something that day. When a person is coming to understand something on their own, pushing them from the side only makes them step back. From that day on, I stopped trying to convince my brother of anything.

"…Then why doesn't he just say so from the start?" my brother asked, frustration bleeding into his voice. "Then I wouldn't have to get angry like this every single time."

"…Would you not be angry, if he did?" I asked.

My brother was quiet a moment.

"…No," my brother said finally. "I'd probably still get angry. Nothing about Father makes sense anyway."

"…Then it wouldn't change anything, even if he told us," I said.

My brother looked at me. He seemed about to say something back, then let it go.

I didn't have an answer to that question either. But a strange thought crossed my mind, then. That maybe being told in advance is worse than walking on together, still afraid.

"…And," my brother added, low, "being angry is exhausting too. But if I stop being angry, then I don't know what I'm supposed to do."

I thought that was the most frightening thing my brother had said on this whole journey. Anger was proof that my brother still expected something. Without any expectation left, there'd be no anger either.

I stopped being afraid of my brother's anger from that moment on. I understood that anger was proof something was still alive inside him.

I'd been afraid, before, that my brother would break down. Looking back now, I don't think he was breaking down at all. My brother was searching for a place other than anger.

I couldn't say anything back to that. I just walked quietly beside him. I learned again, that day, that silence is sometimes better than any answer.

Father had been listening quietly to that whole exchange, and then asked.

"…Who is that person?"

Only then did I realize Father had been listening to every word. I'd thought he mostly let our conversations pass right through him. Not that day.

My brother couldn't answer right away. After a long pause, he finally said,

"…I don't really know either. But… he seems to keep being near us. Without ever explaining."

My brother himself looked surprised at his own words as he said them. I heard him admit that for the first time that day.

"…Why doesn't he explain?" Father asked.

"…I don't know," my brother said. "That's the part that frustrates me most, too."

"…I'm one of the ones who can't explain," Father said. "Not explaining, and being unable to explain — I suppose they must look the same, from outside."

My brother stopped walking at that.

Father smiled a little at what he'd said.

"…Then I guess I'm a bit like him," Father said. "I can't explain who I am either. And yet here I still am."

My brother stopped a moment, standing still. I saw him swallow something back before he could say it.

"…Yeah," my brother finally said. His voice was low. "A bit like him."

"…So are you angry at me too, then?" Father asked. It came out like a joke, but it didn't quite sound like one.

"…No," my brother said quickly. "I'm not angry at you."

"…Why not?"

My brother took a while to answer.

"…Because you're here," my brother finally said.

Father tilted his head at that. Not the face of someone who'd fully understood. But the face of someone who'd received something warm.

I still count that answer, even now, as one of the most important answers in this whole story. My brother hadn't wanted an answer. He'd wanted someone to stay beside him. I don't think my brother himself knew that, until the words came out of his own mouth.

Father heard that and reached out to pat my brother's shoulder, lightly. He didn't say anything more. But that single gesture was, in that moment, enough.

I saw my brother's breath go out of him in one long rush the moment that hand touched his shoulder. It sounded like several days' worth of held breath being let out all at once.

I understood, from that day on, that people can be undone by words, but also undone by a wordless hand. What undid my brother wasn't the silence of the night before. It was that hand, that afternoon.

I felt, in that short answer, that my brother had set something down, just a little. Not all of the anger — but a new place seemed to have opened up right beside it.

We walked late into that evening. It wasn't until the sun had fully set that we made camp. I noticed, that night, that my brother spent a little longer than usual looking up at the sky.

We kept the fire small, exactly as the merchant had advised. Small enough that the three of us had to press close together to feel any warmth at all. Father sat in the middle. No one had suggested it. It simply became his place, naturally.

The small firelight lit each of our three faces in turn. In that flickering light, I felt our three expressions growing, little by little, to resemble each other.

That night I wrote in my paper: "Today, for the first time, my brother called Father 'my father' to a stranger." I wrote that one line and, for a long while, couldn't bring myself to fold the paper closed again.

Father glanced at that paper and said nothing. Instead he laid a gentle hand on my shoulder. I understood from that touch that whether or not he understood the sentence, he sensed that something precious had been written down.

I set that line down as this chapter's last line. I saw an expression on my brother's face in that moment that I'd never seen before. It wasn't anger. It wasn't certainty either. It was simply the face of someone who had opened his heart, just a very little.

(Continued in Chapter 35.)

Chapter 35. He Called My Name

_Previously_ — From a merchant, we heard about an old man who had led Haven's search party the wrong way. Neither of us said it aloud, but we both knew, without a doubt, who that had to be. At one careless word from Father, something crossed Jeteo's face for the first time — not anger, not certainty, but something in between.

Days later, we were resting by a small stream.

The water ran low and quiet. Sunlight broke into small pieces on its surface. I watched that glimmer for a long time. It was the first peaceful afternoon we'd had after days of walking like we were being chased.

The stream wasn't wide. Anyone could have jumped across it. But the water was clear enough that I could see every stone on the bottom, one by one. I started counting them and gave up. There were too many.

A few small birds, ones I didn't know the name of, landed at the water's edge and flew off again. No one chased them away. I couldn't remember the last time I'd watched something that wasn't being chased.

I still remember that afternoon with unusual clarity, even now. It felt like an afternoon where nothing would happen. I learned that day that the biggest things happen on afternoons exactly like that.

Jeteo sat with his feet in the water, eyes closed. Watching him, I saw, for the first time in a long while, the tension gone from his face. Father was a little way off, washing his hands.

Jeteo scooped up a handful of water and washed his face with it. Then he looked up at the sky, his face still wet. I didn't bother checking whether what was on his face was only water.

Father washed his hands for a long time. Longer than he needed to. I understood that he wasn't really washing — he was just touching the water. He kept dipping his hands in and pulling them out, staring at them the whole time.

"…The water's cold," Father said, half to himself.

No one answered. He didn't seem to be waiting for an answer either.

Then, still washing his hands, Father called out to me, without warning.

"…Elpis."

I froze where I sat. So did Jeteo.

He said it completely plainly — the way you'd say "hand me that" or "look over there." That's how people say a name when they're just calling someone. That plainness undid me more than anything else could have.

The stream kept running. But to me, even that sound seemed to stop for an instant. It felt like every sound in the world had made way for that one name.

We had told Father my name many times by then. But that had only ever been an introduction. This was different. This time, Father had called me. The difference looked small. To me, it was as large as the whole world.

I still find that difference hard to explain, even now. An introduction is information. Being called is a relationship. Information can be written on paper. Being called has to come out from inside a person. That day, that name came out from inside Father.

Jeteo stood frozen too, watching me. Neither of us could say anything. Only the water filled the silence between us.

In that silence, I watched something waver, little by little, in my brother's eyes. He hadn't decided yet whether to be happy, or whether his heart should tangle over the fact that his own name hadn't come yet.

In that moment I felt sorry for him. Glad, and sorry, at once. That day I learned for the first time that both those feelings could live in one body together.

Father looked startled by his own words.

He stood frozen, hand still raised. Water dripped from his fingertips. He didn't seem to notice even that.

I watched the small ripples those drops made as they fell into the stream. One, two, three. Ever since, whenever I remember this moment, those ripples are the first thing that comes back to me.

"…What did I just say?" Father asked.

"…My name," I managed. My voice was shaking. "You called my name."

My voice shook as much as his did. I didn't try to hide the shaking. There was no reason to.

Father touched his own mouth with his hand, as if checking where the words had come from.

Even the feel of the stream against my ankles felt strange in that moment. Every sense in my body had gathered around that one name.

"…How did I know that," Father murmured. "It just… came out on its own."

"…On its own again," I said. I was laughing and crying at the same time. It didn't even strike me as strange, laughing and crying together like that.

"…Again?" Father asked.

"…The song came out on its own too," I said.

Father's face went thoughtful at that.

"…Then it's still in me," Father said. "I just can't bring it out. It's not gone."

I copied that sentence down exactly, that night, into this ledger. It was the truest thing Father ever said about himself.

There was bewilderment in Father's eyes, mixed with something close to overwhelm. I thought his expression looked like someone whose long-shut door had opened just a crack.

In that moment I looked at Jeteo. He stood there unable to say anything. I saw tears gather in his eyes for the first time.

I went to Father and took his hand.

I looked into his eyes. They were still unfamiliar eyes. But inside that unfamiliarity, something very small seemed to live there now — something that recognized me.

"…Say it again," I said.

"…Elpis." Father said it again. This time with certainty. Tears filled his eyes. "Elpis. Elpis…"

He repeated the name over and over, like a song he'd just learned. His voice trembled more each time. In that trembling, I thought I heard something — a very old lock, turning open a little at a time.

Jeteo turned his back on that sound. I knew he didn't want to be seen crying. Without letting go of Father's hand, I reached my other hand out toward him. He didn't come. But a moment later, his hand settled on top of mine.

That name sounded, to me, like it went past my own name — like Father was saying something to himself. Like he was holding onto that one name and trying to work his way back to himself.

In that moment I wrapped both my hands around Father's. It was still cold and damp. Even that cold felt welcome to me. It was a living hand.

There was an old scar on the back of Father's hand. A scar from carrying bricks. I touched it with my fingertip. Father didn't know why it was there.

The scar was shaped like a small crescent moon. I'd seen that shape many times back in Brick Hollow — Father rubbing that spot absently every evening as he washed his hands.

"…Do you know where you got this?" I asked.

"…No idea," Father said.

"…I know," I said. "I'll tell you later."
