Father didn't press for more. He just nodded and looked down at the back of his hand once more.

I didn't tell the story of that scar that day. To tell it, I would have had to start with why Father became what he became in Brick Hollow. I wanted to tell it once Father could bear hearing it himself.

Father closed his eyes, saying my name. Behind those closed eyes, I could feel him straining to hold onto something — the frayed, still-unbroken end of a thread of memory.

The sound of the stream seemed unusually loud in that moment. I listened to it and tried, for a while, not to think anything at all. I wanted to feel this moment with my body alone.

I closed my eyes there for a moment. A memory rose up — the last night in Brick Hollow, Father lifting me up while calling my name. I felt that memory overlap with this one.

That night, lifting me up, Father had said, "…Don't be scared, Elpis." Even then, I'd known it was a lie. But that lie was what kept me alive that night.

"…What about me?" Jeteo asked, his voice shaking. "Do you know my name too?"

Father looked at Jeteo for a long time. His face held something that seemed almost within reach, and yet wasn't.

His mouth moved several times. No sound came out. He looked like someone who'd opened a drawer only to find it empty.

Seeing Father struggle made it hurt more. Father didn't want to disappoint Jeteo, so he was trying to force something into being that wasn't there.

"…It's okay. You don't have to force it," Jeteo said first. But his voice was already wet when he said it.

"…I'm sorry," Father said. "That one… hasn't come yet."

I could hear that Father's sorrow was real. I hoped that sorrow could be some small comfort to Jeteo.

Jeteo's face fell apart. I watched him fall into disappointment, right there. In that brief moment, too many feelings crossed his eyes for me to read them all. Disappointment, shame, and the strain of trying to hold both down.

In that moment, I wanted to be angry on my brother's behalf. Not at Father — at someone, I didn't even know who. I wanted to demand why only one name had come back, why not both.

I think, in that moment, I understood a little of what it had felt like for Jeteo, every day, looking up at that sky. Something that comes back only partway can sometimes be harder to bear than something that never comes back at all.

But Jeteo smiled, strangely.

"…It's okay," Jeteo said. "My name can come later. Right now… it's enough that Elpis's did."

His voice shook, but it didn't waver. I could tell that steadiness wasn't something he'd forced out — he really felt it.

I was startled to hear that. That was the first time I ever saw my brother take someone else's joy before his own. Back in Brick Hollow, and in Haven too, Jeteo had never been someone who looked out for himself first. But that wasn't because he couldn't look after himself — it was because he was someone who looked after others first. That day, I confirmed that fact all over again.

"…Jeteo," I said. "You don't have to force yourself to be okay."

"…I'm not forcing it," Jeteo said.

"…So a little, then?"

Jeteo looked at me and laughed, briefly.

"…A little," he said.

Father went to Jeteo and put his arms around his shoulders. It was an awkward gesture. But Jeteo didn't pull away from that awkward embrace.

Father patted his back a couple of times. The rhythm was clumsy. But somehow that clumsiness made it feel more real.

"…I don't know your name," Father told him. "But I know your face. I see it every day."

At that, Jeteo leaned his forehead against Father's shoulder for a moment. Just a moment.

I watched the two of them from a little way off. The stream still ran low and quiet. I thought that sound was carving this moment into something, somewhere.

That night, Jeteo spoke to me quietly.

"…I kept collecting evidence, this whole time. To protect the name, to remember it. But today I learned something. What's coming back inside Father — it wasn't because I gathered evidence. It was just… because I stayed close."

He took a bundle of paper scraps from inside his coat and spread them on his lap. In the firelight, the writing on them had all faded. Some of it was hard to make out at all anymore.

"…What is all this?" I asked.

"…Everywhere Father's name might turn up, I wrote it down," Jeteo said. "Work-crew numbers, ration slips, taskmasters' names. I thought it might have survived somewhere."

I looked at those papers one by one. Only then did I understand how long he'd been carrying this alone.

"…So you don't need the evidence anymore?" I asked.

"…No," Jeteo said. "I still need it. But now I know. Evidence alone wouldn't have been enough. Staying close had to happen too."

"…You can do both," I said.

Jeteo looked down at the bundle of papers for a moment at that. He ran his fingertips along the edges, then looked at me again.

"…Can you, though," he said.

"…You did today," I said.

Jeteo couldn't answer that for a moment. Then he folded the papers carefully again and tucked them back inside his coat. His hands moved more carefully than before.

I thought of the worn scraps of paper he'd carried deep in his coat all this time — evidence gathered all the way back from Brick Hollow. I felt that he was learning how to hold onto those without letting go, while also holding something new at the same time.

I sat down a little closer to him. He put his arm around my shoulder. It was a familiar weight I hadn't felt in a long time.

Father was already asleep a little way off. Even asleep, his lips moved very slightly now and then. I thought it might be my name. I didn't go check.

I sat quietly beside Jeteo. Somewhere far off, night insects were singing. Listening to them, I felt all over again how enormous a day this had been for us.

The embers of the fire were dying down with small crackling sounds. Listening to that, I could feel the day's afterglow still lingering somewhere in my body.

I tried to write down what had happened that day, then gave up. My hand was shaking too much to write properly. In the end, I wrote down only one name. My own. The name Father had called.

"…Jeteo," I said.

"…Yeah?" he answered.

"…I believe Father will call your name too, someday," I said.

"…Why do you believe that?" he asked.

"…I don't know," I said. "I just do."

"…That's not based on anything," he said.

"…Yeah," I said. "Nothing at all."

I didn't know exactly what I meant by that, back then. I understand what it means to believe without grounds far better now than I did then. It's not that you believe because there's no evidence. You believe before all the evidence has gathered.

Jeteo looked at me and smiled quietly. He didn't say anything. But that smile was his answer.

The last embers sparked faintly and scattered into the dark. I watched those fragments of light glimmer and vanish for a long while. I thought that today, too, would stay somewhere inside us, like a small light like that.

I set that thought down as the last line of this chapter. That night, I watched Jeteo hold two things together for the first time. Keeping, and staying close. I didn't know then, not exactly, that it was his first step toward trust.

(Continued in Chapter 36.)

Chapter 36. A Place to Plant

_Previously_ — Father called Elpis's name on his own, for the first time. Jeteo realized that what had returned in Father hadn't come back because he'd gathered evidence, but because he'd stayed close — and began holding keeping and staying close together, both at once.

"…Where should we go now." Jeteo asked one evening. It was a question for Father as much as for me.

We were watching the sunset from a small hill that day. Watching the sky turn red, I thought, for the first time, that even a journey like ours needed an evening. Not more walking — an evening to stop and decide where to go.

Below the hill, the road split in two. One led north, the way we'd come; one led south. I looked at that fork for a long time. Looking down at it from above like that, I couldn't tell which way was farther, or which was safer.

The wind had died down. It was the first quiet evening we'd had in days. The sunset shifted from red to orange, then to gray. I found it strange how that change happened so slowly, and yet, at some point, it had suddenly become a different color altogether. I thought people's hearts were probably like that too.

There was a limit to how long we could keep walking like we were running from something. Father still tired easily, and he still couldn't fully settle at night.

Our feet were a problem too. Father's shoes had already split at the toes, and Jeteo's didn't look like they'd last much longer either. I started counting up all these things, then stopped. The more I counted, the more it all pointed to the same conclusion — we couldn't keep going like this.

I studied Father's face. He'd put on a little weight since we first found him. But there was still a restless look in his eyes, like he was searching for something. I thought that restlessness wouldn't fully go away until we had a place before we had an answer.

I felt, in that moment, that what we needed now wasn't an answer anymore. It was a place. Not a direction to walk in — somewhere to put down roots.

I thought that, and pressed my fingertips into the ground. The soil still held the day's warmth. I felt like that warmth was holding us somewhere too.

I thought the word "roots" had just occurred to me for the first time. Looking back now, it was a word Father used to use, back in Brick Hollow. He used to say, sometimes, "We still don't have roots." I hadn't known, then, what he meant by that.

I read back over the record I'd been keeping. From the last night in Brick Hollow up to now, page after page was filled. I realized how few answers were in it. But there was something in it that outnumbered the answers. Days we had walked together.

I turned the pages one by one. The night we met Temel, the night we found Father, the counting gesture, the humming, the searching light, the nights on the run, and my own name, called that very morning by the stream. I saw, on every one of those pages, both tear-marks and traces of laughter, side by side.

I understood, in that moment. This record had started as a way of gathering evidence. But somewhere along the way, it had become something else. A record that held onto the simple fact that we had walked together.

Jeteo glanced at those pages over my shoulder.

"…Is that all about us?" he asked.

"…Yeah," I said. "Not all of it. Just what I can remember."

"…Can I read it later?" he asked. His voice was careful, unlike him.

"…Of course," I said. "It's yours too, really."

Jeteo looked at me for a moment at that.

"…Mine?"

"…Yeah," I said. "You're in it more than anyone."

"…What did you write?" he asked.

"…Read it later," I said.

Jeteo huffed a laugh at that. I was glad he was curious. Being curious meant thinking about a "later."

Before the sunset faded completely, the last red light lit up the side of Jeteo's face. I felt, all over again, how different that face was now from the brother who'd left Brick Hollow.

"…What if we went back to Speon's?" I said. "That well wasn't dangerous. And Speon… he was alone too."

Saying it, I pictured Speon's well-yard — quiet, unhurried, nothing but the low voices of people drawing water. I realized, only then, that it had been the least threatening place we'd found since we first fled.

And there was one more thing about that well. No one there had ever asked us what we wanted to file. Only then did I understand what a difference that made.

Jeteo turned the idea over.

"…Doesn't sound bad," he said. "We need help, and Speon needs help too."

"…Would Speon take us in?" I asked. "There's three of us."

"…No idea," Jeteo said. "But we can ask."

I found it strange, hearing that from Jeteo. The old Jeteo never started anything he thought might be refused. I thought I knew whose doing that change was.

Father asked quietly.

"…If we go there… would it be all right for me to be there?"

Asking it, he didn't look at either of our faces. He kept his eyes on the sunset. I understood — he was afraid to hear the answer.

There was still that same carefulness in his voice. I found that I no longer found it strange. It had simply become part of who Father was.

Jeteo looked straight at Father.

"…Of course," he said. This time, without a moment's hesitation.

I took note of how quickly he answered. Back in Chapter 32, faced with the same question, Jeteo had needed a long while before he answered at all. This time it was instant. I thought that whole gap of time held the entire road Jeteo had walked since then.

"…Just one thing," Father said. "If I get like that again, at night. If another night like last time comes."

Jeteo was quiet for a moment.

"…Then I'll be there again," he said. "That's about the only thing I know how to do."

Father bowed his head at that. I thought he was crying, but when he lifted his head, his eyes were dry. Instead, his face had loosened just slightly.

The sunset had almost gone. The last thread of light stretched our three shadows out long, joining them into one. Watching that shadow, I felt, for real, that we had chosen a direction now.

We started walking south again, toward Speon's well.

The road back felt shorter than it had going the other way. It wasn't only because we knew the way.

That day, we walked while singing, for the first time. Father sang as far as he remembered, and I picked up where he left off. Jeteo didn't sing, but he kept time with the pace of it.

Just a few days earlier, even making a sound out loud had felt frightening. I couldn't say exactly what had changed in those few days. The world itself surely hadn't.

I passed, along that road, through the same scenery we'd already crossed once. The rock we'd hidden behind, the bushes where we'd muffled our footsteps, the fork where we'd met the merchant. The same scenery — but it looked completely different now. We simply walked, this time, through places we'd once fled through in fear.

I thought, then, that the same road, walked a second time, could become a different road. Maybe someday, looking back, this whole journey of ours would look completely different too.

Father recognized a few spots along the way.

"…We were here, weren't we?" he asked, in front of some rock.

"…Yes," Jeteo said. "We hid here."

"…So we did," Father said. He spoke like someone who knew it by feeling, not by memory.

I decided not to count moments like that. If I started counting, there would be days that added up and days that didn't, and I felt like my heart would break on the days that didn't. So I decided to just take whatever came.

Speon looked startled to see us. And even more startled when he saw Father.

"…You found him," Speon said. There was real gladness in his voice.

Speon's bucket stopped dead in his hand. I could feel, in that frozen gesture, just how startled he was.

"…Yes," Jeteo said. "But not completely found. We're still finding him."

I set Jeteo's words down here exactly as he said them. He'd learned, by now, to tell the difference between "found" and "still finding."

Speon didn't ask anything else at that. He didn't demand an explanation either. He just nodded once and went back to hauling up the bucket.

I think that nod was the biggest welcome we received that day. In Haven, everything began with filing and explaining. Here, it didn't.

Speon drew Father a bowl of water. Father drank it, looking around the well.

Father's hand trembled faintly around the bowl. Whether that was from the water after such a long walk, or something else, I couldn't tell.

"…This place… feels strangely comfortable," Father said.

After he finished the water, he held the bowl for a long while. Like someone who'd forgotten to give it back. I thought that was a hand trying to hold onto this place a little longer.

"…Makes sense," Speon said quietly. "This place has always been like that."

Speon ran his hand over an old stone beside the well.

"…I wasn't the only one who kept this well," Speon said. "People passed through before me. What they left behind is still here."

"…Like what?" I asked.

Speon thought for a moment before answering.

"…Things like the order you set the stones in," he said. "No one taught it to me. There are things you just come to know, if you stay here long enough."

Listening to that, I thought of that leaf at the flowerbed — the one that had grown there without anyone planting it. I thought maybe this world had more than one place like that.

Speon's hand slowly ran over the stone again. I could tell that gesture was a habit, repeated over a long time. I thought you could tell how long someone had kept a place just by watching the way their hand moved.

That evening, I planted a few seeds in the small patch of ground beside the well. The leaf I'd brought from that flowerbed in Haven had dried up long before. But I still remembered the hands that had given me those seeds, at that very spot.

The soil was soft. I dug small holes with my fingertips, dropped a seed into each one, and covered it back over with earth. Repeating that simple motion, I felt my heart settle in a strange way.

The evening soil smelled of the day's warmth. I didn't mind the dirt under my fingernails. I thought of touching soil every day back in Brick Hollow. There, the soil had been meant to become bricks. Here, the soil was meant to grow something. Same soil, different purpose.

Jeteo watched from a little way off, then came over.

"…Can I plant one too?" he asked.

I handed him a seed. He dug into the dirt with clumsy movements. I was glad for that clumsiness. It meant he wanted to plant something in this place just as much as I did.

"…How long does this take?" he asked.

"…I don't know," I said.

"…You don't know again?" he said.

"…Yeah," I said, laughing. "But you have to plant it to find out."

Jeteo didn't say anything to that. He just finished covering the seed with dirt. But his hands moved more carefully than before.

Father sat beside us, watching.

"…What are you planting?" he asked.

"…I don't know yet," I said. "But I guess we'll find out, once it grows."

Father turned that answer over quietly. I found that silence comfortable. It was a silence where not knowing the answer was all right.

Father smiled quietly at that.

"…I think I'm like that too," he said. "I don't know yet what's inside me. But I know something's planted there."

Jeteo heard that and came to sit beside us. The three of us sat in a row, looking down at the freshly turned soil. There was nothing to see there. But I could believe there was something inside it.

"…You know what?" Jeteo said quietly. "I never used to believe in anything I couldn't see."

"…And now?" I asked.

Jeteo looked at the soil for a while before answering.

"…I still don't believe all of it, even now. But that something's been planted — that, I'm trying to believe."

"…That alone is a lot," I said.

"…Is it," Jeteo said.

"…Yeah," I said. "You used to say you didn't believe in anything."

Jeteo went quiet for a moment at that.

"…That's still a little true, even now," he said. "I'm still not over being angry."

"…You don't have to be over it," I said.

"…Do you really think that?" he asked. "That you can believe, even while you're still angry?"

"…I think so," I said. "Believing and being angry — I think they can live together."

Jeteo looked like he was turning that over for a long time. He didn't answer. But I could feel him putting it away somewhere inside himself.

Listening to that, I looked up at the sky where stars were coming out, one by one. Somewhere up there, I thought, that person who'd led us the wrong way, kindly, must still be walking. That was the first time I wanted to call him something other than just "he." I still didn't know that name.

Speon called out to us from a distance to come eat supper. We got up from where we sat. Father held out his hand first and pulled me up, and Jeteo took Father's other hand. I looked down for a moment at the shape those three joined hands made.

None of those three hands was especially large. Father's hand was thin. Jeteo's was still growing. Mine was the smallest. But joined together like that, they were hard to let go of.

On the short walk back to the well, I felt the warmth of each hand in turn. Father's hand was still a little cold. Jeteo's was warm. I knew that difference would shift again in a few days, but I wanted to remember today's warmth anyway.

As he stood, Father asked,

"…We'll still be here tomorrow, won't we?"

"…Yes," Jeteo said. "Tomorrow too."

I set that line down as the last sentence of this Part. That evening, we settled at Speon's well-yard. The answers still hadn't all come. Neither had my brother's name, nor his full certainty. But that day, for the first time, I knew we had found a place to put down roots.

(Continued in Part Four.)

The Weeping Ledger, Part Four

By the Well

Chapter 37. Days by the Well

_Previously_ — Sensing they needed a place more than an answer, the siblings went back to Speon's well and put down roots. Elpis planted seeds again, and Father said, "I know something's been planted."

That was how we began to live at the well.

For the first few nights, sleep wouldn't come, strangely. It took me a long time to understand why. Ever since we'd left Haven, our bodies had always tensed first, not knowing what the next day would bring. Here, our bodies still hadn't learned to believe that tomorrow wouldn't look very different from today.

A low stone wall ran around the well. Nameless weeds grew in the gaps between the stones. Every morning, dew gathered on their leaves. I got into the habit of touching that dew with a fingertip, every time I went to draw water.

There was a small hut by the well. Speon had used it for a long time. We made a small place for ourselves beside it. It was smaller than our house in Brick Hollow. Strangely, I didn't mind the smallness at all.

We had almost no belongings. Just what we'd carried out of Haven, and the few things Father had. Jeteo never once complained about how little we had. I knew that the old Jeteo would have called even that unfair. Not any more.

Among our things was the seed pouch. It was nearly empty now. I kept carrying that empty pouch instead of throwing it away. Speon once asked why I kept an empty pouch. I only smiled, not knowing how to answer. I couldn't have put it into words then — that emptiness is also the mark of something that was once full.

The days weren't very different from one another. Draw water in the morning, walk with Father, leave a record in the evening. That sameness felt strange to me at first. Haven, and the road we'd fled along — both had always been time spent being chased by something.

That was when I first understood how precious an ordinary day was. I hadn't known, in Brick Hollow, what a rare gift a day with nothing happening in it could be.

I gradually settled on a spot for writing my evening record — beside the hut door, where the stone wall supported my back. From that spot I could see the whole well. I liked the angle. It was a place where I'd know at once if anything happened. Lately, though, not much happened. I didn't mind that at all.

The morning routine was simple. Draw water, take Father's hand and walk once around the well, and in the evening, write a short note about the day. Writing felt like homework at first. But after a few days, it stopped being homework and became a way of holding on to the day.

Speon taught us the well's rules, one at a time. Always hang the bucket in the same spot. Give water to anyone newly out before you ask them anything. I could feel that these rules were nothing like Haven's. Haven's rules existed for processing requests. These rules existed for people.

"…How many rules are there?" I asked.

"…Never counted," Speon said. "One got added whenever it was needed. There weren't any at first."

"…Which one came first?"

Speon paused, hand still, and thought.

"…Not asking," Speon said. "Not asking why someone came out of the well, or what they'd lost before. You wait until they say it themselves. That one came first, for me."

Hearing that, I remembered how the intake window in Haven had always asked first. What is your name. What is the issue. I felt that difference clearly, for the first time, right then.

"…So what do you do with your curiosity?" I asked. "Just hold it in?"

"…Draw them another cup of water," Speon said. "As much as you're curious."

I thought about that for a long time. A person who turned curiosity into water instead of questions. From that day on, I got into the habit of filling a water jar first, whenever someone came out of the well. And strangely, that was how — later — the person would come to speak first.

Father improved little by little. He still didn't know Jeteo's name. But during the day, he began trying to do things on his own for a few minutes at a time — drawing water, moving a stone. No one had to ask him.

Father seemed to especially like drawing water. That repetition of lowering and pulling the bucket seemed to steady him. I saw him a few times just standing there, gripping the bucket rope, still. Even when he wasn't moving, he never let go of that rope.

Father slept badly at night. He woke often, and when he did, he sat for a long while without speaking. Every time, I went and sat beside him. I said nothing. We just watched the dark together. I thought that was all I could do. I learned later that it wasn't just all I could do — it was the most important thing.

"…It's easier when I'm doing something," Father said. "When my body moves."

"…What are you most at ease doing?" I asked.

Father thought about the question a moment, rubbing the dirt floor with his fingertips.

"…Moving stones," Father said. "I don't know why. I just like having something in my hands."

Hearing that, I thought of the bricks of Brick Hollow. Even then, Father's hands had always been holding something. Something heavy. Something hard. It struck me, strangely painfully, that the habit had stayed with him this way.

Hearing that, I thought of Father in Brick Hollow. Those hands had always been moving too. I felt, then, that something inside Father was coming back before his name did.

Jeteo began helping Speon look after the well. He mended the bucket, tended the stone wall, and at night took turns keeping watch with Speon.

"…Why are you helping with this?" I asked.

"…Speon did this alone for so long," Jeteo said. "I think I understand it now. Why waiting becomes a kind of work."

"…What do you mean?" I asked.

"…I used to think waiting was doing nothing," Jeteo said. "But mend a bucket every day and you find out how much work goes into it. Waiting was like that too. Work that takes a lot of hands."

Hearing that, I understood that Jeteo had begun to understand Speon's life. It wasn't an understanding he'd arrived at from an answer. It was an understanding earned by doing it alongside him.

Jeteo's hands had grown much rougher in those few weeks. Calluses were starting to form where the bucket rope rubbed. Every time I saw those hands, I remembered his hands carrying bricks in Brick Hollow. Back then those hands had moved to dodge a taskmaster's rod. Now these hands moved with no one making them. I kept that difference close to my heart.

As Speon taught Jeteo how to mend the bucket, he let a little of his old story slip out now and then — how he'd once been so afraid that he'd fled and left someone behind. Jeteo didn't press him for more. He just listened.

"…Why don't you ask?" Speon asked.

"…I've learned lately that it's better for people to talk when they want to," Jeteo said.

Speon looked at him a long time at that.

"…When you first came here, you did press me," Speon said. "Asked why I'd waste my time keeping some well out here like this."

Jeteo looked a little embarrassed at that.

"…Sorry, about back then," Jeteo said.

"…Nothing to be sorry for," Speon said. "That question made me think it over again too. Why I'm here."

"…Did you find an answer?"

"…Still looking," Speon said, smiling. "But I'm not in a hurry any more."

"…What happens to something you're not in a hurry about?" Jeteo asked.

Speon thought a moment, winding the bucket rope.

"…You just have to go through a few days where the answer doesn't come and the day passes anyway," Speon said. "Then you find out. That the answer was never the thing holding your day up."

Jeteo didn't answer that. But I saw his face take on the look of someone filing a thing away rather than discarding it.

"…You seem like a different person than a few months ago," Speon said.

"…I feel that way sometimes too," Jeteo said. "But I can't really explain what's changed."

Listening to that conversation from the side, I looked at Jeteo again. I couldn't point to an exact day when this had started in him. Some change comes that way — not overnight, but seeping in like water.

That evening, Speon taught Jeteo how to measure the depth of the well — tying a knot in the rope every so often and counting which knot the water reached. Jeteo practiced it over and over until he had it. In that repetition, I felt that the very way Jeteo learned things had changed. He used to ask first why he had to learn something. Now he simply learned.

"…Why measure this depth?" Jeteo asked, counting the knots one by one.

"…You need to know when a drought comes," Speon said. "If you know the water's dropping ahead of time, you can warn people ahead of time."

"…It's not dropping now, though."

"…No," Speon said. "But you measure every day. If you only measure on the day it drops, it's already too late."

Jeteo's hand stopped at that. I saw something pass over his face. It was the exact opposite of Haven's logic — a city that only ever took in a request after the problem had already happened. Here, there was someone keeping watch before the problem ever came.

One night, there was a sound of movement at the well.

It was an unusually quiet night. No wind. In that stillness, hearing that sound, I suddenly thought of our last night in Brick Hollow. I realized, in that instant, that the memory was still lodged somewhere in my body.

Moonlight lay scattered in pieces on the water. I watched that trembling light and, for a moment, held my breath. From a well that had made not a single sound of water, a splashing sound suddenly rose. I strained my ears not to lose it.

Father woke to the sound too. He walked to the well a little later than we did, but with a steady stride. Watching that walk, I felt that Father no longer fled from unfamiliar sounds.

The four of us ran there together. A woman was crouched trembling, just out of the bottom of the well. Her eyes held nothing in them.

Jeteo went to her first.

"…It's all right," Jeteo said. It was the very voice we had heard from Father. "Breathe slowly. You're safe here."

Watching that scene, my breath caught. A few months ago, Jeteo had stood in the exact spot he was most afraid of. Now he stood there most calmly of all.

I sat quietly beside the woman and held out a water jar. Her hands shook too badly to take it. I took her hand instead and let the water trickle in, little by little. That was exactly what we had done for Father, when we first met him.

Speon only watched from a distance. He didn't step in. I felt, then, that I understood how he'd been able to keep this well for so long. He wasn't a man who stepped in every time — he was a man who knew when to step in only when it was needed.

Father stood a little apart too. Neither stepping in nor stepping back. I felt that Father was studying the woman's trembling shoulders closely. He murmured something, low.

"…Was I like that too?" Father asked me.

For a moment, the question stopped my words.

"…Yes," I finally answered. "But now you can ask a question like that."

Father nodded at that. And looked at the woman again. It was the first time I'd seen him recognize himself in a stranger.

After a long while, Father lifted one of the water jars and pushed it a little toward the woman. He said nothing. He simply set it within reach of her hand and stepped back.

Watching that small gesture, my throat went hot. Father still didn't know who he was. But who he had once been — his hands remembered that first.

The woman stayed by the well for a few days, and then remembered part of her name. Not the whole thing. But even that much was enough to make her laugh through tears.

The day the woman first spoke a fragment of her name aloud, I happened to be carrying a water jar. I stopped in my tracks at the sound. "…Mi…" the woman said. That was all. But she held on to that one syllable and wept for a long time. I couldn't tell if that weeping was sorrow or joy. Probably both.

The woman barely spoke for days. But every morning, she came out on her own to fill her water jar. I had already learned from watching Father that this small movement was the beginning of recovery.

Speon didn't call her by a name. He just called her "the guest." At first I thought that was rude. Later I understood. Speon was refusing to define her by any name at all until she found her own.

The day before she left, she pressed a small stone into my hand. She didn't explain why. I didn't ask, and took it. I still have that stone.

"…When I remember my name, all of it, I'll come back," the woman said. Her voice still trembled a little. But it was a different trembling than the one she'd arrived with.

"…Come back whenever you're ready," I said. "This place isn't going anywhere."

The woman laughed at that. A laugh I hadn't seen before. Short, but clear.

The morning she left, Speon carved a small mark into the well's stone wall. I asked what it was for.

"…It's a count of everyone who's passed through," Speon said. "Since we can't write their names, we mark it, at least."

I looked at that stone wall closely for the first time. There were a great many marks. I began thinking that every one of those marks was someone's day, and then stopped counting partway through.

Before I fell asleep that night, I thought for a moment of the day we'd first arrived at the well. Everything had been unfamiliar then. Now the sound of the bucket, the sound of Speon's footsteps — all of it sounded familiar. I didn't know when that familiarity had come. But that it had come — that much was certain.

That night, Jeteo said to me,

"…I still don't know who that man is. I can't say I believe in God either. But watching that woman today, I knew something. I know how to sit beside someone now. That much is certain."

"…When did you learn that?" I asked.

"…I don't know," Jeteo said. "I just came to and realized I already knew. No one taught me."

"…Maybe you learned it from Father," I said. "Just being beside us."

Jeteo was silent at that for a moment.

"…Maybe so," Jeteo finally said.

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. I knew that Jeteo still couldn't use the word faith. But he was already doing the work faith does. I decided not to think that order strange.

(Continued in Chapter 38.)

Chapter 38. Mena Came

_Previously_ — Life at the well settled into something steady. Jeteo helped Speon tend it and learned how to sit beside someone. "I can't say I believe in God. But I know how to sit beside someone."

About a month in, a familiar figure came walking from far off.

It was Mena. She had the record box she always carried slung over her shoulder.

The box had grown much thicker than before. Paper stuck out past its edges. Mena's shoes were caked in dirt. It showed how long she'd been walking.

The shoulder of Mena's coat was worn through too — the strap of the box must have rubbed that same spot for a long time. Looking at that worn patch, I tried to guess how long she'd been carrying that weight.

The wind stirred the grass around the well, gently. I was the first to see a figure appear beyond that grass. At first I didn't know who it was. Only once the walk became familiar did I know it was Mena.

The moment I saw her, I ran. Mena remembered us. And when she saw Father, she stopped walking.

"…Did you come to bring Speon news?" I asked.

"…That too," Mena said. Her face flushed a little. "I wanted to know how you were, too."

"…How long did it take you to get here?" I asked.

"…About ten days," Mena said. "Had to stop at a few places along the way. Didn't feel tired, though. I was too curious what I'd find here."

"…What did you think you'd find?" I asked.

Mena thought for a moment.

"…I don't know," Mena said. "I just wanted to see with my own eyes how you were living here. There's something a written name alone doesn't give you."

I didn't understand what that meant right away. But over the next few days, I came to. For Mena, a name was only a beginning. Watching how that name lived — that was what Mena really wanted to do.

The first evening Mena arrived at the well, she asked nothing at all. She simply watched us live. She watched us draw water, watched us walk with Father, watched us share a meal. That gaze didn't make me uncomfortable. If anything, I felt it had been a long time since someone had watched over our whole day like that.

"…What are you looking at so much?" I asked.

"…Just — I wanted to see it all," Mena said. "Writing down a name is only half of it. You have to see it lived to make it real."

I took Mena's hand. Her fingertips were cracked. I could guess how many names those hands had written down.

"…What happened to your hand?" I asked.

"…Must be from holding a pen too long," Mena said, laughing. "It's fine, it doesn't hurt."

I didn't believe that. But I decided not to press. It was the first rule I'd learned at the well.

Instead, I refilled a water jar and set it in front of Mena. She glanced at me, seeing that.

"…You've got a face like there's something you want to ask," Mena said.

"…There is," I said. "But here, water first."

Mena laughed out loud at that. And once she'd drunk it all, she started telling me things I hadn't even asked about.

"…Is this…" Mena couldn't finish the sentence.

"…Yes," Jeteo said. "We found him. His name still hasn't all come back, but."

Mena's eyes welled with tears. I had never seen Mena show emotion like this. She had always been someone who wrote names down calmly. I understood, only in that moment, that the calmness had never been indifference.

Mena carefully took Father's hand. Father didn't pull away. Even though it was an unfamiliar hand, he simply let it stay.

"…Among the names I've recorded, meeting one again like this is really rare," Mena said. "Thank you. For not giving up."

Mena wiped her eyes with the back of her hand, laughing. I thought that laugh was so tangled with weeping that they were almost impossible to tell apart.

"…It's thanks to you, Mena," Jeteo said. "If you hadn't recorded his name, we'd have just been walking around in circles."

Mena shook her head.

"…I only wrote it down," Mena said. "You're the ones who walked all this way to find him."

Jeteo shook his head at that.

"…If it hadn't been written down, we wouldn't even have known where to go," Jeteo said. "I don't think that's a small thing."

Mena went quiet at that for a moment. I saw something long held down loosen in her face.

"…Honestly, sometimes this work has felt pointless," Mena said quietly. "There are far more names I write down that I never see again. Every time, I wondered what the point even was."

"…And now?" I asked.

"…Now I know," Mena said. "If I meet even one person again, then the names I never met before weren't wasted either. I've got a reason to keep carrying them now."

Father sat there, watching Mena quietly. His face was one that didn't quite remember who she was. But he didn't look at her like a stranger.

"…Do you know me?" Father asked carefully.

"…We crossed paths once, a long time ago," Mena said. "You didn't know me then. I didn't really know you either. But I wrote your name down."

Father nodded at that. It was less understanding than simple acceptance.

"…What kind of person was I, back then?" Father asked again.

Mena fell into thought for a moment. I could feel her choosing her answer carefully.

"…A quiet person," Mena finally said. "You didn't say much. But your eyes stayed with people, long after."

"…What kind of look was it?"

"…A look like you were trying to protect something," Mena said. "I didn't know what it was, then. I think I understand it now."

Father glanced toward us at that. I felt something pass through his eyes that matched what Mena had just described.

"…Did I protect it well?" Father asked.

Before Mena could answer, Jeteo spoke first.

"…You did," Jeteo said. His voice was lower than usual. "We're right here now."

Father said nothing to that. But I saw his shoulders drop, ever so slightly. Like someone who had been holding tension for a long time, letting go of it for a moment.

Watching that short exchange, I thought about it. How many of the names Mena had recorded would ever face her like this again? I didn't know the answer. But looking at Mena's eyes, I could tell she was someone who would keep writing whether she knew the answer or not.

Speon heard the news and came running from the well. The moment he saw Mena, he stopped in his tracks.

The two of them said nothing for a while.

In that silence, I watched Speon's face. He was usually calm here at the well. Not in that moment, though. I saw something old shaking loose in that face.

Speon's hand still held the bucket handle. I saw it trembling, faintly. As if to hide it, he set the bucket down carefully beside the well.

Mena stood where she was too, unmoving. She hadn't even set her pack down. It struck me, strangely, that both of them seemed to be hesitating to be the first to come closer.

"…You said you were still waiting," Mena said first.

"…Yeah," Speon said. "That still holds."

"…How many years is that now?" Mena asked.

Speon started to count on his fingers, then stopped.

"…Stopped counting," Speon said. "Counting made it heavier."

Mena's eyes dropped slightly at that.

Mena laughed at the answer. I couldn't tell if that laugh was relief or something else. Speon laughed along with her. The two laughs looked alike.

The wind passed between them. I thought that wind was slowly sweeping away an old silence. Only then did Speon take Mena's pack from her. It was a completely natural gesture. I felt it wasn't the first time. The two of them seemed like people who had already been through this moment many times before.

I felt, in that short exchange, how much time had passed between them. I still didn't know what had happened between the two of them. But that day, I decided for the first time not to ask. I had learned that some stories deserve to be respected without explanation.

Mena stayed at the well for several days after that. I watched, more than once, secretly, the two of them sitting side by side filling water jars. They didn't say much. But that silence was nothing like any silence I'd seen before.

During the day, Mena still spread out her record box and wrote things down — the names of people she'd newly met, the things they'd lost. I peeked at those papers beside her. A short line was written next to each name. "Looking for her daughter." "Hand injured, still can't use it." I understood that these short sentences were Mena's way of seeing the world. Not resolution. Memory.

I looked for our own names in a corner of that paper. Jeteo. Beside it, written briefly: "Looking for his father." And below that, in smaller writing, one more line: "Has not given up." I stared at that sentence for a long time. This was how Mena had been recording us.

In the evenings, Mena sometimes showed the box to Speon. He turned the pages one at a time, pointing with a fingertip whenever he found a name he knew. I felt that this scene looked nothing like anything we'd seen in Haven. The Ledger erased names off a screen. Mena left names on paper.

One evening, I sat beside Mena and turned through the papers in the box with her.

"…Do you memorize all of this?" I asked.

"…Not all of it," Mena said. "But when you write it by hand, you never completely forget. Strangely enough."

"…Isn't it hard? Writing down nothing but sad stories?"

Mena turned a page and fell silent for a moment. The evening wind lifted the edge of the paper slightly. Mena pressed it down with a fingertip.

"…It's hard," Mena finally said. "But if no one writes it down, that name really did never exist. That scares me more."

I thought about those words for a long time. I felt, then, that I understood why Mena walked so far. Mena was doing the thing the Ledger could never do. Not forgetting.

"…When did you start doing this, Mena?" I asked.

"…A very long time ago," Mena said. "At first I just meant to write down one person's name. But once you start writing, you end up writing the person next to them too, and the person next to them. Before I knew it, the box had become this."

"…Who was the first name you wrote?"

Mena went silent at that question for a long time. I felt something very old held inside that silence.

"…I'll tell you that later," Mena said. "Not yet."

I didn't ask again. I already knew, now, that some stories need time.

Instead, I looked at the inside of the box's lid. A name was written there too. Unlike the other papers, that name alone was written directly on the lid. The ink had faded a great deal, but it hadn't been erased.

I didn't read that name aloud. I couldn't be the one to bring up what Mena had chosen not to say yet.

That night I thought for a long time about the inside of that lid. Mena carried every name in the world inside that box. But there was exactly one name she carried carved into the box itself.

The night before Mena left, we all sat together around the fire. Speon hummed an old song for the first time in a long while. I couldn't make out the words. But the melody alone stirred something.

The smell of burning firewood spread over the whole well-yard. Smelling it, I thought of the smell of the brick kiln in Brick Hollow. A different smell. But both were the kind that made people feel warm.

Jeteo asked Speon what the song's words meant. Speon just smiled for a moment and didn't answer.

"…You don't have to know what it means," Speon said. "Some things you understand just by listening."

Listening to that, I thought, strangely, of the man. He was always like that too. Things you understood without an explanation.

That night, Mena took a piece of paper out of her record box and showed it to Father.

"…This is your name," Mena said. "Exactly as the children told it to me. I wrote it down and carried it with me."

Father looked at that paper for a long time. Even reading his own name, his face didn't quite believe it belonged to him.

I looked at the handwriting of Father's name on that paper. It was Mena's writing. There were marks of pressing hard as she wrote. I felt that pressure spoke of how carefully Mena had written that name.

The firelight flickered over the paper. Father traced the letters one by one with a fingertip. Very slowly. That gesture, I thought, looked like a child learning letters for the first time.

I saw the creases where the paper had been folded and unfolded. It looked like it had been folded many times over. I guessed at how long Mena had carried that paper close.

"…Is this… my name?" Father asked.

"…Yes," Mena said. "A name your children have never once forgotten."

"…Why show it to me now?" Father asked.

Mena chose her words for a moment.

"…I thought the time had come," Mena said. "The first time I came, I thought it still wasn't. Seeing your face today, I thought it was all right to give it to you now."

Father nodded at that. His fingertip still rested on the paper.

"…Can I keep this?" Father asked.

"…That's why I brought it," Mena said.

Father folded the paper again, following the creases already there. Then he put it inside his coat, in an inner pocket. It was the same spot where Father had always kept his important things, back in Brick Hollow. I recognized that, and swallowed once.

The fire crackled loudly once. No one flinched at the sound.

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. In that moment, I heard Father read his own name aloud. The pronunciation was still unfamiliar. But within that unfamiliarity, I felt something coming back, very slowly.

(Continued in Chapter 39.)

Chapter 39. I've Been There

_Previously_ — Mena came to the well and met Father. When she showed him the name she'd kept, he read his own name aloud for the first time. Still unfamiliar, but something was slowly coming back.

Even after Mena left again, the days at the well didn't change much. Drawing water, walking, leaving a record in the evening — the repetition continued. Strangely, that repetition set my mind at ease. In Haven, every day had felt like a new incident. Here, every day resembled yesterday. I already knew now how precious that resemblance was.

Someone else came out of the well.

This time it was a young boy. He looked about ten. The moment he came out, he couldn't even cry — he just sat there, dazed.

The sky was unusually overcast that day. Under that overcast sky, I thought the boy's face was the same gray as the clouds. This wasn't the first time I'd seen a face like that at the well. But I never got used to it, no matter how many times.

His clothes were old and thin. His shoes didn't match. In that sight, the clothes of Brick Hollow's children overlapped in my mind. I didn't know which hollow he'd come from. But his eyes were not unfamiliar to me.

There was an old scar on his knee. I looked at that scar and started to guess whether it was recent or very old, then stopped myself. Either way, that wasn't what mattered right now.

Watching him, I thought of when we first met Father, months ago. I remembered how helpless we had been then. It was different now. We already knew what we had to do.

Speon, as always, brought water first. The boy couldn't even drink it once he had it. His hands were shaking.

The boy's reflected face wavered on the surface of the water jar. Watching that trembling reflection, I wondered if the boy still found even his own face unfamiliar. People just out of the well were often like that. They treated their own hands, their own faces, as if seeing them for the first time.

I saw that trembling. And for the first time, I realized this place had been holding more people than Speon alone could carry. Now we were with him too.

Speon set the water jar down in front of the boy and stepped back without a word. I knew that stepping back was a kindness. Speon had long known how to never approach anyone first.

Father was watching the boy from a little way off too. Something familiar crossed his face. I didn't know exactly what expression it was. But I felt that Father was recognizing something in that boy.

Father slowly walked toward him. I tensed for a moment. Father was still clumsy about growing close to strangers. But that day was different. Father quietly crouched down in front of the boy.

"…You're scared, aren't you?" Father asked. In a very small voice.

The boy looked up at Father. He didn't answer. But he nodded, just barely.

"…So was I," Father said. "Still am, sometimes."

I held my breath, hearing that. It was the first time I'd heard Father speak his own fear aloud like that.

"…Then when does it stop being scary?" the boy asked.

Father couldn't answer that question for a long moment. I knew he was trying hard not to pretend to know what he didn't.

"…I don't know," Father finally said. "But you can still drink the water even while you're scared. That much I know."

The boy looked down at the water jar at that. His hands still shook. But with that shaking hand, he pulled the jar a little closer to himself.

Watching that scene, I thought — Father hadn't given the boy an answer. Father had no answer to give. But Father had given him the fact that he was still scared too. That stayed with the boy longer than any answer could have.

Jeteo sat quietly beside the boy.

"…I've been there too," Jeteo said. "Our father came out here just like this. He didn't know anything at first, either."

The boy looked at Jeteo. For the first time, there was some response in his eyes.

"…And now?" the boy asked. His voice cracked.

"…Now he can read his own name out loud," Jeteo said. "Not all of it. But it's coming back, bit by bit."

Hearing that, the boy took the water jar carefully in both hands. And began drinking, very slowly. Watching that, I realized only then that I'd been holding my breath.

I thought, in that moment, of the day we'd first handed Father water, months before. We hadn't known anything then. There was nothing else we could do besides hand him water. Jeteo could do far more now than he could then. But in the end, what he was doing was still the same thing. Handing over water. Sitting beside him.

"…I don't remember what my name was. At all," the boy said. His voice was choked.

"…That's okay," Jeteo said. "It doesn't have to come to you right now. Our father took months."

"…Months?" the boy asked, startled.

"…Yeah," Jeteo said. "But he wasn't alone during those months. I think that was the most important part."

"…Is it not okay to be alone?" the boy asked.

"…It's not that it's not okay," Jeteo said. "But it takes longer, alone. I've seen it for myself."

The boy gripped the water jar a little tighter, as if turning that over in his mind.

"…Why are you being so nice to me?" the boy asked again. His voice still carried a trace of wariness.

Jeteo laughed a little at the question.

"…You already asked me that," Jeteo said.

"…I wanted to ask again," the boy said.

Jeteo really laughed at that. I thought it was the first bright laugh I'd seen from him in days.

"…You can ask," Jeteo said. "As many times as you want."

"…Even the same thing again?"

"…Yeah," Jeteo said. "I asked the same thing about a hundred times myself. No one ever told me to stop asking."

Hearing that, I thought of Haven's intake window. There, you couldn't file the same thing twice. It was already a resolved entry. Only then did I know, in a single word, what that city had lacked. A place to ask again.

From that day on, the boy followed Jeteo everywhere. He tried to copy everything Jeteo did.

When Jeteo mended the bucket, the boy watched from beside him, then slid his own hand in to help. When Jeteo walked around the well with Father, the boy followed a few steps behind. I thought that looked like the old Jeteo. He too had once followed someone's footsteps like that, wordlessly.

One day the boy picked up a stone and handed it to Jeteo. He didn't explain why. Jeteo didn't ask why either — he just took it. Watching that scene, I thought of Father. Father used to collect stones for no reason too. I thought this resemblance might not be an accident.

Jeteo carried that stone in his pocket. A few days later I saw him take it out and set it back down in front of the boy.

"…Here, take this back," Jeteo said.

"…Why? I gave it to you."

"…I already received it," Jeteo said. "Now you hold on to it, and later give it to someone else. I think that's better."

The boy tilted his head at that, but took the stone back and put it in his pocket. Watching that, I felt something flowing from Jeteo to this boy, and someday, to yet another person.

Speon watched that scene from a distance and said quietly to me,

"…Your brother's turned into a completely different person now."

"…How so?"

"…He used to try to find answers," Speon said. "Now he's building a place beside him. I think that's a much harder thing to do."

"…Why is it harder?" I asked.

"…An answer, once you find it, is over," Speon said. "Being beside someone has to be rebuilt every day. Just because you were there yesterday doesn't mean you're there today."

I heard that and looked toward our hut. Father sat in the shade, watching the boy. It looked like a spot where nothing was happening. I already knew now that it wasn't nothing at all.

"…Why are you helping me?" the boy asked one day.

Jeteo thought for a moment before answering.

"…Because I've had it done for me," Jeteo said. "Being sat beside — I've had that too. So I can do it too."

"…By who?" the boy asked.

Jeteo glanced toward Speon for a moment, and toward the shade of the tree.

"…Lots of people," Jeteo said. "My sister. Some man. I'm still receiving it, right here."

The boy repeated that back, as if turning it over.

"…Because you've had it done for you…" the boy murmured.

"…Yeah," Jeteo said. "You'll be that for someone else someday too. You probably can't believe that now."

The boy tilted his head. It wasn't a face that had fully understood the words. But something seemed to have stayed with him. From that day on, whenever he filled his water jar, he scooped out one extra ladleful and set it aside, even though it wasn't his to give. Not even knowing who it was for.

Hearing that answer, I understood one complete sentence for the first time. A person who has never been loved has a harder time learning how to love. Jeteo was giving away, exactly as he'd received it.

I copied that sentence straight into my evening record right then. My hand shook as I wrote it. I knew it was a sentence about Jeteo, and at the same time a sentence about me. I was giving away what I'd received too. Seeds. Waiting. How to sit beside someone.

I'd been listening to that conversation from the side. I knew that the old Jeteo couldn't have answered a question like that. The old Jeteo would have said "just because." This Jeteo knew the reason.

I thought that difference was truly huge. Between "just because" and "because I've had it done for me" lay the whole of the months Jeteo had passed through. Having watched those months beside him, I knew the weight of that sentence better than anyone.

Within a few days, the boy began to smile a little. He still didn't know his own name. But he liked the temporary name Jeteo had given him.

Jeteo named him "Little Shadow," because the boy always followed behind him like a shadow. At first the boy laughed at the name. But after a few days, he really did come to like being called that.

"…This isn't really my name, though," the boy said one day.

"…I know," Jeteo said. "But a name needs to hold the place until the real one comes."

The boy nodded, turning that over.

"…Will I get to find my real name too, someday?" the boy asked.

"…Yeah," Jeteo said. "Slowly. But it'll definitely come back."

"…How do you know it'll definitely come back?"

Jeteo glanced toward Father for a moment. He was just then pulling the bucket up at the well.

"…Because I've seen it," Jeteo said. "Our father didn't know anything at first either. Now he reads his own name out loud. I've seen it with my own eyes. So believe it."

The boy smiled for the first time at that. A short laugh. But a clear one. I recorded that laugh briefly in that evening's record. "Today, the nameless boy smiled for the first time."

Watching that laugh, I felt something strange. A few months ago, Jeteo had been someone who couldn't say anything to anyone with certainty. Now Jeteo was handing certainty to someone else, based on what he himself had seen.

Speon came over to us that evening and stroked the boy's head.

"…You can stay here until your name settles," Speon said. "No hurry."

"…Can I stay here even without a name?" the boy asked. His voice still carried some anxiety.

"…You're a person even without a name," Speon said. "There's no rule here that says people without names can't stay."

The boy smiled toward Speon for the first time at that. A small smile. But I noticed it was the second smile the boy had shown in a few days. I was glad that smile was coming a little more often.

The boy nodded. And for the first time, got up on his own to go fill a water jar.

I watched him from behind. His steps were still careful. But they were clearly steadier than the day before. I didn't miss that small difference — I held it carefully in my eyes.

That night, Jeteo said to me,

"…I felt something strange talking to that boy today."

"…What was it?"

"…I was saying, word for word, what I learned from that man. 'Even without a name, you're here right now. That's enough.' I never once memorized that. It just came out."

I said nothing for a moment, hearing that. Only the sound of the night wind brushing the trees by the well.

"…Doesn't that scare you?" I asked.

"…What?"

"…You becoming like that man."

Jeteo thought about that for a long time. In that silence, I saw starlight flicker in his eyes now and then. The sound of night insects filled the well-yard.

"…It feels a little scary," Jeteo finally said. "But it doesn't feel bad. That's the strange part."

I didn't say anything back to that. I just sat a little closer beside him. Jeteo seemed to notice, and without a word, let his shoulder be there for me.

Far off, the boy made some sound — talking in his sleep, maybe. I turned my head toward it for a moment, then back to Jeteo. The boy was sleeping soundly. His sleeping face looked much more at ease than the day we'd met him.

"…That boy will say that to someone else someday too," I said.

"…I hope so," Jeteo said.

"…Then what will he call you, when he does?"

Jeteo didn't laugh at that for a long moment.

"…He doesn't have to remember my name," Jeteo finally said. "He just has to remember that someone sat beside him. I don't even know that man's name either."

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. I knew, then. Jeteo still couldn't explain who that man was. But he was already carrying that man's words in his body. I was sure, for the first time that day, of how close that was to the beginning of faith.

(Continued in Chapter 40.)

Chapter 40. Going to Him First

_Previously_ — A nameless boy newly came out at the well. Jeteo passed on to him, exactly as he'd received it, how to sit beside someone. And realized for himself that he was carrying out the words he'd learned from the man at the flowerbed in his body, without ever having memorized them.

The man appeared and vanished with no fixed time.

For the first few days, I tried marking the days he came on a calendar. But I soon gave up. I couldn't find any pattern at all. And yet, he had never once come on a day he wasn't needed.

Some days he'd be touching the soil by the well. Some days he'd sit quietly beside someone newly out of it. No one ever knew when he'd come.

Some days I only realized afterward that he'd been there. The bucket sitting a little differently than usual, a stone the boy had been playing with moved to another spot. Every time I saw those small traces, I knew he'd passed through. He never left much of a mark of himself.

His clothes were always similar — plain, the kind you'd see anywhere. At first I thought that was strange. Later, I came to think it suited him instead. A man who didn't want to stand out.

His shoes were always worn too. I couldn't tell if that wear came from long use or from long walking. Probably both. Every time I looked at those shoes, I felt that wherever he came from, he was never a being who simply stood still.

I'd once tried to trace back the places he appeared. When someone newly out couldn't lift a water jar, when the boy woke alone at night crying, when Speon, rarely, looked worn out. He'd be standing quietly in those spots. I thought that couldn't be coincidence. But he never made a show of having come knowing in advance, either.

That was when I began to notice that irregularity was, itself, a kind of rule. He was always there, at the moment he was most needed.

I'd once even tried to work out a relationship between when he came and the weather. Did he come more often on rainy days, less on clear ones? In the end I found no correlation at all. Strangely, I liked even that failed observation. Some beings couldn't be caught by calculation.

Speon, and Mena too, never asked him certain things. When he'd come. Why now. I came to understand, living at the well, that this silence wasn't resignation. It was a kind of ease that only someone who had watched a long time could have.

I envied that ease at first. But living there, I came to understand it wasn't a natural-born ease. Speon must have been impatient too, at first, like me. I came to understand, little by little, at the well, that this ease had been made by time.

I once asked Speon about it.

The sound of him mending the bucket creaked a few times in the pause. I asked, carefully, with that as my backdrop.

"…Where does that man stay?"

Speon answered without stopping his work.

"…I don't know either," Speon said. "Haven't known for decades. But I've never once felt shorted. He came when he was needed."

"…Have you never once wondered?"

"…I've wondered," Speon said. "But at some point, that wondering stopped turning into fretting. There are things you can leave as just wondering."

"…Elpis, aren't you curious too?" Speon asked.

"…Yes," I said. "But lately that curiosity doesn't scare me."

"…Why?"

"…I learned it's all right to be without knowing," I said.

"…Where'd you learn that?" Speon asked.

"…From a seed," I said. "You don't know when a seed will sprout. But you keep giving it water anyway. I know now that that's what faith is."

Speon looked at me a long time at that. His face had something startled in it.

"…How old did you say you were?" Speon asked.

"…Thirteen," I said, laughing.

"…Sometimes you seem like you've lived longer than me," Speon said.

"…But I get impatient too, sometimes," I said. "Not every day. Some nights."

"…What do you do then?" Speon asked.

"…I water something," I said. "Even though you're not supposed to water at night. I just need to do something, is all."

Speon didn't laugh at that. Instead he nodded slowly.

"…I wipe down the bucket at night too, for no reason," Speon said. "Even though I already wiped it down during the day."

I held on to that for a long time. I didn't know, then, that it would someday reach Jeteo too.

The boy — the nameless one too — flinched the first time he saw the man. I understood that reaction. A body tenses first when a stranger suddenly appears. But after a few days, the boy grew easy with him too. Whenever the man appeared, the boy would slip over and sit beside him. He said nothing. Just sat there. Watching that, I understood that the boy was already learning how to be beside someone without speaking to them.

The man never tried to teach the boy anything. He simply touched the dirt alongside him when the boy fiddled with it. When the boy stacked stones, he watched without knocking them down. In that, I felt something like what Father had shown, the first time he came to us.

Once, a stone tower the boy was building collapsed. He looked like he was about to burst into tears right there. The man, beside him, picked the stones up one by one without a word and handed them back. The boy took them and started stacking again. Watching that scene from a distance, I understood that this was always how he rebuilt what had fallen. Not building it back up for you — handing you what you needed to build it back up yourself.

That evening the boy came and asked me,

"…Why doesn't that man help? It'd be faster if he just built it for me."

I couldn't answer that question right away. In Haven, the answer would have come instantly. File a request, and the tower would have arrived already built.

"…I guess fast isn't always best," I managed to say.

The boy tilted his head. But the next day, too, he stacked that tower alone. And the day he finished it, without anyone telling him to, he called me over to show me. I knew, then, that a tower built for you has no one to call.

I thought back to when I'd first met him. In an alley in Haven, he'd seemed like just someone passing by. I hadn't known, then, that he'd be a person who stayed with us this long. Now, on a day he didn't come, it left a strange emptiness. I didn't know exactly when that emptiness had started.

I wrote that emptiness into my evening record once. "That man didn't come today. The day felt strangely long." Reading that sentence back, I laughed. I'd somehow started measuring the length of my days by whether he came or not.

One afternoon, he sat alone in the shade of the tree by the well.

Jeteo walked over. I didn't follow. I knew that was his place to go.

It was late afternoon. Sunlight slanted in through the shade of the tree. Jeteo's shadow stretched long, moving toward the man. I watched that shadow gradually shorten. Jeteo's steps were slow. But they didn't stop.

I watched Jeteo's back. His shoulders weren't as tense as they used to be. Watching that back, I felt for the first time that you could tell what someone was walking toward just from the shape of their walking away.

"…Can we talk?" Jeteo asked. Not in his old accusing tone.

I watched from a distance. I saw, for the first time, Jeteo going to find him. It had always been the man appearing first. This time Jeteo approached first.

Watching that back, I thought of our last night in Brick Hollow. The night Jeteo shouted at the sky. Back then Jeteo had approached in anger. Now he was approaching by walking. I knew that difference was enormous.

The man gestured with his hand to the spot beside him. Jeteo sat.

I watched the way Jeteo sat beside him. In the old days he would have crossed his arms, or turned his body away. That day he simply sat side by side. Legs stretched out, hands resting on his knees. I understood, then, how much a single small posture could say.

The smell of earth in the tree's shade drifted on the wind to where I stood. Breathing it in, I fixed the sight of the two of them sitting there in my eyes. From a distance it was just an ordinary sight — two people sitting under a tree, nothing at all. But I knew how long it had taken to make that sight possible.

Neither spoke for a long while. Even from a distance, I could feel that the silence wasn't awkward.

I sat by the bucket and watched the two of them. Speon quietly came and sat beside me. He said nothing. We sat there together, side by side, watching the two of them from afar.

Every time the wind stirred the tree's leaves, the shadow shifted a little across the two of them. I tried to gauge how much time had passed by watching that shadow. But I couldn't quite manage it. That silence didn't seem to be something you could measure with time.

I knew, then, that some conversations have already begun, even in silence. Jeteo and the man hadn't said a word yet. But I felt that something was already passing between them.

I leaned toward Speon and asked in a very small voice,

"…What do you think those two are going to talk about?"

Speon shook his head.

"…I don't know either," Speon said. "But whatever comes out after a silence like that usually stays with you a long time."

I heard that and looked toward the two of them again. Still no words. But I wasn't afraid of that silence any more.

The bucket rope swayed lightly in the wind, making a small sound. A bird landed on the well's stone wall, then flew off again. I listened to those small sounds as if counting each one. If I didn't do at least that, the silence felt too heavy to bear.

"…I still don't really know," Jeteo finally said. "Who you are. Why you keep showing up. Whether I'm supposed to believe in God."

He didn't rush to answer, hearing that. In that silence, I felt Jeteo was preparing his next words.

"…I've come this far not knowing," Jeteo added. "But somehow, that not-knowing doesn't make me as angry as it used to. That's the stranger part."

"…I know," he said.

"…Don't just say you know — tell me what you know," Jeteo said. There was a trace of old irritation mixed into his voice.

He laughed a little at that. Not an angry laugh.

"…I know you still want an answer," he said. "And I know you're sitting here even though it hasn't come. That's a beginning."

"…But there's one thing I do know," Jeteo said. "I know how to sit beside someone now. But when I think about where I learned it… it comes down to you, in the end."

"…Do you remember how you learned it?" he asked.

Jeteo thought about that question for a moment.

"…You never once taught me," Jeteo said. "I just kept watching you do it. And somewhere along the way, I found myself doing it too."

"…That's what learning is," he said. "Even without words."

"…So did I learn you, or did I believe in you?" Jeteo asked.

"…The two don't always come in that order," he said.

Jeteo laughed a little at that. Closer to an exasperated laugh.

"…Talk like that and I'll never get an answer."

"…Right," he said. "But you keep coming."

I heard that from a distance and my chest went hot. That man had never once tried to win an argument against Jeteo's questions. He had simply recognized, before Jeteo did, the fact that Jeteo kept coming back.

He didn't answer that. But even from a distance, I saw his shoulder move, ever so slightly. I didn't know what that movement meant. But I could tell it wasn't a bad one.

He turned and faced Jeteo directly for the first time at that.

I felt that his eyes were different from usual. Eyes that were always gentle were, in that moment, looking straight at Jeteo.

"…Let me ask you one thing," he said. "Why can't you still trust me?"

The well-yard suddenly seemed to go very quiet. Even the bucket, even the birds, seemed to pause for a moment. In that stillness, I held my breath.

Far off, I felt Speon's hands go still too. Both of us knew, in our bodies, that this moment mattered.

Jeteo was silent for a long time. In that silence, I saw his hand tighten into a fist on his knee.

I watched Jeteo's profile. His jaw trembled faintly. I realized, for the first time, that trembling wasn't anger, but something else. Jeteo was fighting with something now — not with him, but with something old inside himself.

He didn't rush that silence. Sunlight filtering through the leaves drew small patterns on the dirt between them. I watched those patterns shift, trying to gauge how much time had passed.

"…Because I'm scared," Jeteo finally said. "If I trust you and it ends up like it did with Father again… I really don't think I could bear it this time."

Jeteo's voice was very small. But I felt the whole well-yard had gone quiet as if it too had heard those words. I knew, in that moment, that Jeteo was showing the deepest bottom of his heart for the first time in years.

He didn't argue with that at all. I found that strange. The Ledger of Haven would have had eight grounds of comfort ready on the spot. He didn't do that. He simply shifted his knee a little, turning his body further toward Jeteo.

"…That fear — I can't make it go away," he finally said.

"…Then what can you do?" Jeteo asked.

"…I'll be scared alongside you," he said.

I set down that answer as the last line of this chapter. I heard Jeteo say, for the first time, the real reason behind his doubt. Not logic. Fear. I knew, in that moment, that Jeteo had come just a little bit closer.

(Continued in Chapter 41.)

Chapter 41. I've Lost Someone Too

_Previously_ — For the first time, Jeteo went to find the man at the flowerbed on his own. To the question "Why can't you still trust me?", Jeteo gave, for the first time, his real reason — "If I trust you and it ends up like it did with Father again… I really don't think I could bear it this time."

I still remember the air of that afternoon. It was warm. But Jeteo's voice made that air feel heavy.

I had thought I knew Jeteo's fear. But hearing that word from Jeteo's own mouth, that day, was a completely different thing.

Up until that moment, I had only ever thought of him as a person with too much anger in him. Only after hearing those words did I understand. There had always been fear underneath the anger, first. I was frustrated with myself for not noticing that sooner.

I had never once hated Jeteo. But I had often mistaken myself for knowing him completely. That mistake was, right then, the thing I felt most ashamed of.

Jeteo's words seemed to hang in the air. I could tell, just from the tremor in his voice, how long they had been held down.

Hearing that, I thought again of our last night in Brick Hollow. The night Jeteo shouted at the sky — I had thought, then, that he was only angry. Thinking of it now, the Jeteo of that night must already have been carrying this same fear. I hadn't been able to read it, then.

He heard Jeteo's words and fell silent for a long time.

I watched that silence from a distance. I knew it wasn't a dismissive silence. It was a silence weighing something.

I held my breath and waited to see how long that weighing would last.

Evening was drawing near. The sky had begun to turn a pale orange. I watched that light touch Jeteo's face. His expression was far more open than usual.

The tree's shadow stretched long, all the way to their feet. Watching that shadow's edge lengthen little by little, I gauged how fast the evening was coming. A wait that would normally have bored me wasn't the least bit boring, that day.

The well's bucket rope swayed lightly in the wind, creaking. That sound seemed unusually loud to me.

A bird cried somewhere far off. That cry seemed strangely unbothered by the moment, I thought. The world went on turning as usual. It felt like only inside us was time flowing differently.

I stood a little apart. Speon quietly came and stood beside me. He said nothing. Both of us just watched that spot.

I heard Speon's breathing beside me, slow. I tried to steady my own breath to match it. But it didn't work well. In the end I simply chose to hold my breath instead.

The wind stirred the trees by the well. Jeteo endured that silence and stayed right where he was. He didn't flee. That alone told me how far Jeteo had come.

I saw that Jeteo's feet didn't move at all this time. In the old days he would already have turned and walked off. Watching those feet, I understood how many moments Jeteo had learned to endure.

I saw his toes pressing slightly into the dirt. As if trying to anchor himself to that spot. Watching that, I felt both pity and pride.

Only then did I realize my own hand was gripping the hem of my clothes tightly. I'd been tense without knowing it.

I understood, then, for the first time — I was as afraid of this moment as Jeteo was. If Jeteo broke, I felt I would break too.

I didn't want Jeteo to know that fear. So I hid my clenched hand behind my back. I knew Jeteo had no room, in that moment, to look after me too. That was mine to bear alone.

Just then, Speon, without a word, brought his own hand behind my back. And laid it over my hidden hand. He didn't look at me. He kept watching Jeteo.

I was startled by the weight of that hand. I hadn't told anyone. But Speon already knew. I learned, then, in my own body, for the first time — that the person beside you knows even without being told.

"…I know that feeling," he finally said.

The answer felt so simple that it struck me as strange. Jeteo must have thought so too — his brow creased slightly.

"…How would you know that?" Jeteo asked. Still edged.

I heard the sharpness still left in Jeteo's voice. But under that sharpness, I felt, for the first time, that a real question was there too.

There was no anger in the man's face. Watching that expression, I thought he seemed like someone who had already taken in Jeteo's sharp words countless times. Not that he wasn't hurt — he was someone who looked, first, past the tone to what lay behind it.
