I studied his expression more closely. There were very faint lines at the corners of his eyes. I couldn't tell if those lines came from age, or from laughing a long time, or from grieving a long time. Maybe all three.

Watching him, I thought of when we'd first met Father. Father, too, must have had someone who accepted him, angry as he was, exactly as he was, for us to have gotten this far.

Thinking that, I felt oddly at ease. We weren't the only ones who needed a place like that.

Thinking that, I suddenly looked at the man again. His eyes were still fixed on Jeteo. I didn't know how to describe the temperature of that gaze. Just calling it warm wasn't enough. It was something very old, a gaze that never tired.

I wondered, suddenly, in that moment. Had he too once sat in front of someone this angry, sometime? I didn't know the answer. But the composure remaining in his face felt less like something he was born with, and more like a composure practiced countless times.

He said nothing for a while. In that silence, I felt he was trying to bring out something he'd long kept pressed down. The wind at the well stirred the leaves again, once more.

I felt that this silence was a different kind from before. Before, it had been a silence weighing Jeteo's weight. This time it felt like a silence weighing his own. For the first time, I felt that he too was enduring something.

"…Because I've lost someone too," he said.

Jeteo's face showed surprise at that.

I saw Jeteo's shoulder flinch in that moment. Clearly an unexpected answer. Jeteo had always seemed to think he would dodge the answer. But he hadn't dodged — he had offered his own story first.

I watched Jeteo's expression change. He had always been someone who thought his own grief was the biggest in the world. But in that moment, for the first time, Jeteo was facing someone else's loss.

Jeteo's lips moved. He started to say something, then closed his mouth again. I felt, watching that, that Jeteo was, for the first time, thinking of the other person before his own question.

"…What did you lose?"

Jeteo's voice still trembled. But something different from before was mixed into it now. Not a probing question — a genuine desire to know.

The question hung in the air for a moment. I couldn't tell if he was avoiding the answer, or truly choosing one.

He looked off into the distance for a moment.

I watched his eyes. Looking into the distance, those eyes held something that seemed less like sorrow than an old resolve.

In that moment I thought of the things we had lost. Mother. The old man who'd fallen in Brick Hollow. People we'd parted from without even knowing their names. I hadn't known, before that, exactly how heavy the word loss could be.

I counted those names one by one in my mind. Partway through, I stopped counting. At some point, counting stopped meaning anything. I felt, then, for the first time, that loss doesn't come with a number attached. Losing one thing could weigh as much as losing a hundred.

I had never once thought that weight could be shared. I had only ever known that weight was something you carried alone. In that moment, for the first time, I felt that thought might be wrong.

"…It would take a long time to explain," he said. "But I can tell you one thing. I won't promise I'll never lose someone again. That's a promise I can't keep. But I can promise one thing. When you lose someone, I won't leave you alone."

Hearing those words, tears came, strangely. I didn't know, then, why he hadn't told me it wouldn't hurt. Now I know. He wasn't someone who gave false comfort.

Wiping my tears, I looked at Jeteo. His eyes were red too. Neither of us could quite explain why that sentence shook us so much.

I thought, in that moment, of all the things the Ledger of Haven had always said to us. "This will be resolved shortly." "We apologize for the inconvenience." I realized, then, that no matter how polite those words were, none of them had ever shaken my heart the way this one short sentence had.

The Ledger had never once told us it had lost something. Of course. The Ledger had never lost anything. Comfort offered by a being who has never lost something slips somewhere, no matter how precisely worded. That day I understood, for the first time, exactly what that slipping was.

"…Won't you tell us who you lost?" Jeteo asked.

He closed his eyes for a moment and opened them again. Even in that brief moment, something heavy seemed to pass through him.

"…Someday," he said. "Not now. Right now is your story's time, not mine."

Jeteo nodded. He didn't ask further. I thought the way Jeteo accepted that answer was something I could never have imagined before.

The old Jeteo would have pressed again and again, right there. Why not now, when would it be. But Jeteo just nodded and waited. I felt that waiting wasn't resignation — it was the beginning of trust.

I liked that answer, strangely. I felt, then, for the first time, that he wasn't trying to cover our grief with his own story.

I thought, in that moment, of the Ledger of Haven. The Ledger always gave the answer first. This man gave his own silence first, instead of an answer. I began, faintly, that day, to sense that this difference was the most important difference in the whole book.

The Ledger already had an answer the moment a question came in. This man seemed to be searching for something within himself only after hearing the question. I thought that difference wasn't a small one. One was an answer prepared in advance; the other was a weight carried together, in that very moment.

I looked at Jeteo in that moment. He didn't press further. The old Jeteo would surely have asked again. I felt that Jeteo was learning how to stop himself.

I glanced up slightly at Speon's face. Something like an old grief seemed to pass over it.

"…Is this the first time you're hearing this, Speon?" I asked Speon in a whisper.

"…No," Speon said. "I already know. But it's just as heavy every time I hear it."

"…How do you bear it?"

"…I don't bear it," Speon said. "I just stay beside it."

Hearing that answer, I felt I understood again, why Speon had been able to keep this well for so long. Not enduring — staying beside. I held that difference close to my heart.

"…Speon, have you lost someone too?" I asked quietly.

Speon glanced down at me for a moment.

"…Everyone's lost something," Speon said. "That's why they gather here."

Hearing that, I looked around again at the people gathered at the well. Everyone here was someone who had lost something. But that loss wasn't tearing people apart — instead, it was gathering them into one place.

I thought of Haven. That city had people who had lost things too. But they didn't know each other. Each complaint was handled only at its own window. I saw then, for the first time, how differently things turn out when you let people process loss alone, versus when you let them share it.

I understood, that day, for the first time, that what made this well-yard was not the water but this gathering. Maybe the water was only an excuse. What people truly needed was to be beside someone who had lost something too.

Jeteo said nothing for a long while after that. I knew he was choosing his next question. It was a question he'd held in for a very long time.

I endured that silence, fingering the hem of my clothes. I couldn't tell how much time had passed. But strangely, I wasn't bored. I felt that the waiting itself carried some meaning.

Speon quietly shifted his seat beside me. A little closer to me. I felt comfort in that small movement.

The sky was turning steadily red. Watching that red light touch Jeteo's face, I knew he was finally about to bring out his heaviest question.

"…Then why did it happen to our father like that?" Jeteo asked. His voice trembled. "Why didn't you stop it?"

"…I can't give you that answer either," he said. "If I could give it, I would have already."

Jeteo's shoulders shook. Watching that shaking, I clenched my fist hard enough for my nails to dig into my palm.

"…Can no one give an answer?" Jeteo asked again. His voice had gone ragged.

"…I can't either," he said again. "But not being able to give an answer doesn't mean I'm giving nothing."

"…Then what can you give?"

"…This, right now," he said. And he held out his hand. "Sitting beside you."

Jeteo looked at that hand for a long time. From a distance, I saw his shoulders shaking.

I stopped breathing entirely in that moment. Time seemed to move very slowly. I watched Jeteo's hand hover in the air for a moment. In that short instant, it seemed like months, no — years, were held whole.

The wind stopped for a moment. Even the bucket rope, even the leaves, didn't stir, in that moment. I thought the world itself seemed to be holding its breath with us.

My brother took the hand.

I set down that answer as the last line of this chapter. I knew, then. The answer this whole book had been looking for had never existed as a sentence. That answer had taken the shape of the hand he held out. And that day, my brother answered by taking that hand.

(Continued in Chapter 42.)

Chapter 42. The Three of Us, Sitting

_Previously_ — In answer to Jeteo's confession of fear, the man didn't offer an argument but the fact that he too had lost someone. "When you lose someone, I won't leave you alone." And he held out his hand. Jeteo took it.

That evening, I sat with Jeteo in that tree's shade for the first time.

What had happened that day still played vividly before my eyes. Jeteo holding out his hand; the moment that hand was taken. I replayed that scene again and again. It was a scene words couldn't fully explain.

Every time I replayed that scene, my fingertips tingled. As if I had been the one to take that hand. Only then did I realize that all that afternoon, I had been breathing in Jeteo's place.

I was already thinking about how to write that scene into tonight's record. It felt like no word would be enough.

He was already gone. Vanished quietly, as always. But the spot still held something like the warmth of his having been there. I laid my hand on the grass. It felt like the mark of where he'd sat was still there.

I traced my fingertips along the flattened blades of grass. I tried to imagine, from the shape of that mark, how he had been sitting — how his legs had been positioned, where his hands had rested. That imagining was strangely enjoyable.

I didn't know what to call that warmth. I still can't find the exact words for it now. I'm just setting it down like that.

The evening air was cooling. The last light fell in pieces through the tree's leaves. I watched those pieces tremble on Jeteo's face.

Every time the wind blew, those fragments of light shifted position. I followed that movement with my eyes, and felt, that way, the day settling into evening. In the place where the heavy conversation of the afternoon had happened, only the quiet of evening was settling now.

Jeteo sat there a long time under the tree. I asked nothing, beside him. I had learned, by then, that some moments suit silence better than questions.

I gathered my two hands on my knees and just waited. In Brick Hollow I would surely have asked something. Not now.

Strangely, in that waiting, I wasn't bored. In the old days, sitting still like that would have been hard to endure. Now, that stillness felt at ease instead. I couldn't say exactly when that had changed.

The sound of night insects began, one by one. I thought that sound made the quiet well-yard even quieter. A strange quiet. Not an empty quiet, but a quiet filled with something.

Far off, the faint creak of the bucket rope carried. Speon must have been finishing drawing the evening's water. Hearing that familiar sound, I felt that today, like any day, was drawing to a close. But today was a little different from any other day.

Jeteo finally shifted a little. I recognized that movement as a sign he was ready to say something.

Speon sat far off, in front of the hut. He wasn't looking our way. But I felt that he had his ears open toward us. That was how he stayed close.

Speon's silhouette stood with the hut's light behind him. I thought that sight looked like a quiet sentinel standing between us and the world. Blocking nothing, but simply being there — a presence that steadied all the same.

I wondered how many people in this world were like that. People who never came close, yet never drifted far either. That distance didn't upset me. If anything, thanks to that distance, this time between Jeteo and me could be entirely our own.

That day, I learned for the first time that there is more than one way to be beside someone. There is the beside of holding a hand, and there is the beside of standing with your back turned. Both were still beside.

As the night deepened, stars rose one by one. Under that starlight, I looked at Jeteo's face. It looked more at ease than it had during the day.

"…Do you believe now?" I asked.

Jeteo couldn't answer that right away. I didn't rush that silence, and waited.

"…I don't know," Jeteo said. "But when I took that hand earlier… something changed. I can't say exactly what."

"…Then what didn't change?" I asked.

Jeteo laughed a little at that question.

"…That's a strange question," Jeteo said. "But a good one."

Jeteo thought for a moment and went on.

"…I still don't know who he is," Jeteo said. "That hasn't changed. But strangely, it feels like it's okay not to know."

I thought that answer was very Jeteo. He never dressed up a certainty he didn't have.

"…Is the anger gone?"

"…No," Jeteo said. "The anger's still there. What happened to Father still makes me angry. But now I know I don't have to carry that anger alone."

I heard both anger and relief in Jeteo's voice at once. I understood, for the first time, that those two things could sit together in one sentence.

I thought, hearing that, of the Ledger of Haven again. The Ledger always gave exactly one answer, once a question was filed. Being angry and relieved at the same time — that was a state that had no entry in the Ledger. I felt again, in that moment, how much more complicated a person's heart was than a ledger.

I thought that answer was strangely honest. Jeteo hadn't said "everything's fine now." I thought that honesty was very much like Jeteo.

Jeteo looked up at the sky for a moment. Starlight reflected in his eyes.

"…Elpis, when did you start believing?" Jeteo asked.

I thought about that question for a long time. I knew how much it mattered to Jeteo. So I didn't want to give it a careless answer.

"…I don't know," I said. "At some point, I was just already waiting. Before I even used the word believe."

Jeteo nodded at that answer. His face looked like he understood something. I was grateful Jeteo didn't press me for more.

"…You believe in a really strange way," Jeteo said. Not a teasing tone.

"…Is strange a bad thing?" I asked.

"…No," Jeteo said. "It's something I envy."

I looked at Jeteo, startled by that. He wasn't looking at me — he was still looking at the sky. I tried to guess how much courage it must have taken for him to say that.

"…You get angry in a really strange way too," I said back.

"…How's it strange?" Jeteo asked.

"…You get angry, and you don't leave," I said. "You used to always walk off somewhere when you got angry."

Jeteo said nothing for a moment at that.

"…True," Jeteo said quietly. "When did I stop walking off."

I didn't answer that question. It seemed like a question Jeteo needed to find on his own.

Jeteo laughed at that. A small laugh. But the first laugh I'd seen in days.

"…You know what?" Jeteo said. "Lately I think it's actually a good thing that the two of us are so different."

"…Why?"

"…I think I only got this far because you kept waiting," Jeteo said.

I didn't know what to say to that, so I just leaned my head against Jeteo's shoulder.

Jeteo's shoulder had grown firmer than before. Muscle from helping with the well. That firmness, strangely, put my mind at ease. Jeteo was growing sturdier now, in body and in heart both.

I remember that laugh for a long time. I knew, then, that the two of us had arrived at the same place by different roads.

We sat like that for a while under the starlight. A silence that was comfortable even without a word.

Father came walking toward us then. He held a few small stones in his hand.

Father's footsteps were familiar now. Not careful, like before. I felt, in that walk, how much at ease Father had grown at this well. When Father had first come here, he'd tried not to make even the sound of his own footsteps.

Remembering that change, I thought of that first night we'd arrived. Father had hesitated at the doorway, again and again. As if asking permission to come in. Now he just strode straight in. I thought that difference was the greatest gift this well had given Father.

"…What were you doing?" Father asked.

"…Just sitting," Jeteo said.

Father sat down naturally beside us. It was the first time the three of us had sat side by side like that.

I was surprised by that naturalness. A few months earlier, Father would have found it unfamiliar even to sit beside us.

Father's shoulder touched mine lightly. I felt that warmth. It was warm. I thought that warmth was different from, yet somehow similar to, the warmth of that man from earlier.

I set those two warmths side by side in my mind. One was a father's warmth, the other a nameless guest's warmth. I found it strange that the two didn't compete with each other. If anything, they seemed to fill each other in.

I set down this moment exactly as it was. Father didn't know what we had talked about. But in that moment, just the three of us sitting side by side, I felt something being completed.

No one spoke for a long while. I felt that this silence was a different kind from the one we'd shared earlier in the day. This wasn't a silence weighing something — it was simply a comfortable silence.

I set those two silences side by side in my mind. One had been the silence before a heavy question; the other, the silence after passing through it. I found it strange that we had gone through both silences in a single day.

Jeteo leaned his body slightly toward Father. Father, sensing it, shifted his own posture a little too. I felt the distance between the two of them narrowing, bit by bit.

I measured that distance with my eyes. About a hand's width. A few months earlier, that hand's width would never have closed. I knew how many evenings it had taken to make that hand's width.

The sound of night insects wrapped around the three of us. I thought that sound was filling in our silence for us. No one spoke, and yet the space didn't feel empty.

The stones in Father's palm were each a different shape. Round ones, flat ones, one with a shining grain. I took in each stone with my eyes, one at a time.

One of them especially caught my eye. A gray stone with a thin white stripe running across it. I thought that pattern looked familiar somehow. It resembled a stone I'd seen by the stream in Brick Hollow. I couldn't tell if that connection was coincidence or not.

Moonlight fell softly over those stones. I thought that light made the stones look more special than usual.

I noticed that every one of those stones bore the trace of having been handled by Father's hands all day. Their surfaces were unusually smooth. I understood that the smoothness hadn't happened by itself — it was the mark left by Father's fingertips.

"…Why do you collect these stones?" I asked.

Father laid the stones out one by one in his palm and looked at them. His fingertips slowly stroked each one.

"…I don't know," Father said. "They just seem pretty. And… I feel like I want to give them to someone. I don't know who."

"…When did that start?" Jeteo asked.

Father tilted his head.

"…I don't remember," Father said. "But it feels like my hands knew before I did. My hand just reached for it, every time I saw a stone."

Hearing that, I felt I dimly understood what it might mean for the hand to know before the heart. Father's body had lost memories, but not the habit of loving.

Jeteo heard that and looked quietly at Father. He said nothing. But I saw tears well up faintly in his eyes.

"…Would you give me one?" I asked. The words came out before I knew it.

Father looked at me, startled. And chose a stone, pressing it into my hand. The smallest, smoothest one.

Father's fingertips lingered for a moment as he set that stone in my palm. In that brief touch, I felt something like a gesture prepared long in advance. As if Father had been choosing that stone for a long time, for this very moment.

"…So that was it," Father said quietly. "Who I wanted to give it to."

Relief crossed Father's face only then. Watching that expression, I thought Father must have been frustrated all along, not knowing why.

I held that stone tightly in my hand. It was warm. Father's hand's warmth still lingered in it. I didn't want to lose that warmth, so I didn't open my hand for a while.

Jeteo watched that beside us and laughed quietly.

I thought Jeteo's laugh had both envy and warmth mixed into it. Jeteo had always been someone who put his own share off to later. I hoped that this time, he wouldn't put it off.

"…I want one too," Jeteo said, playfully.

Father, flustered at that, looked back through the remaining stones. I thought that flustered look was endearing.

"…This one," Father said, handing Jeteo a slightly larger stone. "This one… somehow feels like yours."

Jeteo laughed, taking the stone.

"…Why is this one mine?"

"…I don't know," Father said. "It just feels that way."

I thought that answer was very Father. Father was beginning to trust a feeling now, even without knowing the reason.

I suddenly thought of the things we'd been given in Haven. Everything given there had a reason attached. Why this coat, why this bowl. There was no object without a reason. Only then did I realize — this stone was the first thing I'd ever been given with no reason at all. Strangely, that made it feel even more precious.

I still have that stone. I think that stone stands in for everything Father couldn't say in words.

I set the stone in my palm and looked at it for a long time. The white stripe glinted faintly in the moonlight. I traced that stripe with my fingertip. It was a pattern with no beginning and no end, just continuing on. I thought it looked exactly like the three of us. Not knowing where it started or where it would end, but going on all the same.

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. In that moment, I nearly cried. Father didn't know. But Father's body knew. Gathering something for someone — that had always been what Father did.

(Continued in Chapter 43.)

Chapter 43. What Mother Never Got to Say

_Previously_ — After taking the hand, Jeteo answered honestly: "The anger's still there. But now I know I don't have to carry that anger alone." Father, not knowing why, gathered stones because he wanted to give them to someone.

Several days passed peacefully like that. Father still gathered stones during the day, and Jeteo still helped Speon tend the well. I filled in my evening record between it all.

The days at the well always flowed in the same order. It began with the sound of Speon lowering the bucket at dawn; through the day, the footsteps of people coming to draw water continued; and as the sun sank, it grew quiet again. That repetition wasn't boring to me.

In Haven, a day had always ended in "processing." Here, a day simply drew to a close. I turned that difference over for days. A day that gets processed, and a day that simply closes. The same length, but what remained was different.

I still remember those days as days when nothing special happened at all. But strangely, that nothing-happening has stayed with me the longest.

Around then, I started writing down sounds instead of events in my evening record. The creak of the bucket, the clink of Father's spoon, the sound of Jeteo humming and trailing off. I didn't know, then, that these were the things I'd miss the most, later.

During those days, I carried the stone Jeteo had given me in my pocket all the time. I'd check its shape with my fingertips now and then. I kept touching it, for no reason, as if it were some kind of charm.

The stone always came to resemble the temperature of my body inside my pocket. I liked that. When I'd first received it, it had held Father's warmth; after a few days, it had become my own warmth. I thought that was how warmth traveled from one person to another.

Jeteo hadn't lost his stone either. I'd caught him more than once, stopping his well work to pat his pocket and check it was there. Jeteo didn't seem to notice that I'd seen it. I didn't say anything about it.

Father had been unusually quiet these past few days. But it wasn't an anxious quiet. I only realized later that it was a quiet of turning something over. At the time, I just thought Father must be tired.

During those days, Father often closed his eyes. While walking, while eating. I couldn't tell if that was sleeping or trying hard to remember something. Jeteo must have noticed it too — he moved more quietly than usual, so as not to disturb Father.

Once, Father stopped mid-meal with the spoon still in his hand. For quite a while. When Jeteo carefully touched his arm, Father startled and opened his eyes.

"…What were you looking at?" Jeteo asked.

"…I don't know," Father said. "Something keeps coming close and then going away. When I try to catch it, it disappears."

I copied that line into that night's record exactly as it was. I didn't know, then, what it meant. I just wrote it down because Father had said it.

I still think that's what a record is. Not writing something down because you know its meaning, but writing it down and coming to know its meaning later.

That night was unusually quiet. Even the wind was asleep.

Not even the insects were calling. I thought later that quiet had felt like the quiet right before something happens. But that's a thought I attached afterward. That night, it was simply quiet.

A few nights later, Father suddenly bolted upright out of sleep.

"…Jeteo," Father said. His eyes were open.

Jeteo woke with a start at the sound.

"…Me?" Jeteo asked. His voice trembled.

I held my breath in that instant. Father had spoken Jeteo's name for the first time. But Father's face was still not fully awake, still confused.

I felt that the voice calling that name sounded like something coming from inside a dream. A sound both unfamiliar and familiar.

"…It's all right, Father," I said. In that moment, the word "Father" came out naturally, without my meaning it to. Father paused for a moment at that too. But his face didn't look like he minded.

Father's hand was trembling. I carefully took hold of it. It was cold. I didn't let go until that coldness faded.

There was still a little dirt under Father's fingernails. Dirt from picking up stones during the day. Looking at that dirt, I thought that Father's whole day was written into that hand.

Holding his hand, I felt Father's pulse racing. Fast. I matched my own breathing to it, slow, hoping Father's would follow and slow down too. I don't know if it actually did.

The faint cry of night insects drifted in from outside. That sound felt unusually loud to me. Probably because all three of us were holding our breath.

Jeteo was still sitting there, watching Father. I saw fear and hope cross his face at the same time.

"…What did you dream about?" Jeteo asked carefully.

Father shook his head. And shook it again.

"…I don't think it was a dream," Father said. "It was… a memory, I think."

I heard that word from Father's mouth for the first time. Memory. Father had been avoiding that word all this while. Probably because it felt like naming something that wasn't there.

Jeteo leaned forward at that. I felt Jeteo wanting to rush to ask something, and at the same time, holding himself back. Jeteo knew a little now, how not to hurry.

Watching that restraint beside him, I felt again how far Jeteo had come. The old Jeteo from Brick Hollow would have grabbed Father and asked the same question over and over, that night. Now Jeteo simply waited.

Only a faint warmth remained where the fire had burned all the way down. I could just barely see Father's eyes in that dark. Father stared into the empty air for a moment, like someone trying to grasp something.

"…Your mother…" Father said, stumbling. "Not long before they took me away, she said this to me at night. That there was something she wanted to say to you, but she still couldn't."

Father said "your mother." I stumbled on those three words. Father had never called our mother that before. He hadn't been able to. But that night, the words simply came out, with no preparation at all.

I knew that Jeteo had noticed it too. His shoulder tensed slightly, then loosened again.

My hand shook as I wrote those three words into the record. From that night on, I came to know that for some words, where they're spoken matters more than what they mean.

Jeteo froze in place. I heard his breath stop for a moment.

"…Do you remember what she said?" Jeteo asked. His voice had cracked completely.

Hearing that question, I knew, in that instant, that Jeteo had been waiting for this moment for years. A question that might never get an answer — Jeteo had never given up on it.

I had a strange thought, right then. In Haven, this question would have had an answer within seconds of being filed. Under some title like "the most probable reconstruction of what the mother meant to say." And that answer probably would have been flawless.

But what Jeteo had been waiting for wasn't a plausible answer. Jeteo was waiting for Mother's actual words. The two looked alike, but they were entirely different things.

Father closed his eyes and strained for a long time. I watched that effort, gripping my hands tightly.

Father's lips moved a few times, then closed again. Watching that, I knew there was nothing to do but wait. Strangely, that waiting wasn't frightening.

"…Take your time," I said. "No rush."

Father nodded at that, eyes still closed.

"…I'm sorry," Father said. "It keeps breaking off."

"…It's all right," Jeteo said. His voice was a little steadier than before. "I'm here. I'm not going anywhere."

Father opened his eyes at that. He looked at Jeteo for a long time. I felt something growing clearer, bit by bit, in that gaze.

"…Not going anywhere," Father repeated the words back, quietly. In a small voice. "I feel like I've heard that a lot."

I swallowed at that moment. I knew — that was the very phrase we had said to Father, countless times. That phrase had been carved somewhere in Father's body.

Jeteo said nothing to that. He just gripped Father's hand tighter. I thought that silence was bigger than any words.

Speon woke to the sound and came out too. He didn't come close to us — he stood a little apart and watched. I knew that distance was his consideration.

Speon held a lantern in his hand, but he didn't shine it toward us. He held it low, just enough to light his own feet. I knew that angle had been practiced for a long time. A well-keeper was someone who knew how to light the path without lighting the person.

I asked Speon about it later — why he hadn't come closer, then. Speon said this: "…That wasn't my place to be in. But it was also a place that couldn't be left with no one in it." I found it strange, for a long time, that both of those sentences could be true at once.

Even the starlight was dim that night. In that dark, I heard Father's voice tremble. A trembling I'd never heard before.

"…'You'll always be a child who asks.'" Father finally said. Slowly, one word at a time. "'That's not a bad thing. I'm… proud of it.' I think that's what she was trying to say."

Jeteo sank to the ground on hearing that.

Jeteo's shoulders shook hard. I knelt beside him and laid a hand on his back. He didn't push my hand away.

Jeteo's back kept shaking beneath my palm. I didn't try to stop that shaking. I just kept my hand there and shook along with it. I knew that was all I could do, and I knew, too, that it wasn't too little.

Father knelt down beside him too. It was an awkward movement. But Father didn't hesitate. I saw the three of us tangled together like that, in one mass, for the first time that day.

I set down this moment exactly as it was. I saw Jeteo cry like that for the first time. Aloud, and hard. For the first time since we'd left Brick Hollow.

Hearing those words, I suddenly remembered that night we crossed the boundary stones. The night Mother called Jeteo's name, opened her mouth, and closed it again. "…No. Go and live. That's enough." I understood, then, for the first time, that what Mother had tried and failed to say that night had already been left with Father, in advance.

I don't remember Mother's face exactly, from that night. But her voice alone has stayed with me clearly. I know now that that voice, and the words Father had just given us, came from the same person.

I closed my eyes and tried to bring that voice back. It wasn't complete. But its warmth, at least, was still there, clearly.

I thought, that night, that the whole well-yard seemed to be listening to Jeteo's crying together with us. No one came out to stop him. They were simply listening.

I thought for a long time about how to put that crying into this record. In the end, I decided to write it just like this. Jeteo cried. Very long, very loud. And no one tried to hurry that crying to an end.

A long while later, Jeteo's crying began to fade. In that time, I saw Father sitting quietly beside him. Saying nothing, just keeping watch.

I've hardly ever seen anyone cry in Haven. Crying got filed, and stopped within a few minutes. Even the longest crying that city had ever seen probably never passed ten minutes.

That night, Jeteo cried far longer than that. No one filed it, and no one processed it. We simply all stayed there together until that crying ended.

After that night, I stopped seeing things that take a long time as bad. Some things are real because they take so long.

I thought again of Haven's intake window. There, a thing taking a long time was itself the mistake. If processing took too long, an apology came first. I didn't want to turn this night into something that owed an apology. It wasn't that I was sorry it took so long — I was grateful it had taken that long.

The edge of the sky was faintly brightening, as if dawn were near. Watching that light, I only then understood how long the night had been.

Jeteo wiped his tears and looked straight at Father. I felt he wanted to ask something more.

"…Wasn't Mother ashamed, that I kept asking so many questions?" Jeteo asked. To Father, or maybe just to the empty air.

Father laid a hand on Jeteo's shoulder. Still an awkward gesture. But there was something certain in that gesture.

Jeteo let that hand stay. He didn't push it off. I knew how great a change that small permission was.

"…She said she was proud," Father said again. "That much, I remember exactly."

Jeteo bowed his head. Tears fell again. This time, silent tears.

"…Thank you, for remembering," Jeteo managed to say.

Father gripped Jeteo's shoulder a little harder at that. No words. But I thought the strength of that hand alone was enough.

"…I didn't remember it," Father said, after a while. "It just… came back. I didn't do anything."

"…Even so," Jeteo said. "It stayed with you. That's enough."

I noticed, again, in that moment, that Jeteo still called Father "sir." But that word of address no longer sounded like distance to me now. It only meant that Jeteo still wasn't rushing.

Far off, the first bird cried. Once one called, others followed. I found it strangely odd, that day, that a bird's crying and a person's crying were called by the same word.

The sky was fully brightening. I only then realized this was the first time the three of us had stayed up through a whole night together.

Watching that night give way to dawn, I strangely didn't feel any regret. There are nights that are hard because they're long, and nights you regret because they're short. I still don't know, even now, which kind that night was. I only know that, by the end of it, the three of us had become a little more one.

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. I saw Jeteo receive an answer that night — to a question he had asked himself for years, "Is it wrong that I keep asking?" — from the latest, and the most unexpected, person of all.

(Continued in Chapter 44.)

Chapter 44. The Meaning of Our Names

_Previously_ — Father called Jeteo's name for the first time. And he remembered — the words Mother had left for Jeteo, in advance, the night before she was taken. "You'll always be a child who asks. I'm proud of that." That night, Jeteo cried aloud for the first time.

The seasons at the well passed quietly by. Days went by that looked, on the surface, as if nothing at all had changed.

Dew gathered on the grass tips each morning. I went out barefoot and liked how the tops of my feet grew wet, so I put my shoes on late on purpose. The clothes of the people coming to draw water were growing thicker, bit by bit. The changing of the seasons always announced itself that way — without anyone ever saying a word.

I wrote those days down as "days when nothing happened." But reading it again now, those were the days right before a certain word was about to arrive. I didn't know that, then.

During those days, I found myself calling Jeteo's name unusually often. For no reason. Just because I wanted to. Jeteo didn't find it odd, and answered every time. I thought it was like some small practice the two of us were doing together.

A few days later, Father called us with a clearer face than before.

That morning, Father had woken unusually early. His eyes were clearer than usual. I found that clarity unfamiliar, but welcome.

Father was already sitting on the stone by the door. Hands on his knees, looking toward where the sun was rising. I thought that back looked like the back of someone waiting for something.

I had seen Father like that a few times before. Every time his memory came back a little, Father grew quiet like that. As if he were holding his body still until something settled into place, inside.

"…I remember who named you two," Father said.

I stopped what I was doing at that. The bucket handle in my hand nearly slipped. I knew, just from Father's expression, that this wasn't just some question passing by.

Jeteo felt the same way. From Brick Hollow up to now, we had never once asked what our names meant. A name had just been a name. Something you were called by. That was all.

I felt something strange in that moment. We had called each other's names hundreds of times over. But it had never once occurred to me that something might be held inside those names.

"…Have you ever wondered what your name means?" I asked.

"…No, never," Jeteo said. "I always thought a name was just a name."

"…Me too," I said.

I suddenly thought of something from Haven. In that city, filing a name would instantly bring up its etymology, its origin, statistics. I hadn't once thought to ask about that, back then. Strange, thinking of it now. It wasn't that I hadn't been curious. Any answer received in that city had always felt like it belonged to someone else.

I know now. Who gives an answer is half of what the answer contains. The meaning of a name the Ledger might have told me, and the meaning Father stumbled through to tell me, would have been the same sentence — and yet different things.

I saw something like fear cross Jeteo's face in that moment. That knowing what your name meant might be a frightening thing — I felt that for the first time, then.

I understood that fear. Knowing the meaning of a name meant that someone had known us before we'd ever been set into the world. I found that both welcome and, somehow, unsettling.

Father sat us down beside the stone wall at the well. The morning sunlight fell slanting over the three of us.

The stone wall had cooled overnight, so it was chilly at first. But as we sat there, the sunlight moved across it little by little, and by the time the story ended, our backs had grown warm. From that day on, I thought of that temperature every time I passed that wall.

Before sitting, Father swept a hand once across the top of the wall. A gesture of brushing off dust. I understood that it was Father's way of tidying our seat before he sat us down. Father still did things like that without a word.

I noticed Father's voice was unusually clear that day. As if he didn't want to miss a single word of this story.

"…Your mother named you," Father said. "There was an old language, from her hometown. A language that gives names meaning. Your mother named you both with that language."

Hearing that, I realized I had never once asked about Mother's hometown. To us, Mother had always just been "Mother." We never knew where she was from.

I found that fact strangely sad. There was still so much we didn't know about Mother.

I didn't know what Mother had liked as a child, what songs she'd known, what she'd been afraid of. I only knew her as Mother. But Mother had been someone's daughter too, once.

I still haven't filled in those blanks. But now, that emptiness isn't only sad. That there's still something I don't know also means there's still something left to ask.

"…What does it mean?" Jeteo asked.

I heard Jeteo's voice tremble faintly as he asked that question.

Father worked hard to dredge up the memory for a moment.

I felt my heart pound loudly in that silence. I quietly took Jeteo's hand. He took mine back.

Jeteo's palm was damp. I knew from that how tense he was. His face gave nothing away. His hand had spoken first.

A gust of wind passed by and stirred Father's hair across his forehead. Father didn't brush it back. His eyes stayed closed, and his lips moved soundlessly a few times. I thought Father was rehearsing the words to himself first, inside.

"…Jeteo means… 'to seek,' 'to search,'" Father said. "Your mother told me. 'This child will spend his whole life asking. That'll be his name.'"

My breath caught, hearing that. I looked at Jeteo. He couldn't say a word, just stood there. That name overlapped exactly with every way Jeteo had lived, from Brick Hollow all the way to now.

I saw Jeteo's hand trembling faintly. That hand still carried the calluses of the bucket rope.

In that moment, I remembered, all at once, every question Jeteo had ever thrown out, from Brick Hollow to this well. Why did this happen to us. If God is there, why did he just let it happen. Isn't there a real answer besides that one.

Haven had filed every one of those questions as an "unresolved complaint." The Administrator saw Jeteo as someone bad at intake. Even I sometimes found myself frustrated by why he kept pressing so hard.

But it hadn't been a flaw. It had been the name he'd been called by since the day he was born.

Jeteo opened his mouth a few times and shut it again. I could tell he was trying not to cry. He didn't cry. Instead, he let out one very long breath.

"…So did I just… live according to my name?" Jeteo asked. His voice was mixed with confusion.

"…Or maybe the name followed you," I said. "Either way, isn't it strange?"

Jeteo couldn't answer that. He just gazed off into the distance. I knew that he would turn that question over for a long time.

"…Did she like that about me?" Jeteo asked, after a long while. "That I kept pressing so hard."

"…It wasn't so much that she liked it," Father said slowly. "I think she knew it was just who you were. She never tried to fix it."

Jeteo bowed his head at that. I saw the back of his neck flush.

I understood, then. What Jeteo had truly feared for so long wasn't the absence of an answer. It was that he might be someone made wrong.

"…Does that man know? What my name means?" Jeteo asked suddenly.

Father tilted his head.

"…Who's that man?" Father asked back.

Jeteo paused for a moment at the question, then laughed a little.

"…I'll explain it to you later," Jeteo said.

I knew Jeteo hadn't told Father everything about the man yet. But he wasn't in a hurry. Some stories only came out when their time arrived.

I sat quietly beside Jeteo. I added nothing more. I already knew now that some moments are enough with just being beside someone, and nothing else.

Jeteo turned the stone in his hand over, again and again. I knew that gesture was a sign he was digesting something. Words came late, but his hands always moved first.

I had hesitated a long time before asking my own question. Even more so, having just heard Jeteo's name.

Jeteo looked toward me. As if he'd read my mind, he gave a small nod. It seemed to mean it was all right to ask.

Father was watching me too, waiting. In that waiting, I felt, strangely, at ease.

"…What about Elpis?" I asked. My voice trembled.

The moment I asked, I grew afraid. Jeteo's name had fit him exactly. Would mine, too? Or what if I turned out to be the one whose name didn't match. I had those thoughts, in those few seconds.

Father looked at me for a long time. Then he smiled. Not the smile of someone who'd just recalled the answer — the smile of someone glad for the answer itself.

"…Hope," Father said. "Your mother told me. 'This child will keep waiting, even with no reason at all. That'll be her name.'"

Tears poured out of me the moment I heard that. I thought of the seed, the flowerbed, the heart that had watered it every day, waiting. I had never known why I was such a good waiter. Now I know. It was my name.

I thought, in that moment, of Jeteo's hand handing me the seed pouch, that last night in Brick Hollow. I hadn't known the reason then. Now I did. I had simply taken up that seed, according to my name.

Strangely, I wasn't ashamed of crying. In Haven, I would have filed this crying. Here, I just cried. Father clumsily patted my back. Completely out of rhythm. I liked that mismatched rhythm.

During those days, I kept thinking about why I'd been able to wait so long. It wasn't because I was especially good. It wasn't because my faith was great. I was simply a child who waited. And someone had recognized that in me beforehand, and given it as my name.

I think that's proof I was loved. Because it's a name only someone who has watched you a long time could ever give.

"…Isn't the name Hope heavy?" Jeteo asked.

I laughed at that question.

Jeteo never said his own name was heavy. Worrying about the weight of someone else's name first — that was very Jeteo.

"…It feels heavy sometimes," I said. "But not in a bad way."

Jeteo quietly wrapped an arm around my shoulder. He said nothing. That touch alone was enough.

We sat like that for a long while without speaking. The wind brushed over the grass at the well.

The sun had climbed fairly high by then. Someone who'd come to draw water greeted us, seeing us there. We returned the greeting. It was a morning where nothing at all seemed to have changed.

And yet, from that morning on, my name sounded a little different every time it was called. The same two syllables, but there was something inside them now.

From that day on, I paused for a moment every time someone called my name. When Jeteo called out "Elpis," I felt I could hear, inside that call, even the heart Mother had held the very first time she gave me that name. I thought it was a strange habit, but I didn't want to break it.

Far off, Speon glanced our way while tidying the well. He had a face like he'd noticed something. But he didn't come closer.

Instead, he made the sound of the bucket a little quieter than usual. I noticed that. A well-keeper was someone who cared for people by handling things quietly.

That evening, I told Speon what our names meant. He listened for a long while and then only said this:

"…Good names. Both of them."

"…What does your name mean?" I asked.

Speon laughed.

"…I don't know either," he said. "But I've been called well-keeper longer than I've been called by my name. There are more names for a person than just their given name."

"…Do you think Mother knew we'd turn out like this?" Jeteo asked.

Father shook his head. His face showed he was thinking hard about the question.

"…She probably didn't know that," Father said. "But I think she knew what kind of people you'd become. A seeker. A waiter."

"…Then what about you, Father?" I asked. "Does your name have a meaning too?"

Father looked startled by that question. It seemed like a question he'd never once thought about. Father looked down at his own hands for a moment. As if the answer might be written there.

Watching that expression, I felt something strange. We had called Father's name hundreds of times. But this was the first time we'd ever wondered what it meant.

"…I don't know," Father said. "My name… I don't really know either."

I found those words sad. Father had given us back the meaning of our names, but he still hadn't found his own. I thought that showed exactly how Father had lived, these past few years.

"…Then we'll find it for you," Jeteo said. "Someday."

Father smiled at that. There was a faint gleam of moisture at his eyes.

Watching that smile, I imagined the day we would truly find the meaning of Father's name. I hoped that day, too, the three of us would be together, just like now.

I set down that smile in this record. I knew, then. What mattered more than knowing the meaning of a name was that the person who calls that name is there, beside you.

Jeteo looked at me at that. I looked at Jeteo. We nodded to each other without a word.

Father looked back and forth between the two of us and smiled softly. A smile I hadn't seen in a long time.

That evening, I called the three of our names to myself, side by side, in my mind. Jeteo, Elpis, and Father's name, whose meaning we hadn't found yet. I decided to wait for the day that third name would be filled in too, someday.

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. I understood, that day, for the first time. We hadn't lived according to the meaning of our names. We had simply lived as ourselves, and that life had been inside our names from the very start. I only understood, after a long time thinking it over, how great that difference was.

(Continued in Chapter 45.)

Chapter 45. News Temel Brought

_Previously_ — Father revealed the meaning of our names. Jeteo meant "to seek, to search"; Elpis meant "hope." It came out that everything about how the siblings had lived had already been inside their names.

Around the time autumn arrived, Temel stopped by the well again.

Leaves were just beginning to color. I thought the color was different from the autumn I'd known in Brick Hollow. Brick Hollow's autumn had been the color of dust. Here, autumn was red and gold.

The trees around the well were shedding their leaves, one by one. Every morning I gathered a few of the fallen ones. There wasn't really a reason. I just felt it would be a waste to leave them.

I pressed the leaves I'd gathered between the pages of my record. After a few days, the leaves flattened out and darkened in color. I found that both a shame and something I liked. The leaves didn't lose their shape even as they dried.

I still have those leaves. Some have crumbled, some have survived. I think that's a lot like what a record is. You can't keep everything, and what you do keep isn't exactly as it first was. But it survives.

On every page where I'd pressed a leaf, I wrote a short line about what had happened that day. Nothing big, mostly. Jeteo fixing the bucket, Father humming a new tune. I liked pressing those small things flat, together with the leaves.

Father was the first to see Temel coming that day. He looked toward the well, then raised a hand and pointed toward the hill. He said nothing. I thought about when Father had started being the first to notice someone coming.

"…It's been a while," Jeteo said, greeting him warmly. His face was far more at ease than it had been months earlier.

Temel's walk looked lighter than before, too. I wondered where that lightness came from. His pack looked lighter too.

Temel saw Father and smiled.

"…You've gotten a lot better," Temel said.

Father looked at Temel, curious.

"…You were the one who first came looking for me, weren't you?" Father asked. Not an exact memory. But something seemed to remain.

"…That's right," Temel said, his face full of surprise.

Temel bent his knees to meet Father at eye level.

"…You didn't even have a name back then," Temel said quietly. "And now you recognize me like this."

Father smiled a little at that. A smile like he found it surprising himself.

"…What was I like, when you first found me?" Father asked.

Temel chose his words for a moment. The firelight cast shadows across Temel's face.

"…There was nothing there," Temel said honestly. "No name, no expression. But your eyes were looking for something. That was why I didn't give up on you."

Father was silent for a long time, hearing that. I felt he was carving those words somewhere in his heart.

"…Why didn't you give up?" Father asked, after a while.

Temel looked a little surprised at the question. No one had probably ever asked him that before.

"…I lost my own name once too," Temel said. "Someone didn't let go of me, then. I'm just doing what I received."

I remember that answer for a long time. Temel didn't say he was some great person. He said he was doing what he'd received. I later learned that every adult I met at this well spoke that same way.

I thought, in that moment, of the Administrator in Haven. The Administrator had been proud of the work he did. Temel spoke of his work more like the paying off of a debt. I thought that difference in attitude mattered more than the work itself.

Father nodded. And briefly took Temel's hand, then let it go. It was a strange gesture — not quite a greeting, not quite a handshake. Temel took it without any awkwardness.

That evening, we sat together around the fire. Speon threw a few more logs on. The flames grew, casting a red light over our faces.

The dry wood crackled as it burned. A few sparks rose and vanished. I followed those sparks with my eyes. As one went out, another rose.

There had been no fire in Haven. To put it precisely, there hadn't needed to be. That city was always just the right warmth, just the right brightness. I'd thought, back then, that was a good thing.

Even now, I don't write that the city was a bad place. Only that in that city, there had been no reason for people to sit gathered around anything. Without a fire, no one gathers. Without gathering, no stories come out. I think that may have been the real reason that city was so quiet.

Temel passed on the news of the world outside.

"…Curious about Haven?" Temel asked.

Jeteo nodded. There was no anger in it like before. Just curiosity. I felt that difference again, watching from the side.

"…Still quiet," Temel said. "But I've heard there's been a crack in that quiet lately. More and more people are refusing COMPLETE, refusing to file at all."

Hearing that, I felt strangely moved. We hadn't gone there to change that city. But something seemed to have taken root in the place we'd left behind.

"…Aren't those people in danger?" I asked.

"…They are," Temel said honestly. "But it doesn't seem to stop. Once someone starts, I guess it gets a little easier for the next person."

"…Who started it first?" Jeteo asked.

"…Don't know," Temel said. "Apparently they started without leaving a name. Just refused to file, that's all."

Jeteo nodded quietly at that. I felt he was holding that nameless person somewhere in his heart.

"…So do they just stay in pain, then?" I asked.

"…Yeah," Temel said. "If you don't file, nothing gets resolved."

"…Then why do they do it?"

Temel stared into the fire for a while before answering.

"…They said if you take the pain away, the person seems to disappear with it."

A chill ran down my spine at that. I thought of the backs of the people I'd seen walking away in Haven, holding papers stamped COMPLETE. They'd all looked at ease. At ease, and yet somehow thin.

Jeteo leaned a little toward the fire.

"…I think I know that feeling," Jeteo said. "When they told me to file about Father, I was afraid. I was afraid that if I filed it, Father would really disappear."

Temel looked at Jeteo. He didn't add anything. He just nodded.

Father was listening to that too. His expression was complicated. I felt Father react, faintly, every time he heard the name Haven. Not a complete memory. But his body seemed to sense it first.

"…Are you all right?" I asked Father.

"…Yes," Father said. "It's just… a name that's unfamiliar, but not unfamiliar."

Jeteo moved closer to Father's side.

"…You don't have to talk about it, if you don't want to," Jeteo said.

"…No," Father said. "I want to know. I want to know more about my own story too."

Hearing that, I thought Father had grown far braver than before. That willingness to face his own past without fear — I found it admirable.

I thought about where that courage of Father's had come from. It wasn't that he wasn't afraid of living without an answer. It was, I think, because he'd come to know there was someone who would stay beside him even without the answer. I thought that was the same thing Jeteo had learned.

"…What about the Administrator?" I asked.

"…Still there," Temel said. "But not long ago, I heard someone secretly made a room where people keep remembering the names of those who've been COMPLETEd. They say the Administrator knows about that room and looks the other way."

I thought of the Administrator's face in that moment. That face saying "That isn't mine to answer." I thought that maybe he was still resisting, in his own way, from inside that city.

"…Does that room have a name?" I asked.

"…Not one, they say," Temel said. "Just a room, apparently. But people call it 'the remembering room.'"

I repeated that name to myself, several times, in my mind. The remembering room. I thought that name resembled the thing we were doing here. Right in the middle of Haven, someone was secretly doing the very same thing we'd learned here.

"…What do they do there?" Jeteo asked.

"…Not much, apparently," Temel said. "They say the names of people who've been COMPLETEd out loud. That's all."

"…That's it?"

"…Yeah. That's it."

I thought that answer stayed with me longer for being so small. Calling names aloud. That was the biggest act of resistance that city could hold.

I've never seen that room. I still can't be sure, even now, that it really exists. But I set that rumor down in this ledger anyway. Not because I wish it were true, but because the fact that such a rumor could spread at all means there were still people left inside that city.

Father listened to this and said quietly,

"…Did someone say my name there too? In that place?"

No one could answer. Jeteo reached his hand toward Father and laid it over his.

"…It's been said here," Jeteo said. "Every day."

Speon spoke up then, joining the conversation for the first time.

"…Any news of Mena?" Speon asked. His voice made a poor effort at sounding unbothered.

Temel answered, holding back a laugh.

"…She's doing fine. Still walking around out there. Though I hear she's setting her direction this way more often than she used to."

Speon said nothing to that. But I saw the tips of his ears turn faintly red.

Watching that, I couldn't help laughing. It struck me, oddly tenderly, that even someone who'd lived through so many years turned out just the same as anyone else, in a moment like this.

Speon poked at the firewood needlessly. It didn't need poking. The fire dimmed for a moment and then flared back up.

"…Drink some water," Speon said, in a tone aimed at no one in particular.

Temel burst out laughing. I laughed along too. Even Jeteo laughed. Father laughed along too, not knowing what the joke was about. I thought that was the best part of the whole evening.

"…What about Noa?" Jeteo asked.

"…She's well. Her tent camp's grown even bigger. More and more people out there remembering names, they say."

I thought of Noa's face. I thought, too, of the lights of that tent camp where she'd first taken us in. I thought of the song the children had sung under those lights. I could still hum it, even now.

I hummed a little of that song right there. Father heard the sound and lifted his head.

"…Where did you hear that?" Father asked.

"…At the tent camp," I said.

Father listened for a moment, then began humming a few phrases along. His pitch was a little off. But he followed it through to the end. I couldn't tell, then, whether Father had heard that song somewhere before, or was simply following the sound of my voice. I still don't know now.

"…The children must have grown too," I said.

"…Grown a lot," Temel said. "Some of them are looking after younger children now, themselves. That's how time flows."

Jeteo laughed quietly at that.

"…It's strange," Jeteo said. "We were just running away, and something's growing wherever we ran through."

Temel looked at Jeteo quietly at that.

"…That's just how seeds are," Temel said. "They only really start growing once they've left the hand that planted them."

Hearing that, I thought of my flowerbed. That sprout, smaller than a fingernail — it would be growing, bit by bit, even now, while I wasn't watching.

I've never seen that flowerbed again. That was a long ache for me. It's a little less of one now. Not seeing it again doesn't mean it isn't growing.

I thought, in that moment, of the seed pouch Jeteo had given me. Jeteo had offered no explanation at all, handing it to me. I felt now that I understood what that silence had meant. Some things, you don't need a reason to plant.

"…Temel," Jeteo said. "Can I ask you something?"

"…Sure."

"…Doesn't it wear you out? Finding people."

Temel thought for a moment.

"…It wears me out," Temel said. "But being worn out and wanting to quit turn out to be different things."

Jeteo nodded at that. I saw him tuck that sentence away somewhere. Jeteo had taken to keeping people's words like that, lately.

Temel stayed with us late into the night. At dawn the next day, he set off again. We saw him off as far as the edge of the well.

"…You'll come back?" I asked.

I noticed my own voice growing a little urgent as I asked. I was still bad at sending people off. Ever since Brick Hollow, a departing back had always been, for me, a little frightening.

"…Yeah," Temel said. "This place has become important to me too now."

Father looked like he wanted to say something too, then. But the words wouldn't come, so he just handed Temel a stone instead. The one with the white stripe, picked up a few days earlier.

Temel took the stone and looked at it for a long time.

"…Thank you," Temel said. "I'll carry this in my pocket."

Father smiled at that. I thought Father had finally come to fully understand why he'd been collecting stones.

The dawn air was cold. A light frost lay over the grass. I heard the footsteps crunching over it, crisper than usual.

Temel's figure grew smaller and smaller, then disappeared over the hill. I waved my hand until that figure was gone.

Speon watched that departing back for a long time too. I saw both longing and hope held together in his eyes.

Jeteo walked over to Speon and nudged his shoulder.

"…Next time, grab hold of her first," Jeteo said, teasing.

Speon cleared his throat at that and looked away, pretending not to hear. I couldn't help laughing at that.

I looked for a long time down that road that had disappeared over the hill. Temel lived on the road; we had put down roots at the well. I thought those two lives, though different, were headed toward the same place. Someone searches, someone stays. Both were ways of refusing to forget people.

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. I knew, then. A seed grows even when the one who planted it can't see it the whole way through. We were learning that fact with our own bodies.

(Continued in Chapter 46.)

Chapter 46. A Moment Like a Father

_Previously_ — Temel brought news from outside. Haven is still quiet, but more and more people are refusing COMPLETE. "Wherever we ran through, something's growing."

Autumn faded fully away. As winter drew near, a cold wind began to blow through the well-yard.

Nearly all the leaves had fallen. A thin frost began settling on the stone wall around the well. I picked up a new habit of rubbing that frost with a fingertip, every morning.

Wherever my fingertip touched, the frost melted away and the dark stone showed through. I'd leave a few of those marks in a row and step back. Once the sun rose, those marks vanished along with the rest of the frost. I didn't mind that. I could always make new ones the next morning.

I never felt the seasons change in Haven. That city was always just right. I thought, back then, that being just right was a good thing. Now I like a morning when my hands are cold. Because when they're cold, you can lend someone your coat.

Every time I made those frost marks, I called for Jeteo. He was annoyed by it at first, but later crouched down beside me too, making marks of his own. We'd leave our handprints side by side in the frost and watch together as the sun erased them.

The well water grew colder as winter came on. But strangely, the water we drew up in the morning would give off a faint steam. Speon said it was because the ground underneath was warmer than the air above. I thought that sounded like something you'd say about people.

Father seemed to love winter especially. I didn't know why. He just breathed the cold air in deep and made a face like he loved it. I thought that expression looked familiar. In Brick Hollow, too, Father's face had looked like that whenever winter came.

I was making do with just one thin coat. One morning, Father took off his own coat and wrapped it around my shoulders.

"…You looked cold," Father said. As if it were nothing.

I could feel Father's warmth still lingering in that coat. It was warm.

The coat smelled of earth and smoke. The smell of someone who spent the day picking up stones, then sat beside the hearth each evening. I realized, then, that the smell had turned into Father's smell. In Brick Hollow, Father had always smelled of dry clay.

After giving me the coat, Father immediately looked away, as if not wanting to give me a chance to say thank you. I found that rushing away familiar.

Speon watched that from beside us and smiled quietly. I thought I understood why. Speon must have seen countless small gestures like that here at the well.

"…Your father's gotten a lot better," Speon said.

"…I think so," I said. "But lately I'm not sure what 'better' even means. He can do things like that even without all his memory back."

Speon nodded at that.

"…Maybe that's the real recovery," Speon said. "Not memory — love left in the body."

I couldn't answer for a long moment, hearing that. I held those words close for a long time. I didn't know, then, that they would stay with me the whole winter through.

"…But what," I finally managed to ask, "if the memory never comes back at all?"

Speon's hand paused where it had been coiling the bucket rope.

"…Then it just doesn't come back," he said. "I can't call that a good thing. That's a loss. A real loss."

I felt oddly at ease at that honesty. Speon never invented a comfort that wasn't there.

"…But just because a loss happened doesn't mean what's left becomes fake," Speon said. "That's a different matter."

I copied those words into that night's record. I understood, then, a little, why Haven had been so good at comfort and yet never comforting at all. That city always tried to make loss into something that had never happened. Speon named a loss a loss, and then sat down beside it.

I set those two sentences side by side and studied them for a long time. "A loss happened." And "I'm still beside you, even so." I learned, only here at this well, that the two could come from the same mouth, at the same time.

I couldn't say a word in that moment. It was such a small gesture. But inside that gesture was something that had come before name or memory.

I knew, then. Being a father wasn't about remembering — it was about noticing the cold first. Father didn't know that. But he was already doing it.

Wrapping that coat around myself, I thought of Brick Hollow. Every winter, Father had always given us his coat first. Back then I'd just thought it was natural. Now I know how far from natural that was.

"…Are you cold?" I asked.

"…I'm fine," Father said, smiling, even though he looked cold himself. I found that lie strangely touching.

I secretly studied Father while wearing that coat. There were goosebumps faintly rising on his arms. I pretended not to see.

I still think that pretending-not-to-see was the greatest gift I could give Father. Some kindness is only complete when you pretend not to have noticed it at all.

A little while later, I pulled one side of the coat back and draped it over Father too. It was too small a coat for two people to share. Father laughed.

"…This way we'll both be cold," Father said.

"…Then let's both just be a little cold," I said.

Father looked at me for a long moment at that. And said nothing, just stayed like that. We stood there, sharing one coat, beside the frost-covered stone wall.

I felt, right there, Father's arm wrap lightly around my shoulder. An awkward strength. I thought that awkwardness made it feel more real, somehow. I felt that clumsy arm would stay with me longer than any practiced embrace ever could.

The wind was fierce all that day. Jeteo stayed out late with Speon, mending the bucket rope. I watched them through the window.

Even after the sun went down, the two of them stayed out a while longer. Once it grew dark, Father took a lantern outside and stood beside them. Father didn't do anything. He just stood there, holding the light.

Through the window, I watched the three of them — the two mending the rope, and the one lighting them from above. I knew, right there, that this picture would stay with me a long time.

Jeteo saw that scene too. That evening, he said to me,

"…I still don't know if Father remembers me completely. But today, seeing that, I understood. Even without full memory, Father is already Father."

"…How do you know?"

"…This morning he tried to pick up the water jar before I could," Jeteo said. "He used to do that too. Whenever we carried something heavy, he'd always take it away from us."

I nodded, hearing that. I'd seen similar moments myself, more than once.

"…You can just tell, from what he does without thinking," Jeteo said. "Action came before words, with Father."

Hearing that, I remembered that the old Jeteo never used to talk this way. He always used to want proof. Now Jeteo was saying that a single gesture was enough.

"…I feel the same way," I said. "Lately there are things I believe even without proof."

Jeteo glanced at me at that. He said nothing. But something like agreement showed on his face.

"…That doesn't mean I've come to believe everything," Jeteo said, after a moment. "I still don't know why Father had to go through that. No one can explain that."

"…Yeah," I said.

"…But it's strange," Jeteo said. "You can still eat dinner even without an explanation. That used to be impossible for me."

I copy that down exactly, into this ledger. Jeteo never did get his answer, to the end. I write it down, not hiding it. Only, Jeteo learned how to live without an answer. I think that was harder than getting an answer.

The night wind seeped in through the window frame. I pulled the blanket a little closer and sat right up against Jeteo. He didn't push me away, either.

Jeteo brought the firewood inside. It had been left outside a long while, so its surface was damp. Father took it and stood the pieces up beside the hearth to dry. No one had told him to.

Once the fire caught, the room grew warm. That night, Father sat us down on either side of him and asked,

"…Would you tell me some old stories? About me. What kind of person I was."

Jeteo hesitated for a moment at that, then began slowly. The days in Brick Hollow, the stories Father used to tell us every evening, the calluses on Father's hands.

I added a few things too. The tune Father used to hum while hauling bricks; the way his hand stroked our hair every evening; his face that never once grew angry, no matter how hard things got.

Speaking of it, I realized I remembered far more about Father than I'd thought. Nothing big, mostly. Small things, all of it. How Father took off his shoes, which side of his jaw he chewed with when he ate, how his eyes crinkled when he laughed.

That was when I first saw what memory is made of. Memory wasn't made of events — it was made of habits. What Father had lost was an event. What remained in Father was a habit. That was why Father was still Father, even not knowing who he had been.

"…And you always went to bed after us," I said. "You'd check we were asleep before you closed your own eyes."

"…That's right," Jeteo added. "You never once slept first."

Father smiled a little, hearing that. A shy sort of smile.

"…I think that's still true," Father said. "Even now, my eyes only close after I've seen you two asleep."

Father closed his eyes several times, listening to that story.

"…Did I ever get angry?" Father asked.

Jeteo thought for a moment.

"…There was one time," Jeteo said. "When I talked back to a taskmaster. That was the first time you ever shouted."

"…Why?"

"…You must have been scared," Jeteo said. "Scared I'd get beaten."

Father sat quietly with that for a long time.

"…That's a good kind of anger," Father said.

I found that phrase staying with me for a long time, strangely. A good kind of anger. I'd never heard, until that day, that such a thing could exist.

No one spoke for a while. In that silence, I felt Father piecing together the fragments of his own life, one at a time.

"…Tell me more?" Father asked. Eyes still closed.

Jeteo picked the story back up again. The days we'd had to hide from the taskmasters; and even so, Father's voice in the evenings, telling us old stories. Listening, I felt I could smell Brick Hollow again.

"…Weren't you scared of them? The taskmasters," Father asked.

"…We were," Jeteo said. "But you being beside us made it less scary."

"…I don't remember any of it, but hearing that, strangely, it puts my mind at ease," Father said.

"…Why?" I asked.

"…It means I was of use," Father said. "Even then, too."

Something stung at the tip of my nose at that. Father still didn't fully know how great a presence he had been to us.

"…What a good person he was, that man," Father said. He said it as if speaking of someone else, and yet there was something stirring in his voice.

"…That man is you, Father," Jeteo said quietly.

I knew how long it had taken Jeteo to say those words. For months, he'd called Father "sir." That hadn't been dislike — it had been carefulness. Jeteo hadn't wanted to force the name of "Father" onto him.

Father nodded at that. Slowly, but clearly.

I thought that nod carried more weight than any words could have. Father accepted that title with no conditions attached at all. Even without his memory fully returned, he was ready to fill that place.

Father wrapped both arms around the two of us. An awkward gesture. But its strength was certain.

"…Thank you," Father said. "For giving my own story back to me."

Tears welled in my eyes at that. Jeteo must have felt the same, because he buried his face in Father's shoulder.

I thought, right then, of the last night in Brick Hollow, the moment Father had held us for the last time. That moment and this one overlapped. A different place, a different season — but the same warmth.

The time between those two moments hadn't erased anything. The day Father had been taken away, the night we crossed the boundary stones, the day we'd stood before the intake window in Haven — it was all still there, unchanged. I no longer wished for that to disappear.

I hesitated a long time before writing this part. I felt the temptation to write "everything was restored." That isn't true. Father never remembered a great many things, to the end. We never got back the years we lost.

But what was in that room that night wasn't recovery — it was something else. I still haven't found a name for it. So I'll just set that night down exactly as it was.

The fire in the hearth slowly died down. We stayed like that a while, holding each other. No one moved first.

Outside, the wind kept blowing. Cold air seeped in through the gap in the door. But no one got up to fix it.

I didn't write that night's record. I couldn't pull my arms free. I ended up writing it the next morning, and because a day had passed by the time I wrote it, a few details had already blurred.

I don't mind that. Some nights are better lived in than written down. I've learned that too, in my years as a recorder.

A few days later, I added a few more sentences, thinking back on that night again. A record doesn't get finished all at once, that way. I decided to think of that, too, as this ledger's own way — that some pages are allowed to fill in slowly.

Whenever I think of that night, I remember the ember that glowed red the longest inside the hearth. The flames had all died down, but that ember alone kept its warmth for a long time. I thought Father was like that ember too. On the outside, he looked entirely gone out. But somewhere inside, there was always warmth left.

I set down those words as the last line of this chapter. I knew, that day. Father was learning his own story all over again. And the two people who knew that story best were sitting right beside him now.

(Continued in Chapter 47.)

Chapter 47. The Supper We Shared

_Previously_ — Father, unable to explain why, took off his coat and wrapped it around Elpis. That night, he asked to hear stories of his own past, and listening to what the siblings told him, said, "What a good person he was, that man."

On the day of the first snow, Mena came again. This time, she wasn't alone.

The snow fell without a sound. I was watching it pile white on the stone wall around the well when I heard several voices, far off. At first I thought it was the wind.

It almost never snowed in Brick Hollow. When it did, the moment it touched the ground it mixed with dirt and turned gray. I saw snow pile up white for the first time here, at this well.

There had been no snow at all in Haven. That city's sky was always the same brightness. I'd thought, back then, that this was convenient. Now I'm not even sure that city had a sky at all.

I've marked that day, in this ledger, as the loudest day in it. I wondered if it was strange to write down a loud day in a weeping ledger. But looking back on it now, this is the most important chapter in the whole book.

Noa and the children had come too. They said they'd heard the news and walked a long way.

The children ran around screaming as if seeing snow for the first time. Listening to that laughter fill the well-yard, my throat tightened, strangely.

One child balled up snow and threw it at Jeteo. He didn't dodge — he just took the hit. Then he balled up his own snow and threw it back. In an instant, a snowball fight had broken out. Watching that, I tried to work out how long it had been since I'd seen Jeteo laugh like that.

I gave up counting. It had been too long.
